### [1] [Xemo-Talker: Unlock Emotions Explicitly for Audio-Driven Talking Portrait Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14700)
*Chaolong Yang,Yinuo Guo,Kai Yao,Yuyao Yan,Jie Sun,Guangliang Cheng,Shibin Wu,Bin Dong,Kaizhu Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Xemo-Talker is a framework for precise emotion control in audio-driven talking heads. It discovers that focusing discriminative supervision on less-principal motion components (rather than the full motion space) achieves a better balance between lip synchronization and emotion control. It uses a neutral speech-to-motion backbone with a lightweight emotion branch guided by less-principal subspace supervision and a Tri-Loss for emotion control, achieving state-of-the-art emotion accuracy with competitive lip sync and high efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio-driven talking heads rely on implicit emotion regulation, leading to indirect and insufficient emotion control. Training with explicit emotion-related losses across the entire motion space suffers from a trade-off between accurate lip synchronization and fine-grained emotion control.

Method: The method first learns a neutral speech-to-motion mapping for stable articulation and lip sync, then introduces a lightweight emotion branch guided by less-principal subspace supervision. A Tri-Loss is designed consisting of inter-class separation, intra-class compactness, and less-principal contrastive learning. Inputs include audio, reference image, and emotion label.

Result: Xemo-Talker achieves state-of-the-art emotion classification accuracy while maintaining competitive lip synchronization and high inference efficiency, with performance approaching that of real videos.

Conclusion: Concentrating discriminative supervision on less-principal components yields a better emotion-lip synchronization balance than full-space supervision, as principal components mainly encode high-energy articulation and pose variations.

Abstract: Precise emotion control in audio-driven talking heads remains a challenge due to the reliance on implicit emotion regulation in existing systems, which often leads to indirect and insufficient control. Additionally, training with explicit emotion-related losses across the entire motion space poses significant difficulties due to the inherent trade-off between accurate lip synchronization and fine-grained emotion control. In this paper, we reveal a key finding: although emotional cues are distributed throughout the motion space, concentrating discriminative supervision on less-principal components achieves a better emotion-lip synchronization balance, as principal components mainly encode high-energy articulation and pose variations. Building on this insight, we propose Xemo-Talker, which first learns a neutral speech-to-motion mapping for stable articulation and lip synchronization, and then introduces a lightweight emotion branch guided by less-principal subspace supervision. To enhance emotion control, we design a Tri-Loss consisting of inter-class separation, intra-class compactness, and less-principal contrastive learning. Given an audio input, a reference image, and an emotion label, Xemo-Talker achieves state-of-the-art emotion classification accuracy while maintaining competitive lip synchronization and high inference efficiency, with performance approaching that measured on real videos.The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/chaolongy/Xemo-Talker.

</details>


### [2] [Periocular Soft Biometrics: A Survey and Applications to Multimedia Forensics and Disinformation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14701)
*Fernando Alonso-Fernandez,Kevin Hernandez-Diaz,Josef Bigun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A survey of demographic attribute estimation (gender, age, ethnicity) from periocular images, covering datasets, methodological trends, applications in multimedia forensics, and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft-biometric attributes provide valuable ancillary evidence when full identity recognition is infeasible, and the periocular region is a robust source of these cues since it often remains visible when other facial parts are occluded in forensic and surveillance scenarios.

Method: A comprehensive survey covering publicly available datasets, methodological evolution from handcrafted descriptors to deep learning, and state-of-the-art performance in gender, age, and ethnicity prediction from periocular images.

Result: Presents the current state of the art in gender, age, and ethnicity prediction from periocular images, along with use cases in multimedia forensics and disinformation detection.

Conclusion: Highlights open challenges including dataset bias, cross-domain generalisation, fairness, ethical considerations, and the lack of forensic-oriented benchmarks, indicating the need for further research in these areas.

Abstract: Soft-biometric attributes such as gender, age, and ethnicity provide valuable ancillary evidence when full identity recognition is not feasible, supporting applications in forensic investigation, identity verification, surveillance, or detection of synthetic and manipulated media. Among biometric modalities, the periocular region is a robust source of soft-biometric cues, as it often remains visible when other parts of the face are occluded, a frequent condition in forensic evidence and surveillance footage, and can be captured across a wide range of acquisition conditions. In this paper, we provide a survey of demographic attribute estimation from periocular images, covering publicly available datasets, methodological trends from handcrafted descriptors to deep learning architectures, and the state of the art in gender, age, and ethnicity prediction. We discuss use cases relevant to multimedia forensics and disinformation-detection applications, including demographic filtering in surveillance footage, age verification, and the detection of demographic inconsistencies in synthetic data. We also highlight open challenges, including dataset bias, cross-domain generalisation, fairness, ethical aspects, and the lack of forensic-oriented benchmarks.

</details>


### [3] [Deep Analog: Open-Set Film Emulation with Reference-Conditioned 3D LUTs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14702)
*Yitong Mu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents Deep Analog, a film emulation pipeline that predicts a single 3D LUT from a reference film frame to generalize open-set to unseen film stocks without paired data. It identifies and fixes two degenerate training failures (gate collapse and residual-scale collapse) and adds histogram-based tone matching plus a physics-informed optical renderer for grain and halation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time image enhancement using a bank of fixed 3D LUTs is closed-set, freezing the achievable looks at training time and failing to generalize to unseen film stocks. The paper targets open-set film emulation—matching any reference film frame from a single example.

Method: The paper first shows that a bank of LUTs with per-image weights is a gated mixture of experts that suffers from gate collapse, fixed with an entropy term (recovering ~1 dB PSNR). It then discards the fixed LUT basis and predicts a single 3D LUT as a residual from a reference image (StyleLUTNet), trained via self-supervision on procedurally generated color transforms. Around this backbone, Deep Analog adds histogram-based tone matching and a physics-informed optical renderer (multi-scale grain, per-channel halation) with parameters regressed by an inverse network.

Result: On 350 self-supervised pairs, the color stage achieves 22.05 dB PSNR / 0.925 SSIM, the full pipeline 21.72 dB / 0.923. The color path runs in 5.2 ms at 1080p (192 FPS) and exports a portable .cube LUT.

Conclusion: The conditional design removes the gate and generalizes open-set to unseen film stocks without paired data or retraining. A second degeneracy (residual-scale collapse) shares a root cause with gate collapse, yielding a general principle: auxiliary regularization must remain subordinate to reconstruction.

Abstract: Film emulation reproduces the look of an analog film stock on a new digital photograph. We target its open-set form -- matching any reference film frame from a single example -- with a 3D lookup table (LUT) predicted from that reference. Real-time image enhancement predicts per-image weights over a fixed bank of 3D LUTs and blends them. We show this is a gated mixture of experts and inherits its failure: trained end-to-end against reconstruction, the gate collapses onto a single expert, so a bank of K LUTs delivers the capacity of one. An entropy term, the enhancement-setting analogue of mixture-of-experts load balancing, restores utilization and recovers about 1 dB PSNR. The deeper constraint survives: a fixed LUT basis is closed-set, freezing the achievable looks at training time. We therefore discard the basis and predict a single 3D LUT as a residual from a reference image (StyleLUTNet), trained by self-supervision on procedurally generated color transforms. The conditional design removes the gate and generalizes open-set to unseen film stocks without paired data or retraining. Around this color backbone we build Deep Analog, a film-emulation pipeline that adds histogram-based tone matching and a physics-informed optical renderer -- multi-scale grain and per-channel halation driven by parameters an inverse network regresses from the reference. On 350 self-supervised pairs the color stage reaches 22.05 dB PSNR / 0.925 SSIM and the full pipeline 21.72 dB / 0.923; the color path runs in 5.2 ms at 1080p (192 FPS) and exports a portable .cube LUT for standard editing tools. A second degeneracy in conditional LUT training -- residual-scale collapse -- shares the root cause and yields a general principle: auxiliary regularization must stay subordinate to reconstruction.

</details>


### [4] [On Cross-Validation for Hyperparameter Optimization of Deep Learning Image Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14705)
*Ljubomir Buturovic*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper empirically compares three hyperparameter optimization protocols—fixed holdout, reshuffled holdout, and 5-fold cross-validation—for deep learning image classifiers, focusing on small-sample medical imaging scenarios. Cross-validation consistently yields lower absolute performance-estimation error (AEE) between validation and test AUROC, with the largest benefits at small sample sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HPO significantly impacts deep learning classifier performance, but there is little empirical guidance on how to derive the validation signal driving it, especially for small sample sizes common in medical imaging. The paper addresses this gap by directly comparing three HPO protocols.

Method: Three HPO protocols (fixed holdout, reshuffled holdout, 5-fold cross-validation) were compared while holding the search space, sampler, training procedure, architecture, and test set identical. Evaluations used three datasets (RSNA pneumonia, binarized HAM10000, Tiny ImageNet) across varying development set sizes and two backbones (ResNet-18 and ViT-S/16), measuring absolute performance-estimation error (AEE).

Result: Cross-validation was favored over both holdout protocols on medical datasets for every point estimate, with AEE reductions largest at small sample sizes and diminishing as n increased. Fixed holdout had lower mean AEE than reshuffled holdout in 11 of 12 medical conditions. On Tiny ImageNet, AEE was negligible under all protocols, and test AUROC was generally similar among protocols.

Conclusion: For small-sample medical image classification, cross-validation-based HPO is recommended when computational resources permit, as it trades additional computation for a more reliable development-time estimate of subsequent test performance.

Abstract: Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) can materially affect the performance of deep learning (DL) image classifiers, but there is little empirical guidance on how to derive the validation signal that drives it, especially for the small sample sizes common in fields such as medical imaging. We compared three HPO protocols in terms of {\em
  absolute performance-estimation error} (AEE; the absolute difference between the winning configuration's validation AUROC and its test AUROC): fixed holdout (F), reshuffled holdout (R), and 5-fold cross-validation (C). The search space, sampler, training procedure, architecture, and test set were held identical across protocols. We evaluated the protocols on three public datasets spanning two regimes: binary medical imaging (RSNA pneumonia radiographs and binarized HAM10000 skin lesions) and 200-class natural imaging (Tiny ImageNet), across a range of development set sizes $n$ and two backbones (ResNet-18 on all datasets, Vision Transformer (ViT-S/16) on RSNA). On the medical datasets, every point estimate favored cross-validation over both holdout protocols, with reductions in AEE largest at small sample sizes and diminishing as $n$ increased. This pattern remained robust under conservative family-wise adjustment. On Tiny ImageNet, AEE was negligible under all three protocols. Test AUROC was generally similar among protocols. Fixed holdout had lower mean AEE than reshuffled holdout in 11 of 12 medical conditions, although this secondary finding was less uniformly supported. For small-sample medical image classification, we recommend cross-validation-based HPO when computational resources permit because it trades additional computation for a more reliable development-time estimate of subsequent test performance.

</details>


### [5] [Equilibrium Forcing: Adaptive Video Generation Without Noise Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14706)
*Hansen Jin Lillemark,Alex Rojas,Zachary Novack,Runqian Wang,Yilun Du,Yian Ma,Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick,Rose Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EqF is a simplified framework for video denoising generative models that removes noise level conditioning, enabling modular training and inference-time designs that adapt to data via closed-loop feedback, improving video quality on autoregressive video generation benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard autoregressive video generation algorithms based on Diffusion and Flow Matching use rigid training objectives and static sampling schedules, limiting their ability to adapt inference procedures to the data being generated.

Method: Equilibrium Forcing (EqF) is a framework for video denoising generative models that operates without noise level conditioning. It decouples learning the denoising field from sampling, enabling modular training- and inference-time designs. Inference-time algorithms operate in a closed loop by adapting to feedback from the sample.

Result: EqF improves video quality and consistency on challenging autoregressive video generation benchmarks, surpassing standard noise level-conditional denoising video methods.

Conclusion: Removing noise level conditioning enables EqF's data-dependent inference properties to outperform standard noise level-conditional denoising video methods, with extensive analysis clarifying the exact mechanism behind this advantage.

Abstract: Standard autoregressive video generation algorithms based on Diffusion and Flow Matching rely on rigid training objectives and static sampling schedules, limiting inference procedures from adapting to the data. We introduce Equilibrium Forcing (EqF), a simplified framework for video denoising generative models without noise level conditioning. EqF pioneers modular training- and inference-time designs for noise-unconditional generation that decouple learning the denoising field from sampling. This flexibility allows for inference-time algorithms that operate in a closed loop by adapting to feedback from the sample, improving video quality and consistency on challenging autoregressive video generation benchmarks. Extensive analysis elucidates exactly how removing the noise level conditioning enables EqF's data-dependent inference properties to surpass the performance of standard noise level-conditional denoising video methods.

</details>


### [6] [PE-CSNet: An equivariant network architecture with learnable patch-based sparse representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14708)
*Kai Li,Haitao Long,Bo Zhang,Haiwen Zhang,Zhi Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PE-CSNet is a patch-based equivariant deep unrolling architecture for compressive sensing that generalizes traditional patch-based transform sparsity with learnable transforms, solved via a block coordinate descent algorithm unrolled into a deep network with end-to-end training and a stochastic equivariant training strategy for data efficiency. It achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on CS-MRI and CS-CDP tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing effective task-specific sparse transforms and optimization procedures for high-quality CS recovery is challenging, requiring expert domain knowledge and laborious parameter tuning. The authors aim to automate and improve this process through a learnable, optimization-driven approach.

Method: The paper establishes a generalized patch-based CS model solved via a block coordinate descent (BCD) algorithm. This BCD solver is unrolled into a deep neural network with all CS model and solver parameters learned end-to-end. A stochastic equivariant training strategy exploits the patch-wise network structure for data efficiency, and a parameter-shared version is provided with convergence analysis, while stage-specific parameters enhance performance in practice.

Result: On CS magnetic resonance imaging (CS-MRI) and CS coded diffraction patterns (CS-CDP) tasks, PE-CSNet achieves state-of-the-art accuracy with fast computational speed, outperforming traditional CS methods and existing deep unrolling approaches.

Conclusion: PE-CSNet demonstrates that generalizing patch-based transform sparsity with learnable transforms and unrolled optimization enables high-quality CS recovery, improving both accuracy and data efficiency over existing methods.

Abstract: Compressive sensing (CS) enables accurate signal reconstruction from sparse measurements and is widely applied in medical imaging, remote sensing, and image compression. However, designing an effective, task-specific sparse transform and the corresponding optimization procedure for high-quality CS remains challenging. This process typically requires expert domain knowledge and laborious parameter tuning. To address this issue, we present a Patch-based Equivariant deep unrolling architecture, termed PE-CSNet, for accurate CS recovery. While traditional CS methods generally use predefined patch-based transform sparsity, we generalize this idea by incorporating learnable transform sparsity that adapts to the specific CS task through an optimization-driven process. Specifically, we first establish a generalized patch-based CS model, which we solve via a block coordinate descent (BCD) algorithm. The BCD solver is then unrolled into a deep neural network, where all parameters of both the CS model and solver are learned through end-to-end training. To improve data efficiency, we introduce a stochastic equivariant training strategy that exploits the patch-wise structure of the network, enabling PE-CSNet to learn effectively even from limited data. We further provide a simpler, parameter-shared version of PE-CSNet and briefly discuss its convergence as an iterative solver. For practical applications, the network uses stage-specific (non-shared) parameters to enhance its expressive power and thereby improve its performance. On the tasks of CS magnetic resonance imaging (CS-MRI) and CS coded diffraction patterns (CS-CDP), PE-CSNet achieves state-of-the-art accuracy with fast computational speed, outperforming traditional methods and existing deep unrolling methods.

</details>


### [7] [Path2ST: Hierarchical Cell-Tissue Grounded Cross-Modal Translation for Spatial Transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14710)
*Ruochen Liu,Wei Lou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Path2ST is a hierarchically grounded autoregressive framework that predicts spatial gene expression from H&E-stained images by treating it as a cross-modal semantic translation task, incorporating hierarchical cell-tissue conditioning, scale-adaptive autoregressive generation, and a full-spectrum loss function to achieve state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing H&E-to-ST prediction methods treat H&E images as generic visual inputs and ignore the intrinsic biological hierarchy where spatially organized cell types collectively form functional tissue microenvironments that govern local gene expression programs.

Method: Path2ST features (i) a Hierarchical Cell-Tissue Conditioning mechanism fusing explicit/implicit cellular features with tissue-level semantic representations, (ii) a Scale-Adaptive Autoregressive Generation process over a hierarchical semantic vocabulary for coarse-to-fine expression synthesis, and (iii) SpectraLoss, a full-spectrum objective enforcing ordinal fidelity, modeling transcriptional bursts, and aligning semantic structures with cell types.

Result: Extensive experiments on three datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, validating that Path2ST generates highly accurate and spatially coherent transcriptomic profiles.

Conclusion: Path2ST successfully bridges the gap between H&E image analysis and spatial transcriptomics by leveraging biological hierarchy, generating accurate and spatially coherent gene expression predictions. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Predicting spatial gene expression from hematoxylin and eosin (H\&E)-stained images offers a cost-effective alternative to spatial transcriptomics (ST). However, existing methods treat H\&E images as generic visual inputs and ignore their intrinsic biological hierarchy, where spatially organized cell types collectively form functional tissue microenvironments that govern local gene expression programs. To bridge this gap, we formulate H\&E-to-ST prediction as a cross-modal semantic translation task and propose Path2ST, a hierarchically grounded autoregressive framework featuring three key components: (i) a Hierarchical Cell-Tissue Conditioning mechanism that fuses explicit and implicit cellular features with tissue-level semantic representations to construct hierarchical conditioning signals; (ii) a Scale-Adaptive Autoregressive Generation process over a hierarchical semantic vocabulary, enabling coarse-to-fine, biologically consistent expression synthesis; and (iii) SpectraLoss, a full-spectrum objective that jointly enforces ordinal fidelity, models transcriptional bursts, and aligns semantic structures with cell types. Extensive experiments on three datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, validating that Path2ST generates highly accurate and spatially coherent transcriptomic profiles. The related code is released at https://github.com/RuochenLiu23/Path2ST.

</details>


### [8] [Local Gains and Fixed-Assignment Set Losses in Shared Set Decoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14717)
*Ze Zhang,Yang Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper examines the tension between query-relation deletion improving an edited slot versus reducing the utility of the prediction set containing it, using paired image-relation units from two ResNet-50 DETR-family checkpoints (710 units each). The authors employ a target-minus-control composite contrast (subtracting a matched active control) and find locally positive but fixed-assignment negative effects in both checkpoints, with opposite-sign patterns in 302/710 (DETR) and 460/710 (DINO) units. After rematching, DETR intervals cross zero while DINO intervals remain negative, indicating persistence differs by checkpoint. The study concludes that local intervention success does not determine set-level consequences, and supports selection-conditional deletion sensitivity whose persistence depends on readout and intervention operator—without identifying an intervention-invariant edge mechanism, detector-level degradation, or prevalence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to resolve the tension between targeted query-relation deletion (which improves individual edited slots) and its potential negative impact on the utility of the broader prediction set. They seek to determine whether local intervention success translates to global consequences in object detection models.

Method: The study uses recorded, selection-conditional evidence from 710 paired image-relation units per checkpoint across DETR and DINO ResNet-50 backbones. The primary analysis subtracts a matched active control (deleting the same leader source at a different recipient) from the selected target deletion, forming a composite contrast. They compare target-minus-control effects, apply rematching procedures, and also use a fixed-map comparison between hard deletion and mass-preserving edit.

Result: Target-minus-control contrast is locally positive and fixed-assignment negative in both checkpoints. Opposite-sign patterns appear in 302/710 DETR and 460/710 DINO units (285/710 and 433/710 after rematching). DETR intervals cross zero after rematching while DINO intervals remain negative, showing checkpoint-dependent persistence. The fixed-map comparison also differs before rematching.

Conclusion: Local intervention success does not determine the consequence for a jointly decoded set. The supported conclusion is selection-conditional deletion sensitivity whose persistence depends on the readout and intervention operator. No intervention-invariant edge mechanism, detector-level degradation, population prevalence, or training-time regularizer value was identified.

Abstract: A query-relation deletion can improve the edited slot while reducing the utility of the prediction set that contains it. We study this tension in two related ResNet-50 DETR-family checkpoints using recorded, selection-conditional evidence from 710 paired image-relation units per checkpoint. The primary comparison subtracts a matched active control, which deletes the same leader source at a different recorded recipient, from the selected target deletion. It is therefore a composite contrast rather than a same-recipient placebo.
  The target-minus-control contrast is locally positive and fixed-assignment negative in both checkpoints. The opposite-sign pattern occurs within 302/710 DETR units and 460/710 DINO units. After rematching, the corresponding counts are 285/710 and 433/710. Rematching and native selection absorb enough of the mean loss for DETR intervals to cross zero, whereas DINO intervals remain negative, so persistence across readouts differs by checkpoint. A fixed-map comparison between hard deletion and a mass-preserving edit also differs before rematching. That comparison is conditional on the outcome-blind map and does not establish same-dose transport.
  Local intervention success therefore does not determine the consequence for a jointly decoded set. The supported conclusion is selection-conditional deletion sensitivity whose persistence depends on the readout and intervention operator. We do not identify an intervention-invariant edge mechanism, detector-level degradation, population prevalence, or the value of a training-time regularizer.

</details>


### [9] [VideoGAIA: A Benchmark for General AI Assistants on Agentic Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14718)
*Fan Zhang,Guangming Yao,Jinyang Wu,Hao Wu,Zheng Lian,Xinyu Geng,Jingdong Chen,Yi Yuan,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoGAIA is a new agentic video understanding benchmark that formulates video understanding as multi-turn, tool-augmented interactions rather than one-shot QA. It contains 271 model-human co-designed tasks verified by three human experts each, and all evaluated MLLMs (including frontier models like GPT-5.5 and Kimi-K3) score below 60%, demonstrating it is a challenging and timely benchmark for next-generation multimodal models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video understanding benchmarks like Video-MME are becoming saturated (leading models reach ~90% accuracy), making them insufficient for assessing the intelligence of advanced MLLMs. There is a need for benchmarks that move beyond conventional single-turn video question answering toward more complex agentic scenarios involving tool use and multi-turn interactions.

Method: VideoGAIA formulates video understanding as a multi-turn, tool-augmented interaction process where models must iteratively perceive videos, invoke external tools, gather complementary information, and integrate multimodal evidence across turns. The benchmark contains 271 tasks co-designed by humans and models, with each instance independently verified by three human experts.

Result: All evaluated MLLMs, including frontier models such as GPT-5.5 and Kimi-K3, achieve less than 60% accuracy on VideoGAIA, in contrast to ~90% accuracy on Video-MME, highlighting the benchmark's difficulty and its value as a high-quality benchmark for next-generation MLLMs.

Conclusion: VideoGAIA serves as a timely and high-quality benchmark that facilitates the transition from conventional video understanding toward agentic video understanding, filling the gap left by saturated conventional benchmarks.

Abstract: Video understanding is a fundamental task for evaluating the capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing leading models have already achieved approximately 90% accuracy on the Video-MME leaderboard, suggesting that conventional single-turn video understanding tasks are becoming increasingly saturated and insufficient for assessing the intelligence of advanced MLLMs. Towards this end, we introduce VideoGAIA, an agentic video understanding benchmark for general artificial intelligence (AI) assistants. Moving beyond one-shot video question answering, VideoGAIA formulates video understanding as a multi-turn, tool-augmented interaction process, where models must iteratively perceive videos, invoke external tools, gather complementary information, and integrate multimodal evidence across turns. VideoGAIA contains 271 model-human co-designed tasks covering diverse and complex real-world scenarios. Each video-question-answer instance is independently verified by three human experts to ensure both correctness and appropriate difficulty. All evaluated MLLMs, including frontier models such as GPT-5.5 and Kimi-K3, achieve less than 60% accuracy on VideoGAIA, highlighting its value as a high-quality and timely benchmark for evaluating next-generation MLLMs. We hope that VideoGAIA will facilitate the transition from conventional video understanding toward agentic video understanding.

</details>


### [10] [DeCo-MIL: Debiased Counterfactual Reasoning for Long-Tailed Whole Slide Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14719)
*Xiaoxiao Li,Xitong Ling,Jiawen Li,Weiming Chen,Zhenyang Cai,Xidong Wang,Tian Guan,Benyou Wang,Yonghong He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DeCo-MIL is a multiple instance learning framework for weakly supervised whole slide image analysis that addresses the nested dual long-tail problem—inter-slide class imbalance and intra-slide sparse diagnostic evidence—through frequency-debiased counterfactual reasoning, redundancy masking, and tail-aware oversampling with consistency regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MIL-based WSI analysis suffers from a nested dual long-tail problem where tail classes not only have few training slides but their discriminative evidence is also scarce within those slides, concentrated in a few patches and obscured by redundant tissue. This coupled long-tail biases models toward head classes and degrades rare-class recognition.

Method: DeCo-MIL jointly alleviates the nested dual long-tail through: (1) inner long-tail handling via clustering patches into tissue-morphology anchors, performing counterfactual intervention by replacing anchors with normal prototypes, and estimating counterfactual contributions using class-frequency-corrected predictions to guide redundancy masking; (2) outer long-tail handling via constructing anchor-stratified pseudo-bags from redundancy-reduced bags, combined with tail-aware oversampling and consistency regularization to increase supervision for tail classes while preserving tissue-morphology composition.

Result: Extensive experiments on three long-tailed WSI benchmarks show DeCo-MIL achieves state-of-the-art performance in both tail-class recognition and overall classification.

Conclusion: DeCo-MIL effectively addresses the nested dual long-tail problem in WSI analysis through counterfactual reasoning and frequency-debiased strategies, demonstrating superior performance on long-tailed WSI benchmarks.

Abstract: Multiple instance learning (MIL) is widely used for weakly supervised whole slide image (WSI) analysis. However, under long-tailed distributions, MIL-based WSI analysis faces a nested dual long-tail: an inter-slide class long tail and an intra-slide long tail of instance-level discriminative evidence. The two long tails are coupled: tail classes have few training slides, while their limited diagnostic evidence is concentrated in a few patches and obscured by abundant within-bag redundancy. This coupling biases models toward head classes and degrades rare-class recognition. To address this, we propose DeCo-MIL for long-tailed WSI analysis, which jointly alleviates the nested dual long-tail through frequency-debiased counterfactual reasoning. For the inner long tail, DeCo-MIL clusters patches into tissue-morphology anchors, replaces each anchor with its matched normal prototype to perform a counterfactual intervention, and estimates its counterfactual contribution to the ground-truth class using class-frequency-corrected predictions. These contributions guide redundancy masking to preserve scarce discriminative instances. For the outer long tail, DeCo-MIL constructs anchor-stratified pseudo-bags from redundancy-reduced bags and combines tail-aware oversampling with consistency regularization, increasing effective supervision for tail classes while preserving tissue-morphology composition. Extensive experiments on three long-tailed WSI benchmarks demonstrate that DeCo-MIL achieves state-of-the-art performance in both tail-class recognition and overall classification.

</details>


### [11] [AeroGround: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Aerial-Ground Collaborative Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14721)
*Shenghong Yi,Lin Zhang,Muzian Li,Jiakang Yuan,Haoyu Zhang,Peng Ye,Jiayuan Fan,Huafeng Qin,Tao Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces AeroGround, a benchmark for evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) in aerial-ground collaborative reasoning scenarios. It contains ~29,000 multimodal observation groups and 2,250 question-answering instances covering cross-view correspondence, spatial understanding, and reasoning. Evaluation of 16 pretrained VLMs plus 2 domain-adapted variants shows the best model achieves only 54.4% accuracy versus 93.3% human performance, revealing a substantial gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UAV benchmarks focus primarily on aerial-view scenarios, and it remains underexplored whether current VLMs can handle aerial-ground collaborative scenarios, which are practically important for real-world applications like rescue and infrastructure inspection.

Method: AeroGround is constructed from a simulated aerial-ground dataset with approximately 29,000 multimodal observation groups across diverse open environments, providing 2,250 high-quality QA instances covering cross-view correspondence, spatial understanding, and reasoning. The benchmark evaluates 16 pretrained VLMs and 2 domain-adapted variants.

Result: The best VLM achieves an average accuracy of 54.4%, while humans reach 93.3%, demonstrating a substantial performance gap between current models and human-level understanding in aerial-ground collaborative reasoning.

Conclusion: AeroGround systematically reveals the strengths and limitations of existing VLMs in aerial-ground collaborative reasoning and provides a foundation for developing more capable aerial-ground collaborative embodied intelligence systems.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have been widely employed in understanding and reasoning tasks for unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs). Existing UAV benchmarks primarily focus on aerial-view scenarios. However, whether current VLMs can perform well on understanding and reasoning tasks in aerial-ground collaborative scenarios which are practical in real-world applications like rescue and infrastructure inspection remains underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce AeroGround, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating VLMs in aerial-ground collaborative reasoning. AeroGround is built upon a simulated aerial-ground dataset containing approximately 29,000 multimodal observation groups from diverse open environments, and provides 2,250 high-quality question-answering instances covering cross-view correspondence, spatial understanding, and reasoning. Experiments on 16 pretrained VLMs, together with two domain-adapted variants, reveal a substantial gap between current models and human performance: the best model achieves an average accuracy of 54.4%, whereas humans reach 93.3%. By systematically revealing the strengths and limitations of existing models in aerial-ground collaborative reasoning, AeroGround provides a foundation for developing more capable aerial-ground collaborative embodied intelligence systems.

</details>


### [12] [Braided Vision Transformer for Stroke Detection in Multi-view Retinal Fundus Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14722)
*Aysen Degerli,Mika Hilvo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes the Braided Vision Transformer (BViT), the first vision transformer model for stroke and TIA detection using multi-view retinal fundus images from both eyes. BViT captures both representative features from individual images and inter-view relationships across eyes, achieving an AUC of 0.75 on the Stroke-Data dataset and outperforming regular vision transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stroke is a leading cause of mortality and morbidity, requiring accurate and immediate assessment. While conventional neuroimaging is effective, retinal fundus imaging offers a non-invasive, cost-effective, and portable alternative for rapid stroke screening. However, existing approaches using this modality have not fully exploited multi-view information from both eyes, motivating the need for a more powerful model to capture retinal biomarkers associated with cerebrovascular events.

Method: The paper introduces the Braided Vision Transformer (BViT), the first vision transformer model applied to retinal fundus imaging for stroke assessment. BViT processes macula-centric and optic nerve head-centric views from both eyes, extracting representative features from each multi-view image while simultaneously modeling inter-view relationships across both eyes to enable a more informative understanding of retinal patterns linked to stroke and TIA.

Result: Experiments on the collected Stroke-Data dataset show that BViT achieves an AUC score of 0.75 for stroke detection, outperforming regular vision transformers.

Conclusion: Retinal fundus imaging combined with the proposed BViT model demonstrates feasibility for stroke and TIA detection, leveraging multi-view and cross-eye relationships to improve performance over standard vision transformers, offering a promising portable screening solution.

Abstract: Stroke remains a leading cause of mortality and morbidity worldwide, emphasizing the importance of its accurate and immediate assessment. Retinal fundus imaging has emerged as a promising modality for stroke assessment, as the retina reflects cerebrovascular and neurological risk factors. Contrary to conventional neuroimaging techniques, retinal fundus imaging offers a non-invasive, cost-effective, and portable alternative for rapid screening. This paper explores the feasibility of retinal fundus imaging for stroke and transient ischemic attack (TIA) detection using macula-centric and optic nerve head-centric views captured from both eyes. Our study introduces, to the best of our knowledge, the first vision transformer model for retinal fundus imaging in stroke assessment, offering a novel approach for capturing retinal patterns. Thereby, we propose the Braided Vision Transformer (BViT) model, which extracts representative features from the given multi-view images while simultaneously capturing inter-view relationships across both eyes, enabling a more informative understanding of retinal biomarkers associated with cerebrovascular events. Experiments conducted on our collected Stroke-Data dataset demonstrate that BViT achieves an AUC score of 0.75 for stroke detection, outperforming regular vision transformers.

</details>


### [13] [A Vision Transformer for ECG-Based Detection of Left Ventricular Systolic Dysfunction Across Multiple Clinical Sites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14723)
*Burcu Ozek,Aruna Mohan,David Vorchheimer,Daniel Weiss,Eyal Kedar,Tamar Sobol,Or Zilbershot,Fatemeh Afghah*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents an ensemble of vision transformers trained from scratch to detect reduced left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF ≤40%) from 12-lead ECGs. Trained on 10,142 patients across seven sites and validated on 4,092 external patients, the model achieved an AUROC of 0.88 with 81.2% sensitivity and 81.0% specificity. Beat-level attention maps revealed consistent focus on the QRS complex, supporting the use of routine ECGs as a scalable first-pass triage for echocardiography referral.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduced LVEF is often asymptomatic and only detected after advanced heart failure develops. Although ECGs are recorded routinely, they are underused for this condition because reduced LVEF has no single diagnostic waveform. The authors aim to leverage the ubiquity of ECG recordings to identify patients who should undergo echocardiography.

Method: An ensemble of vision transformers was trained from scratch to analyze each heartbeat individually within 12-lead ECGs. Training used 10,142 patients from seven sites across three US health systems. Beat-level attention maps were used for interpretability.

Result: On a held-out external cohort of 4,092 patients (real-world reduced-LVEF prevalence of 8.72%), the model achieved AUROC of 0.88 (95% CI 0.86-0.89), sensitivity of 81.2%, specificity of 81.0%, and negative predictive value of 97.8%. Sensitivity remained high across demographic and comorbidity subgroups, while specificity was lower in older patients and those with atrial fibrillation or cardiomyopathy. Attention maps showed consistent focus on the QRS complex.

Conclusion: Routine ECGs analyzed by this transformer ensemble have the potential to serve as a scalable first-pass triage step to identify patients who should undergo echocardiography for reduced ejection fraction across diverse patient populations.

Abstract: Reduced left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) is frequently asymptomatic and often detected only after advanced heart failure develops. Electrocardiograms are recorded routinely yet underused for this condition, because reduced LVEF has no single diagnostic waveform. We trained an ensemble of vision transformers from scratch to detect reduced LVEF ($\leq$40%) from 12-lead ECGs, analyzing each heartbeat individually, using 10,142 patients across seven sites in three US health systems. In a held-out external cohort of 4,092 patients from three geographically independent US clinical sites at a real-world reduced-LVEF prevalence of 8.72%, the model achieved an AUROC of 0.88 (95% CI 0.86-0.89), sensitivity 81.2%, specificity 81.0%, and negative predictive value 97.8%. Sensitivity remained high across sex, race, ethnicity, and comorbidity subgroups, while specificity was lower in older patients and those with atrial fibrillation or cardiomyopathy. Beat-level attention maps provided interpretability into the model's predictions, showing consistent focus on the QRS complex rather than the P wave. These findings support the potential of routine ECGs as a scalable first-pass triage step to identify patients who should undergo echocardiography for reduced ejection fraction across diverse patient populations.

</details>


### [14] [Privacy-Preserving Dataset Curation for Kuala Lumpur Urban Traffic: Grounded Vision-Language Detection with Spatial Vehicle-Context Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14724)
*Mohammed Abdul Al Arafat Tanzin,Rudzidatul Akmam Dziyauddin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents an automated PII anonymization framework for the Kuala Lumpur Road Dataset, combining Grounding DINO (a zero-shot vision-language transformer) with a Spatial Vehicle ROI Containment Engine to obfuscate faces and license plates in complex tropical urban environments. The system achieves ~95% success on 1,266 frames where legacy YOLOv8 and Haar cascades fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-modal urban traffic datasets are essential for autonomous driving research, but curating high-fidelity video in tropical urban environments (Kuala Lumpur) poses severe PII anonymization challenges due to high motorcycle density, dark acrylic license plates, dynamic camera tilt, and extreme glare. Legacy detection methods (Haar cascades and YOLOv8) fail under these conditions, producing false positives and missing rotated or occluded targets.

Method: The framework integrates Grounding DINO, a zero-shot open-set vision-language transformer, with a novel Spatial Vehicle ROI Containment Engine. License plate centroids must fall within validated vehicle boundaries, which suppresses environmental false positives. The pipeline automatically obfuscates faces, heads, and license plates, and is augmented with temporal persistence mechanisms and an automated quality-control auditor.

Result: Initial evaluation on 1,266 frames demonstrates a ~95% success rate. Remaining failures are confined to small, heavily occluded, oblique, or ambiguous targets. The framework minimizes privacy-related false negatives while preserving scene context for downstream vision tasks.

Conclusion: The publicly available pipeline and demonstration notebook provide an auditable preprocessing stage for privacy-aware dataset curation. While formal legal compliance depends on broader governance procedures, the framework represents a practical solution for privacy-aware curation of complex urban traffic datasets.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of intelligent transportation systems and autonomous driving relies heavily on multi-modal urban traffic datasets. However, curating high-fidelity video imagery in complex tropical urban environments---specifically Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia---presents severe challenges for Personally Identifiable Information (PII) anonymization due to high motorcycle density, dark acrylic license plates, dynamic camera tilt, and extreme tropical glare. We propose an automated anonymization framework tailored for the Kuala Lumpur Road Dataset, captured via a mobile cycling platform at 2 FPS. We document how legacy Haar cascades and YOLOv8 fail under these conditions---generating false positives on background elements while missing rotated or occluded targets. Our architecture resolves this by integrating Grounding DINO---a zero-shot open-set vision-language transformer---with a novel Spatial Vehicle Region of Interest (ROI) Containment Engine. By requiring license plate centroids to reside within validated vehicle boundaries, the pipeline suppresses environmental false positives while automatically obfuscating faces, heads, and license plates. An initial evaluation on 1,266 frames demonstrates a $\sim$95\% success rate, with remaining failures restricted to small, heavily occluded, oblique, or ambiguous targets. Coupled with temporal persistence mechanisms and an automated quality-control auditor, the framework minimizes privacy-related false negatives while preserving scene context for downstream vision tasks. While formal legal compliance depends on broader governance procedures, this publicly available pipeline and demonstration notebook provide an auditable preprocessing stage for privacy-aware dataset curation.

</details>


### [15] [Spatial Attention Noise Masking for Causally Sufficient Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14725)
*Benjamin Formby,Kuang-Ching Wang,D Hudson Smith*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a causal interpretability framework for computer vision that dynamically masks input images before classification. The framework uses a UNet-style mask generator and a ResNet18 encoder with a linear classifier, regularizing masks to be sparse and smooth while keeping masked/unmasked embeddings consistent. The resulting masks serve as feature attribution maps with strong causal explanations, achieving near-baseline classification performance across five tasks and robustness to distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most interpretability methods operate passively on already-trained models, yielding correlational rather than causal explanations. Existing causal interpretability methods are limited to post hoc analysis, weakening causal claims, and active methods lack explanations that explicitly assign responsibility to input features. High-stakes fields like medical imaging, security, and autonomous driving require more reliable causal explanations.

Method: The framework comprises: 1) a UNet-style mask generator that produces spatial attention noise masks, and 2) a ResNet18 encoder with a linear classifier that classifies both masked and unmasked versions of an input image. Masks are regularized for sparsity and spatial smoothness, and masked image embeddings are constrained to remain consistent with unmasked embeddings. This enables the model to learn which features are causally sufficient for prediction.

Result: Quantitative evaluations show mask faithfulness, near-baseline classification performance across five classification tasks despite substantial image masking, and robustness to distribution shifts including background swapping and natural adversarial examples. Qualitative comparisons demonstrate competitive interpretability relative to state-of-the-art feature attribution methods.

Conclusion: The proposed "active" causal interpretability framework produces feature attribution maps that are competitive with existing methods while providing stronger causal explanations of model predictions, making it more suitable for high-stakes applications.

Abstract: We present a novel causal approach to interpretability for computer vision models that dynamically masks the input image prior to classification. The interpretability of deep learning predictions is critical in high-stakes fields such as medical imaging, security, and autonomous driving. Most interpretability methods are applied passively to already trained models, which typically result in correlational rather than causal explanations. Existing causal interpretability methods are limited to post hoc analysis, weakening the causal claims. Additionally, existing active methods generally lack explanations that explicitly assign responsibility to input features. This work proposes a spatial attention noise masking framework that provides causal explanations about the features sufficient for the prediction. The proposed framework consists of: 1) a UNet-style mask generator, and 2) a Resnet18 encoder and linear classifier that classifies both masked and unmasked versions of an input image. The generated masks are regularized to be sparse and spatially smooth, while masked image embeddings are constrained to remain consistent with embeddings from the corresponding unmasked images. The resulting masks can be interpreted as feature attribution maps that are competitive with related interpretability methods while additionally providing strong causal explanations of model predictions. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate mask faithfulness, near-baseline classification performance across five classification tasks despite substantial masking of image information, and robustness to distribution shifts such as background swapping and natural adversarial examples. Qualitative comparisons further demonstrate mask behavior and competitive interpretability relative to state-of-the-art feature attribution methods.

</details>


### [16] [Low Cost Two-Stage Fabric Defect Detection at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14727)
*Rasel Hossen,Diptajoy Mistry,Mosaddek Hossain Kamal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents a two-stage cascade for knit-fabric defect inspection on a Jetson Nano: a cheap autoencoder anomaly detector (Stage 1) flags suspicious frames, which then trigger a YOLOv5n detector (Stage 2). While the cascade flags all defective images with high recall, its speedup (1.36x) is mostly due to overlapping JPEG decode with inference (91%) rather than the cascade itself (5.1%). The authors caution that measured cascade speedups are often inflated by data-path effects and position the system as AI-assisted triage rather than autonomous acceptance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fabric inspection in low-income economy garment mills is manual because commercial vision systems are too expensive. A cascade using a cheap anomaly detector followed by a full detector seems natural for sparse defects, but the actual speedup contribution of the cascade versus other pipeline factors (like I/O overlap) needs careful decomposition to avoid misleading conclusions.

Method: A two-stage cascade: Stage 1 is a compact convolutional autoencoder with decoder attention gates, edge-weighted reconstruction loss, and feature-level distillation from a frozen YOLOv5n teacher. Stage 2 is YOLOv5n invoked only on flagged frames. Deployed end-to-end on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano with TensorRT FP16. Speedup is decomposed by isolating JPEG decode overlap versus cascade-driven inference reduction.

Result: Stage 1 flags all 20 defective images (95% CI 0.83-1.00) at a 49.3% false-positive rate, improving over a plain autoencoder by 19.3%. The parallel pipeline runs at 13.45 FPS vs 9.86 FPS (1.36x), but 91% of that speedup is from overlapping JPEG decode with inference, not the cascade (which only gives 5.1% reduction, p=0.534). Forwarding is false-positive-limited (85% of forwarded frames are false alarms), though calibration could yield 29-45% inference reduction.

Conclusion: The cascade provides good recall for defect detection, but the headline speedup is largely an I/O artifact. Real cascade inference savings are small at current thresholds. The authors recommend positioning such systems as AI-assisted triage rather than autonomous acceptance, and urge careful data-path-controlled benchmarking for cascade evaluations.

Abstract: Fabric inspection in the garment industries of low-income economies remains largely manual, and commercial vision systems are priced beyond most small and medium mills. Because defects are sparse under controlled production, a natural response is a cascade: screen every frame with a cheap anomaly detector and invoke a full detector only on suspicious frames. We build such a cascade for four knit-fabric defect classes and deploy it end-to-end on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano with TensorRT FP16. Stage 1 is a compact convolutional autoencoder with decoder attention gates, an edge-weighted reconstruction loss, and feature-level distillation from a frozen YOLOv5n teacher; Stage 2 is YOLOv5n, invoked only on flagged frames. On a 249-image benchmark disjoint from detector training (20 defective, 229 non-defective), Stage 1 at a recall-prioritised threshold flags all 20 defective images (95% CI 0.83-1.00) at a false-positive rate of 49.3% (113/229), reducing false positives by 19.3% relative to a plain autoencoder (p=0.011). The parallel pipeline reaches 13.45 FPS against 9.86 FPS for a sequential YOLO-only loop. Our central finding comes from decomposing that 1.36x: 91% of it is attributable to overlapping JPEG decode with inference rather than to the cascade, which contributes only a 5.1% inference reduction at the measured forwarding rate p = 0.534. We further show that forwarding here is false-positive-limited rather than prevalence-limited - 85% of forwarded frames are false alarms - and quantify the 29-45% inference reduction attainable under tighter calibration. We report this as a caution for cascade speedups measured without controlling the data path, and position the system as AI-assisted triage rather than autonomous acceptance.

</details>


### [17] [Do CNNs Internally Represent Real and Fake Images Differently? A Hidden-Layer Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14729)
*Moumita Sen Sarma,Pascal Hitzler,Eugene Y. Vasserman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether CNNs process real and fake images differently at the hidden-layer level, finding that fake images (generated via Stable Diffusion variants) evoke different neuron activation patterns compared to real images with similar semantic content, even when accounting for image degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fake/synthetic images are increasingly prevalent, yet it is unclear whether CNNs internally process real and fake images in the same way. Understanding these internal differences could inform better fake image detection methods.

Method: The authors use trained CNN models for scene recognition, extract dense-layer activations, and apply neurosymbolic methods to label selected neurons semantically. For each real test image, they generate corresponding fake images with similar semantic content using object-label-guided text-to-image and image-to-image generation (Stable Diffusion variants). Paired real-fake activation patterns are statistically compared, with additional experiments varying dataset, CNN architecture, generative model, and degradation (JPEG/blur) for robustness.

Result: Fake images evoke different hidden-neuron activations compared to real images, and these differences are not fully explained by simple image degradation alone. Findings suggest real and fake images differ in CNN hidden-layer activation behavior, at least in some settings.

Conclusion: The observed activation differences between real and fake images open the door for follow-up research leveraging this behavior to improve fake image detection.

Abstract: Fake/synthetic images are increasingly prevalent, but it remains unclear whether Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) process real and fake images in the same internal manner. This work examines the hypothesis that CNNs represent real and fake images differently, such that fake images induce different hidden-layer activation patterns even when semantic content is preserved. The hypothesis is evaluated in scene recognition settings using trained CNN models. Dense-layer activations are extracted, and neurosymbolic methods assign semantic labels to selected neurons. For each real test image, corresponding fake images are generated with similar semantic content using object-label-guided text-to-image and image-to-image generation based on Stable Diffusion variants. Paired real-fake activation patterns are then compared statistically. Additional experiments with another dataset, CNN architecture, generative model, and JPEG/blur degradation analysis assess robustness. Results suggest that fake images evoke different hidden-neuron activations, and these differences are not explained only by simple image degradation. Overall, the findings indicate that real and fake images differ in CNN hidden-layer activation behavior at least in some settings, which opens the door for follow-up work on making use of this different behavior to improve fake image detection.

</details>


### [18] [IP Protection in the Era of Visual Generative AI: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14730)
*Zhuan Shi,Shunchang Liu,Alireza Dehghanpour Farashah,Qian Yang,Han Yu,Cao Yang,Chaochao Chen,Yuping Yan,Yaochu Jin,Golnoosh Farnadi,Lingjuan Lyu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A survey presenting a two-dimensional taxonomy for intellectual property protection in visual generative AI, organizing methods by control logic (Information Exposure Control, Generative Behavior Constraint, Attribution & Accountability) and asset dimension (Data IP vs. Model IP).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surveys organize IP protection literature by lifecycle stage or technical mechanism, which obscures the protective intent of different methods. The authors aim to provide a principled framework that clarifies what risk each method addresses.

Method: Proposes a two-dimensional taxonomy: (1) a Control Logic View with three categories—Information Exposure Control, Generative Behavior Constraint, and Attribution & Accountability—based on the risk variable each method regulates; and (2) a cross-cutting asset dimension distinguishing Data IP from Model IP. The survey systematically reviews protection methods, aligns evaluation protocols with protection objectives, and highlights open challenges.

Result: Presents a systematic review of IP protection methods within the proposed framework and identifies key open challenges: proactive model-level safeguards, standardized evaluation, robustness against adaptive attacks, and explainable evidence.

Conclusion: The survey offers a principled, systematic, and accessible overview for both novice and experienced researchers, aiming to guide future work in visual generative AI IP protection.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of visual generative AI has introduced a wide range of intellectual property risks, spanning the unauthorized learning, reproduction, extraction, misuse, and redistribution of protected data and model assets. To address these risks, a growing body of technical defenses has been proposed. However, existing surveys typically organize this literature by lifecycle stage or technical mechanism, which can obscure the protective intent of different methods. This survey presents a two-dimensional taxonomy for IP protection in visual generative models. The primary axis is a Control Logic View, which classifies methods into Information Exposure Control, Generative Behavior Constraint, and Attribution & Accountability according to the risk variable they regulate. The secondary axis distinguishes Data IP from Model IP as cross-cutting asset dimensions. Under this framework, we systematically review protection methods, align evaluation protocols with protection objectives, and discuss open challenges including proactive model-level safeguards, standardized evaluation, robustness against adaptive attacks, and explainable evidence. This survey aims to offer a principled, systematic, and easy-to-follow overview for both new and experienced researchers in visual generative AI IP protection.

</details>


### [19] [Emergence of Transfer Learning towards Specific Identification of Alzheimer's Disease A Prospective Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14731)
*Soumik Podder,Chandramouli Haldar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This review paper examines the application of Transfer Learning (TL) in Alzheimer's Disease (AD) diagnosis using neuroimaging data, highlighting how TL addresses challenges of overfitting and limited data that plague traditional DL/ML approaches, while also incorporating explainable AI for improved interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AD affects millions of seniors worldwide, and while DL/ML models can identify AD patterns in neuroimaging, they suffer from overfitting and require global optimization. CNN approaches can be misled by sudden changes in MRI images or limited preprocessing, motivating the need for Transfer Learning as a more robust approach for AD diagnosis.

Method: The paper conducts a comprehensive review of Transfer Learning applications in AD classification and identification, including assessment of AD conversion. It evaluates strengths and limitations of TL in improving diagnostic accuracy with limited data, and uniquely incorporates explainable AI (XAI) into TL-based AD diagnosis systems.

Result: The review assesses how TL improves diagnostic accuracy in AD despite limited neuroimaging data, and demonstrates the value of integrating explainable AI for interpretable TL-based AD diagnosis.

Conclusion: The review serves as a comprehensive guide for new researchers in TL-induced neurodegenerative disease detection, particularly in AD diagnosis with limited data and enhanced interpretability through explainable AI.

Abstract: Worldwide, millions of senior citizens are suffering from Alzheimer disease abbreviated as AD, a well- versed form of dementia. AD is featured by amnesia, intellectual disability, and difficulty with consciousness. DL and ML models are undoubtedly explored to identify AD related patterns on large dimensional neuroimaging data but they need global optimization and are suffering from overfitting issue that might yield dissatisfactory result in testing data set. DL overcomes the issue by convolution of input image with kernel but any sudden change in the MRI image or human manipulation, limited pre- processing of the images can mislead CNN in achieving highly accurate detection. Transfer Learning (TL) has proved itself in AD diagnosis by utilizing pre-trained models on large data sets to guide novice model in a new neuroimaging dataset. This review provides an inclusive glimpse of TL implication in classification, identification including the conversion of AD. Keeping in view, we have assessed the strengths and limitations of TL in improvising diagnostic accuracy even with limited data. The uniqueness of the present review is the incorporation of explainable AI in TL based AD diagnosis system. Finally, it can be claimed that the review will guide the new re-searchers in the area of TL induced neurodegenerative disease detection.

</details>


### [20] [From Dense Prediction to Visual Editing: Structured Supervision for Unified Image and Video Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14740)
*Zhefan Rao,Bin Zou,Haoxuan Che,Xuanhua He,Chong Hou Choi,Yanheng Li,Rui Liu,Qifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified framework for image and video creation that incorporates depth and surface-normal prediction as denoising targets, providing dense structured supervision within the same multimodal diffusion transformer backbone. The framework uses Mutual Context Attention and progressive training to connect structural cues to temporally localized editing, achieving the highest overall score (4.15) among compared unified systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic-only conditioning and creation-only training fail to explicitly supervise local structure needed for precise, temporally consistent editing in unified image/video creation. There is a need to incorporate dense structured supervision to improve editing locality and preservation while handling diverse instructions.

Method: The framework formulates depth and surface-normal prediction as image-form denoising targets, using dense tasks as structured visual supervision in the same creation interface. It decouples semantic interpretation from spatially aligned visual injection while sharing one multimodal diffusion transformer (MMDiT) backbone across tasks. Key components include Mutual Context Attention (MCA), a paired-video data-construction procedure, and a progressive training curriculum.

Result: A single checkpoint achieves the highest overall score (4.15) in reported comparisons of unified systems. Dense supervision improves OpenVE Overall from 3.98 to 4.06 and Local Add from 3.92 to 4.18. The improvements are specifically in editing locality and preservation, not in standalone dense prediction performance.

Conclusion: Perception-oriented dense supervision transfers useful structural knowledge to downstream creation tasks, especially for editing locality and preservation. The authors deliberately bound their claim: they do not assert superiority as a standalone dense predictor, only that dense supervision benefits unified creation.

Abstract: Unified image and video creation requires a model to follow diverse instructions while preserving identity, geometry, and temporal structure from visual context. However, semantic-only conditioning and creation-only training do not explicitly supervise the local structure needed for precise, temporally consistent editing. We therefore formulate depth and surface-normal prediction as image-form denoising targets, using these dense tasks as structured visual supervision within the same creation interface. Our framework decouples semantic interpretation from spatially aligned visual injection while sharing one multimodal diffusion transformer (MMDiT) backbone across all tasks. Mutual Context Attention (MCA), a paired-video data-construction procedure, and a progressive training curriculum then connect the learned structural cues to temporally localized editing and reference-conditioned creation. A single checkpoint obtains the highest overall score in the reported comparison of unified systems (4.15); adding dense supervision improves OpenVE Overall from 3.98 to 4.06 and Local Add from 3.92 to 4.18. These results support a deliberately bounded conclusion: perception-oriented dense supervision transfers useful structural knowledge to downstream creation, especially editing locality and preservation; we do not claim superiority as a standalone dense predictor.

</details>


### [21] [PolyComp: A Polycube-based Benchmark for Compositional 3D Spatial Reasoning in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14741)
*Siddharth Patel*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PolyComp is a procedurally generated and verified benchmark with 120 problems testing visual recognition and compositional spatial reasoning, where models must identify which of four options shows polycube components that combine to form a target solid. Across 360 presented problems per model (3 presentation formats), GPT-5.6 Sol achieves 50.0% accuracy, Claude Fable 5 achieves 39.4%, and Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview achieves 27.5% (near the 25% random guessing baseline).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a rigorous, procedurally generated benchmark that stresses visual recognition and compositional spatial reasoning, providing standardized evaluation of frontier multimodal models on complex geometric assembly tasks.

Method: The benchmark was procedurally generated and verified, containing 120 problems across four geometry families. Each problem has three presentation formats (single or multiple images), and models are evaluated by selecting which of four options shows a pair of polycube components that can combine to form a target solid. A problem development and evaluation protocol with cost and token accounting is provided.

Result: GPT-5.6 Sol with max effort attains 50.0% accuracy at $0.951 per problem; Claude Fable 5 attains 39.4% at $0.701; Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview attains 27.5% at $0.350, near the 25% random baseline. Accuracy spread across geometry families is larger than across presentation formats.

Conclusion: Current frontier models struggle significantly with compositional spatial reasoning, with only GPT-5.6 Sol clearly exceeding random chance while Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview performs near the random guessing baseline, indicating substantial room for improvement in this capability.

Abstract: We introduce PolyComp, a procedurally generated and verified benchmark that stresses visual recognition and compositional spatial reasoning. In each problem, a model must identify which of four options shows a pair of polycube components that can be combined to form a target solid. The benchmark contains 120 problems across four geometry families, and each problem has three different presentation formats using either a single image or multiple images. The random guessing baseline is 25%. Across the three presentations (360 presented problems per model), GPT-5.6 Sol with max effort attains 50.0% accuracy (95% problem-cluster CI 43.3-56.7%) at a mean cost of \$0.951 per presented problem, Claude Fable 5 with max effort attains 39.4% (33.1-46.1%) at \$0.701, and Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview with thinking level high attains 27.5% (22.8-32.5%), near the 25% random guessing baseline, at \$0.350. The observed accuracy spread across geometry families is larger than across presentation formats. We present a problem development and evaluation protocol, cost and token accounting, and release the 120 problems.

</details>


### [22] [Beyond Boundary Noise: Aggregated Aleatoric Uncertainty Fails to Capture Presence Ambiguity in 3D Lung Nodule Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14766)
*Simon Baur,Arne Schernich,Ekin Böke,Wojciech Samek,Jackie Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates whether entropy-based aleatoric uncertainty reflects clinically meaningful presence ambiguity (i.e., whether a pathology exists at all) in medical image segmentation, using 3D lung nodule segmentation across four architectures on LIDC-IDRI and LNDb. The authors find that entropy-based uncertainty captures boundary noise but fails at presence ambiguity, while a lightweight supervised ambiguity head trained on frozen features significantly outperforms all baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty estimation is critical for safe clinical deployment of deep learning in medical segmentation. While aleatoric uncertainty theoretically captures irreducible data ambiguity, it is unclear whether entropy-based measures reflect clinically meaningful ambiguity—specifically case-level disagreement about whether a pathology is present. Most prior work focused on pixel-wise boundary disagreement, so the authors systematically evaluate how well aleatoric uncertainty captures presence ambiguity.

Method: The evaluation spans 3D lung nodule segmentation across four architectures using Monte Carlo dropout and deep ensembles, on LIDC-IDRI and an external validation cohort (LNDb). The authors compare entropy-based uncertainty maps against a lightweight supervised ambiguity head trained on frozen segmentation features. They also compare against methods that explicitly model ambiguity under disagreement supervision (Probabilistic U-Net, Annotator-Confusion 3D-UNet), plus a qualitative feature-space analysis.

Result: Entropy-based uncertainty maps align with boundary noise and minor drawing variation but lack sufficient discriminative signal for presence ambiguity. The supervised ambiguity head substantially outperforms all entropy-aggregation-based baselines across architectures, metrics, and both cohorts, and matches or exceeds methods explicitly modeling ambiguity under disagreement supervision. Feature-space analysis reveals presence ambiguity is already encoded in frozen encoder features but discarded by the segmentation output and entropy aggregation.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental mismatch between the theoretical promise of aleatoric uncertainty and its practical behavior. Practitioners should not rely on entropy-based uncertainty as a proxy for clinical ambiguity in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Uncertainty estimation is critical for the safe clinical deployment of deep learning in medical image segmentation, with aleatoric uncertainty theoretically designed to capture irreducible data ambiguity. However, whether entropy-based measures reflect clinically meaningful ambiguity, i.e. case-level disagreement about whether a pathology is present at all, remains poorly understood. Contrary to most prior work, which focused on pixel-wise boundary disagreement, we systematically evaluate how well aleatoric uncertainty captures presence ambiguity. Our evaluation spans 3D lung nodule segmentation across four architectures with Monte Carlo dropout and deep ensembles, on LIDC-IDRI and an external validation cohort (LNDb). We find that entropy-based uncertainty maps align with boundary noise and minor drawing variation but carry insufficient discriminative signal for presence ambiguity. In contrast, a lightweight supervised ambiguity head trained on frozen segmentation features substantially outperforms all entropy-aggregation-based baselines across architectures, metrics, and both cohorts, and matches or exceeds methods that explicitly model ambiguity under disagreement supervision (Probabilistic U-Net, Annotator-Confusion 3D-UNet). A qualitative feature-space analysis shows that presence ambiguity is already encoded in the frozen encoder features of pixel-wise-trained networks, only to be discarded by the segmentation output and its entropy aggregation. Our findings expose a fundamental mismatch between the theoretical promise of aleatoric uncertainty and its practical behavior, and suggest that practitioners should not rely on entropy-based uncertainty as a proxy for clinical ambiguity in safety-critical applications.

</details>


### [23] [NARRATE: A Multimodal Real-World Australian Driving Dataset for Human-Centred Explanations in Automated Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14767)
*Ashkan Yousefi Zadeh,Zishuo Zhu,Xiaomeng Li,Andry Rakotonirainy,Sebastien Glaser,Ronald Schroeter,Patricia Delhomme,Zahra Mehraban*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NARRATE is a multimodal real-world Australian driving dataset with 2,050 annotated events from 35 experienced drivers, featuring synchronized sensor streams, driver-elicited explanations, and span-level Situational Awareness annotations across four benchmark tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing driving explanation datasets are observer-written, post-hoc, simulation-based, or sensor-generated rather than elicited from the actual driver performing the action, limiting their relevance for building human-centred explanation models for automated vehicles.

Method: The dataset collects events from 35 experienced drivers and instructors on public roads, each grounded in synchronized visual, localisation, motion, and LiDAR streams, paired with driver in-vehicle and/or post-drive free-text explanations, plus action labels, scenario-context labels (6 high-level, 32 fine-grained categories), and span-level SA annotations.

Result: Four benchmark tasks (SA, scenario-context, driver-action classification, and explanation generation) show the structure is learnable from driver language, though fine-grained context recognition and explanation generation remain challenging.

Conclusion: NARRATE paves a path towards more human-centred and domain-aware explanation models for automated driving by grounding explanations in real driver language and multimodal data.

Abstract: Automated vehicles must explain their decisions in ways that passengers can understand, monitor, and trust. Existing language-annotated driving datasets are mostly observer-written, post-hoc, simulation-based, or generated from sensor inputs, rather than elicited from the driver performing the action.
