We introduce NARRATE, a multimodal real-world Australian driving dataset comprising 2,050 annotated events from 35 experienced drivers and driving instructors on public roads. Each event is grounded in synchronised visual, localisation, motion, and LiDAR streams and paired with in-vehicle and/or post-drive free-text explanations. NARRATE provides action labels, scenario-context labels spanning six high-level and 32 fine-grained categories, and span-level Situational Awareness (SA) annotations over driver explanations for Perception, Comprehension and Projection. Four benchmark tasks (SA, scenario-context, driver-action classification, and explanation generation) show that this structure is learnable from driver language, while fine-grained context recognition and explanation generation remain challenging. NARRATE paves a path towards more human-centred and domain-aware explanation models for automated driving.

</details>


### [24] [Uncertainty Identifies Difficult Samples Across Methods: A Multi-Task Study on a Heterogeneous Skin Lesion Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14768)
*Leon Koole,Jiapan Guo,Matias Valdenegro-Toro*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates five uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods—MC Dropout, DropConnect, Flipout, Deep Ensembles, and DUQ—on a pooled ISIC skin lesion dataset using a shared backbone with dual heads (binary malignant/non-malignant and five-class diagnostic). Deep Ensembles performs best for calibration and uncertainty decomposition, while difficulty ranking of samples is largely method-agnostic, and uncertainty-based deferral effectively reduces errors through selective referral.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skin lesion classifiers can be confidently wrong on critical cases, making it clinically important to know when predictions should not be trusted. Reliable uncertainty quantification is needed to support safe clinical deployment.

Method: A shared backbone with two jointly learned heads (binary malignant/non-malignant and five-class diagnostic) is trained on a pooled ISIC dataset. Five UQ methods (MC Dropout, DropConnect, Flipout, Deep Ensembles, DUQ) are compared across accuracy, calibration, uncertainty decomposition, and risk-coverage metrics.

Result: Difficulty ranking is largely method-agnostic (per-sample entropy correlations of 0.54–0.91). Deep Ensembles is the clear winner for calibration and uncertainty decomposition. Uncertainty-based deferral removes a disproportionate share of errors, supporting selective referral in-distribution.

Conclusion: The choice of UQ method matters more for calibration and uncertainty decomposition than for identifying difficult cases. Uncertainty-based selective referral is a viable clinical strategy for reducing errors.

Abstract: Skin lesion classifiers can be confidently wrong on the cases that matter most, so knowing when a prediction should not be trusted is clinically as useful as the prediction. We study uncertainty quantification on a dataset pooled from many ISIC sources, with a shared backbone and two jointly learned heads: a binary malignant versus non-malignant head and a five-class diagnostic head. Five UQ methods (MC Dropout, DropConnect, Flipout, Deep Ensembles, DUQ) are compared on accuracy, calibration, uncertainty decomposition, and risk-coverage. Difficulty is largely method-agnostic: even methods with narrow entropy distributions rank the same samples as hard (per-sample entropy correlations of $0.54$ to $0.91$). The choice of method matters more for calibration and uncertainty decomposition, where Deep Ensembles is the clear winner, than for finding difficult cases. The ranking is also good enough that deferring the most uncertain cases removes a disproportionate share of errors, supporting uncertainty-based selective referral, evaluated here in-distribution only.

</details>


### [25] [Artificial Intelligence as a Tool for Combating Child Labour: A Real-Time Edge Vision Pipeline for Child Detection and Age Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14770)
*Mark Nowak*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a real-time computer-vision research prototype pipeline for continuous, presence-based child labour monitoring, combining detection, age estimation, tracking, re-identification, and track-level fusion, validated on proxy video and a 17-day field pilot in Zimbabwe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An estimated 138 million children worldwide remain in child labour, and existing monitoring systems built on periodic household visits and interviews systematically under-detect them. The paper motivates a continuous, presence-based evidence channel to supplement CLMRS.

Method: The pipeline combines a multi-task person and face detector (YOLO26x backbone in CerberusDet), cascaded age estimation (MiVOLO v2 with a child-specialist model for ages 0-12), ByteTrack tracking, ArcFace and DINOv2 re-identification, and track-level fusion producing per-person records. FP8 TensorRT compilation is used for acceleration.

Result: Person mAP@0.5 improved from 0.390 to 0.683 over baseline; child-specialist reaches 1.944 years MAE (vs 18-23 years for open-source stacks); 1.77x speedup with FP8 at +0.002 years MAE, running above twice real-time on embedded hardware. Found 634 unique child candidates vs 285 on proxy video. Field pilot: 36-fold yield improvement via tuning, over-reporting cut from 9.1x to 1.8-3.9x with zero proven-false merges.

Conclusion: The system demonstrates feasibility of continuous presence-based child labour monitoring, documenting both successes and failures in training/quantisation, and emphasizes required data-protection and human-in-the-loop safeguards.

Abstract: An estimated 138 million children remain in child labour worldwide, and the monitoring systems used by affected sectors, built on periodic household visits and interviews, systematically under-detect them. We present a real-time computer-vision pipeline, built and operated solely as a research prototype, that studies the feasibility of giving Child Labour Monitoring and Remediation Systems (CLMRS) a continuous, presence-based evidence channel. The pipeline combines a multi-task person and face detector (YOLO26x backbone in the CerberusDet framework), cascaded age estimation pairing MiVOLO v2 with a child-specialist model for ages 0-12, ByteTrack tracking, ArcFace and DINOv2 re-identification, and track-level fusion producing reviewable per-person records. The detector raises person mAP@0.5 from 0.390 to 0.683 over the previous-generation baseline; the child specialist reaches 1.944 years MAE on children-only validation, where widely used open-source stacks err by 18-23 years. FP8 TensorRT compilation yields a 1.77x speedup at +0.002 years MAE, bringing the pipeline above twice real-time on embedded hardware. On 26.8 hours of proxy video the system finds 634 unique child candidates versus 285 for its predecessor. We further report a seventeen-day unattended field pilot on a farm in Zimbabwe (38.7 million frames, six cameras) evaluated against a daily attendance register: software tuning improved detection yield 36-fold, and identity consolidation under a simultaneity veto cut over-reporting from 9.1x to 1.8-3.9x with zero proven-false merges. We document training and quantisation failures alongside successes, and the data-protection and human-in-the-loop safeguards such a system requires.

</details>


### [26] [AMPLIFAI: A Multiphase CT Dataset for Benchmarking Clinical Reasoning in LI-RADS Assessment of Liver Lesions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14778)
*Pranav Kulkarni,Nikhil Shah,Amritansh Suryavanshi,Jana Delfino,James Tonascia,Jade Wong-You-Cheong,Barton Lane,Joseph Chirico,Jeffrey D. Hirsch,Ang Li,Heng Huang,Florence X. Doo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces AMPLIFAI, the first publicly available dataset of multiphase abdominal CT scans annotated with LI-RADS categories and segmented for three key LI-RADS features (arterial phase hyperenhancement, washout, and enhancing capsule), aimed at enabling AI model development for automated HCC diagnosis. The dataset is documented following the Datasheets for Datasets format for transparency and reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early detection of hepatocellular carcinoma improves survival significantly, and LI-RADS criteria provide a fully imaging-based framework for HCC diagnosis. However, the lack of large, publicly available datasets with high-quality labels has hindered the development of AI models for LI-RADS characterization.

Method: The authors constructed the AMPLIFAI dataset of multiphase abdominal CT scans annotated with LI-RADS categories and segmentation for three major LI-RADS features. The dataset documentation follows the Datasheets for Datasets format, detailing composition, curation process, and annotation pipeline.

Result: The AMPLIFAI dataset is introduced as the first public multiphase abdominal CT dataset with LI-RADS category annotations and feature segmentations, providing a benchmark for AI research in HCC diagnosis.

Conclusion: By releasing this dataset with detailed documentation, the authors aim to facilitate transparent, reproducible research and accelerate AI model development for automated HCC diagnosis based on LI-RADS criteria.

Abstract: Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is the third leading cause of cancer-related mortality worldwide, with early detection improving survival from <20\% to >70\%. The standardized LI-RADS criteria establish a biopsy-free, fully imaging-based framework that can serve as a foundation for automating HCC diagnosis with artificial intelligence (AI). However, the lack of large, publicly available datasets with high-quality labels has limited the development of AI models for LI-RADS characterization. We introduce the \textbf{AMPLIFAI} dataset, the first public dataset of multiphase abdominal CT scans annotated with LI-RADS categories and segmented for three major LI-RADS features: arterial phase hyperenhancement, washout, and enhancing capsule. Following the \emph{Datasheets for Datasets} format, this paper details the dataset's composition, curation process, and annotation pipeline to facilitate transparent, reproducible research.

</details>


### [27] [MegaParts: Scaling Part-Aware 3D Object Generation to 300 Parts via Token-Efficient Autoregressive Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14783)
*Manwen Liao,Xinyu Lian,Jian Mao,Kaixu Chen,Li Luo,Jinghao Yan,Wanshui Gan,Qiao Yu,Weitian Zhang,Chunhua Shen,Guang Chen,Bo Dai,Xudong Xu,Zhaoyang Lyu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MegaParts is a scalable autoregressive 3D generation framework that combines structured sequence modeling with a token-efficient vector-quantized shape tokenizer to generate part-aware 3D objects with up to 300 parts and 256k token sequences, achieving better mesh quality than baseline autoregressive and diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing part-aware 3D generation methods do not scale well to highly complex objects because increasing the number of parts makes generating detailed geometry prohibitively expensive in token length and memory.

Method: MegaParts combines a token-efficient vector-quantized shape tokenizer that learns discrete latent representations for part-level geometry via adaptive-length tokenization based on geometric complexity, with an LLM trained to generate object bounding boxes, part bounding boxes, and part shape tokens in a unified structured sequence, using efficient long-context training.

Result: The method scales to objects with up to 300 parts and sequence lengths up to 256k tokens, achieving higher mesh quality than baseline autoregressive and diffusion models, demonstrating that compressed discrete part tokens improve both scalability and fidelity.

Conclusion: LLM native token-efficient autoregressive modeling is a compelling alternative to diffusion for large-scale part-aware 3D generation, enabling fine-grained part-level control while preserving compositional structure.

Abstract: Part-aware 3D object generation is essential for graphics applications such as controllable modeling, editing, and articulation, where objects are represented as coherent assemblies of semantic parts. However, existing part-aware generation methods, do not scale well to highly complex objects. As the number of parts increases, generating detailed geometry becomes prohibitively expensive in token length and memory. We introduce MegaParts, a scalable autoregressive 3D generation framework to address this challenge by combining structured sequence modeling with a token-efficient vector-quantized shape tokenizer. Our tokenizer learns discrete latent representations for part-level geometry by minimizing token usage subject to high-fidelity reconstruction, enabling adaptive-length tokenization based on geometric complexity. On top of this compact representation, we train a large language model to generate object bounding boxes, part bounding boxes, and part shape tokens within a unified structured sequence. Combined with efficient long-context training strategy, our token-efficient formulation scales to objects with up to 300 parts and sequence lengths up to 256k tokens. This substantially extends the scale of part-aware 3D generation while preserving compositional structure and enabling fine-grained part-level control. Our method achieves higher mesh quality than baseline autoregressive and diffusion models, showing that compressed discrete part tokens improve not only scalability but also the achievable fidelity of generated geometry. These results suggest that LLM native token-efficient autoregressive modeling is a compelling alternative to diffusion for large-scale part-aware 3D generation. The project page is available at https://expmaster.github.io/megaparts_webpage.

</details>


### [28] [Qwen-Video-Edit: Instruction-Based Video Editing by Repurposing an Image Editing Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14790)
*Yunpeng Bai,Yossi Gandelsman,Michaël Gharbi,Qixing Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This report shows that a strong instruction-based image editing model (Qwen-Image-Edit) can be adapted to video editing with minimal modifications by operating directly on video-VAE latents. The approach arranges latent frames as tiles of a large virtual image, reuses image positional encodings per tile, and bridges latent spaces with lightweight input/output projections warm-started from the editor's own patchify/unpatchify layers. Fine-tuning on Ditto-1M plus optional Wan 2.2 denoising steps yields strong results, demonstrating that per-frame video latents remain close enough to the image domain for mature image editing priors to transfer effectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional instruction-based video editing builds on video-pretrained generative backbones, requiring expensive adaptation of video diffusion transformers. The authors explore a cheaper alternative: leveraging a strong image editing model to edit videos directly, motivated by observations that the image editor already edits video frames (e.g., as contact sheets) zero-shot with recognizable quality, leaving only a fidelity gap to close via fine-tuning.

Method: Starting from Qwen-Image-Edit, the method arranges video-VAE latent frames (from Wan 2.1) as tiles of one large virtual image, reuses the editor's image positional encoding per tile, and bridges the two latent spaces with lightweight input/output projections warm-started from the editor's own patchify and unpatchify layers. At initialization, a static video is embedded exactly as an image. The system is fine-tuned on Ditto-1M editing triplets, with optional Wan 2.2 denoising steps as a temporal enhancer.

Result: The design is motivated by a chain of zero-training observations: the stock image editor can edit videos shown as contact sheets, is indifferent to joint vs. per-frame latent encoding, and edits genuine video latents zero-shot to a recognizable degree. After fine-tuning, the system closes the fidelity gap and achieves strong video editing performance, suggesting mature image editing priors transfer with minimal adaptation.

Conclusion: Despite the large investment in training video latent spaces, per-frame video latents remain close enough to the image domain that image editing priors transfer effectively. This indicates a promising, low-cost route for video editing by adapting existing image editing models with lightweight bridges and minimal fine-tuning.

Abstract: Instruction-based video editing is commonly built on video-pretrained generative backbones: a video diffusion transformer is adapted, at considerable cost, to condition on a source video and an editing instruction. In this report we explore a different route and show that a strong instruction-based image editing model can edit videos by operating directly on video-VAE latents. Starting from Qwen-Image-Edit, we arrange the latent frames of a Wan~2.1 video VAE as tiles of one large virtual image, reuse the editor's image positional encoding for every tile, and bridge the two latent spaces with a pair of lightweight input/output projections warm-started from the editor's own patchify and unpatchify layers, so that at initialization a (static) video is embedded exactly as an image the model already understands. The whole system is then fine-tuned on the public Ditto-1M editing triplets, and a few denoising steps of Wan~2.2 serve as an optional temporal enhancer. We motivate the design with a chain of zero-training observations: the stock image editor already edits a video presented as a contact sheet; it is indifferent to whether the sheet's tokens come from one joint encode or from per-frame encodes stitched in latent space; and it even edits genuine video latents zero-shot to a clearly recognizable degree, leaving fine-tuning only a fidelity gap to close. Our results suggest that, despite the large investment in training video latent spaces, per-frame video latents remain close enough to the image domain that mature image editing priors transfer with minimal adaptation. Project Page: https://yunpeng1998.github.io/Qwen-Video-Edit-Page; Code: https://github.com/yunpeng1998/Qwen-Video-Edit; Model: https://huggingface.co/yunpeng1998/Qwen-Video-Edit.

</details>


### [29] [Zero-Shot Adaptation of Medical Vision Foundation Models for High-Frequency Micro-Ultrasound Prostate Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14796)
*Ayusha Abbas,Saram Abbas,Kabita Adhikari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents the first zero-shot prostate segmentation pipeline for micro-ultrasound using MedSAM (pre-trained foundation model) combined with CLAHE, binary dilation, and Fourier smoothing post-processing. The pipeline reduces boundary-distance error by 45%, achieving Dice 0.865 on a held-out test set (from 0.749 baseline), with performance statistically indistinguishable from non-expert raters while being 38-52% more consistent. Requires only an approximate bounding box, enabling deployment without data collection or retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prostate cancer kills one person every 80 seconds, and early detection depends on accurate gland boundary segmentation for PSA density calculation and biopsy decisions. Conventional ultrasound blurs boundaries (missing 1 in 3 high-risk cancers), and while micro-ultrasound (29 MHz) provides 3x better resolution, it introduces dense acoustic speckle causing >10% area disagreement between clinicians. Supervised methods are costly and generalize poorly across scanners, motivating the need for a zero-shot, training-free approach.

Method: The pipeline uses MedSAM (pre-trained on 1.5M+ medical images) for zero-shot prostate localization, requiring a spatial prompt. Two prompt strategies are evaluated: bounding-box and point-click. Post-processing consists of CLAHE to sharpen the outer wall, binary dilation to recover missed pixels, and Fourier smoothing (4 modes, s=1.05) to refine the boundary. Evaluation is conducted on 75 patients (2,621 slices) from the Micro-Ultrasound Prostate Segmentation dataset, with a 20-patient held-out test set.

Result: The pipeline reduces mean boundary-distance error by 45% (Dice improved from 0.749±0.043 to 0.865±0.029; HD95 from 217.2±36.9 to 120.1±26.1 px), reaching Dice 0.859 across the cohort. Mean overlap shows no significant difference from three non-expert rater groups (p>0.19) but is 38-52% more consistent (lower inter-patient standard deviation). Bounding-box prompts work; point-click prompts fail (best Dice=0.350) due to speckle lacking stable local contrast.

Conclusion: A foundation model can segment the prostate in micro-ultrasound zero-shot with no training data, achieving clinician-competitive accuracy with greater consistency. The method is deployable in any clinic since it requires only an approximate bounding box—no data collection, annotation, or retraining needed. Point-click prompting is unsuitable for this modality due to speckle characteristics.

Abstract: Prostate cancer claims a life every 80 seconds. Early detection is needed to prevent disease progression, and both PSA density calculation and biopsy decisions rely on knowing the exact boundary of the gland. Conventional ultrasound at 6-12 MHz blurs this boundary, missing one in three high-risk cancers. Micro-ultrasound (29 MHz) improves resolution threefold but introduces dense acoustic speckle that obscures the outer wall; given the same image, two clinicians draw outlines differing by over 10% in area. Supervised methods are costly and generalise poorly across scanners. Can a foundation model segment the prostate with no training data?
  We present the first zero-shot pipeline for this modality: MedSAM, pre-trained on over 1.5 million medical images, localises the prostate; we then apply CLAHE to sharpen the outer wall, binary dilation to recover missed pixels, and Fourier smoothing (4 modes, s=1.05) to refine the boundary. MedSAM requires a spatial prompt, so we evaluate bounding-box and point-click strategies across 75 patients of the Micro-Ultrasound Prostate Segmentation dataset (2,621 slices).
  On the 20-patient held-out test set, the pipeline reduces mean boundary-distance error by 45% (Dice 0.749+/-0.043 to 0.865+/-0.029; HD95 217.2+/-36.9 to 120.1+/-26.1 px), reaching Dice 0.859 across the cohort. Its mean overlap shows no significant difference from the three non-expert rater groups (p>0.19), while segmenting 38-52% more consistently (lower inter-patient standard deviation). Point-click prompts fail regardless of placement (best Dice=0.350), because speckle gives no stable local contrast. Only an approximate bounding box is required, so any clinic can deploy it without data collection, annotation, or retraining.

</details>


### [30] [Where the Cost Falls: A Deployment-Aware Adoption Order for Stability Enhancements to Cycle-Consistent Adversarial Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14811)
*Rowan Hussein,Mohamed Ouf*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper analyzes four common enhancements to cycle-consistent adversarial networks (CycleGAN-style) for unpaired image-to-image translation, showing that while they are typically compared only on output quality, they also divide sharply by where their computational cost falls. Three enhancements (Wasserstein + gradient penalty, VGG19 perceptual loss, multi-scale discriminators) only affect training, while self-attention persists into the deployed generator with quadratic memory growth, making it the component a resource-constrained team should defer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Teams using cycle-consistent adversarial networks face persistent failures: adversarial training oscillation/collapse, cycle consistency preserving coarse layout while finer texture drifts, and single discriminators missing local artifacts. Four enhancements address these, but they are usually compared only on output quality, ignoring how their computational cost differs and whether they impact deployed inference.

Method: The authors integrate all four enhancements (Wasserstein objective with gradient penalty, VGG19 perceptual loss on cycle reconstruction, multi-scale discriminators, and self-attention) onto a lightly tuned baseline for horse-to-zebra translation. They introduce each mechanism one at a time on a fixed control, then combined, documenting the failure mode each targets and how it integrates. They report FID and KID for the combined model and specify a protocol needed for ranking on measured evidence.

Result: The key result is the architectural division of cost: Wasserstein + gradient penalty, VGG19 perceptual loss, and multi-scale discriminators change training only (adoptable/droppable without altering shipped code), while self-attention persists into the deployed generator with memory growing quadratically with feature-map size. This yields an adoption order for teams under compute or latency budgets. The baseline produced collapse and reconstruction-artifact modes, documented through visual inspection.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that these four enhancements divide by where their cost falls, which follows from architecture rather than any particular run. This yields a deployment-aware adoption order: resource-constrained teams should adopt the three training-only enhancements first and defer self-attention, since it is the only component with persistent inference cost growing quadratically in feature-map size. A rigorous protocol is still needed to rank these enhancements on measured evidence.

Abstract: Teams that adopt cycle-consistent adversarial networks for unpaired image-to-image translation meet the same obstacles: adversarial training oscillates or collapses, cycle consistency preserves coarse layout while finer texture drifts, and a single discriminator judging global realism misses local artifacts. Four enhancements address these failures, and they are usually compared on output quality alone. We show that they also divide sharply by where their cost falls, and that this division, which follows from the architecture and not from any particular run, yields an adoption order for teams under a compute or latency budget. A Wasserstein objective with gradient penalty, a VGG19 perceptual loss on the cycle reconstruction, and multi-scale discriminators change training only, so a team can adopt or drop them without altering what ships. Self-attention alone persists into the deployed generator, with memory growing as the square of the feature-map size, which makes it the one component a resource-constrained team should defer. We integrate all four onto a lightly tuned baseline for horse-to-zebra translation, introduced one at a time on a fixed control and then combined, and for each we give the failure mode it targets and how it integrates. We document the collapse and reconstruction-artifact modes the baseline produced, report what visual inspection of saved samples showed for each variant, and report Fréchet Inception Distance and Kernel Inception Distance for the combined model. We specify the protocol still needed, covering the individual variants, perceptual similarity, and downstream segmentation, to rank these enhancements on measured evidence.

</details>


### [31] [OvDSGG: End-to-End Open-Vocabulary Dynamic Scene Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14835)
*John Helsby,Yi Yang,Bodo Rosenhahn,Michael Ying Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OvDSGG is the first end-to-end open-vocabulary dynamic scene graph generation framework, using a Triplet Feature Extraction Module and a Visual-Language Alignment Module with adaptive decision boundaries, achieving 10-20.4 percentage point improvements over baselines on a new open-vocabulary DSGG benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic scene graph generation (DSGG) methods are closed-set, limited to fixed training vocabularies and struggling with long-tailed distributions of rare concepts, while existing open-vocabulary approaches rely on expensive multi-stage training with pretrained large language models.

Method: OvDSGG builds on an open-vocabulary Spatial Backbone and Temporal Backbone, with a Triplet Feature Extraction Module bridging them and a Visual-Language Alignment Module that learns an adaptive decision boundary in joint visual-language feature space (without knowledge distillation).

Result: OvDSGG outperforms open-vocabulary baselines across all metrics with zero-shot Recall@K scores 10.0-20.4 percentage points higher than the next-best baseline, while remaining competitive with state-of-the-art models on closed-set DSGG.

Conclusion: The paper introduces the first end-to-end open-vocabulary DSGG framework along with a rigorous open-vocabulary benchmark adapted from Action Genome, demonstrating significant improvements in zero-shot performance.

Abstract: Dynamic scene graphs (DSGs) capture spatio-temporal interactions across videos as $\langle$subject, predicate, object$\rangle$ triplets, and underpin downstream tasks such as video captioning, video question answering, and action analysis. However, end-to-end dynamic scene graph generation (DSGG) methods are closed-set: they recognize only objects and predicates from a fixed training vocabulary and struggle with the long-tailed distribution of rare concepts, severely limiting their real-world applicability. Existing open-vocabulary models typically inherit pretrained large language models, resulting in multi-stage training and inference with substantial cost. We introduce OvDSGG, the first end-to-end framework for open-vocabulary DSGG. OvDSGG builds on top of an open-vocabulary Spatial Backbone and a Temporal Backbone; we further propose a Triplet Feature Extraction Module that bridges them, and a Visual-Language Alignment Module that preserves open-vocabulary recognition by learning an adaptive decision boundary in the joint visual-language feature space, without expensive knowledge distillation in existing methods. We further introduce a rigorous open-vocabulary DSGG benchmark adapted from Action Genome, with disjoint Base/Novel splits for both objects and predicates. OvDSGG significantly outperforms open-vocabulary baselines across all metrics, with zero-shot Recall@$K$ scores 10.0--20.4 percentage point higher than the next-best baseline, while on closed-set DSGG remaining competitive with state-of-the-art models. Code and benchmark are publicly available at https://github.com/jhelsby/OvDSGG/.

</details>


### [32] [Zero-MELO: Test-Time Evidence Calibration with Multimodal LLMs for Zero-Shot Micro-Gesture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14854)
*Chengyan Wang,Hanliang Xie,Yueyi Yang,Haoyu Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Zero-MELO, a test-time evidence calibration framework for micro-gesture recognition (MGR) using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It addresses the poor performance of MLLMs on fine-grained, motion-centric micro-gesture tasks through a tree search mechanism for localized visual evidence acquisition, a test-time calibration module to mitigate score biases, and a multi-cue fusion module for robust prediction. The method achieves significant improvements over the Qwen2.5-VL baseline on iMiGUE (26.84% vs 16.15%) and MA-52 (22.10% vs 10.20%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs underperform on fine-grained, motion-centric tasks like micro-gesture recognition (MGR). While MGR is critical for implicit affective analysis, MLLMs lack motion-sensitive representation ability due to their single-pass forward inference, leading to insufficient localized evidence and severe score biases from language and motion-agnostic appearances.

Method: The authors propose Zero-MELO, a test-time evidence calibration framework with three key components: (1) a tree search mechanism that progressively acquires localized fine-grained visual evidence, (2) a test-time calibration module to mitigate score biases, and (3) a multi-cue fusion module that integrates evidence from multiple cues for final prediction rather than relying on a single cue.

Result: The framework achieves mean-class accuracies of 26.84% on iMiGUE and 22.10% on MA-52, significantly outperforming the Qwen2.5-VL baseline which produces 16.15% and 10.20% respectively.

Conclusion: Carefully designed test-time guidance can substantially enhance the motion-sensitive representation ability of MLLMs for fine-grained micro-gesture recognition, overcoming the inherent constraints of single-pass forward inference.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel in general video understanding, their capability in fine-grained and motion-centric tasks remains limited. This limitation is particularly critical in micro-gesture recognition (MGR), where micro-gestures (MGs) - subtle, short-duration, and spatially localized human movements - serve as key discriminative signals for implicit affective analysis, yet are easily neglected following common prompting practices. Although MGR has been intensively studied by many discriminative approaches, the use of MLLMs for MGR is underexplored, with notably poor performance. We hypothesize that the motion-sensitive representation ability of MLLMs is constrained by their inherent single-pass forward inference, which can be substantially enhanced through carefully designed test-time guidance. Motivated by this, building on our prior findings regarding temporal insensitivity in Video LLMs, we diagnose zero-shot MGR errors in the Negative Log-Likelihood (NLL) space. We observe that MLLMs suffer from two bottlenecks: 1) insufficient localized evidence and 2) severe score biases driven by language and motion-agnostic appearances. Thus, we propose a novel test-time evidence calibration framework that improves both reasoning details and prediction reliability. Specifically, we introduce a tree search mechanism to progressively acquire localized, fine-grained visual evidence, coupled with a test-time calibration module to mitigate score biases. The multi-cue fusion module then integrates evidence from multiple cues without relying on a single cue for final prediction. Our framework achieves mean-class accuracies of 26.84\% on iMiGUE and 22.10\% on MA-52, significantly outperforming the Qwen2.5-VL baseline, which produces 16.15\% and 10.20\%, respectively. The code will be available at https://zero-melo.github.io/Zero-MELO.

</details>


### [33] [Beam-Wise Statistical Background Subtraction for Static Roadside LiDAR: A Cross-Sensor Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14868)
*Alexander Baumann,Marcel Vosshans,Thao Dang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a benchmark for beam-wise statistical background subtraction in static roadside LiDAR, introducing the HighwayScene dataset and extended CoopScenes annotations. It shows that beam-wise statistical modeling with spatial consistency filtering achieves robust, transferable, real-time background subtraction across heterogeneous sensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Background subtraction is essential for infrastructure-based LiDAR perception, but systematic cross-sensor evaluations and reproducible studies for static roadside LiDAR are currently missing. This gap hinders fair comparison and deployment of such methods.

Method: The authors formulate background estimation as a per-beam temporal modeling problem, investigating complementary statistical strategies that capture dominant and multi-modal background structures, combined with spatial filtering in both angular and 3D domains. They also introduce a new multi-LiDAR dataset (HighwayScene) and extend CoopScenes with static/dynamic point-wise annotations for reproducible evaluation.

Result: Beam-wise statistical modeling provides a robust and transferable solution across multiple scenes and heterogeneous sensing technologies. Combining lightweight per-beam models with spatial consistency filtering substantially improves precision while maintaining high recall and real-time capability.

Conclusion: Beam-wise statistical background subtraction is an effective, transferable approach for static roadside LiDAR. The authors publicly release all datasets, annotations, and implementations to enable reproducible research in this area.

Abstract: Background subtraction is a key preprocessing step for infrastructure-based LiDAR perception, enabling efficient isolation of dynamic traffic participants without semantic annotations. However, systematic cross-sensor evaluations and reproducible studies for static roadside LiDAR are missing. This paper presents a comparative benchmark of beam-wise statistical background subtraction for statically mounted LiDAR sensors. We formulate background estimation as a per-beam temporal modeling problem and investigate complementary statistical strategies that capture dominant as well as multi-modal background structures, combined with spatial filtering in the angular and 3D domain. To enable reproducible evaluation, we introduce HighwayScene, a new multi-LiDAR dataset recorded in a static roadside setup, and extend the public CoopScenes dataset with static/dynamic point-wise annotations. Across multiple scenes and heterogeneous sensing technologies, we demonstrate that beam-wise statistical modeling provides a robust and transferable solution. Combining lightweight per-beam models with spatial consistency filtering substantially improves precision while maintaining high recall and real-time capability. All datasets, annotations, and implementations are publicly released.

</details>


### [34] [SpIn-ViT: Designing a Sparsity-Induced Vision Transformer That Is Mechanistically Interpretable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14922)
*Philip H. Lee,Parth Padalkar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SpIn-ViT jointly trains a pretrained Vision Transformer with a modified Sparse Autoencoder end-to-end, aligning sparse patch-level representations directly with image classification. It substantially outperforms post-hoc SAE methods in accuracy, interpretability scores, and human evaluations, while also producing smaller and more accurate neurosymbolic rule-sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-hoc SAEs trained on frozen ViT representations produce sparse features that are not aligned with the downstream classification objective, limiting their interpretability and usefulness.

Method: SpIn-ViT jointly trains a pretrained ViT with a modified SAE end-to-end, aligning sparse patch-level neuron activations with the image classification task. Interpretable rule-sets are extracted from SAE neurons to build neurosymbolic models.

Result: Compared to SOTA post-hoc SAE: +8.84% average classification accuracy, ~4x AI-based interpretability score, >2x human evaluation score. Neurosymbolic models achieve +5.97% accuracy with 58.8% smaller rule-sets.

Conclusion: Jointly training ViTs with SAEs directly aligned to classification objectives yields semantically coherent and more interpretable features without sacrificing predictive performance, enabling more compact and accurate neurosymbolic models.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability has recently expanded to Vision Transformers (ViTs), with Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) increasingly used as post-hoc tools to decompose internal representations into sparse and more interpretable features. However, because post-hoc SAEs are trained on frozen representations after the ViT has already been optimized, their latent features are not directly aligned with the downstream classification objective. We introduce SpIn-ViT, a framework that jointly trains a pretrained ViT and a modified SAE end-to-end, directly aligning sparse patch-level representations with image classification. SpIn-ViT learns semantically coherent neuron activations that localize meaningful image regions while maintaining competitive predictive performance. We evaluate SpIn-ViT across nine image-classification benchmarks using classification accuracy, quantitative interpretability metrics, AI-based and Human evaluations. Compared with the previous state-of-the-art post-hoc SAE method, SpIn-ViT achieves 8.84% higher average classification accuracy, an AI-based interpretability score nearly four times as high, and a human-evaluation score more than twice as high. We further extract interpretable rule-sets using the SAE neurons to create neurosymbolic models which achieve 5.97% higher average classification accuracy while requiring a 58.8\% smaller rule-set than the neurosymbolic models created from the SOTA post-hoc SAE method.

</details>


### [35] [PaSTel: Anchoring Histology in Spatial Transcriptomics via Multi-Scale Hierarchical Bio-Prior Contrastive Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14924)
*Azim Dehghani Amirabad,Junchao Zhu,Pushpak Pati,Walid Abdelmoula,Tommaso Mansi,Rui Liao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PaSTel is a hierarchical multimodal pretraining framework for spatial transcriptomics that integrates biological priors at three levels—spot (TF-IDF gene reweighting), functional (KEGG pathway anchors), and regional (spatial clustering)—to learn more informative and transferable representations than existing vision and vision-omics encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing spatial transcriptomics multimodal pretraining methods have two key limitations: (1) spatially informative gene selection is dominated by ubiquitous housekeeping genes, producing weakly discriminative representations, and (2) independent spot-patch alignment neglects spatial dependencies critical for tissue organization.

Method: PaSTel is a hierarchical multimodal pretraining framework with three levels of biological priors: (1) spot-level TF-IDF reweighting to identify spatially informative genes, (2) functional-level curated KEGG pathways used as anchors to encode global biological semantics, and (3) regional-level spatial clustering that aggregates neighboring spots to model meso-scale tissue structure.

Result: PaSTel consistently outperforms existing vision and vision-omics encoders across multiple downstream tasks in spatial transcriptomics.

Conclusion: Incorporating multiscale biological priors yields more informative and transferable representations for spatial transcriptomics, demonstrating that hierarchical integration of biological knowledge improves multimodal pretraining quality.

Abstract: Spatial transcriptomics (ST) links tissue morphology with molecular programs, motivating multimodal pretraining methods that align histology images with gene expression. However, existing approaches suffer from two key limitations: spatially informative gene selection is often dominated by ubiquitous housekeeping genes, leading to weakly discriminative representations, and independent spot-patch alignment fails to capture spatial dependencies that are critical for tissue organization. To address these challenges, we introduce PaSTel, a hierarchical multimodal pretraining framework that integrates biological priors at three levels. At the spot level, TF-IDF reweighting is used to identify spatially informative genes; at the functional level, curated KEGG pathways serve as anchors for encoding global biological semantics; and at the regional level, spatial clustering aggregates neighboring spots to model meso-scale tissue structure. Across multiple downstream tasks, PaSTel consistently outperforms existing vision and vision-omics encoders, demonstrating that incorporating multiscale biological priors yields more informative and transferable representations for spatial transcriptomics.

</details>


### [36] [Looks Can be Deceiving: Annotator and Reviewer Performance Across Imagery Sources in Crowd-Sourced Aerial Damage Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14942)
*Thomas Manzini,Priyankari Perali,Raisa Karnik,Stephen Johnson,Robin R. Murphy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The first empirical study of annotator and reviewer performance across multi-source aerial imagery (drone, crewed aviation, satellite) for post-disaster damage assessment, finding that label revision rates rise steeply with decreasing image resolution and that uniform review allocation leaves the most residual disagreement in lower-resolution imagery, leading to recommendations for adaptive task assignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing aerial imagery datasets rely predominantly on single-source imagery, so there is no established practice for efficiently allocating human labor when curating large-scale, multi-source aerial datasets.

Method: Analyzed annotator and reviewer performance in a post-disaster building damage assessment dataset spanning 9 disasters, with 20041 drone, 20695 crewed aviation, and 33392 satellite building labels provided by 187 annotators, refined through a single-reviewer pass and then a consensus-committee review.

Result: Initial annotations were revised by the final committee at rates rising from higher- to lower-resolution sources (25.27% crewed aviation, 36.95% satellite). After a single review, committees still revised 6.85% of drone, 14.05% of crewed, and 20.86% of satellite labels, showing lower-resolution imagery retains the most residual disagreement under uniform review allocation.

Conclusion: Uniform review allocation leaves the most residual disagreement in lower-resolution imagery, and the paper offers three recommendations for multi-source dataset curation based on adaptive task assignment and budget-aware quality control principles.

Abstract: This paper presents the first known empirical investigation of annotator and reviewer performance across multi-source remotely sensed imagery, evaluating human labeling across drone, crewed aviation, and satellite views. Because existing aerial imagery datasets rely predominantly on single-source imagery, there is no currently established state of practice for efficiently allocating human labor to curate large-scale, multi-source aerial datasets. This work addresses this limitation by analyzing annotator and reviewer performance within a post-disaster building damage assessment dataset of 9 disasters, where 20041 buildings in drone, 20695 buildings in crewed aviation, and 33392 buildings in satellite imagery were labeled. These labels, provided by 187 annotators, were then refined through two successive quality-control stages: a single-reviewer pass followed by a consensus-committee review. Our analysis reveals two findings that raise questions for standard crowd-sourcing practices. First, initial annotations were revised by the final committee at rates that rise steeply from higher- to lower-resolution sources (25.27% for crewed aviation and 36.95% for satellite), with the same ordering at every observed workflow stage. Second, a single individual review reduced but did not resolve this disagreement: after review, the committee still revised 6.85% of drone, 14.05% of crewed, and 20.86% of satellite labels. These observations suggest that, in workflows like this one, uniform review allocation leaves the most residual disagreement in lower-resolution imagery. Based on this evidence, and consistent with prior work on adaptive task assignment and budget-aware quality control, this paper offers three recommendations for multi-source dataset curation.

</details>


### [37] [Benchmarking Frontier Text-to-Image Models on Image-Description Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14976)
*Sajjad Abdoli,Ghassan Al-Sumaidaee,Ahmed Rashad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates four production text-to-image systems (Hunyuan 3.0, Gemini 3 Pro Image, FLUX.2, Ideogram 3.0) on the 48 hardest compositionally demanding prompts from the DataSeeds.AI dataset, using an independent-judge MECE rubric graded by Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview. Gemini 3 Pro Image ranks first (84.8/100), slightly ahead of FLUX.2 (82.3/100); leading systems fail mainly through object miscounting and geometric artifacts, while trailing systems struggle with garbled text and element omission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard text-to-image evaluation uses average-case prompts, which masks performance gaps between systems on complex, compositionally demanding requests (precise counts, attribute binding, embedded text, spatial constraints). This paper addresses the need for rigorous stress-testing of production systems on such hard prompts.

Method: The evaluation selects 48 hardest prompts from the DataSeeds.AI Sample Dataset via automated complexity scoring. GPT-5.4-Pro authors an atomic, weighted MECE rubric; Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview independently judges criterion satisfaction. Four production systems are scored: Hunyuan 3.0, Gemini 3 Pro Image, FLUX.2, Ideogram 3.0.

Result: Gemini 3 Pro Image scores 84.8/100 and ranks first, narrowly ahead of FLUX.2 at 82.3/100. Ideogram 3.0 scores 65.7/100 and Hunyuan 3.0 scores 63.3/100. Failure analysis reveals leading systems lose points mainly on object miscounting and geometric artifacts, while trailing systems more often output garbled text; Ideogram 3.0 also frequently omits requested elements.

Conclusion: Even top-tier production text-to-image systems exhibit meaningful weaknesses on compositionally demanding prompts, particularly in object counting and geometric correctness. The gap between leading and trailing systems is substantial, justifying stress-test-based evaluation as complementary to average-case reporting.

Abstract: Text-to-image models are typically reported on average-case prompts, which understates the gap between systems on compositionally demanding requests involving precise object counts, multi-object attribute binding, legible embedded text, and explicit spatial constraints. We evaluate four production text-to-image systems: Hunyuan 3.0, Gemini 3 Pro Image ("Nano Banana Pro"), Black Forest Labs FLUX.2, and Ideogram 3.0. The evaluation uses the 48 hardest prompts drawn from the DataSeeds.AI Sample Dataset (DSD), selected through an automated complexity-scoring pass over the full corpus. Every generated image is graded using an independent-judge rubric. GPT-5.4-Pro authors an atomic, weighted, mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive (MECE) evaluation rubric, while Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview independently determines whether each criterion is satisfied. Gemini 3 Pro Image ranks first with a score of 84.8/100, narrowly ahead of FLUX.2 at 82.3/100. Ideogram 3.0 and Hunyuan 3.0 score 65.7/100 and 63.3/100, respectively. Failure analysis shows that the leading systems primarily lose points through object miscounting and geometric artifacts, whereas the trailing systems more frequently produce garbled text. Ideogram 3.0 also frequently omits requested elements. Full per-sample rubrics, scores, and failure annotations are available from the authors upon request.

</details>


### [38] [Risk-Adaptive Edge--Cloud Visual Reasoning for Communication-Efficient Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14991)
*Meng Ma,Shuyang Li,Naigang Wang,Ruimin Ke*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a risk-adaptive edge-cloud architecture for autonomous driving, where an onboard VLM and lightweight detector assess traffic conditions to decide when to request cloud-based VLM reasoning. The system reduces cloud requests by 54.1% while maintaining task success rates, with fewer AEB activations compared to periodic cloud access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud-hosted VLMs offer superior contextual reasoning but frequent visual uploads introduce communication overhead and latency that hinder tactical driving decisions. The authors aim to balance the benefits of cloud reasoning with the constraints of real-time onboard decision-making.

Method: A risk-adaptive edge-cloud architecture where an onboard VLM and lightweight detector monitor temporal traffic conditions and path-relative hazards. The onboard system provides conservative local responses and selectively triggers cloud access based on traffic assessment. Cloud models provide tactical advice, while validation, vehicle control, and automatic emergency braking remain local. Evaluated in CARLA with a delayed-roadwork ablation and three emulated network profiles.

Result: The method matched the task success rate of periodic cloud access while reducing cloud requests by 54.1% and recording fewer AEB activations. In the delayed-roadwork ablation, semantic events triggered requests before scheduled audits. Across all three network profiles, cloud traffic was reduced, though lane changes took longer than with periodic access.

Conclusion: Onboard traffic assessment serves as a practical and effective trigger for selective cloud VLM inference, achieving efficient cloud usage without compromising safety or task performance in the tested scenarios.

Abstract: Cloud-hosted vision-language models (VLMs) offer greater contextual reasoning capabilities than smaller onboard models, but frequent visual uploads increase communication overhead and add network and inference latency to tactical decisions. We present a risk-adaptive edge-cloud architecture in which onboard traffic assessment determines when cloud reasoning is requested. An onboard VLM and a lightweight detector capture temporal traffic conditions and path-relative hazards for conservative local response and selective cloud access. The cloud model provides tactical advice, while validation, vehicle control, and automatic emergency braking remain local. In CARLA experiments, our method matched the task success rate of periodic cloud access while reducing cloud requests by 54.1% and recording fewer automatic emergency braking (AEB) activations. In a delayed-roadwork ablation, semantic events triggered requests before the next scheduled audit. Across three emulated network profiles, the method continued to reduce cloud traffic, although lane changes took longer than with periodic access. Onboard traffic assessment therefore served as a practical trigger for selective VLM inference in these experiments.

</details>


### [39] [Registration-Free Hyperspectral Reconstruction from RGB via a Permutation-Invariant Gram-Matrix Principle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14994)
*Jiangsan Zhao,Masayuki Hirafuji,Seishi Ninomiya,Jakob Geipel,Wei Guo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a permutation-invariant supervision principle for reconstructing high-resolution hyperspectral images (HR-HSI) from low-resolution HSI and high-resolution RGB images, eliminating the need for precise registration, known camera response function (CRF), and paired supervision. The method matches Gram matrices of unmixed abundance maps to learn RGB-to-HSI mapping, achieving accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art methods while remaining robust when traditional assumptions are violated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing HR-HSI reconstruction from LR-HSI and HR-RGB typically assumes precise spatial registration and a known camera response function (CRF). These assumptions are difficult to satisfy in real multi-sensor scenarios, limiting practical applicability.

Method: The method leverages a permutation-invariant supervision principle: the Gram matrix of an unmixed abundance map depends on shared material composition but not pixel ordering. By matching abundance Gram matrices, RGB-to-HSI mapping can be learned without spatial correspondence, a predefined CRF, or paired supervision. A residual spectral super-resolution function maps HR-RGB directly to HR-HSI.

Result: Under full random pixel permutation, a state-of-the-art fusion method collapses while the proposed reconstruction remains unchanged. The method achieves accuracy comparable to approaches requiring registration and known CRF across indoor, natural-scene, and remote-sensing benchmarks, while remaining robust when these assumptions are violated. Loss ablations show reconstruction accuracy is largely insensitive to the specific Gram matrix discrepancy used.

Conclusion: The permutation-invariant principle is the primary source of performance rather than loss tuning, providing a robust framework for HR-HSI reconstruction that removes both the registration and CRF assumptions.

Abstract: Reconstructing a spatially and spectrally high-resolution hyperspectral image (HR-HSI) from a low-resolution HSI (LR-HSI) and a high-resolution RGB image (HR-RGB) usually assumes precise registration and a known camera response function (CRF). Both assumptions are difficult to satisfy with different sensors. We remove both through a permutation-invariant supervision principle: the Gram matrix of an unmixed abundance map depends on shared material composition but not on pixel ordering. Matching abundance Gram matrices therefore allows RGB-to-HSI mapping to be learned without spatial correspondence and without a predefined CRF. Under a full random permutation of HR-RGB pixels, a state-of-the-art fusion method collapses, whereas our reconstruction is unchanged after inverse reindexing for evaluation. Building on this principle, a residual spectral super-resolution function maps HR-RGB directly to HR-HSI without registration, known CRF, or paired supervision. Across indoor, natural-scene, and remote-sensing benchmarks, the method achieves accuracy comparable to approaches that require these assumptions while remaining robust when they are violated. Loss ablations further show that reconstruction accuracy is largely insensitive to the specific discrepancy used to match the Gram matrices, indicating that performance arises primarily from the permutation-invariant principle rather than loss tuning.

</details>


### [40] [FZ-VLM: A Two Stage Florence-Zephyr Vision Language Model Framework for Pulmonary Nodule Characterization and Clinical Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15004)
*Pramit Dutta,Jenita Manokaran,Richa Mittal,Ryan Appleby,Eranga Ukwatta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FZ-VLM is a two-stage Florence-Zephyr Vision Language Model framework for unified structured pulmonary nodule characterization in lung CT. Stage 1 extracts radiological attributes (location, diameter, margins, attenuation) via Florence-2, and Stage 2 uses Zephyr-7B to generate nodule descriptions, follow-up recommendations, and longitudinal analyses. The framework outperforms GPT-4-based and human baselines in attribute extraction and achieves strong expert-evaluated performance in Stage 2, representing the first two-stage VLM framework for structured nodule characterization and clinical decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The post-detection workflow of manually assessing pulmonary nodule attributes (location, diameter, margin, attenuation) is time-consuming and suffers from inter-observer variability. Existing AI methods tackle isolated tasks rather than providing a unified, clinically grounded interpretation framework.

Method: A two-stage VLM framework: (1) a fine-tuned Florence-2 model extracts radiological attributes from expert-annotated 2D axial CT slices, and (2) a Zephyr-7B LLM uses these attributes to generate nodule descriptions, follow-up recommendations, and longitudinal analyses.

Result: Stage 1 achieved 77.18% accuracy for anatomical location, 67.96% for margin characteristics, 79.13% for attenuation type, and a MAE of 2.58 mm for diameter, outperforming GPT-4-based and human baselines. Stage 2 expert evaluation showed 93.9% accuracy, 98.6% completeness, 76.1% clinical relevance, and 89.5% overall score. Safety analysis indicated most outputs were clinically safe, though some follow-up recommendations required expert review.

Conclusion: FZ-VLM demonstrates the feasibility of a unified two-stage VLM framework for structured pulmonary nodule characterization and clinical decision support, though some follow-up recommendations still require expert oversight for safety.

Abstract: Lung cancer remains one of the leading causes of cancer-related mortality worldwide, and Computed Tomography (CT) is a primary imaging tool for screening and followup assessment. After pulmonary nodule detection, radiologists manually assess anatomical location, diameter, margin characteristics, and attenuation type to support risk assessment and clinical decision-making. However, this post-detection workflow is time-consuming and can be affected by inter-observer variability. Existing Artificial Intelligence methods often focus on isolated tasks, limiting their use as a unified, clinically grounded interpretation framework. This study presents FZ-VLM, a two-stage Florence-Zephyr Vision Language Model framework for unified structured pulmonary nodule characterization in lung CT. The framework uses a fine-tuned Florence-2 model to extract radiological attributes from expert-annotated 2D axial CT slices, while a Zephyr-7B model uses these attributes to generate nodule descriptions, follow-up recommendations, and longitudinal analyses. Results showed that the Stage 1 model achieved 77.18\% accuracy for anatomical location, 67.96\% accuracy for margin characteristics, and 79.13\% accuracy for attenuation type, with a Mean Absolute Error of 2.58 mm for diameter estimation, outperforming evaluated GPT-4-based baselines as well as the human baseline. Expert radiologist evaluation of Stage 2 showed 93.9\% accuracy, 98.6\% completeness score, 76.1\% clinical relevance, and an overall score of 89.5\%. Safety analysis showed that most outputs were clinically safe, although some follow-up recommendations still required expert review. To the best of our knowledge, this study presents the first two-stage Vision-Language Model framework for structured nodule characterization and clinical decision-making.

</details>


### [41] [MetaReason: Precise Interleaved Multimodal Reasoning via Editing Meta Information for Solving Geometry Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15006)
*Penghao Yin,Haomin Wang,Qihong Tang,Xiaoye Qu,Hongjie Zhang,Xiao-Ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MetaReason is a framework for multimodal reasoning in plane geometry that parses geometric images into structured meta-information, performs controllable edits to synthesize high-fidelity visual states for auxiliary-line construction, and reasons over augmented views. It is supported by the TutorGeo dataset (17k image-to-meta samples, 60k text-only and 60k interleaved multimodal reasoning traces) and evaluated on the ExamGeo benchmark. MetaReason outperforms open-source models and matches proprietary models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language models for geometry rely heavily on text-only reasoning. Prior methods introducing intermediate visual states are hindered by inaccurate geometric representations and low rendering fidelity, leading to unreliable outputs in complex geometry tasks.

Method: The framework (1) parses geometric images into structured meta-information, (2) performs controllable edits with predefined tools to synthesize high-fidelity intermediate visual states (e.g., auxiliary lines), and (3) conducts reasoning based on these augmented views. Training combines supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning on the TutorGeo dataset (17k image-to-meta conversions, 60k text-only and 60k interleaved multimodal reasoning traces).

Result: MetaReason significantly outperforms existing open-source models and achieves competitive performance against proprietary models on the ExamGeo benchmark, which evaluates across varying difficulty levels using real-world examination problems.

Conclusion: Structured meta-information and high-fidelity synthesized visual states enable more reliable multimodal reasoning in plane geometry, setting a new state-of-the-art among open-source approaches.

Abstract: Although visual reasoning is crucial for solving complex geometry tasks, existing vision-language models rely heavily on text-only reasoning. Some recent methods introduce intermediate visual states to facilitate reasoning, but they are often hindered by inaccurate geometric representations and low rendering fidelity, ultimately leading to unreliable outputs. To address these limitations, we propose MetaReason, a framework for multimodal reasoning in plane geometry that leverages structured meta-information to enable accurate auxiliary-line construction. The framework first parses geometric images into meta-information, performs controllable edits with predefined tools to synthesize high-fidelity visual states, and then conducts reasoning based on these augmented views. To support this framework, we construct TutorGeo, a comprehensive dataset containing 17k image-to-meta conversion samples, 60k text-only reasoning traces, and 60k interleaved multimodal reasoning traces. Using this dataset, we combine supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning to develop robust multimodal reasoning capabilities. We also introduce ExamGeo, a benchmark derived from real-world examination problems that enables systematic evaluation across varying difficulty levels. Experimental results demonstrate that MetaReason significantly outperforms existing open-source models and achieves competitive performance against proprietary models.

</details>


### [42] [DualMiT-Net: Local-Global Transformer-Convolutional Fusion for Breast Mass Segmentation in Mammographic Regions of Interest](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15019)
*Alibek Kamiluly,Milana Muratova,Yash Patel,Fan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DualMiT-Net is a dual-branch network for breast mass segmentation that combines a local branch (Mix Transformer MiT-B5 encoder) for mass details and a global branch (EfficientNet-B5 encoder) for surrounding tissue context, achieving a mean Dice of 0.9375 and IoU of 0.8834 on CBIS-DDSM, outperforming six encoder-decoder baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Breast mass segmentation is challenging because masses often have low contrast, irregular shapes, and unclear boundaries that blend with surrounding tissue, making accurate segmentation difficult with standard approaches.

Method: DualMiT-Net uses a dual-branch architecture: a local branch with a Mix Transformer (MiT-B5) encoder captures mass shape, texture, and boundary details, while a global branch with an EfficientNet-B5 encoder learns broader breast context. Features are shared at deeper encoder levels and progressively fused in a decoder with a spatial gate controlling global information contribution. Inputs use percentile-windowed mammograms combined with a Gabor texture response, trained with a patient-level split and exponential moving average weights.

Result: DualMiT-Net achieved a mean Dice coefficient of 0.9375 and mean IoU of 0.8834 across three training runs on the CBIS-DDSM mass subset, outperforming six standard encoder-decoder baselines under identical data and training settings.

Conclusion: Combining local mass information with wider breast context yields accurate and consistent breast mass segmentation, demonstrating the effectiveness of the dual-branch design.

Abstract: Breast mass segmentation is an important step in computer-aided mammography, but it remains difficult because masses can have low contrast, irregular shapes, and boundaries that blend with surrounding breast tissue. To address this problem, we present DualMiT-Net, a dual-branch network that uses both a focused view of the mass and a wider view of the surrounding tissue. The local branch uses a Mix Transformer (MiT-B5) encoder to learn mass shape, texture, and boundary information, while the global branch uses an EfficientNet-B5 encoder to learn surrounding breast context. Features from the two branches are shared at the deeper encoder levels and are then progressively fused in a single decoder. A spatial gate controls how much global information is added during decoding. We also evaluated four input representations and selected a percentile-windowed mammogram combined with a Gabor texture response. The model was trained and evaluated on the mass subset of the Curated Breast Imaging Subset of the Digital Database for Screening Mammography (CBIS-DDSM) using a patient-level split. Across three training runs, DualMiT-Net with exponential moving average weights achieved a mean Dice coefficient of 0.9375 and a mean Intersection over Union of 0.8834. It also achieved better Dice and IoU scores than six standard encoder-decoder baselines trained using the same data and training settings. These results show that combining local mass information with wider breast context can provide accurate and consistent breast mass segmentation.

</details>


### [43] [Geometry-Calibrated Closed-Form Shrinkage for SAR Despeckling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15028)
*Xuran Hu,Mingzhe Zhu,Djordje Stanković,Yujie Zhu,Zhenpeng Feng,Yifang Ban,Ljubiša Stanković*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a deterministic, training-free SAR despeckling estimator based on a nonlocal sparse approach with log–Yeo–Johnson transformation, group-wise left singular dictionaries, and coefficient shrinkage. It shows that three previously tunable quantities are analytically fixed by the construction, yielding a closed-form solution and geometry-calibrated regularization. The method ranks first in most PSNR/SSIM benchmarks and achieves the lowest ratio-image deviation on real SAR data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SAR despeckling is challenging due to multiplicative non-Gaussian noise, and existing estimators often rely on tunable parameters. The paper aims to show that a specific nonlocal sparse construction actually determines these parameters analytically, removing the need for tuning and training while retaining strong performance.

Method: The method revisits a nonlocal sparse estimator that: (1) applies a log–Yeo–Johnson transformation, (2) stacks similar patches into groups, (3) codes each group on its own left singular basis, and (4) shrinks coefficients. The authors prove three key structural results: the group dictionary is orthonormal so weighted Lasso reduces to coefficient-wise soft-thresholding; the dictionary's subspace absorbs speckle proportionally to group aspect ratio γ=p²/K, allowing regularization to be collapsed into one geometry-calibrated parameter via random-matrix theory; and singular projection makes coefficient noise near-Gaussian at all tested look numbers.

Result: The estimator is deterministic and training-free, using one analytically determined setting for all images and sensors. It ranks first in 18 of 24 PSNR/SSIM comparisons against twelve published methods on three synthetic benchmarks, and achieves the lowest mean deviation of the ratio image from the theoretical speckle model over six real-SAR configurations from five sensors.

Conclusion: The construction's geometry constrains all previously tunable parameters into a single analytically determined degree of freedom, removing iterative solvers, training, and per-image tuning while achieving state-of-the-art despeckling performance across both synthetic and real SAR data.

Abstract: Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) despeckling is an inverse-recovery problem in which multiplicative non-Gaussian noise must be suppressed without erasing scattering structures. We revisit a nonlocal sparse estimator that applies a log--Yeo--Johnson transformation, stacks similar patches into groups, codes each group on its own left singular basis, and shrinks the resulting coefficients. Three quantities usually treated as tunable are shown to be fixed by this construction. First, the group dictionary is orthonormal, so the weighted Lasso admits an exact coefficient-wise soft-threshold solution: the iterative inner solver is unnecessary, and the two apparent weighting matrices are the numerator and denominator of a single threshold field rather than independent modules. Second, because the dictionary is estimated from the noisy group itself, its retained subspace absorbs speckle in proportion to the group aspect ratio $γ=p^2/K$; a random-matrix argument converts the corresponding regularization constant into a geometry-calibrated correction and collapses patch size, group size, and shrinkage scale into one analytically determined degree of freedom. Third, singular projection makes the coefficient noise nearly Gaussian at every tested look number, which locates the point at which an exact speckle likelihood ceases to be informative. The resulting estimator is deterministic, training-free, and applies one set of analytically determined settings to every image and sensor. It ranks first in 18 of 24 PSNR/SSIM comparisons against twelve published methods on three synthetic benchmarks, and attains the lowest mean deviation of the ratio image from the theoretical speckle model over six real-SAR configurations from five sensors. Code is available \href{https://github.com/Teriri1999/Geometry-Calibrated-Closed-Form-Shrinkage-for-SAR-Despeckling}{here}.

</details>


### [44] [Generation of Synthetic Fingerphotos with GANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15029)
*Conor Miller-Lynch,Sandip Purnapatra,Syed Konain Abbas,Lambert Igene,Faraz Hussain,Soumyabrata Dey,Stephanie Schuckers*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper explores generating synthetic contactless fingerprint images (fingerphotos) using StyleGAN2-ADA and StyleGAN3, and quantitatively evaluates them for realism, privacy preservation, and variety by comparing biometric feature statistics and match scores against real fingerphotos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contactless fingerprinting lacks sufficient real data, and sharing real fingerprints poses privacy/security risks. Generating synthetic fingerphotos can supplement or replace real data for developing and evaluating contactless fingerprinting systems.

Method: The authors train StyleGAN2-ADA and StyleGAN3 on real fingerphotos to generate synthetic images. They evaluate the synthetic data by (1) comparing biometric feature statistics between real and synthetic fingerphotos, (2) computing match scores between real and synthetic pairs, and (3) computing match scores between different synthetic fingerphotos, to assess realism, privacy preservation, and variety.

Result: The paper provides a quantitative comparison of StyleGAN-generated synthetic fingerphotos against real ones, offering benchmark results for realism, privacy preservation, and variety as a reference point for future synthetic fingerphoto evaluations.

Conclusion: StyleGAN-based generation is a viable approach for producing synthetic fingerphotos, and the quantitative evaluation framework provides a useful baseline for the community. The code is released publicly for reproducibility.

Abstract: Contactless fingerprinting is an emerging approach to biometric authentication that allows users to scan their fingerprints without touching a scanner. Due to the limited amount of contactless fingerprint data available and the security risks associated with sharing real individuals' fingerprints, it is valuable to explore methods of generating synthetic data that can be used in place of - or in conjunction with - real data to develop and evaluate contactless fingerprinting systems. In this paper, we present and evaluate synthetic fingerphotos generated using StyleGAN2-ADA and StyleGAN3, existing image generation architectures. We evaluate the realism, privacy preservation, and variety of the synthetic fingerphotos by comparing their biometric feature statistics to those of real fingerphotos, computing match scores between real and synthetic fingerphotos, and computing match scores between different synthetic fingerphotos. This paper provides a quantitative comparison point for future evaluations of synthetic fingerphotos. The evaluation code is made available at https://github.com/cmillerlynch/fingerphoto-gan.

</details>


### [45] [MOSS-VL Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15045)
*Pengyu Wang,Chenkun Tan,Shaojun Zhou,Qirui Zhou,Yanxin Chen,Xingyang He,Huazheng Zeng,Jijun Cheng,Chenghao Wang,Xiaomeng Qian,Pengfei Wang,Zhan Huang,Shanqing Gao,Wei Huang,Longjun Cao,Wu Ran,Jie Liu,Changtai Zhu,Hongkai Wang,Yixian Tian,Chenghao Liu,Zhen Ye,Xinghao Wang,Botian Jiang,Guoguo Feng,Zhaoye Fei,Ruixiao Li,Mingshu Chen,Yang Gao,Qinyuan Cheng,Shimin Li,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MOSS-VL is an open vision-language model family designed for real-time interaction, using gated cross-attention so the language decoder can perceive incoming frames while generating. It combines a strong offline foundation (MOSS-VL-Instruct) with a light real-time training stage, achieving competitive offline performance and state-of-the-art streaming results, with up to 5.1x time-to-first-token advantage over Qwen3-VL-8B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language models are typically optimized for offline, single-turn understanding and lack the capability for real-time interaction where the model must perceive streaming visual input while simultaneously generating responses. The authors aim to make real-time perception-while-speaking a first-class capability.

Method: The model is co-designed across the stack: (1) architecture-wise, the language decoder attends to vision only via gated cross-attention, enabling perception of incoming frames during generation; (2) data-wise, a synthesized interaction corpus supervises speaking, silence, and revision decisions; (3) training-wise, a staged curriculum concentrates all real-time-specific training in a single light final stage built on a strong offline foundation.

Result: MOSS-VL-Instruct is competitive at comparable scale offline and leads temporal-reasoning video benchmarks. On four streaming benchmarks, MOSS-VL-Realtime achieves the best average on three (second on the fourth) among open-source streaming models, sweeping all three subsets testing proactive behavior (66.0 vs. 37.5 on OmniMMI Proactive Alerting). With 11.3B parameters and visual tokens outside the decoded sequence, it widens the time-to-first-token advantage over Qwen3-VL-8B from 2.8x to 5.1x as visual context grows.

Conclusion: MOSS-VL demonstrates that strong offline performance and real-time interaction capability can be combined effectively. All five checkpoints, training curriculum, and real-time inference code are released to the community.

Abstract: We present MOSS-VL, an open vision-language model family that treats real-time interaction -- perceiving while it speaks -- as a first-class capability. It is co-designed across the stack: the language decoder attends to vision only through gated cross-attention, so the model can naturally see incoming frames while generating; a synthesized interaction corpus supervises when to speak, when to stay silent, and when to revise; and a staged curriculum concentrates all real-time-specific training in one light final stage over a strong offline foundation. Offline, MOSS-VL-Instruct is competitive at comparable scale and leads temporal-reasoning video sets. Across four streaming benchmarks, MOSS-VL-Realtime posts the best average on three (second on the fourth) among open-source streaming models, sweeping the three subsets that squarely test proactive behavior -- 66.0 vs. 37.5 for the best baseline on OmniMMI Proactive Alerting. With 11.3B parameters but visual tokens outside the decoded sequence, MOSS-VL widens its time-to-first-token advantage over same-backbone Qwen3-VL-8B from 2.8x to 5.1x as visual context grows.
