TL;DR: This paper proposes a computationally-efficient video deblurring method for UAV footage that enhances target detection. It introduces an Adaptive Latent Scale Selector to dynamically adjust resolution based on motion intensity and a Multi-Frame Alignment and Learnable Gating module for temporal consistency, achieving superior deblurring and improved detection accuracy on real UAV benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAV aerial videos suffer from severe motion blur caused by rapid flight, vibrations, and camera panning, which degrades downstream tasks like target detection. Existing methods are computationally expensive, so there is a need for an efficient yet effective deblurring approach tailored to UAV applications.

Method: The method introduces two key components: (1) an Adaptive Latent Scale Selector that dynamically adjusts latent space resolution based on UAV motion intensity to balance detail preservation and inference efficiency, and (2) a Multi-Frame Alignment and Learnable Gating module that warps and gates preceding frames to fuse only relevant temporal information while suppressing misaligned or uninformative features.

Result: Extensive experiments on real UAV benchmarks show the method achieves superior deblurring performance and significantly boosts target detection accuracy compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed method is computationally efficient, temporally consistent, and highly applicable for robust aerial vision tasks, effectively recovering sharp details from UAV video streams while enhancing downstream target detection.

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) play a crucial role in various scenarios ranging from disaster response to traffic surveillance. However, aerial video footage often suffers from severe motion blur due to rapid flight maneuvers, vibrations, and camera panning, which can significantly degrade downstream tasks such as target detection. Our goal is to explore a computationally-efficient and effective video deblurring approach to enhance UAV target detection performance. To reduce computational cost, we first propose an Adaptive Latent Scale Selector that dynamically adjusts the latent space resolution according to the intensity of UAV motion, thus balancing detail preservation with inference efficiency. To ensure temporal consistency, we introduce a Multi-Frame Alignment and Learnable Gating module to warp and gate the preceding frames, allowing the model to fuse only relevant temporal information and suppress misaligned or uninformative features. Our method can effectively recover sharp details from the UAV video stream. Extensive experiments on real UAV benchmarks demonstrate that our method not only yields superior deblurring performance but also significantly boosts target detection accuracy, making it highly applicable to robust aerial vision tasks.

</details>


### [70] [VGGT-Align: Bridging Local Reconstruction and Global Consistency for Long-Sequence 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15260)
*Wei Zhang,Yihang Wu,Songhua Li,Qi Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A scale-consistency enhancement framework for long-sequence 3D reconstruction that uses geometric invariants from structured environments to constrain inter-chunk scale drift, reducing 7-DoF Sim(3) alignment to 6-DoF rigid-body transformation and incorporating test-time adaptation for improved intra-chunk predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale drift in long-sequence 3D reconstruction causes estimation errors to compound multiplicatively, distorting global trajectories and point cloud geometry. Existing chunk-based inference pipelines leave the scale degree of freedom in sequential Sim(3) alignment unconstrained.

Method: SGIA (Scene Geometric Invariant Anchoring) extracts dominant geometric invariants from each chunk's predicted point cloud via coarse-to-fine robust estimation, using their cross-chunk consistency to establish scale constraints independent of registration. This degenerates 7-DoF Sim(3) alignment into 6-DoF rigid-body transformation. A lightweight test-time adaptation strategy fine-tunes only normalization-layer parameters via multi-objective self-supervision.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on multiple long-sequence benchmarks, reducing absolute trajectory error by up to 32% with significant gains in trajectory stability and reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: Both modules are plug-and-play and require no offline retraining, effectively severing chain-wise scale error propagation at its source for long-sequence 3D reconstruction in structured environments.

Abstract: Maintaining global geometric consistency is a central challenge in long-sequence 3D reconstruction, with scale drift being the most critical failure mode. In chunk-based inference pipelines, the scale degree of freedom in sequential Sim(3) alignment is left unconstrained, causing estimation errors to compound multiplicatively and distort global trajectories and point cloud geometry. We present a scale-consistency enhancement framework built on a key insight: in structured environments such as driving scenes, geometric quantities arising from environmental regularity remain inherently invariant across temporal segments, and discrepancies in their per-chunk measurements directly expose inter-chunk scale drift. We propose Scene Geometric Invariant Anchoring (SGIA), which extracts dominant geometric invariants from each chunk's predicted point cloud via coarse-to-fine robust estimation and exploits their cross-chunk consistency to establish scale constraints independent of point cloud registration, explicitly degenerating 7-DoF Sim(3) alignment into 6-DoF rigid-body transformation and severing chain-wise scale error propagation at its source. We further introduce a lightweight test-time adaptation strategy that fine-tunes only normalization-layer parameters via multi-objective self-supervision, progressively improving intra-chunk predictions along the sequence. Both modules are plug-and-play and require no offline retraining. Experiments on multiple long-sequence benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, reducing absolute trajectory error by up to 32% with significant gains in trajectory stability and reconstruction quality. Code: https://github.com/WZ-CS/VGGT-Align

</details>


### [71] [Boundary-Aligned Contribution Routing for Robust Optical--SAR Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15261)
*Haifa Zhang,Yijing Wang,Haoyu Wang,Zheng Li,Zhiqiang Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses negative cross-modal transfer in optical-SAR fusion for remote-sensing object detection by introducing task-conditioned contribution routing mechanisms. Two router designs are proposed—a Feature Router for shallow-interaction architectures and a Dual-Statistic Semantic Router for dual-backbone architectures—both trained with detection supervision only. Results show improved mAP50 across full-input, missing-modality, and modality-corruption scenarios, with routing weights correlating with leave-one-modality-out utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-modal fusion does not guarantee improved performance—imperfect spatial, temporal, and semantic correspondence between optical and SAR imagery can cause negative cross-modal transfer, where an otherwise valid modality stream becomes conditionally harmful to detection.

Method: Two routing mechanisms are proposed: (1) a Feature Router performing cross-conditioned, group-addressable modulation near the input for architectures with frequent shallow interaction, and (2) a Dual-Statistic Semantic Router predicting stream-level contribution weights from modality-specific average and maximum statistics before late semantic fusion. Both are trained using detection supervision alone, without explicit utility supervision, quality labels, reconstruction, or distillation.

Result: On M4-SAR and SpaceNet6-OTD benchmarks, routing improves full-input mAP50 by 0.5–5.9 points, raises missing-modality mAP50 by 7.6–41.6 points over modality-dropout baselines, and reduces negative-transfer rate by up to 12.7 percentage points. Spearman correlations between routing weights and leave-one-modality-out utility range from 0.45 to 0.66.

Conclusion: Learned routing weights reflect model-specific task utility of each modality, providing an effective and lightweight way to mitigate negative cross-modal transfer in optical-SAR fusion without requiring additional supervision signals.

Abstract: Optical imagery provides rich appearance cues, whereas synthetic aperture radar (SAR) offers observations that are less sensitive to illumination and weather, making optical--SAR fusion attractive for remote-sensing object detection. However, the presence of multiple modalities does not guarantee beneficial fusion: imperfect spatial, temporal, and semantic correspondence can make an otherwise intact stream conditionally harmful and induce negative cross-modal transfer. We handle this issue through a model-specific task-utility perspective and learn task-conditioned contribution routing using detection supervision alone. The proposed fusion-boundary-aligned routing regulates each modality's contribution before the first learned cross-modal feature-value mixing operation. For architectures with frequent shallow interaction, a Feature Router performs cross-conditioned, group-addressable modulation near the input; for dual-backbone architectures, a Dual-Statistic Semantic Router predicts stream-level contribution weights from modality-specific average and maximum statistics before late semantic fusion. The routers require no explicit utility supervision, quality labels, reconstruction, or distillation. Experiments on M4-SAR and SpaceNet6-OTD cover nominal full inputs, controlled correspondence shifts, missing modalities, and four nonzero modality-corruption scenarios. Across the reported clean-training controls, routing improves full-input $\text{mAP}_{50}$ by 0.5--5.9 points. Relative to the corresponding modality-dropout baselines, it raises missing-modality $\text{mAP}_{50}$ by 7.6--41.6 points and reduces the negative-transfer rate by up to 12.7 percentage points. Spearman correlations between the learned routing weights and model-specific leave-one-modality-out utility range from 0.45 to 0.66, supporting the task-utility interpretation of the routing coefficients.

</details>


### [72] [On the Adversarial Robustness of Remote Sensing Semantic Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15267)
*Weikang Yu,Yonghao Xu,Pedram Ghamisi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a task-specific adversarial robustness evaluation framework for semantic change detection (SCD), a bitemporal dense-prediction task. It separates output-side attack objectives from input-side temporal perturbation access and systematically evaluates component vulnerabilities, cross-temporal propagation, and architecture transferability across four datasets and six models. Key findings show semantic-change predictions can be severely corrupted while binary change localization remains stable, perturbations from one timestamp propagate to the other, and cross-model transfer is weaker than white-box attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic change detection couples two temporal inputs with timestamp-wise semantic prediction, change localization, and final semantic-change decoding, creating adversarial dependencies that conventional robustness protocols fail to capture. Existing evaluations are inadequate for this coupled bitemporal task structure.

Method: The paper proposes a two-dimensional evaluation framework that (1) separates output-side attack objectives from input-side temporal perturbation access, (2) evaluates component-level and temporal objectives, (3) tests single- and dual-timestamp perturbations, and (4) applies multiple attack methods across representative CNN-, Transformer-, and state-space-based models on four datasets.

Result: Final semantic-change predictions can be severely corrupted even when binary change localization remains relatively stable. Perturbations or attack objectives from one timestamp propagate to the other's prediction. These behaviors appear across architecture families, while direct cross-model transfer is considerably weaker than white-box attacks.

Conclusion: Adversarial robustness in SCD depends on the complete bitemporal prediction pathway rather than any individual branch or backbone family. The study provides a structured protocol for evaluating robustness in coupled bitemporal image analysis.

Abstract: Semantic change detection (SCD) is a bitemporal dense-prediction task that jointly identifies changed regions and their semantic states before and after change. Unlike single-image segmentation or binary change detection, SCD couples two temporal inputs with timestamp-wise semantic prediction, change localization, and final semantic-change decoding, creating adversarial dependencies that are not captured by conventional robustness protocols. We present a task-specific evaluation framework that separates output-side attack objectives from input-side temporal perturbation access, enabling systematic analysis of component vulnerability and cross-temporal propagation. Experiments on four datasets and six representative CNN-, Transformer-, and state-space-based models evaluate component-level and temporal objectives, single- and dual-timestamp perturbations, multiple attack methods, and cross-architecture transferability. The results show that final semantic-change predictions can be severely corrupted even when binary change localization remains comparatively stable, and that perturbations or attack objectives associated with one timestamp can propagate to the prediction of the other. These behaviors occur across different architecture families, while direct cross-model transfer remains considerably weaker than white-box attacks. The study demonstrates that adversarial robustness in SCD depends on the complete bitemporal prediction pathway rather than on an individual branch or backbone family, and provides a structured protocol for evaluating robustness in coupled bitemporal image analysis. Code is available at https://github.com/EricYu97/AdvSCD.

</details>


### [73] [Memory-Bounded Continuation of Greedy Sampling for Continual Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15277)
*Yoon Gyo Jung,Jaewoo Park,Kuan-Chuan Peng,Seongdeok Bang,Octavia Camps*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ContCore introduces greedy-continued coreset sampling for continual anomaly detection under fixed memory constraints, maintaining representative summaries of normal data across sequentially arriving tasks. It alternates greedy expansion on new task features with greedy consolidation to enforce memory budgets, providing theoretical guarantees that the resulting coreset approximates an oracle within a bounded gap. Experiments show state-of-the-art performance across 11 task schedules on MVTecAD and VisA, and robust performance in online continual settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual anomaly detection requires maintaining compact, representative coresets of normal data across sequentially arriving tasks. While greedy sampling with unbounded memory (coreset accumulation) works straightforwardly, practical deployment demands fixed memory regardless of task count. The key question is whether greedy sampling can maintain representativeness under strict memory limits without catastrophic degradation.

Method: ContCore constructs a greedy-continued coreset through two operations: (1) greedy expansion on new task features to incorporate incoming data, and (2) greedy consolidation to enforce the fixed memory budget by pruning the combined set. The method provides theoretical justification that the resulting greedy-continued coreset approximates the oracle coreset within a bounded gap.

Result: ContCore achieves state-of-the-art performance across 11 task schedules on MVTecAD and VisA benchmarks. It also extends effectively to online continual anomaly detection settings where prior methods degrade significantly.

Conclusion: Greedy-continued coreset sampling offers a principled approach to continual anomaly detection under fixed memory constraints, degrading gracefully rather than catastrophically when discarding data. This provides an alternative to neural methods prone to catastrophic forgetting and naive coreset accumulation that requires unbounded memory.

Abstract: Greedy sampling produces a compact yet representative summary of normal data, which is essential for reliable anomaly detection that relies on measuring distance from normality. For continual anomaly detection where tasks arrive sequentially, extending greedy sampling is straightforward with unbounded memory through coreset accumulation. However, practical deployment requires fixed memory where the coreset size remains constant regardless of task count. We observe that continued greedy sampling, which iteratively applies greedy selection over previously greedy-sampled sets, effectively preserves representativeness under strict memory limits. Despite discarding data at each step to satisfy the memory constraint, coreset quality degrades gracefully rather than catastrophically, enabling reliable anomaly detection across the tasks. We provide theoretical justification by showing that resulting greedy-continued coreset approximates the oracle coreset within a bounded gap. We instantiate this principle in ContCore, which constructs a greedy-continued coreset through greedy expansion on new task features followed by greedy consolidation to enforce the memory budget. Unlike neural methods susceptible to catastrophic forgetting or naive coreset accumulation requiring unbounded memory, ContCore maintains fixed memory with theoretical guarantees. Empirically, ContCore achieves state-of-the-art performance across 11 task schedules on MVTecAD and VisA, and extends effectively to online continual AD settings where prior methods degrade significantly. Code: https://github.com/jungyg/ContCore

</details>


### [74] [Geometry-Aware Spatio-Temporal Context Modeling for 4D Occupancy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15279)
*Sitao Chen,Zhuangwei Zhuang,Hui Luo,Qingyao Wu,Mingkui Tan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GAST is a geometry-aware spatio-temporal context modeling method for 4D occupancy forecasting that uses progressive explicit-implicit generation and dual-path spatio-temporal modeling to achieve state-of-the-art performance on Occ3D-nuScenes, outperforming prior methods by 7.67% mIoU and 6.44% IoU with a 2.84x speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 4D occupancy forecasting methods rely on discrete tokenization with autoregressive prediction, which suffers from geometric distortion in static structures and inconsistent temporal coherence over long forecasting horizons. These limitations hinder reliable corner-case simulation in autonomous driving.

Method: GAST consists of two key modules: (1) a generation module that produces per-frame occupancy via pose-driven warping, motion-aware feature modulation, and attention-based feature refinement, ensuring high geometric fidelity and semantic plausibility; (2) a spatio-temporal module that enhances spatial consistency through global context aggregation while capturing scene evolution via temporal dynamics extraction. The framework is trained end-to-end with joint optimization of historical reconstruction and future forecasting through progressive explicit-implicit generation and dual-path spatio-temporal modeling.

Result: On Occ3D-nuScenes, GAST outperforms the state-of-the-art by 7.67% in mIoU and 6.44% in IoU, achieves a 2.84x speedup, and maintains strong performance in long-term forecasting.

Conclusion: The unified design of GAST demonstrates that integrating explicit geometric cues (pose/warping) with implicit feature learning across spatial and temporal dimensions can jointly improve forecasting accuracy, efficiency, and long-horizon consistency for 4D occupancy prediction in autonomous driving.

Abstract: 4D occupancy forecasting models the spatio-temporal evolution of 3D scenes and is crucial for autonomous driving, especially for corner-case simulation. Existing methods often rely on discrete tokenization followed by autoregressive prediction, yet struggle with geometric distortion in static structures and inconsistent temporal coherence over the forecasting horizon. In this work, we propose a Geometry-Aware Spatio-Temporal context modeling method (GAST) for 4D occupancy forecasting, built upon progressive explicit-implicit generation and dual-path spatio-temporal modeling. Specifically, the generation module produces per-frame occupancy with high geometric fidelity and semantic plausibility through pose-driven warping, motion-aware feature modulation, and attention-based feature refinement. Subsequently, the spatio-temporal module enhances spatial consistency through global context aggregation while capturing scene evolution through temporal dynamics extraction. This unified design enables joint optimization of historical reconstruction and future forecasting in an end-to-end manner. Extensive experiments on Occ3D-nuScenes demonstrate the superiority of our method, outperforming the state-of-the-art by 7.67% in mIoU and 6.44% in IoU with a 2.84x speedup, while maintaining strong performance in long-term forecasting.

</details>


### [75] [SOS! : A Streamlined Object-Conditional Transformer for Model-free Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15295)
*Jiaqi Hu,Junwen Huang,Hongli Xu,Peter KT Yu,Nassir Navab,Benjamin Busam,Slobodan Ilic*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SOS is a model-free segmentation framework that eliminates the need for 3D object model priors, using only a single reference image per target object via a novel Object-Conditional Transformer to unify mask generation and target identification in one feed-forward pass, achieving state-of-the-art performance on unseen object segmentation benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation segmentation models produce high-quality class-agnostic masks but cannot associate them with specific target objects, limiting downstream applications like robotic manipulation. Existing solutions rely on exhaustive 3D object model priors, introducing prohibitive computational overhead and complex multi-stage pipelines.

Method: SOS introduces a Streamlined Object-conditional Transformer that learns identity-anchored queries, unifying mask generation and target identification into a single feed-forward pass. It requires only a single reference image per target object, completely eliminating 3D model reliance.

Result: Extensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate SOS establishes a new state-of-the-art for model-free unseen object segmentation, delivering both accurate and high-efficiency performance.

Conclusion: By removing the dependency on exhaustive 3D priors and simplifying the pipeline to a single-stage, unified architecture, SOS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy while significantly improving structural and computational efficiency for unseen object segmentation.

Abstract: Foundation segmentation models excel at generating high-quality, class-agnostic masks, but they struggle to associate these proposals with specific target objects. This semantic gap severely hinders their deployment in downstream applications like robotic manipulation, which demand precise unseen objects segmentation. Existing approaches attempt to resolve this by relying on exhaustive 3D object model priors, inherently introducing prohibitive computational overhead and complex, multi-stage pipelines. To address these limitations, we propose SOS (Streamlined Object-conditional Transformer for model-free Segmentation). SOS completely eliminates the reliance on 3D models, requiring only a single reference image per target object. Central to our framework is a novel Object-Conditional Transformer that learns identity-anchored queries, unifying mask generation and target identification into a single feed-forward pass. This streamlined design drastically improves both structural and computational efficiency. Extensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that SOS establishes a new state-of-the-art for model-free unseen objects segmentation, delivering accurate and high-efficiency performance. The project page and code are available at https://sos-seg.github.io/.

</details>


### [76] [FMReward: Aligning and Evaluating Audio-Driven 3D Facial Animation with Human Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15296)
*Sijing Wu,Yunhao Li,Zhilin Gao,Huiyu Duan,Yucheng Zhu,Guangtao Zhai,Patrick Le Callet*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a comprehensive framework for improving and evaluating audio-driven 3D facial animation using human preference data. It introduces FMPair, the first human preference dataset for 3D facial animation (65,574 annotated pairs from 8,834 audio clips), a FMReward model that predicts perceptual quality aligned with human preferences, and FMFL, a direct fine-tuning algorithm that uses the reward model to optimize diffusion-based animation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training and evaluation methods for audio-driven 3D facial animation rely on ground-truth-based errors, which do not align well with human perceptual preferences. There is a need for a framework that learns from human preference data to improve perceptual quality.

Method: 1) Constructs FMPair, the first human preference dataset for audio-driven 3D facial animation via a systematic annotation pipeline (65,574 pairs from 8,834 in-the-wild audio clips). 2) Proposes FMReward, a reward model taking audio and 3D facial motion as inputs to predict perceptual quality scores aligned with human preferences. 3) Introduces FMFL, a direct fine-tuning algorithm leveraging the pretrained reward model to optimize diffusion-based 3D facial animation models for better human-preference alignment.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate FMReward's superiority over other metrics in aligning with human preferences, and FMFL's effectiveness in improving the perceptual quality of audio-driven 3D facial animation.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully aligns audio-driven 3D facial animation training and evaluation with human preferences through a reward model learned from pairwise preference data, and effectively fine-tunes diffusion-based models to produce higher perceptual quality animations.

Abstract: Audio-driven 3D facial animation is essential for advancing immersion and interactivity in virtual experiences. Although recent advances have shown promising capabilities, the training and evaluation of existing methods typically rely on ground-truth-based errors, which fall short of aligning with human preferences. To address this, we present a comprehensive framework that learns an automatic perceptual model from human preference data and leverages it to improve and evaluate the perceptual quality of audio-driven 3D facial animation. To begin with, we construct FMPair (Facial Motion Pairwise preference), the first human preference dataset for audio-driven 3D facial animation, which is built through a systematic annotation pipeline and comprises 65,574 annotated 3D facial motion pairs from 8,834 distinct in-the-wild audio clips. Based on the pairwise comparison dataset, we propose a Facial Motion Reward model, termed FMReward, which takes audio and 3D facial motion as inputs and predicts a perceptual quality score aligned with human preferences. Building upon FMReward, we further introduce Facial Motion reward Feedback Learning (FMFL), a direct fine-tuning algorithm that leverages a pretrained reward model to optimize diffusion-based audio-driven 3D facial animation models for better alignment with human preferences. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of FMReward over other metrics in aligning with human preferences and the effectiveness of FMFL in improving the perceptual quality of audio-driven 3D facial animation.

</details>


### [77] [TinyDETR-Pose: Towards End-to-End Real-Time Single-Stage 6DoF Object Pose Estimation with Lightweight Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15297)
*Paul Julius Kühn,Duc Anh Nguyen,Saptarshi Neil Sinha,Michael Weinmann,Arjan Kuijper*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TinyDETR-Pose is a lightweight, end-to-end, single-stage transformer-based framework for real-time 6DoF object pose estimation on edge hardware. Built on LW-DETR, it jointly detects objects and regresses their full 6D pose in a single forward pass using dedicated MLP heads, eliminating PnP, NMS, and iterative refinement. It achieves comparable ADD-S AUC of 85.9 on YCB-V while using up to 72.7% fewer parameters, and runs at ~4.5 ms per frame on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano with TensorRT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time 6DoF object pose estimation on resource-constrained edge hardware is challenging. Existing correspondence-based and refinement pipelines rely on non-differentiable PnP/RANSAC stages or costly iterative refinement, while foundation-model-based approaches have prohibitive inference costs for edge deployment.

Method: TinyDETR-Pose builds on the LW-DETR architecture and formulates detection and pose estimation as a set-prediction problem. It attaches dedicated MLP heads for rotation, monocular depth, and projected object center regression to each decoder query, eliminating PnP, NMS, and iterative refinement. Object symmetries are handled via a uniform ADD-S loss applied to all objects, and predictions are assigned to ground truth using a symmetry-safe Hungarian matcher based on class and 2D spatial cues.

Result: On YCB-V, TinyDETR-Pose achieves a comparable ADD-S AUC of 85.9 while requiring up to 72.7% fewer parameters than other DETR-based single-stage pose-estimation approaches. It achieves an inference latency of ~4.5 ms per frame on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano using TensorRT, running in real time.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that accurate end-to-end transformer-based 6D pose estimation can be made practical and real-time for edge deployment through a compact, single-stage design that avoids expensive classical post-processing stages.

Abstract: Real-time 6DoF object pose estimation on resource-constrained hardware remains challenging, as accurate correspondence-based and refinement pipelines typically rely on non-differentiable PnP/RANSAC stages or costly iterative refinement, while recent foundation-model-based approaches incur inference costs that are prohibitive for edge deployment. We present TinyDETR-Pose, a lightweight, end-to-end, single-stage framework that jointly detects objects and regresses their full 6D pose in a single forward pass. Built on the efficient LW-DETR architecture, TinyDETR-Pose formulates detection and pose estimation as a set-prediction problem and attaches dedicated MLP heads for rotation, monocular depth, and projected object center regression to each decoder query, eliminating the need for PnP, NMS (non-maximum suppression), or iterative pose refinement. Object symmetries are handled through a ADD-S loss applied uniformly to all objects, without the need for object-specific loss schedules or separate geodesic/ADD supervision. In addition, predictions are assigned to ground truth using a symmetry-safe Hungarian matcher based on class and 2D spatial cues, yielding stable assignment under symmetry and depth ambiguity. On YCB-V, TinyDETR-Pose achieves a comparable ADD-S AUC of 85.9, while requiring up to 72.7% fewer parameters than other DETR-based single-stage pose-estimation approaches. Due to its compact design, TinyDETR-Pose runs in real time and achieves an inference latency of only ~4.5 ms per frame on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano using TensorRT, demonstrating that accurate end-to-end transformer-based 6D pose estimation can be made practical for edge deployment.

</details>


### [78] [Image Denoising via the Adaptive Rank-Cluster Filter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15298)
*Dmitry Pozdnyakov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a spatial-local image-denoising filter that uses Otsu-based clustering on a trimmed 3x3 pixel window to align the central pixel with a statistical majority cluster, followed by fuzzy fusion with the median. It outperforms seven baseline filters (median, adaptive median, Gaussian, bilateral, Wiener, anisotropic diffusion, non-local means) in robustness, especially for mixed salt-and-pepper and Gaussian noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a denoising filter that is robust to varying noise levels and particularly effective against mixed noise containing both salt-and-pepper impulse noise and additive Gaussian noise in different proportions, addressing limitations of existing baseline filters.

Method: The filter operates on a 3x3 window, sorts pixels by intensity and trims to seven elements, then applies optimal Otsu's partitioning to form two clusters. The central pixel's intensity is aligned with the statistical majority cluster, followed by a fuzzy fusion with the window's median intensity.

Result: The proposed filter demonstrates the highest robustness to variations in image noise levels compared to seven baseline filtering algorithms, particularly when processing mixed noise of salt-and-pepper impulse noise and additive Gaussian noise in various proportions.

Conclusion: The Otsu-based clustering with fuzzy-median fusion filter provides superior and more stable denoising performance across varying noise conditions, making it especially suitable for mixed-noise scenarios.

Abstract: A spatial-local image-denoising filter is proposed, and its performance metrics are evaluated in comparison with baseline filtering algorithms, including the median, adaptive median, Gaussian, bilateral, Wiener, anisotropic diffusion, and non-local means. The developed filter is based on aligning the intensity value of the central pixel in a 3x3 window with the statistical majority intensity of one of the two clusters formed by optimal Otsu's partitioning of a pixel set sorted by intensity and trimmed to seven elements. This is followed by a fuzzy fusion of the calculated value with the median intensity of the pixels within the window. The proposed filter demonstrates the highest robustness to variations in image noise levels, particularly when processing mixed noise consisting of salt-and-pepper impulse noise and additive Gaussian noise in various proportions

</details>


### [79] [LightLoc++: Sensor-Robust Representation Learning for Efficient Outdoor LiDAR Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15317)
*Wen Li,Shangshu Yu,Dunqiang Liu,Qiming Xia,Sheng Ao,Siqi Shen,Chenglu Wen,Cheng Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LightLoc++ is a sensor-robust and efficient outdoor LiDAR localization framework that addresses the dependency of decoupled scene coordinate regression methods on pretrained backbones. It introduces SULID, a synchronized multi-LiDAR dataset, and uses cross-sensor consistency learning to train a sensor-robust backbone, achieving state-of-the-art localization performance with the lowest new-scene training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decoupled SCR methods that use frozen pretrained backbones with scene-specific lightweight heads perform well only when LiDAR sensor configurations match those used during pretraining. Their accuracy degrades significantly on datasets from different LiDAR sensors, revealing that efficient LiDAR localization requires sensor-robust representations capturing stable scene geometry across LiDAR configurations.

Method: LightLoc++ introduces SULID, a synchronized urban multi-LiDAR dataset containing 32-, 64-, and 128-beam rotating LiDARs with extensive cross-sensor overlap. A sensor-robust backbone is pretrained via cross-sensor consistency learning on SULID. The framework also incorporates sample classification guidance and redundant sample downsampling to reduce regression ambiguity and computational overhead for efficient new-scene learning.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple outdoor LiDAR localization benchmarks show LightLoc++ achieves state-of-the-art localization performance while requiring the lowest new-scene training cost compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: Decoupling SCR through sensor-robust representation learning combined with training efficiency mechanisms enables both high localization accuracy and practical efficiency across diverse LiDAR sensors, addressing a key limitation in current efficient LiDAR localization paradigms.

Abstract: Scene coordinate regression (SCR) achieves strong performance in outdoor LiDAR localization, but it usually requires scene-specific training that can take days, limiting practical deployment. Recent works improve training efficiency by decoupling SCR into a scene-agnostic backbone and scene-specific prediction heads, where the backbone is pretrained on source datasets and frozen for new scenes, and only lightweight heads are optimized. However, we find that this paradigm heavily depends on the pretrained backbone. Existing decoupled methods can match conventional SCR methods fully optimized for each new scene when LiDAR configurations are similar to those used during backbone pretraining, but their accuracy drops noticeably on datasets collected with different LiDAR sensors. This suggests that efficient LiDAR localization requires representations that capture stable scene geometry across LiDAR configurations. Motivated by this observation, we propose LightLoc++, a sensor-robust and efficient outdoor LiDAR localization framework. To support sensor-robust representation learning, we introduce SULID, a synchronized urban multi-LiDAR dataset with representative 32-, 64-, and 128-beam rotating LiDARs, extensive cross-sensor overlap, and diverse urban scenes. Using SULID, we pretrain a sensor-robust backbone through cross-sensor consistency learning. LightLoc++ further preserves efficient new-scene learning by incorporating sample classification guidance and redundant sample downsampling, which reduce regression ambiguity and computational redundancy in large-scale outdoor scenes. Extensive experiments on multiple outdoor LiDAR localization benchmarks demonstrate that LightLoc++ achieves state-of-the-art localization performance with the lowest new-scene training cost among compared methods. Code and dataset will be made available at https://github.com/liw95/LightLoc-PlusPlus.

</details>


### [80] [SAGE-OR: Semi-supervised Adaptive Scene Graph Generation for Operating Rooms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15336)
*Brandon Leblanc,Charalambos Poullis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAGE-OR is a feature-centric framework for surgical scene graph generation that eliminates localization supervision by using frozen foundation models to encode localization implicitly in pre-computed features, with a lightweight 15M-parameter graph transformer performing relational reasoning. It achieves 86% F1 on the 4D-OR benchmark (within 4 points of fully supervised SOTA) while removing all localization annotations and enabling prompt-level adaptation to new entities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current surgical scene graph generation methods depend on dense multi-modal supervision and specialized hardware (synchronized RGB-D sensors, calibration rigs), making dataset construction expensive and restricting all existing benchmarks to simulated environments.

Method: SAGE-OR replaces the detect-then-reason paradigm with a decoupled representation-reasoning approach: localization is derived from frozen foundation models and encoded in pre-computed features (no localization supervision), while a lightweight graph transformer performs relational reasoning over cached features. A semi-supervised formulation uses general-purpose segmentation prompts to eliminate localization supervision and enable unsupervised context augmentation (e.g., adding hands absent from annotations). General-purpose prompts induce high recall while precision is delegated to attention-based reasoning, enabling adaptation via prompt-level modification.

Result: The core model achieves 76% F1 on 4D-OR, matching the fully supervised baseline while eliminating all localization annotations. With unsupervised hand augmentation, it reaches 86% F1, within 4 points of SOTA requiring dense multi-modal supervision. The model trains in 1.4 hours, runs inference at ~1ms per frame with under 2GB peak memory; feature extraction runs offline at 4.27s per frame.

Conclusion: SAGE-OR provides a practical pathway for adapting surgical scene graph generation to new settings requiring only relationship and class labels, while being suitable for edge hardware in the operating room.

Abstract: Current surgical scene graph generation methods depend on dense multi-modal supervision and specialized hardware (synchronized RGB-D sensors, calibration rigs), making dataset construction expensive and restricting all existing benchmarks to simulated environments. We propose SAGE-OR, a feature-centric framework that replaces the traditional detect-then-reason paradigm with a decoupled representation-reasoning paradigm in which localization is derived from frozen foundation models, encoded implicitly in pre-computed features, and used without any localization supervision, while a lightweight graph transformer performs relational reasoning over cached features. We employ a semi-supervised formulation with general-purpose segmentation prompts to eliminate localization supervision while enabling unsupervised context augmentation through additional prompt-driven entities, such as hands, which are absent from annotations. General-purpose prompts are used to induce near-perfect recall, while precision is delegated to downstream attention-based reasoning, enabling simple adaptation to new entities via prompt-level modification. This design enables a lightweight 15M-parameter graph transformer that trains in 1.4 hours and runs relational inference at $\sim$1ms per frame with peak memory under 2GB, suitable for edge hardware used in the operating room; feature extraction runs offline as a separate caching stage (4.27s per frame). On the 4D-OR benchmark, the core model achieves 76% F1, matching the fully supervised 4D-OR baseline while eliminating all localization annotations, and unsupervised hand augmentation raises this to 86%, within 4 points of state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods requiring dense multi-modal supervision, providing a practical pathway for adaptation to new surgical settings without annotation other than relationship and class labels.

</details>


### [81] [TEA: Text Encoder Alignment for Robust Concept Erasure in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15341)
*Alireza Dehghanpour Farashah,Zhuan Shi,Negar Rostamzadeh,Golnoosh Farnadi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TEA is a lightweight concept erasure framework that fine-tunes only the text encoder to align token-level representations of concept-containing prompts with safe anchor prompts, achieving state-of-the-art robustness against adversarial attacks with zero inference-time overhead and broad model applicability across diffusion architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concept erasure methods for text-to-image diffusion models fail to simultaneously achieve robustness against adversarial/paraphrased prompts, preserve benign generation quality, and avoid persistent computational overhead from inference-time interventions.

Method: TEA formulates concept erasure as a domain alignment problem in the text representation space. It fine-tunes only the text encoder (keeping the generative backbone frozen) using a discriminator that distinguishes token-level representations of concept-containing prompts from safe anchor prompts, while the text encoder is updated adversarially to make these representations indistinguishable.

Result: TEA achieves state-of-the-art erasure robustness against black-box and white-box adversarial attacks on Stable Diffusion v1.4 while preserving benign generation quality. It also achieves the lowest attack success rate on Stable Diffusion v3.5, extending concept erasure to Rectified Flow Transformer architecture with T5 conditioning. It requires only a small number of fine-tuning steps and zero inference-time overhead.

Conclusion: The proposed TEA framework demonstrates that text-encoder-only alignment via adversarial domain adaptation offers an efficient, scalable, and model-agnostic solution to concept erasure, overcoming key limitations of prior methods while extending applicability to newer diffusion architectures.

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models can be misused to generate harmful content through adversarial or paraphrased prompts that bypass built-in safety mechanisms. Existing concept erasure methods often suffer from limited robustness against adversarial prompts, degradation of benign generation quality, or reliance on inference-time interventions that introduce persistent computational overhead. To address these limitations, we formulate concept erasure as a domain alignment problem in the text representation space. We propose a lightweight Text Encoder Alignment framework (TEA) that fine-tunes only the text encoder while keeping the generative backbone fully frozen. Given concept--anchor prompt pairs, our method trains a discriminator to distinguish token-level representations of concept-containing prompts from those of safe anchor prompts, while updating the text encoder to make these representations indistinguishable. TEA introduces zero inference-time overhead and requires only a small number of fine-tuning steps, making it highly efficient to deploy at scale. Despite this efficiency, TEA achieves state-of-the-art erasure robustness against black-box and white-box adversarial attacks on Stable Diffusion v1.4, while preserving generation quality on benign prompts. Furthermore, TEA is model-agnostic and achieves the lowest attack success rate on Stable Diffusion v3.5, extending concept erasure to a Rectified Flow Transformer architecture with T5 conditioning where prior methods remain largely unexplored. Code is available at \href{https://github.com/alirezafarashah/TEA.git}{https://github.com/alirezafarashah/TEA.git}

</details>


### [82] [Feed-Forward Hierarchical Gaussian Diffusion for Extreme CT Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15343)
*Yuezhe Yang,Li Cheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HiGDiff is a feed-forward hierarchical Gaussian diffusion framework for CT reconstruction from severely constrained projections. It decomposes reconstruction spatially and from structure to detail, using physics-conditioned anatomical anchors and a foreground capacity field to allocate Gaussian primitives. A structure diffusion stage recovers global attenuation geometry, which conditions a detail diffusion stage for residual boundaries and tissue transitions, followed by a gradient-isolated residual module for refinement. It achieves state-of-the-art performance across three CT benchmarks, including 5.81 dB PSNR and 0.113 SSIM improvements on LDCT-PD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing 3D CT from severely constrained projections (sparse angular sampling, restricted angular coverage, low photon counts) is highly ill-posed. Existing learned methods are typically tailored to a single dominant degradation, and current diffusion/Gaussian approaches recover global structure and local detail within a shared representation, which limits performance when multiple degradation types occur jointly.

Method: HiGDiff decomposes reconstruction both spatially and hierarchically (structure-to-detail). It uses physics-conditioned anatomical anchors and a foreground capacity field to allocate learnable Gaussian primitives to informative regions. A structure diffusion stage recovers global attenuation geometry, and its learned representation conditions a detail diffusion stage for residual boundaries and tissue transitions. The Gaussian banks are rendered as attenuation fields and refined by a gradient-isolated residual module.

Result: State-of-the-art reconstruction performance across three distinct CT benchmark datasets, covering isolated, paired, and joint degradation settings. Improvements of 5.81 dB in macro-average PSNR and 0.113 in SSIM on the LDCT-PD collection.

Conclusion: The hierarchical decomposition of structure and detail, combined with physics-conditioned spatial allocation of Gaussian primitives, enables robust CT reconstruction across diverse and combined degradation scenarios, outperforming existing single-degradation-tailored and shared-representation approaches.

Abstract: Reconstructing three-dimensional computed tomography (CT) from severely constrained projections is highly ill-posed. Sparse angular sampling, restricted angular coverage, and low photon counts can occur individually or jointly, obscuring global anatomy and local tissue detail. Many learned CT reconstruction methods are tailored to a single dominant degradation. Existing diffusion and Gaussian approaches commonly recover global structure and local detail within a shared representation. We propose HiGDiff, a feed-forward hierarchical Gaussian diffusion framework that decomposes reconstruction both spatially and from structure to detail. Physics-conditioned anatomical anchors and a foreground capacity field allocate learnable Gaussian primitives to informative regions. A structure diffusion stage first recovers global attenuation geometry, and its learned representation conditions a detail diffusion stage for residual boundaries and tissue transitions. The resulting Gaussian banks are rendered as attenuation fields and further refined by a gradient-isolated residual module. Experiments on three distinct CT benchmark datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art reconstruction performance across isolated, paired, and joint degradation settings, including improvements of 5.81 dB in macro-average peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) and 0.113 in structural similarity index measure (SSIM) on the Low Dose CT Image and Projection Data (LDCT-PD) collection. Code and experimental configurations are openly available at https://github.com/Bean-Young/HiGDiff.

</details>


### [83] [ENAF: A Multi-Exit Network with an Adaptive Patch Fusion for Large Image Super Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15349)
*Duong M. Nguyen,Tuan Nghia Nguyen,Xuan Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ENAF is a dynamic network for single image super-resolution that uses multiple early exits and a tiny PSNR-estimation network to adaptively assign image patches to computation paths based on predicted quality, improving the quality-complexity trade-off on large images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic SISR networks rely on handcrafted assessment scores (e.g., edge detection) to determine patch difficulty, which weakly correlate texture with computational complexity. A learned, data-driven approach is needed to better associate patch characteristics with computation cost.

Method: ENAF builds on a SISR backbone with multiple early exits (EEs) to handle the over-parameterized model. A tiny network is plugged in to estimate PSNR, which scores patches and adaptively assigns them to appropriate exits, enabling adaptive patch fusion and dynamic execution paths.

Result: Extensive experiments on common datasets with popular SISR backbones demonstrate ENAF's effectiveness across various settings, improving the quality-complexity trade-off compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: ENAF shows that learning-based quality estimation for adaptive patch routing outperforms handcrafted difficulty scores in dynamic SISR networks, providing a better balance between output quality and computational efficiency.

Abstract: To accelerate single image super-resolution (SISR) networks on large images (2K-8K), many recent approaches decompose an image into small patches and dynamically determine an execution path according to its difficulty (referred to as a dynamic network). To quantify the hardness of a patch, they mainly rely on a handcrafted assessment score, e.g., edge, which weakly associates a patch's texture with the computational complexity of a SISR model. To address the problem, we introduce ENAF - a dynamic network for SISR with an adaptive patch fusion. Built on top of a backbone, ENAF incorporates multiple early exits (EEs) to tackle the over-parameterized SISR model. More importantly, ENAF plugs a tiny network that estimates PSNR to associate data texture with a computation cost at an EE. Based on the scores, ENAF effectively assigns image patches to an exit, enhancing the quality-complexity trade-off. Extensive experiments on common datasets with popular SISR backbones demonstrate the effectiveness of ENAF in various settings. The source code is provided in https://github.com/nmduonggg/ENAF

</details>


### [84] [Decomposing Whole Slide Image Report Generation with Graph-Constrained Multiple Instance Learning Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15353)
*Antony Gitau,Martyna Borak,Bjørn-Jostein Singstad,Martin Paulson,Karl Thomas Hjelmervik,Ola Marius Lysaker,Veralia Gabriela Sanchez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a decomposed framework for whole-slide image (WSI) report generation that separates visual recognition, structured reasoning, and language generation. Frozen Virchow2 tile embeddings are aggregated by MIL classification heads answering organ-specific questions, assembled into a graph-constrained reasoning chain, and realized as a pathology report by an LLM. The approach achieves a chain-Jaccard score of 0.702 on REG2026 and improves report score from 0.330 to 0.495, with organ routing identified as the main bottleneck under domain shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct vision-to-text models can generate fluent reports but obscure the contributions and failure modes of individual components (visual recognition, structured reasoning, and language generation). The authors aim to decompose the pipeline to enable stage-specific error localization and improve structured reasoning.

Method: The framework uses frozen Virchow2 tile embeddings aggregated by multiple-instance learning (MIL) classification heads to answer organ-specific diagnostic questions. An organ-conditioned graph constrains the assembly of answers into a structured reasoning chain, which a language model then realizes as a pathology report.

Result: On REG2026 (2,028 slides), chain-Jaccard score was 0.702. Removing graph constraints (0.420), using organ-agnostic graphs (0.398), or free chain construction by the LM (0.371) degraded performance. Graph-structured chains improved report score from 0.330 to 0.495. On 350 external TCGA WSIs, the expected organ graph was selected in 64.0% (top-3: 86.6%). Providing the correct organ graph improved agreement with TCGA diagnoses from 61.8% to 92.6%.

Conclusion: Organ-conditioned, graph-constrained chain assembly improves structured reasoning and report generation in WSI analysis while enabling stage-specific error localization. Organ routing is identified as the primary bottleneck for domain-shift generalization.

Abstract: Whole-slide image (WSI) report generation requires recognizing spatially distributed pathological features and organizing them into a coherent diagnostic narrative. Although direct vision-to-text models can yield fluent reports, they obscure the contributions and failure modes of visual recognition, structured reasoning, and language generation. We propose a decomposed framework in which frozen Virchow2 tile embeddings are aggregated by multiple-instance learning (MIL) classification heads that answer organ-specific diagnostic questions. An organ-conditioned graph constrains the assembly of these answers into a structured reasoning chain, which a language model realizes as a pathology report. On the REG2026 held-out set of 2,028 slides, the proposed workflow achieved a chain-Jaccard score of 0.702. Performance fell to 0.420 without graph-based chain construction, 0.398 when the organ-specific graphs were replaced by a single organ-agnostic graph, and 0.371 when the language model constructed the chain freely from MIL predictions. Using the same report generator, graph-structured chains improved the report score from 0.330 to 0.495. On 350 external TCGA WSIs spanning the seven REG organs without fine-tuning, the expected organ graph was selected in 64.0% of cases and ranked among the top three in 86.6%. Providing the correct organ graph increased agreement with coarse TCGA primary-diagnosis labels from 61.8% to 92.6%, identifying organ routing as a main bottleneck under domain shift. Overall, organ-conditioned, graph-constrained chain assembly improves structured reasoning and report generation while enabling stage-specific error localization.

</details>


### [85] [A Multi-Annotator Study of Segmentation Noise and Uncertainty in Turbid Underwater Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15363)
*Galadrielle Humblot-Renaux,Vasiliki Ismiroglou,Malte Pedersen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic multi-annotator study of segmentation in real underwater scenes, involving over 100 participants across controlled turbidity levels, revealing that turbidity introduces systematic annotation errors and investigating factors driving label noise along with strategies to improve annotation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Label uncertainty and annotator disagreement are understudied in underwater computer vision, despite underwater datasets being especially susceptible to these issues due to domain expertise requirements, degraded visibility, and difficulty establishing reliable ground truth in inaccessible environments.

Method: A large-scale multi-annotator study with over 100 participants conducting segmentation on real underwater scenes under varying controlled levels of turbidity, including analysis of factors driving label noise and testing annotation improvement strategies such as privileged information, individual effort, and annotator ensembles.

Result: Underwater datasets share many annotation challenges with other vision tasks, while turbidity introduces additional systematic errors in annotations.

Conclusion: The study provides insights into improving annotation quality in turbid underwater environments and releases all collected (meta-)data on the project page.

Abstract: Label uncertainty and annotator disagreement are common challenges in the field of computer vision, yet their study has largely been confined to the medical domain or to generic image-recognition datasets. Underwater datasets are particularly susceptible to these issues due to the need for domain expertise, degraded visibility conditions, and the inherent difficulty of establishing reliable ground truth in inaccessible environments. Despite these challenges, annotation uncertainty in underwater imagery remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present the first systematic multi-annotator study of segmentation in real underwater scenes, with over 100 participants, and across varying, controlled levels of turbidity. We show that underwater datasets face many of the same annotation challenges as other vision tasks, while turbidity introduces additional systematic errors. We further investigate the main factors driving label noise and explore ways to improve annotation quality in turbid underwater environments, including privileged information, individual effort and annotator ensembles. All (meta-) data collected in this study will be available on the project page: https://vap.aau.dk/tubcertainty

</details>


### [86] [JoLT: Joint Latent Trajectories for Context-Guided High-Resolution Tiled Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15395)
*Mathis Koroglu,Guillaume Jeanneret,Hugo Caselles-Dupré,Matthieu Cord,Arnaud Dapogny*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: JoLT (Joint Latent Trajectories) is a text-to-image generation method that jointly denoises low-resolution (LR) and high-resolution (HR) latent images in two interconnected streams at each sampling step, where the LR stream controls global layout and the HR stream controls fine details, enabling the generation of densely detailed, high-resolution images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-image models struggle to generate densely detailed, high-resolution images. Current approaches use a sequential low-to-high-resolution pipeline where an LR image is first generated and then upsampled using the LR image as an additional cue, which is suboptimal.

Method: JoLT uses two parallel denoising streams operating on LR and HR latent images simultaneously at each sampling step. The LR latent controls the overall layout, while the HR latent controls the details. The two branches are interconnected to jointly integrate their information throughout the denoising process.

Result: Extensive validation demonstrates advantages over competing baselines. The resulting images are richly detailed and visually pleasing.

Conclusion: JoLT opens new avenues for artistic creation by producing high-resolution images that are both richly detailed and visually appealing through joint denoising of LR and HR latents.

Abstract: Although text-to-image generative models produce impressive results, they struggle to generate densely detailed, high-resolution (HR) images. Current literature addresses this issue with a low-to-high-resolution approach. First, a low-resolution (LR) image is generated. Then, an upsampled version is generated using the LR image as an additional cue. In this paper, we present Joint Latent Trajectories (JoLT). To generate an image, JoLT uses two streams that jointly denoise LR and HR latent images at each sampling step. The LR latent controls the overall layout, while the HR latent controls the details. We interconnect both branches to jointly integrate their information. We extensively validate our method, demonstrating its advantages over competing baselines. The resulting images are not only richly detailed but also visually pleasing, opening new avenues for artistic creation.

</details>


### [87] [CBX-Bench: A Human-Aligned MLLM Council for Benchmarking Concept Bottleneck Model Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15404)
*Yusuf Meric Karadag,Gulay Oklan,Seref Baris Cagliyan,Umut Ozdemir,Emre Akbas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces CBX-Bench, a benchmark and leaderboard for evaluating Concept Bottleneck Model (CBM) explanation quality. The authors develop a council of five open-weight multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that score CBM explanations against images. They validate this council against human annotations from 2,700 judgments across 900 image-comparison items on three datasets, finding it recovers over 70% of strict human preference rankings (83% on unanimous items). The benchmark provides a human-aligned, scalable alternative to accuracy-based or qualitative-only evaluation of CBM explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CBMs are designed for interpretability but are still evaluated primarily by downstream classification accuracy and isolated qualitative examples. There is a pressing need for quantitative measures of explanation quality, which is complicated by the infeasibility of large-scale ground-truth concept annotation and the lack of consensus on concept lists.

Method: The authors build a council of five open-weight MLLMs that score CBM explanations for a given image. They conduct a human study to establish ground-truth references where annotators compare explanations from different CBM variants on CUB-200, ImageNet-100, and Places365, yielding 2,700 judgments. The council is validated against these human preferences and then deployed in CBX-Bench, a public benchmark where CBM authors submit explanations for scoring and leaderboard ranking.

Result: The five-model MLLM council recovers over 70% of strict human preference rankings and rises to 83% on items where human annotators unanimously agree, demonstrating strong alignment with human judgment of explanation quality.

Conclusion: CBX-Bench provides a human-aligned, scalable evaluation framework for CBM explanations that goes beyond accuracy and qualitative examples. It fills an important gap by enabling quantitative, comparable assessment of interpretability in CBMs through a public benchmark and leaderboard.

Abstract: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) are designed to make visual classification interpretable by expressing predictions through human-understandable concepts. Although interpretability is the central motivation for CBMs, they are still largely evaluated as predictive models by downstream classification accuracy, supplemented by isolated qualitative examples. This highlights a pressing need for quantitative measures, a challenge complicated by the infeasibility of ground-truth concept annotation at scale and the open nature of concept lists due to a lack of consensus. To fill this gap, we develop a multimodal large language model (MLLM) council that, given an image and its CBM explanation, produces an explanation quality score. To ground and validate the council, we first conduct a human study to establish a ground-truth reference for CBM explanation quality: for an image, annotators compare explanations from two of LF-CBM, VLG-CBM, and CBM-Suite and choose the more useful one, or mark them as equally good or equally bad, yielding 2700 judgments over 900 image-comparison items on CUB-200, ImageNet-100, and Places365. Against this human reference, our five-model council, consisting of open-weight MLLMs, recovers over 70% of strict human preference rankings, rising to 83% on items where human annotators unanimously agree. Building on this validated council, we introduce CBX-Bench, a public benchmark and leaderboard: authors of new CBMs can submit their model's explanations, and CBX-Bench scores them with the council and maintains dataset-level rankings of explanation quality. CBX-Bench thus provides a human-aligned, scalable evaluation of CBM explanations beyond accuracy and isolated qualitative examples. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/meric-karadag/cbx-bench.

</details>


### [88] [ArtLang: Structured Language-to-Kinematics Grounding for Articulated 3D Actuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15419)
*Sylvia Yuan,Dan Wang,Ravi Ramamoorthi,Xinrui Cui*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ArtLang is a framework for open-vocabulary language control of articulated reconstructed assets, using a semantic-kinematic articulation graph augmented with language features and graph-constrained motion to ground natural language commands to joint targets via global graph-to-graph matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing articulated-object reconstructions recover geometry and kinematics but their parts remain semantically anonymous, requiring control through part indices and numerical joint parameters, which is unintuitive and limits usability.

Method: ArtLang represents an asset as a semantic-kinematic articulation graph with language features and graph-constrained motion. A typed parser converts commands into directive graphs with referring expressions, actions, magnitudes, reference frames, and relations. A global graph-to-graph grounding problem jointly reasons about semantic, spatial, relational, and kinematic compatibility, supporting null assignments and abstention under ambiguity. Accepted directives are converted to continuous joint targets within observed motion range and executed via forward kinematics.

Result: Experiments on synthetic reconstructions, mesh-based assets, and real captures demonstrate reliable language grounding and continuous articulated control across repeated parts, spatial references, relational commands, and ambiguous instructions.

Conclusion: ArtLang enables intuitive, open-vocabulary language control of articulated assets while gracefully handling ambiguity and uncertain part identities, advancing beyond index-based control.

Abstract: Articulated-object reconstructions recover explicit geometry and kinematics, but their parts often remain semantically anonymous and must be controlled through part indices and numerical joint parameters. We present ArtLang, a framework for open-vocabulary language control of persistent reconstructed articulated assets. ArtLang represents an asset as a semantic-kinematic articulation graph and augments its surface with language features and graph-constrained motion. Open-vocabulary proposals are bound to reconstructed parts while allowing uncertain parts to remain unnamed. A typed parser converts a command into a directive graph containing referring expressions, actions, magnitudes, reference frames, and relations. We then solve a global graph-to-graph grounding problem that jointly reasons about semantic, spatial, relational, and kinematic compatibility, with support for null assignments and abstention under ambiguity. Accepted directives are converted into continuous joint targets within the observed motion range and executed through forward kinematics. Experiments on synthetic reconstructions, mesh-based assets, and real captures demonstrate reliable language grounding and continuous articulated control across repeated parts, spatial references, relational commands, and ambiguous instructions.

</details>


### [89] [HistReNeRF: Historic Image Relocalisation within Contemporary Neural Radiance Field Reconstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15420)
*Benjamin T. Hughes,Stuart James*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HistReNeRF estimates the 6-DoF pose of historic photographs by matching adapted DINOv2 patch features to candidate rays sampled from a contemporary Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) reconstruction, reducing cross-temporal appearance shift via embedding-space domain adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relocalising historic archival photographs within contemporary scene models is challenging due to significant differences in photographic appearance, visible objects, and spatial layout between historic and modern views.

Method: The framework matches adapted DINOv2 patch features to candidate rays sampled from a contemporary NeRF reconstruction. The continuous NeRF representation provides a queryable scene interface enabling domain adaptation between historic and contemporary images directly in the feature representation used for localisation.

Result: On a new cross-temporal dataset with 10,545 contemporary street-level images and 230 archival photographs from three European landmarks, embedding-space adaptation reduces translation and rotation errors by an average of 11% and 16% respectively across three scenes compared to pixel-space methods.

Conclusion: Neural scene relocalisation provides a natural interface for feature-space adaptation, reducing cross-temporal appearance shift without modifying the query image.

Abstract: Relocalising archival photographs within a contemporary scene model is challenging because historic and modern views can differ in photographic appearance, visible objects, and spatial layout. Therefore, we present HistReNeRF, a framework that estimates the 6-DoF pose of a historic photograph by matching adapted DINOv2 patch features to candidate rays sampled from a contemporary Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) reconstruction. The continuous representation of a NeRF provides a queryable scene interface from which candidate rays can be sampled and matched, enabling domain adaptation between historic photography and contemporary images directly in the feature representation used for localisation. We evaluate embedding-space-based domain adaptation against pixel-space methods on a new cross-temporal dataset comprising 10,545 contemporary street-level images and 230 archival photographs from three European landmarks. Embedding-space adaptation reduces translation and rotation errors by an average of 11% and 16%, respectively, across the three scenes. These results show that neural scene relocalisation provides a natural interface for feature-space adaptation, reducing cross-temporal appearance shift without modifying the query image. Code and dataset at https://github.com/ARTUROLab/HistReNeRF.

</details>


### [90] [NumerosityVLM: A Cognitively Inspired Benchmark for Interpreting Numerosity Representations in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15425)
*Yiming Fu,Fangjun Li,Xiujin Liu,Ruidong Ma,Hang Yu,Zhichen Lu,Kanwei He,Alessandro Di Nuovo,Angelo Cangelosi,Zhegong Shangguan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces NumerosityVLM, a cognitively inspired benchmark of 10,800 synthetic images across six controlled conditions that decouple numerosity from size and spatial arrangement. Evaluating seven VLMs zero-shot, the authors find model architecture (especially the language model component) drives performance variance more than visual conditions, and that numerosity signals emerge early in the vision encoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerosity perception is a fundamental cognitive ability that emerges in human infants before language, yet it remains poorly understood in VLMs. Existing counting benchmarks entangle numerosity with correlated visual factors (e.g., size, arrangement), making it impossible to isolate true numerosity perception from confounding visual cues.

Method: The authors construct NumerosityVLM with 10,800 synthetic images across six controlled conditions that orthogonally manipulate object size, spatial arrangement, and numerosity, while progressively ablating texture, shape, and color. Seven VLMs are evaluated zero-shot, with multi-factor analysis (partial omega-squared) and layer-wise probing of the vision encoder to localize numerosity signals.

Result: Model architecture explains the largest proportion of performance variance (partial ω²=0.325), far exceeding visual conditions. Layer-wise probing shows linearly separable numerosity signals consistently emerge at early stages of the vision encoder, and performance differences across models are primarily associated with the language model component rather than the vision encoder.

Conclusion: Numerosity perception in VLMs is primarily governed by model architecture (particularly the language model), not low-level visual features, suggesting that high-level numerosity processing may be bottlenecked by the language generation component. This provides guidance for improving numerosity competencies in VLMs.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) achieve strong performance on high-level multimodal tasks, yet numerosity perception, a cognitive ability that emerges in human infants before language acquisition, remains poorly understood in current models, as existing counting benchmarks entangle numerosity with correlated visual factors. We introduce a cognitively inspired diagnostic benchmark, NumerosityVLM, comprising 10,800 synthetic images across six controlled conditions. The benchmark orthogonally manipulates object size, spatial arrangement, and numerosity, while progressively ablating texture, shape, and color. Evaluating seven VLMs in a zero-shot setting, multi-factor analysis reveals that model architecture explains the largest proportion of performance variance (partial $ω^{2}=0.325$), far exceeding visual conditions. Layer-wise probing further shows that linearly separable numerosity signals consistently emerge at early stages of the vision encoder, while performance differences across evaluated models are primarily associated with the language model component. Code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/fuy3/NumerosityVLM-Benchmark, and https://huggingface.co/datasets/fuy3/NumerosityVLM.

</details>


### [91] [Spatially-Grounded Flow Matching: Structured Source Distributions for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15452)
*Arman Zarei,Mahdi M. Kalayeh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StructFlow is a flow matching method that encodes spatial locality into the source noise distribution by sharing common noise components within small pixel regions, improving local editing, structure preservation, and semantic interpolation in image generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard flow matching uses i.i.d. Gaussian noise as the source, which has no spatial structure and contradicts the inherent locality of natural images (nearby pixels are strongly correlated). This mismatch encourages models to exploit less noisy neighbors as context rather than properly learning true local image structure.

Method: StructFlow modifies the source distribution so pixels within small spatial regions share a common noise component, creating structured source noise that encodes spatial locality. This produces transport paths geometrically aligned with image regions, and can be applied to pre-trained models via a lightweight post-training phase.

Result: Experiments across multiple datasets and conditioning regimes (unconditional, class, text) using different diffusion transformer architectures show StructFlow achieves competitive generation quality while significantly improving localized controllable re-synthesis, including fine-grained local editing, robust structure preservation, and smooth semantic interpolation.

Conclusion: Encoding spatial locality directly into the source noise distribution reconciles the source with the image domain's inductive bias, enabling capabilities (local editing, structure preservation, semantic interpolation) that generic flow matching struggles to provide, and these benefits even extend to large pre-trained models.

Abstract: Current flow matching models learn to transport the source i.i.d. Gaussian noise into the target distribution of natural images, yet this source distribution carries no notion of spatial structure. Images however are fundamentally local since nearby pixels are strongly correlated. By sampling the noise independently, we hypothesize that models are implicitly encouraged to exploit less noisy neighbors as context during training, partially bypassing the need to properly learn the true local structure of images. The source distribution, in other words, works against the inductive bias of the image domain. To ameliorate this design discrepancy, we propose StructFlow which encodes spatial locality directly into the source by having the pixels within a small region share a common noise component. This structured source produces transport paths that are geometrically aligned with image regions - enabling properties that generic flow matching struggles to provide: fine-grained local editing that naturally respects boundaries, robust structure preservation, and smooth semantic interpolation between images. We show that these benefits also extend to large pre-trained models, demonstrating that StructFlow can even be incorporated through a lightweight post-training phase.
