Comprehensive experiments on multiple datasets, in unconditional, class and text-conditioned regimes, using different diffusion transformer architectures confirm that StructFlow not only offers competitive image generation quality, but also significantly improves localized controllable re-synthesis.

</details>


### [92] [AlignJEPA: Predictive Vision-Language Alignment for Remote Sensing Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15456)
*Md Aminur Hossain,Omkumar Vaghasiya,Rajeev Ranjan Dwivedi,Vinod Kurmi,Biplab Banerjee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AlignJEPA is a parameter-efficient JEPA-inspired framework that aligns remote sensing foundation models with natural language by training only a lightweight predictive alignment network, achieving strong vision-language alignment for tasks like image-text retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most remote sensing foundation models are weakly aligned with natural language, limiting capabilities in natural-language archive search, image-text retrieval, and question-conditioned analysis.

Method: AlignJEPA uses a pretrained AnySat visual encoder and a RemoteCLIP text encoder while training only a lightweight predictive alignment network. It predicts remote-sensing text embeddings from masked visual foundation-model tokens using a mask-aware multi-scale predictive aligner with fine, regional, and global scales, a cross-scale Transformer, and learned query pooling projection into text space. Training combines semantic prediction with bidirectional contrastive retrieval.

Result: The framework is evaluated on BigEarthNet.txt for natural-language Sentinel retrieval, RSICD for cross-dataset adaptation, and RSVQA as a closed-set representation probe.

Conclusion: AlignJEPA provides a parameter-efficient route for aligning Earth observation foundation models with language.

Abstract: Remote sensing (RS) foundation models provide transferable Earth observation representations across sensors, resolutions, and geographies, yet most remain weakly aligned with natural language, limiting natural-language archive search, image-text retrieval, and question-conditioned analysis. We propose AlignJEPA, a JEPA-inspired predictive vision-language alignment framework for remote sensing foundation models. AlignJEPA uses a pretrained AnySat visual encoder and a RemoteCLIP text encoder while training only a lightweight predictive alignment network. Instead of relying on global image--text contrastive alignment alone, the framework predicts remote-sensing text embeddings from masked visual foundation-model tokens. Its mask-aware multi-scale predictive aligner aggregates visible tokens at fine, regional, and global scales, jointly models them with a cross-scale Transformer, and projects the resulting representation into the text space using learned query pooling. Training combines semantic prediction with bidirectional contrastive retrieval. We train and evaluate AlignJEPA on BigEarthNet.txt for natural-language Sentinel retrieval, evaluate cross-dataset adaptation on RSICD, and use RSVQA only as a closed-set representation probe. AlignJEPA provides a parameter-efficient route for aligning Earth observation foundation models with language.

</details>


### [93] [GLaQ: Grounding Latent Queries in Visual Evidence for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15517)
*Zesheng Yang,Lingling Zhang,Xinyu Zhang,Cheng Zhang,Pengyu Li,Heng Wang,Lin Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GLaQ replaces autoregressive latent rollout with a fixed set of context-conditioned queries grounded in original visual tokens, improving fine-grained visual understanding in multimodal LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought reasoning in multimodal LLMs struggles to preserve and reuse fine-grained visual evidence across text-based reasoning steps. Existing approaches either require predefined external tools (tool-augmented thinking-with-images) or use autoregressive continuous latent reasoning, where each latent state depends on predecessors and may redundantly repeat information rather than capture complementary visual details.

Method: GLaQ introduces a grounded latent-query framework that uses a fixed set of context-conditioned queries grounded in original visual tokens, replacing sequential latent rollout. Grounded queries are reinjected for answer generation for direct access to source visual evidence. Training uses localized-view supervision followed by reinforcement learning under task-level rewards.

Result: Across five benchmarks for fine-grained visual understanding and perception, GLaQ-7B gains 5.99-9.66% over its base model and outperforms all compared visual latent methods.

Conclusion: Direct query-to-image grounding can recover localized evidence from the full image without external visual operations or autoregressive latent rollouts.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving capabilities of multimodal large language models. Fine-grained visual evidence, however, remains difficult to preserve and reuse across text-based reasoning steps. To address this limitation, tool-augmented thinking-with-images methods maintain visual access externally by revisiting or manipulating the image, but require predefined tools and additional inference-time processing. As an internal alternative, continuous visual latent reasoning retains intermediate computation in hidden states. However, its prevailing autoregressive construction makes each latent state depend on its predecessors, so later states may repeat information already present in the latent sequence rather than capture complementary visual details. We introduce GLaQ, a grounded latent-query framework that replaces sequential latent rollout with a fixed set of context-conditioned queries grounded in the original visual tokens. The grounded queries are reinjected for answer generation, providing direct and coordinated access to source visual evidence. We train GLaQ with localized-view supervision followed by reinforcement learning under task-level rewards. Across five benchmarks for fine-grained visual understanding and perception, GLaQ-7B gains 5.99--9.66\% over its base model and leads all compared visual latent methods, suggesting that direct query-to-image grounding can recover localized evidence from the full image without external visual operations or autoregressive latent rollouts.

</details>


### [94] [Efficient Audio-Visual Generation via Synchrony-Aware Cross-Modal Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15522)
*Shengchuan Gao,Teng Hu,Bohao Feng,Luchen Li,Wenqiang Wang,Hongqian Deng,Ran Yi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a synchronization-aware acceleration framework for audio-visual generation that uses protected sparse attention guided by audio-video cross-attention patterns to reduce inference cost while maintaining quality and synchronization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-visual generation models have high inference cost due to long video token sequences requiring repeated attention computation across denoising steps. Existing acceleration techniques designed for video-only generation overlook audio-visual interactions and may disrupt audio-video synchronization when applied directly.

Method: The method is based on the observation that bidirectional audio-video cross-attention exhibits structured interactions, with high responses concentrated on a few sound-related visual and temporal regions. Guided by this pattern, the authors introduce a protected sparse attention strategy that preserves high-fidelity computation for synchronization-critical tokens while sparsifying redundant attention interactions.

Result: The proposed framework improves inference efficiency while maintaining video quality, audio quality, and audio-video synchronization.

Conclusion: Explicitly accounting for cross-modal dependence during acceleration enables efficient audio-visual generation without degrading quality or synchronization.

Abstract: Recent audio-visual generation models can synthesize synchronized video and sound in a unified diffusion process, but their inference cost remains high because long video token sequences require repeated attention computation across denoising steps.A variety of acceleration techniques have been developed for video generation models, including low-bit quantization, attention sparsification, and feature caching.However, since these methods are originally designed for video generation, directly applying them to audio-visual models overlooks the interactions between the audio and video branches and may therefore disrupt audio-video synchronization.We present a synchronization-aware acceleration framework for efficient audio-visual generation.Our key observation is that bidirectional audio-video cross-attention reveals structured interactions between the two branches, with high responses often concentrated on a few sound-related visual and temporal regions.Guided by this interaction pattern, we introduce a protected sparse attention strategy that preserves high-fidelity computation for synchronization-critical tokens while sparsifying redundant attention interactions.By explicitly accounting for cross-modal dependence during acceleration, our method improves inference efficiency while keeping video quality, audio quality, and audio-video synchronization.

</details>


### [95] [EA-LiteUNet: An Edge-Adaptive and Resource-Efficient U-Net for Boundary-Sensitive Dermoscopic Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15537)
*Wang Jiangtao,Nur Intan Raihana Ruhaiyem,Fu Panpan,Yang Yu,Huang Yan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EA-LiteUNet is an edge-adaptive, computationally efficient U-Net variant for dermoscopic image segmentation that addresses boundary delineation challenges through boundary-aware representation learning, attention-guided feature modulation, and resource-adaptive inference, achieving superior boundary precision (HD95 of 12.89 pixels, Dice 92.08% on ISIC 2018) with only 0.29M parameters and 1.17 GFLOPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate boundary delineation in dermoscopic image segmentation remains difficult due to blurred lesion margins, heterogeneous textures, and complex artifacts. From a signal-processing view, boundaries are high-frequency components prone to aliasing, noise amplification, and information loss, and conventional downsampling in convolutional architectures degrades boundary representations.

Method: The proposed EA-LiteUNet integrates three core mechanisms: (1) boundary-aware representation learning to suppress aliasing and preserve high-frequency structural details; (2) attention-guided feature modulation to selectively enhance boundary-relevant responses across multi-scale features; and (3) a resource-adaptive inference strategy to dynamically balance segmentation accuracy and computational efficiency.

Result: Extensive evaluations on three public dermoscopic datasets show superior boundary precision. On ISIC 2018, HD95 is reduced to 12.89 pixels with a Dice score of 92.08%, achieved with an ultralightweight configuration of 0.29M parameters and 1.17 GFLOPs. Ablation studies confirm the complementary effects of the components for enhanced boundary fidelity and stable optimization.

Conclusion: EA-LiteUNet effectively solves boundary degradation in dermoscopic segmentation while maintaining computational efficiency, and the ablation studies validate that all three mechanisms contribute complementarily to boundary fidelity and optimization stability.

Abstract: Accurate boundary delineation remains a persistent challenge in dermoscopic image segmentation because of blurred lesion margins, heterogeneous textures, and complex background artifacts. From a signal-processing perspective, lesion boundaries represent high-frequency components that are highly susceptible to aliasing, noise amplification, and information loss. Consequently, repeated downsampling and feature transformations in conventional convolutional architectures often lead to severely degraded boundary representations. To address these limitations, we propose EA-LiteUNet, an edge-adaptive and computationally efficient U-Net variant specifically designed for boundary-sensitive medical image segmentation. The architecture integrates three core mechanisms: (1) boundary-aware representation learning to suppress aliasing and preserve high-frequency structural details; (2) attention-guided feature modulation to selectively enhance boundary-relevant responses across multi-scale features; and (3) a resource-adaptive inference strategy to dynamically balance segmentation accuracy and computational efficiency. Extensive evaluations across three public dermoscopic datasets demonstrate that EA-LiteUNet consistently achieves superior boundary precision. Specifically, on the ISIC 2018 dataset, the method significantly reduces the 95% Hausdorff Distance (HD95) to 12.89 pixels while maintaining a robust Dice score of 92.08%. Notably, this strong performance is achieved with an ultralightweight configuration of merely 0.29M parameters and 1.17 GFLOPs. Ablation studies further validate the complementary effects of these components, confirming their contribution to enhanced boundary fidelity and stable optimization.

</details>


### [96] [CrossView: Can Vision-Language Models Reason Across Cameras?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15539)
*Sahil Shah,S P Sharan,Harsh Goel,Manvik Pasula,Adithya Hebbalae,Minkyu Choi,Sandeep P. Chinchali*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces CrossView, a multi-camera video question-answering benchmark spanning autonomous driving, surveillance, egocentric/exocentric video, and robotics. Evaluation shows current multi-modal language models (both proprietary and open-source) perform poorly on multi-camera reasoning, which the authors argue is fundamentally different from single-camera video understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video understanding benchmarks focus on single-camera settings, but real-world applications (autonomous vehicles, security systems, robots) rely on multi-camera networks. No benchmark systematically targets multi-camera reasoning challenges such as scaling context with view count, resolving occlusions, judging relevant views, and integrating evidence across perspectives.

Method: The authors introduce CrossView, a multi-camera video question-answering benchmark spanning four application domains: autonomous driving, security surveillance, egocentric/exocentric video, and robotics. They evaluate proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5.2) and open-source models (e.g., Qwen3-VL) on this benchmark.

Result: Evaluation reveals consistently low accuracy across all models, with open-source models trailing proprietary ones by a wide margin. Performance scales strongly with a model's ability to jointly process multiple viewpoints, validating CrossView as a rigorous benchmark.

Conclusion: Multi-camera reasoning is a distinct and challenging problem that current models struggle with. CrossView serves as a rigorous benchmark to drive progress in multi-camera video understanding, with code and dataset open-sourced.

Abstract: Video understanding benchmarks have long centered on single-camera settings, where modern multi-modal language models achieve strong performance across image and video tasks. Yet, the real world runs on multi-camera networks: autonomous vehicles, security systems, and robots all gather data across many simultaneous views. We argue that this is not simply "more" of the single-camera problem; it is fundamentally different. Multi-camera reasoning requires handling context that scales with the number of views, resolving occlusions visible from only a subset of cameras, judging which views matter, and integrating evidence across perspectives that may overlap or diverge. Current models struggle with exactly these challenges, yet no benchmark systematically targets them. We introduce CrossView, a multi-camera video question-answering benchmark spanning autonomous driving, security surveillance, egocentric/exocentric video, and robotics. Evaluation of proprietary models, such as GPT-5.2, and open-source models, like Qwen3-VL, reveals consistently low accuracy, with open-source models trailing by a wide margin. Performance scales strongly with a model's ability to jointly process multiple viewpoints, positioning CrossView as a rigorous benchmark for multi-camera video. We open-source our code and dataset at https://utaustin-swarmlab.github.io/CrossView.

</details>


### [97] [RigidBench: Evaluating Rigid-Body Physics in Video Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15555)
*Swarnim Jain,Shangzhe Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RigidBench is a simulator-grounded benchmark for evaluating video prediction models with five rigid-body tasks and ten separate measurements for motion, geometry, identity, background stability, and visual similarity, revealing that model rankings depend heavily on what is measured and that no model excels on all metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video prediction metrics mix together different aspects of prediction quality (motion, geometry, identity, background stability, visual similarity), making it difficult to know whether models actually predict objects moving correctly. The authors aim to separate these failure modes with a benchmark that provides ground truth for each aspect.

Method: The authors introduce RigidBench, a simulator-grounded benchmark with five rigid-body tasks varying objects, materials, viewpoints, and scenes. It provides per-frame masks, depth, 6-DoF trajectories, and contacts for scoring. They evaluate eight models on 100 examples with ten separate measurements, and fine-tune Wan 2.2 TI2V-5B on 5,000 training videos with exact simulator state, using teacher-forced probes and targeted interventions to analyze how object position is represented in the diffusion transformer.

Result: No model leads on all ten measurements, and rankings depend strongly on what metric is used. Higher SSIM correlates with larger 3D trajectory error across model means (r = 0.89). Full fine-tuning of Wan 2.2 TI2V-5B reduces 3D trajectory error by ~20% with almost no SSIM change, and analysis shows object position is represented throughout the diffusion transformer and used by denoising.

Conclusion: The authors demonstrate that whole-frame similarity scores can be misleading in video prediction evaluation, since different aspects of prediction quality fail independently and even correlate inversely. RigidBench provides a more granular, simulator-grounded evaluation framework, and the intervention analysis on Wan reveals that motion information is distributed throughout the model architecture rather than localized.

Abstract: Video models are increasingly used to predict what happens next in a scene, yet the metrics commonly used to compare their outputs say little about whether the predicted objects move correctly. Motion, geometry, identity, background stability, and visual similarity can fail independently, but whole-frame scores often mix these errors together. We introduce RigidBench, a simulator-grounded benchmark that compares a generated continuation with a reference rollout from the same initial frame and motion description. Its five rigid-body tasks vary objects, materials, viewpoints, and indoor and outdoor scenes, with per-frame masks, depth, 6-DoF trajectories, and contacts available for scoring. We evaluate eight models on the same 100 examples with ten measurements that keep these aspects separate. The resulting rankings depend strongly on what is measured: no model leads on all ten, and across model means, higher SSIM accompanies larger 3D trajectory error (r = 0.89). RigidBench also includes 5,000 training videos with exact simulator state, which we use to fine-tune and analyze Wan 2.2 TI2V-5B. Full fine-tuning reduces 3D trajectory error by about 20% with almost no change in SSIM, while teacher-forced probes and targeted interventions show that object position is represented throughout Wan's diffusion transformer and used by its denoising computation.

</details>


### [98] [Catching Hallucinated Citations in Video-LLM Question Answering: A Self-Verification Pipeline and Verifier Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15574)
*Yogesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses deceptive hallucinations in video QA systems where models produce timestamped claims that appear grounded but are actually unsupported. The authors build a verification pipeline: a retrieval-augmented language model drafts answers with per-claim timestamps, then each cited frame is independently re-examined before display. They compare three verification designs, finding that direct vision-model verification fails (0% catch rate due to sycophancy), blind re-captioning with a general LLM judge is unstable (0-100% flagging depending on prompt phrasing), and replacing the judge with a small natural language inference model yields a stable, interpretable verifier that catches 79% of fabricated claims on adversarial false-premise questions while preserving true claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language video QA systems produce timestamped claims with high confidence even when unsupported by cited frames. These deceptive hallucinations are dangerous because timestamps imply grounding without ensuring correctness, increasing user trust but not accuracy. This gap between perceived and actual grounding motivates the need for a verification loop that closes this disconnect.

Method: The pipeline uses a retrieval-augmented language model to draft answers with per-claim timestamp citations, then independently re-examines each cited frame before showing it to the user. Three verification designs are compared: (1) directly asking the vision model whether a frame supports a claim, (2) blind re-captioning plus a general LLM judge, and (3) blind re-captioning plus a small natural language inference model as judge. Evaluations run on both Apple Silicon (MLX) and Google Colab (HF Transformers, CUDA).

Result: Direct vision-model verification completely fails with a 0% catch rate on 40 claims due to sycophancy. Blind re-captioning with a general LLM judge improves results but is unstable, oscillating between 0% and 100% flagged depending on prompt phrasing. Replacing the LLM judge with a small natural language inference model yields a stable, interpretable verifier that catches 79% of fabricated claims on adversarial false-premise questions while leaving true claims untouched.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that timestamp citations alone are insufficient to guarantee grounding in video QA. A dedicated verification loop is necessary, and small natural language inference models prove to be the most stable and effective verification mechanism compared to direct vision-model questioning or general LLM judges. The full pipeline, evaluation harness, and implementations for both Apple Silicon and Colab are released.

Abstract: Video question answering systems built on vision-language models often produce timestamped claims with high confidence even when unsupported by the cited frame. This deceptive hallucination arises because timestamps imply grounding without ensuring correctness, increasing user trust but not accuracy. We introduce a pipeline that closes this loop. A retrieval-augmented language model drafts answers with per-claim timestamp citations, and each cited frame is independently re-examined before being shown to the user. We compare against a plain baseline and ablate three verification designs, evaluated on both Apple Silicon (MLX) and Google Colab (HF Transformers, CUDA). Directly asking the vision model whether a frame supports a claim fails completely (0% catch rate on 40 claims) due to sycophancy. Blind re-captioning plus a general LLM judge improves results but is unstable, oscillating between 0% and 100% flagged depending on prompt phrasing. Replacing that judge with a small natural language inference model yields a stable, interpretable verifier that catches 79% of fabricated claims on adversarial false-premise questions while leaving true claims untouched. We release the full pipeline, evaluation harness, and implementations for both Apple Silicon and Colab. Code is available at https://github.com/yogesh-iitj/grounded-video-qa.

</details>


### [99] [PoseAdapter: Dual-Stream 2.5D Controllable Image Generation for Complex Multi-Object Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15583)
*Yufeng Chi,Huimin Ma,Fan Gao,Zhice Niu,Keqin Li,Jianmin Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PoseAdapter is a lightweight framework for 2.5D controllable image generation that uses sparse spatial-angular anchors (object captions, 2D bounding boxes, 3D angles) and a Context-Aware Dual-Stream Representation with parallel masked/unmasked pathways to achieve precise multi-object spatial and orientation control in text-to-image diffusion models, eliminating attribute leakage while preserving scene coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise spatial and orientational control in multi-object T2I diffusion models remains challenging. Existing methods either rely on expensive dense 3D maps or suffer from attribute leakage and "cut-and-paste" artifacts.

Method: PoseAdapter establishes spatial-angular anchors via an efficient condition layout (object captions, 2D bounding boxes, 3D angles) and introduces a Context-Aware Dual-Stream Representation that injects local object tokens and relation-enriched scene tokens into MM-DiT architectures through parallel masked and unmasked pathways. It also constructs the OrientLayout dataset with 2.5D annotations and instance-level decoupled semantics.

Result: Extensive experiments show PoseAdapter outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in spatial accuracy, orientational precision, and multi-object visual fidelity.

Conclusion: PoseAdapter provides an efficient lightweight alternative to dense 3D map-based approaches for controllable image generation, achieving both strict instance isolation and global coherence in multi-object scenes.

Abstract: While Text-to-Image (T2I) diffusion models have achieved remarkable success, precise spatial and orientational control in multi-object scenes remains a persistent challenge. Existing methods either rely on computationally expensive dense 3D maps or suffer from severe attribute leakage and "cut-and-paste" artifacts. To address these limitations, we propose PoseAdapter, a lightweight framework for high-fidelity 2.5D controllable image generation. Instead of dense spatial maps, it establishes precise spatial-angular anchors using an efficient condition layout: individual object captions, 2D bounding boxes, and 3D angles. To resolve the generative trade-off between strict instance isolation and global coherence, we introduce a Context-Aware Dual-Stream Representation. By injecting local object tokens and relation-enriched scene tokens into the visual stream of modern MM-DiT architectures via parallel masked and unmasked pathways, PoseAdapter eliminates attribute leakage while preserving natural inter-object relationships and scene-level coherence. To support this paradigm, we construct OrientLayout, a high-quality dataset featuring standardized 2.5D annotations and instance-level decoupled semantics. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PoseAdapter outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in spatial accuracy, orientational precision, and multi-object visual fidelity. Code and dataset will be available at https://github.com/cyf23/PoseAdapter.

</details>


### [100] [AlloEgo-VLM: Disambiguating Allocentric and Egocentric Reference Frames in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15605)
*Kuan-Lin Chen,Tzu-Ti Wei,Chao-Chi Liao,Yu-Chee Tseng,Jen-Jee Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the ambiguity in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) regarding spatial semantics, specifically the lack of explicit reference frames (allocentric vs. egocentric) in natural language. The authors construct a new dataset (AlloEgo-View) with view-specific (image, query, answer) triplets, develop AlloEgo-VLM—a framework that disambiguates reference frames via supervised fine-tuning and integrates easily into existing VLMs—and validate it on a real robotic platform in NVIDIA Isaac Sim for object searching tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs often produce inconsistent responses due to insufficient training on spatial reference frames and object orientations. Natural language descriptions of spatial relations frequently omit explicit reference frames, causing semantic ambiguity that can lead to serious errors for embodied AI robots.

Method: The authors construct a new dataset, AlloEgo-View, consisting of (image, query, view-specific answer) triplets that capture key object relations from both allocentric and egocentric perspectives. They build AlloEgo-VLM, a framework that disambiguates allocentric and egocentric reference frames even under ambiguous queries, and integrate it into existing VLMs via supervised fine-tuning. The framework is also deployed on an embodied robotic platform in NVIDIA Isaac Sim.

Result: Experiments highlight the limitations of current VLMs in handling view-specific queries and demonstrate the strong disambiguation ability of AlloEgo-VLM in both benchmark evaluations and real-world robotic object searching tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed AlloEgo-VLM framework, built on the AlloEgo-View dataset, effectively improves VLMs' ability to disambiguate spatial reference frames, with demonstrated feasibility in embodied AI applications.

Abstract: This study investigates the challenge of ambiguity faced by Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in understanding spatial semantics. Spatial cognition, shaped by cognitive psychology, spatial science, and cultural context, often assigns directionality to objects. However, natural language descriptions of spatial relations frequently omit explicit reference frames, leading to semantic ambiguity and potentially serious errors for embodied AI robots. Existing VLMs, due to insufficient training on reference frames and object orientations, often produce inconsistent responses. To address this issue, we construct a new dataset, AlloEgo-View, comprising (image, query, view-specific answer) triplets that capture key object relations from both allocentric and egocentric perspectives. The view-specific descriptions follow a structured spatial representation that annotate detailed scene descriptions, reference and target objects, their orientations, reference frames, and view types. Building on AlloEgo-View, we develop AlloEgo-VLM, a framework to disambiguate allocentric and egocentric reference frames, even under ambiguous queries, and to be easily integrated into existing VLMs via supervised fine-tuning. Furthermore, we deploy our framework onto an embodied robotic platform within NVIDIA Isaac Sim to validate its real-world feasibility in open-ended object searching tasks. Experiments highlight the limitations of current VLMs in handling view-specific queries and demonstrate the strong disambiguation ability of AlloEgo-VLM.

</details>


### [101] [EgoGazeLite: On-Device Egocentric Gaze Prediction for Token-Efficient Multimodal LLM Video Input](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15614)
*Matteo Stoiber,Niels Buus Lassen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EgoGazeLite is a lightweight dual-process gaze predictor for egocentric video that replaces dedicated eye-tracking hardware with a software-only solution, achieving comparable downstream MLLM description quality to ground-truth gaze crops while running in real-time (21.6 ms/frame) with only 15.7M parameters and 6.71 GFLOPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLM understanding of egocentric video is constrained by token budgets—high-resolution video becomes expensive to transmit and process at scale. Prior work (GazeLLM) crops video around gaze to reduce visual tokens ~10x, but relies on dedicated eye-tracking hardware unavailable on consumer smart glasses. A software-only substitute must simultaneously be accurate enough to preserve downstream description quality while being light enough to run on-device within smartphone power/compute budgets.

Method: EgoGazeLite is a lightweight dual-process gaze predictor for egocentric video designed as a software-only substitute for hardware eye-tracking, running the full gaze-and-crop pipeline end-to-end in real time on consumer accelerator hardware.

Result: Across two MLLMs, three automated metrics, and two LLM judges, predicted-gaze crops show no significant difference from ground-truth-gaze crops, with equivalence confirmed in all ten cases. EgoGazeLite achieves 15.7M parameters, 6.71 GFLOPs, and runs in real time at 21.6 ms/frame on consumer accelerator hardware.

Conclusion: EgoGazeLite removes the need for eye-tracking hardware for token-efficient, gaze-conditioned egocentric video understanding with MLLMs, demonstrating that a lightweight software gaze predictor can match hardware-based gaze cropping quality while running on-device in real time.

Abstract: The use of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) for egocentric video understanding with wearable devices is constrained by the token budget. Memory and compute cost scale with the number of visual tokens, and high-resolution video quickly becomes expensive to transmit and process at scale. Prior work (GazeLLM) addresses this by cropping the video around the camera wearer's gaze. This reduces the number of visual tokens by about tenfold while maintaining or improving the quality of full-resolution descriptions. However, this compression strategy depends on dedicated eye-tracking hardware, which is unavailable on consumer smart glasses. Building a software-only substitute poses a joint constraint: the predictor must be accurate enough to preserve downstream description quality, yet light enough to run on-device, within the power and compute budget of a smartphone. We address this with EgoGazeLite, a lightweight dual-process gaze predictor for egocentric video. Across two MLLMs, three automated metrics, and two LLM judges, predicted-gaze crops show no significant difference from ground-truth-gaze crops. Equivalence is confirmed in all ten cases. EgoGazeLite achieves this at 15.7M parameters, 6.71 GFLOPs, and runs the full gaze-and-crop pipeline end-to-end in real time (21.6 ms/frame) on consumer accelerator hardware. Together, these results remove the need for eye-tracking hardware for token-efficient, gaze-conditioned egocentric video understanding with MLLMs.

</details>


### [102] [Hierarchical Adaptive Feature Refinement Network for VHR Remote Sensing Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15647)
*Shuaishuai Cao,Meng Tang,Shuwei Peng,Xuan Liu,Min Huang,Jie Chen,Jiacheng Niu,Yong Chen,Edore Akpokodje,Hui Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HAFR-Net is a progressive refinement framework for VHR remote sensing semantic segmentation that adaptively organizes and conservatively refines hierarchical pretrained encoder features via three components: Heterogeneity-Guided Stage-Adaptive Fusion (HG-SAF) for dense stage-weight prediction, a Frequency-Residual Adapter (FRA) for bounded frequency injection, and a Confusion-Aware Tri-Prior Decoder (CATP) for boundary/objectness/class-relation regularization. It achieves consistent mIoU improvements over a matched UPerNet baseline across four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exploiting multi-stage hierarchical representations from pretrained encoders for VHR remote sensing segmentation is difficult because nearby regions need different balances of fine detail and semantic context, aggressive task-specific transformations degrade pretrained features, and conventional semantic supervision lacks structural guidance.

Method: HAFR-Net uses three components: (1) HG-SAF predicts dense per-stage weights conditioned on local feature variation for adaptive fusion; (2) FRA injects frequency information via a bounded, zero-initialized residual branch keeping the fused representation as reference; (3) CATP regularizes predictions with boundary, objectness, and training-derived class-relation cues.

Result: Under matched Swin-B training and single-scale inference, HAFR-Net achieves 84.12%, 87.86%, 55.17%, and 67.70% mIoU on ISPRS Vaihingen, ISPRS Potsdam, LoveDA, and OpenEarthMap, surpassing the matched UPerNet baseline by 0.55, 0.95, 1.55, and 1.84 percentage points, respectively.

Conclusion: Controlled analyses confirm that spatial reweighting beyond content-only routing helps, that boundary and thin-structure accuracy improves over matched spatial/spectral alternatives, and that confusion on pre-declared class pairs is reduced, validating the adaptive and conservative refinement design.

Abstract: Semantic segmentation of very-high-resolution (VHR) remote sensing imagery increasingly benefits from strong pretrained hierarchical encoders, yet exploiting their multi-stage representations remains difficult. Nearby regions demand different balances between fine detail and semantic context, aggressive task-specific transformations perturb useful pretrained features, and conventional semantic supervision provides limited structural guidance. We present HAFR-Net, a progressive refinement framework that adaptively organizes and conservatively refines hierarchical representations instead of replacing them with a monolithic decoder transformation. Heterogeneity-Guided Stage-Adaptive Fusion (HG-SAF) predicts dense stage weights conditioned on local feature variation. A Frequency-Residual Adapter (FRA) then injects frequency information through a bounded, zero-initialized residual branch that keeps the fused representation as its reference. A Confusion-Aware Tri-Prior Decoder (CATP) finally regularizes the prediction with boundary, objectness, and training-derived class-relation cues. Under a matched Swin-B training and single-scale inference protocol, HAFR-Net attains 84.12%, 87.86%, 55.17%, and 67.70% mIoU on ISPRS Vaihingen, ISPRS Potsdam, LoveDA, and OpenEarthMap, improving the matched UPerNet baseline by 0.55, 0.95, 1.55, and 1.84 percentage points, respectively. Controlled analyses further show consistent spatial reweighting beyond content-only routing, improved boundary and thin-structure accuracy over matched spatial and spectral alternatives, and reduced confusion on pre-declared class pairs.

</details>


### [103] [Gaussian-JEPA: Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning for 3D Gaussian Splats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15651)
*Bin Ren,Qi Ma,Yue Li,Zongyan Han,Yidi Li,Yuqian Fu,Rao Muhammad Anwer,Theo Gevers,Fahad Shahbaz Khan,Salman Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Gaussian-JEPA is a self-supervised method that learns reusable 3D Gaussian representations via latent prediction of held-out Gaussian token blocks, outperforming reconstruction-based pretraining in consistency under resampling, instance retention under partial observations, and frozen feature quality for shape completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-budget encoders consume sampled observations of Gaussian assets, meaning the same object can be observed through different primitive realizations. Existing self-supervised methods reconstruct masked Gaussian attributes, tying supervision to one sampled realization and requiring an input-space decoder. Latent prediction offers an alternative, but applying it to Gaussian tokens requires targets that accommodate coupled attributes and heterogeneous spatial support.

Method: Gaussian-JEPA predicts representations of held-out Gaussian token blocks from visible context. An online encoder processes the context, while a shared exponential-moving-average encoder supplies stop-gradient features for multi-scale targets. Complementary target projections and feature-space grounding provide latent supervision without reconstructing Gaussian attributes.

Result: Compared with matched reconstruction pretraining, Gaussian-JEPA is more consistent across resampled inputs, retains more instance information under partial observations, and provides stronger frozen features for Gaussian completion. It is evaluated on Gaussian resampling, partial observations, renderable shape completion, part segmentation, and object classification.

Conclusion: Latent prediction is an effective objective for learning reusable 3D Gaussian representations, supporting the value of Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures for Gaussian token learning.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) represents 3D content with anisotropic primitives that jointly encode geometry and appearance. Fixed-budget encoders consume sampled observations of Gaussian assets, so the same object may be observed through different primitive realizations. Existing self-supervised methods mainly reconstruct masked Gaussian attributes, tying supervision to one sampled realization and requiring an input-space decoder. Latent prediction offers an alternative, but its application to Gaussian tokens requires targets that accommodate coupled attributes and heterogeneous spatial support. We introduce Gaussian-JEPA, which predicts representations of held-out Gaussian token blocks from visible context. An online encoder processes the context, while a shared exponential-moving-average encoder supplies stop-gradient features for multi-scale targets. Complementary target projections and feature-space grounding provide latent supervision without reconstructing Gaussian attributes. We evaluate the features under Gaussian resampling, partial observations, and renderable shape completion, together with transfer to part segmentation and object classification. Compared with matched reconstruction pretraining, Gaussian-JEPA is more consistent across resampled inputs, retains more instance information under partial observations, and provides stronger frozen features for Gaussian completion. These results support latent prediction as an effective objective for reusable 3D Gaussian representations. Code is on the project page (https://amazingren.github.io/Gaussian-JEPA/).

</details>


### [104] [Scalable Black-Box Model Attribution for Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15652)
*Asaf Livne,Amir Jevnisek,Shai Avidan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RPA (Raw-Patch Attribution) is a lightweight CNN that attributes generated images to their source models in a strict black-box setting, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy (98.0% on 25-class DRAGON, 92.9% on 27-class OpenFake) while being data-efficient, cost-independent of model count, and robust to image distortions. The trained representation generalizes beyond closed-set attribution to unsupervised lineage recovery, unseen generator detection, and few-shot adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative models proliferate, identifying which model produced a given image becomes increasingly important. Prior attribution methods have grown increasingly complex, assuming sophistication is required, and they lack robustness and scalability in real-world conditions.

Method: RPA uses a lightweight CNN operating on raw image patches in a strict black-box setting, requiring no access to generator internals. It is trained for closed-set attribution but yields a versatile feature space.

Result: RPA achieves 98.0% accuracy on 25-class DRAGON and 92.9% on 27-class OpenFake, outperforming prior work while attributing more models. It is data-efficient, has cost independent of candidate model count, and remains robust to compression, blur, and resizing.

Conclusion: Sophisticated generative models do not require sophisticated attributors—a simple, lightweight approach can excel. The learned representation is versatile, supporting unsupervised lineage discovery, unseen generator flagging/grouping, and few-shot integration of new models without retraining.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of generative models raises the model attribution problem: given only an image, can we determine which model produced it? Existing methods have grown as elaborate as the generators they target, on the as- sumption that a more sophisticated model demands a more sophisticated attributor. We show it does not. RPA (Raw- Patch Attribution) attributes images in the strictest black- box setting with a lightweight CNN. Despite its simplicity, it attributes more models at higher accuracy than prior work, reaching 98.0% on 25-class DRAGON and 92.9% on 27- class OpenFake; it is data-efficient and runs at a cost inde- pendent of the number of candidate models; and it stays ro- bust to the compression, blur, and resizing images undergo in the wild. Training for closed-set attribution yields a ver- satile feature extractor: the same representation recovers model lineage without supervision, flags and groups unseen generators, and admits new models through few-shot adap- tation rather than retraining.

</details>


### [105] [WorldRover: A Scalable Synthetic Video Data Engine for World Exploration with Rich Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15659)
*Xiaojie Xu,Zhengyuan Lin,Runyi Li,Yihao Liu,Kaipeng Zhang,Yongtao Ge*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WorldRover is a data engine built on Unreal Engine that generates richly annotated, minute-scale exploration videos of artist-built environments. It provides RGB with metric depth, camera trajectories, action signals, optical flow, and long-range 2D/3D point tracks from first-person, third-person, and 360 panoramic viewpoints under different environmental conditions, enabling scalable supervision for world generation and reconstruction models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video datasets lack the dense annotations (geometry, correspondence, control signals) needed for learning to generate or reconstruct explorable worlds. Real capture requires estimation or specialized instrumentation for such signals, while synthetic resources rarely combine all these quantities on the same frames with controllable viewpoint and appearance changes.

Method: WorldRover consists of WorldRover-Engine, an Unreal Engine pipeline that executes and offline-renders minute-scale routes while preserving full trajectories and scene geometry. The same exploration can be replayed from first-person, third-person, and 360 panoramic cameras under different environmental states or with neutral white material. This engine is used to construct WorldRover-10M, a dataset pairing RGB with metric depth, camera trajectories, trajectory-derived action signals, and (for third-person) dense optical flow, long-range 2D/3D point tracks with visibility, and a distinct character trajectory.

Result: The WorldRover-10M dataset provides paired supervision signals including RGB, metric depth, camera trajectories, action signals, optical flow, and long-range point tracks across multiple viewpoints and environmental states, turning long-horizon world exploration into a scalable data-generation problem.

Conclusion: WorldRover enables scalable generation of supervision data for models that must build, maintain, and revisit coherent representations of explorable worlds, filling a gap where real capture and existing synthetic resources fail to provide comprehensive paired signals.

Abstract: Learning to generate or reconstruct explorable worlds requires video paired with more than RGB: camera motion, scene geometry, temporal correspondence and, for interactive models, control signals. Real capture can provide some of these signals, but dense geometry and long-range correspondence usually rely on estimation or specialised instrumentation. Rendering provides these quantities directly, yet existing synthetic resources rarely combine them on the same frames while also supporting controlled changes of viewpoint and appearance. We introduce WorldRover, a data engine for generating richly annotated, long-range explorations of artist-built environments. At its core, WorldRover-Engine is an Unreal Engine pipeline that executes and offline-renders minute-scale routes while preserving their full trajectories and scene geometry. The same exploration can be replayed from first-person, third-person, and 360 panoramic cameras under different environmental states. Using WorldRover-Engine, we construct WorldRover-10M, whose sequences pair RGB with metric depth, camera trajectories, and trajectory-derived action signals throughout each exploration. Third-person subsets additionally provide dense optical flow, long-range 2D/3D point tracks with visibility, and a character trajectory distinct from the camera trajectory. The engine can render a traversal from first-person, third-person and 360 panoramic viewpoints, under different environmental states or with a neutral white material, while preserving the route and scene geometry. WorldRover therefore turns long-horizon world exploration into a scalable data-generation problem, providing supervision for models that must build, maintain, and revisit coherent representations of an explorable world.

</details>


### [106] [BASeg: Boundary-Aware Remote Sensing Segmentation with Structural Penalties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15683)
*Yuexi Song,Kailai Sun,Zhuoyu Wang,Mingyi He,Paul Pu Liang,Shenhao Wang,Jinhua Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces MABL, a Mahalanobis-Angle Boundary Loss that enhances boundary and shape consistency in semantic segmentation, and BASeg, a boundary-aware framework combining a Global Visual State Space module with a Cross-Feature Fusion module. A new global 10-city benchmark dataset (GCD-25k) is also established. BASeg achieves up to 2.8% improvement in mIoU across four remote-sensing benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic segmentation methods struggle to capture fine-grained object features and boundary details in remote sensing imagery. Additionally, current datasets lack city morphology diversity, and segmentation on generative images remains unexplored.

Method: The paper proposes MABL (Mahalanobis-Angle Boundary Loss) which explicitly enhances boundary and shape consistency through Mahalanobis distance-based weighting and angle-aware penalty. Built upon MABL, BASeg integrates a Global Visual State Space module (GSM) for long-range contextual dependencies and a Cross-Feature Fusion module (CFM) for fine-grained local details. A global 10-city benchmark dataset (GCD-25k) is established for building and road segmentation.

Result: BASeg consistently outperforms existing methods across four remote-sensing benchmarks, achieving up to 2.8% improvement in mIoU with more accurate object boundary segmentation. MABL also improves performance when integrated into various existing segmentation architectures.

Conclusion: The proposed boundary-aware loss and framework demonstrate robustness and broad applicability, effectively addressing fine-grained segmentation challenges in remote sensing while providing a new diverse benchmark dataset for the community.

Abstract: Semantic segmentation is a core computer vision task in the remote sensing field, accelerating advancements in ur- ban development, agriculture, ecology, water resources, and environmental monitoring. However, recent methods usually struggle to capture fine-grained object features and bound- ary details. Besides, current widely used datasets often lack city morphology diversity and segmentation on generative im- ages remains largely unexplored. To address these issues, we propose a Mahalanobis-Angle Boundary Loss (MABL) that explicitly enhances boundary and shape consistency. MABL jointly models structural importance and boundary orientation through Mahalanobis distance-based weighting and angle- aware penalty. It can be readily integrated into diverse seg- mentation architectures and consistently improves their accu- racy. Built upon MABL, we introduce BASeg, a boundary- aware remote sensing segmentation framework with Struc- tural Penalties. BASeg integrates a Global Visual State Space module (GSM) with a Cross-Feature Fusion module (CFM) to capture both long-range contextual dependencies and fine- grained local details. Additionally, we establish a global 10- city benchmark dataset (GCD-25k) to facilitate accurate build- ing and road segmentation. Extensive experiments on four remote-sensing benchmarks demonstrate that BASeg consis- tently outperforms existing methods, achieving up to a 2.8% improvement in mIoU while producing more accurate object boundary segmentation across diverse scenes. Moreover, integrating MABL into multiple existing segmentation archi- tectures consistently improves performance across datasets, demonstrating its robustness and broad applicability.

</details>


### [107] [Counterfactual Sensitivity Is Not Repairability: Auditing Replay Probes for Video Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15685)
*Rama AlHamidi,Rasul Khanbayov,Erchin Serpedin,Hasan Kurban*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CARVE is a black-box counterfactual probe that tests whether tool-using video agents actually depend on retrieved visual evidence by comparing answer changes under matched SHAM (re-executing the identical pipeline on the same frames) vs. DESTROY (destroying semantic content of retrieved frames) replays. On a frozen VideoExplorer-style agent, DESTROY changes answers 29.3 percentage points more often than SHAM. At k=3 replay budget, CARVE selects 538 of 1,258 LVBench questions and improves accuracy by 3.26 points, functioning as a routing signal rather than a direct grounding classifier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using video agents retrieve visual evidence but the final answer is not forced to depend on what was retrieved. Existing evaluation methods cannot determine whether the retrieved evidence actually influences the final answer, motivating a counterfactual black-box test.

Method: CARVE introduces a counterfactual probe that compares answer changes under two matched replay conditions: SHAM (re-executes the identical pipeline on the same frames) and DESTROY (destroys the semantic content of retrieved frames). It operates as a black box without requiring internal access to the agent. Evaluated across three independent k=3 runs on a frozen VideoExplorer-style agent, with replay budgets varying from k=3 to k=10.

Result: DESTROY changes answers 29.3 percentage points more often than SHAM across three k=3 runs, showing a large and reproducible aggregate effect. Question-level scores are less stable. At k=3, CARVE selects 538/1,258 LVBench questions and improves accuracy by 3.26 points with higher fallback yield than most random subsets. Increasing replay budget to k=10 reduces ties but weakens the zero-threshold routing policy.

Conclusion: CARVE is best understood as a routing signal for selecting questions where the agent genuinely depends on retrieved evidence, rather than as a direct grounding classifier. The score shows only weak association with annotated temporal coverage, and the implementation is released publicly at https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/CARVE.

Abstract: Tool-using video agents retrieve visual evidence before answering, but the final answer is not forced to depend on what was retrieved. The natural black box test is counterfactual: destroy the semantic content of the frames the agent retrieved and check whether the answer changes, against a matched sham that re-executes the identical pipeline on those same frames. We introduce CARVE, a black-box counterfactual probe that compares answer changes under matched SHAM and DESTROY replays. Across three independent k=3 runs on a frozen VideoExplorer-style agent, DESTROY changes the answer 29.3 percentage points more often than SHAM, yielding a large and reproducible aggregate effect. Question-level scores are less stable, and increasing the replay budget from k=3 to k=10 reduces ties but weakens the original zero-threshold routing policy. At k=3, CARVE selects 538 of 1,258 LVBench questions and improves accuracy by 3.26 points, with higher fallback yield than most matched random subsets. The score shows only a weak association with annotated temporal coverage, so CARVE is best understood as a routing signal rather than a direct grounding classifier. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/CARVE.

</details>


### [108] [Training-Free Long-Term Multi-Object Tracking for Sports Video Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15688)
*Tomasz Stanczyk,Seongro Yoon,Francois Bremond*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: McByte++ is a training-free tracking-by-detection framework for long-term multi-object tracking in sports that integrates lightweight mask propagation, conditional camera motion compensation, and online re-identification. It improves HOTA by up to +3.0 and IDF1 by +6.1 over its predecessor McByte on SoccerNet-tracking and SportsMOT benchmarks, while achieving up to an order-of-magnitude speedup without detector retraining or dataset-specific tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term multi-object tracking in sports is challenging due to frequent occlusions, rapid camera motion, and repeated player reappearances, which existing methods struggle to handle effectively.

Method: The framework integrates three key components in a unified pipeline: (1) lightweight mask propagation replacing heavy segmentation components, (2) conditional camera motion compensation, and (3) online re-identification. The method is training-free and builds upon the previous McByte framework with optimizations in motion modeling and segmentation efficiency.

Result: On SoccerNet-tracking and SportsMOT benchmarks, McByte++ achieves up to +3.0 HOTA and +6.1 IDF1 improvements over McByte in the online setting, with further gains when combined with offline global association. It also achieves up to an order-of-magnitude speed increase over its predecessor.

Conclusion: McByte++ demonstrates that a training-free, efficient tracking framework can substantially improve both accuracy and speed for long-term sports tracking without requiring detector retraining or dataset-specific tuning, making it a practical and broadly applicable solution.

Abstract: Long-term multi-object tracking in sports remains challenging due to frequent occlusions, rapid camera motion, and repeated player reappearances. We introduce McByte++, a training-free tracking-by-detection framework that integrates lightweight mask propagation, conditional camera motion compensation, and online re-identification within a unified pipeline. Compared to its predecessor, McByte++ substantially improves runtime efficiency while enhancing identity preservation. On SoccerNet-tracking and SportsMOT benchmarks, McByte++ achieves up to +3.0 HOTA and +6.1 IDF1 improvements over the original McByte in the online setting, with further gains when combined with offline global association. Replacing heavy segmentation components and optimizing motion modeling yields up to an order-of-magnitude speed increase. All results are obtained without detector retraining or dataset-specific tuning. Code will be made available at https://github.com/tstanczyk95/McBytePlusPlus.

</details>


### [109] [Automated Fetal Brain MRI Biometry in Healthy and Pathological Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15692)
*Ema Masterl,Tina Vipotnik Vesnaver,Nejc Šubič,Žiga Špiclin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents an automated biometric analysis pipeline for fetal brain MRI that localizes 22 anatomical landmarks and derives 11 clinically relevant measurements. The H3DE-Net model significantly outperforms SCN in landmark localization accuracy and diagnostic utility across a heterogeneous cohort of healthy and pathological cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated biometric analysis methods for fetal brain MRI are often limited to few measurements or evaluated only on healthy cases, lacking robustness on heterogeneous cohorts with pathologies. There is a need for a comprehensive, reproducible, and observer-independent pipeline validated across diverse clinical scenarios.

Method: The pipeline localizes 22 anatomical landmarks on NeSVoR-reconstructed 3D volumes and derives 11 clinically relevant measurements spanning supratentorial, ventricular, cerebellar, and midline structures. Two landmark localization models (H3DE-Net and SCN) are compared on 122 acquisitions including both healthy controls and pathological cases. Evaluation uses linear mixed-effects models for localization accuracy, calibrated centile charts for agreement with normative growth trajectories, and a decision tree for classifying ventriculomegaly (VM) severity.

Result: H3DE-Net achieved significantly lower localization error than SCN (mean 1.36 mm vs. 3.58 mm in healthy controls; 1.90 mm vs. 4.13 mm in pathological cases; p < 0.001). It outperformed a GA-based regression baseline in 7 of 11 measurements and yielded higher classification AUC in every diagnostic group, with the clearest advantage in separating healthy controls from VM. Decision tree thresholds for ventricular width aligned with the clinical 10 mm and 15 mm cut-offs for defining and grading VM.

Conclusion: The proposed automated pipeline using H3DE-Net is a robust and clinically relevant tool for fetal brain biometric analysis, demonstrating superior accuracy and diagnostic utility over existing approaches even in heterogeneous, pathological cohorts.

Abstract: Automated biometric analysis of fetal brain MRI enables reproducible, observer-independent quantitative assessment, yet existing methods are often restricted to few measurements or evaluated only on healthy cases. We assemble and evaluate an automated biometric analysis pipeline that localizes 22 anatomical landmarks on NeSVoR-reconstructed 3D volumes and derives 11 clinically relevant measurements spanning supratentorial, ventricular, cerebellar, and midline structures. We compare two landmark localization models, H3DE-Net and SCN, on a heterogeneous cohort of 122 acquisitions (both healthy controls and range pathologies). Localization accuracy was assessed with a linear mixed-effects model, agreement with normative growth trajectories with calibrated centile charts, and diagnostic utility with a decision tree classifying VM severity. H3DE-Net achieved significantly lower localization error than SCN across all landmarks (mean 1.36 mm vs. 3.58 mm in HC and 1.90 mm vs. 4.13 mm in PC; p < 0.001), and outperformed a GA-based regression baseline in 7 of 11 measurements. H3DE-Net measurements yielded higher classification AUC in every diagnostic group, with the clearest advantage in separating healthy controls from VM. Decision tree thresholds for ventricular width fell near the clinical 10 mm and 15 mm cut-offs used to define and grade VM.

</details>


### [110] [RRFC: Recursive Refinement via Feedback Conditioning for Iterative Image-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15694)
*Kareem Hassani,Chaymaa Abbas,Hadi Al Mubasher,Mariette Awad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes RRFC, a feedback-conditioning framework that allows image-to-image generators to iteratively refine their own outputs by feeding their previous prediction back as auxiliary input channels, and evaluates it across 18 architecture-task settings with mixed results: improvements on reconstruction/identity tasks but consistent degradations on semantic-layout tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conditional image-to-image generators are single-shot, producing outputs in one forward pass with no opportunity for improvement, leaving room for adaptive iterative revision.

Method: RRFC feeds the generator's most recent previous prediction back as auxiliary input channels alongside the original input, conditioning the model on its own prior output. It preserves the core generator architecture while modifying only the conditioning interface and training/inference procedure, making it applicable to existing generators without redesign.

Result: Evaluated across six baselines (adversarial, equilibrium, and diffusion models) and three paired image-to-image tasks, yielding across 18 settings: seven Holm-corrected improvements, seven degradations, and four non-significant changes. Gains concentrate on reconstruction-fidelity and identity tasks, while five of seven degradations occur on the semantic-layout task.

Conclusion: Feedback-based refinement is beneficial when the refinement objective overlaps with the evaluated property, with gains concentrated on tasks where this overlap holds; it is not universally beneficial across all task types.

Abstract: Conditional image-to-image generators are single-shot: they map input features to an output in one forward pass and treat it as final, with no opportunity to improve on it. Although trained to produce the best possible result in one step, such a model leaves room for improvement if it can adaptively revise its own output over iterations. We propose Recursive Refinement via Feedback Conditioning (RRFC), a novel feedback-conditioning framework for iterative output refinement that teaches a model to adaptively revise its output by conditioning on a new signal, namely its most recent previous prediction, which is fed back as an auxiliary set of channels alongside the original input. This preserves the generator's core architecture while modifying its conditioning interface and, depending on the model family, its training or inference procedure, so RRFC can be attached to existing generators without redesign. We evaluate RRFC across six baselines spanning adversarial, equilibrium, and diffusion-based models and three paired image-to-image translation tasks. Across 18 architecture-task settings, RRFC yields seven Holm-corrected improvements, seven degradations, and four non-significant changes. The gains concentrate on reconstruction-fidelity and identity settings, while five of the seven degradations fall on the single semantic-layout task, where every model declines. These results indicate that feedback-based refinement helps when its objective overlaps with the evaluated property, and that its gains concentrate on the tasks where that overlap holds.

</details>


### [111] [Bitstream Action Recognition is Byte Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15695)
*Fangcheng Li,Chaoran Huang,Tianyi Liu,Wenyang Liu,Kejun Wu,Qiong Liu,You Yang,Zhengguo Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BRACE is a dual-branch byte-modeling framework for Bitstream Action Recognition that aligns corrupted bitstream representations to their intact counterparts, supplemented by a real-world corruption simulator (RBCS) and the first large-scale BAR dataset (BAR-D), achieving superior robustness to bitstream corruption compared to 14 competing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional action recognition depends on successful pixel decoding, but bitstream corruption during storage or transmission can cause visual artifacts or decoding failure, making reliable action recognition challenging. Bitstream Action Recognition (BAR) aims to overcome this dependency on decoding and vulnerability to corruption.

Method: BRACE is a dual-branch byte-modeling architecture treating a corrupted bitstream and its intact counterpart as two byte realizations of the same action. It uses Intact-Anchored Representation Alignment (IARA) where the intact representation serves as a stable anchor and corrupted one is aligned at embedding and decision levels under Unreliable-Anchor Suppression (UAS), all in representation space. The Real-world Bitstream Corruption Simulator (RBCS) is a four-parameter simulator reproducing bit-flip and byte-loss errors, used to build the BAR-D dataset.

Result: BRACE achieves superior robustness to bitstream corruption compared to all 14 baseline methods from pixel, compressed, and bitstream domains on the newly constructed BAR-D benchmark (BAR-Stanford40 and BAR-PPMI subsets). Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of RBCS augmentation and IARA.

Conclusion: BRACE demonstrates that anchoring corrupted bitstream representations to intact counterparts in representation space—without bitstream repair—is an effective strategy for robust bitstream-based action recognition, and the proposed RBCS simulator and BAR-D dataset provide a foundation for future research in this area.

Abstract: Conventional action recognition typically relies on successful pixel decoding of the bitstream. However, bitstream corruption during storage or transmission may cause severe visual artifacts or even decoding failure, posing a significant challenge to reliable action recognition. Bitstream Action Recognition (BAR) aims to overcome the dependency on decoding and the vulnerability to corruption. In this paper, we propose a novel BAR framework, Bitstream Recognition via Anchoring Corrupted Embeddings (BRACE). BRACE is a dual-branch byte-modeling architecture that treats a corrupted bitstream and its intact counterpart as two byte realizations of the same action. This guides the generation of rich and stable representations for robustness to corruption through Intact-Anchored Representation Alignment (IARA). The intact representation serves as a stable anchor, and the corrupted one is aligned to it at the embedding and decision levels under Unreliable-Anchor Suppression (UAS), entirely in representation space and without repairing the bitstream. To address the scarcity of corrupted bitstreams in practice, we introduce the Real-world Bitstream Corruption Simulator (RBCS), a four-parameter simulator that reproduces bit-flip and byte-loss errors arising in transmission and storage. Building on RBCS, we construct the first large-scale BAR dataset (BAR-D), which comprises the BAR-Stanford40 and BAR-PPMI subsets and spans diverse corruption types and severity levels. Finally, we build a large benchmark on BAR-D involving 14 action recognition methods from the pixel, compressed, and bitstream domains. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BRACE has superior robustness to bitstream corruption than all comparison methods. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of the proposed RBCS augmentation and IARA.

</details>


### [112] [ConceptFormer: Learning Adaptive Latent Concepts for Query-Document Alignment in Visual Document Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15698)
*Peng Chunyi,Xu Zhipeng,Yan Yukun,Liu Zhenghao,Yu Shi,Mei Sen,Sun Yubo,Zhang Yongheng,Zhou Jie,Gu Yu,Yu Ge,Sun Maosong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ConceptFormer introduces a latent concept representation learning framework for visual document retrieval that models query-relevant evidence as continuous, query-conditioned latent concepts, bridging localized visual evidence and semantic relevance without textual intermediaries or raw visual annotations. It achieves 16.7% and 22.1% relative improvements in NDCG@10 over the strongest visual and OCR-based text retrieval baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing finer-grained supervision for visual document retrieval relies on either textual descriptions or localized visual regions as evidence proxies, which may overlook complex visual structures or provide incomplete and inaccurate representations of underlying evidence.

Method: ConceptFormer models query-relevant evidence as continuous, query-conditioned latent concepts that bridge localized visual evidence and semantic relevance. During training, a strong vision-language model dynamically determines the number of latent concept tokens, which serve as intermediate representations to bridge the semantic gap between queries and documents and guide embedding space learning.

Result: ConceptFormer achieves 16.7% and 22.1% relative improvements in average NDCG@10 over the strongest visual retrieval baseline and the strongest OCR-based text retrieval baseline, respectively, across diverse visual document retrieval benchmarks.

Conclusion: Latent concepts effectively connect localized visual evidence with semantic relevance, enabling the retriever to capture both fine-grained textual cues and complex document-level visual structures while preserving strong retrieval alignment.

Abstract: Visual document retrieval is a critical component of multimodal retrieval-augmented generation, aiming to identify query-relevant pages from document collections where evidence is distributed across text, layout, charts, and visual structures. Recent efforts toward finer-grained supervision primarily rely on textual descriptions or localized visual regions as evidence proxies. However, such supervision signals may either overlook complex visual structures or provide incomplete and inaccurate representations of the underlying evidence. To address these limitations, we propose ConceptFormer, a latent concept representation learning framework for visual document retrieval. ConceptFormer models query-relevant evidence as continuous, query-conditioned latent concepts that explicitly bridge localized visual evidence and semantic relevance, without requiring either textual intermediate representations or direct reliance on raw visual annotations. During training, ConceptFormer employs a strong vision-language model to dynamically determine the number of latent concept tokens and uses these concepts as an intermediate representation to bridge the semantic gap between queries and documents, thereby guiding the learning of the embedding space. Experiments on diverse visual document retrieval benchmarks demonstrate that ConceptFormer achieves 16.7\% and 22.1\% relative improvements in average NDCG@10 over the strongest visual retrieval baseline and the strongest OCR-based text retrieval baseline, respectively. Further analysis reveals that latent concepts effectively connect localized visual evidence with semantic relevance, enabling the retriever to capture both fine-grained textual cues and complex document-level visual structures while preserving strong retrieval alignment. Codes and data are available at https://github.com/Neuir/ConceptFormer.

</details>


### [113] [PixelControl: Fine-Grained Condition Fidelity in Text-to-Image Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15705)
*Xin Lin,Haodong Li,Zhifei Zhang,Yutong Yang,Haitian Zheng,Juanxi Tian,Zhe Lin,Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PixelControl is a pixel-space controllable diffusion framework built on a PixelDiT-style backbone that improves fine-grained condition fidelity for text-to-image generation. It avoids the latent bottleneck that weakens high-frequency and small-area condition signals, and introduces Structure-Aware Control Injection and Multi-Scale Pyramid Cycle Loss to strengthen control around boundaries and small regions, achieving better structural fidelity across depth, segmentation, and edge controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VAE-based latent diffusion models often follow global layout of spatial conditions but violate fine-grained structures like object boundaries, thin contours, and medium/small conditioned regions, because spatial compression weakens high-frequency and low-area condition signals.

Method: PixelControl builds on a PixelDiT-style backbone to avoid the latent bottleneck, with two complementary designs: (1) Structure-Aware Control Injection derives a condition structure map to strengthen injected control residuals around spatially sensitive regions; (2) Multi-Scale Pyramid Cycle Loss verifies generated images against condition-derived structures across multiple resolutions. It supports depth, segmentation, and edge through modality-specific control branches with lightweight gated fusion.

Result: Experiments across depth, segmentation, and edge control show PixelControl improves structural fidelity and visual quality over existing controllable generation methods, with especially strong gains on boundaries and medium/small conditioned regions.

Conclusion: PixelControl effectively addresses the fine-grained condition fidelity problem in controllable generation by operating in pixel space and combining structure-aware injection with multi-scale cycle loss, achieving superior performance especially on challenging boundary and small-region cases.

Abstract: Controllable text-to-image diffusion models can often follow the global layout of spatial conditions, yet still violate fine-grained structures such as object boundaries, thin contours, and medium/small conditioned regions. This limitation is especially problematic for VAE-based latent diffusion, where spatial compression can weaken high-frequency and low-area condition signals. We propose PixelControl, a pixel-space controllable diffusion framework for fine-grained condition fidelity. Built on a PixelDiT-style backbone, PixelControl avoids the latent bottleneck and introduces two complementary designs. First, Structure-Aware Control Injection derives a condition structure map and uses it to strengthen injected control residuals around spatially sensitive regions. Second, Multi-Scale Pyramid Cycle Loss verifies generated images against condition-derived structures across multiple resolutions, balancing global layout consistency with local boundary and detail accuracy. PixelControl supports depth, segmentation, edge, and their combinations through modality-specific control branches with lightweight gated fusion. Experiments across depth, segmentation, and edge control show that PixelControl improves structural fidelity and visual quality over existing controllable generation methods, with especially strong gains on boundaries and medium/small conditioned regions. The project page can be found at: https://linxin0.github.io/pixelcontrol_homepage/pixelcontrol-site/

</details>


### [114] [What You Ask is What You Ground: Bridging Question Intent to Temporal Evidence for Grounded VideoQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15708)
*Jinhwan Seo,Kyubeom Han,Jumin Lee,Junhyug Noh,Sung-eui Yoon*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GroundFormer addresses question-invariant grounding in Grounded Video QA by conditioning video features on question intent via learnable communication tokens, using factorized MIL cross-attention and hierarchical contrastive loss, achieving SOTA on NExT-GQA and STAR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify that existing Grounded Video QA models exhibit question-invariant grounding, predicting nearly identical temporal segments regardless of the question. This stems from modality isolation (video representations fixed before question semantics are considered) and weak question injection in the grounding module.

Method: GroundFormer proposes: (1) learnable communication tokens that mediate directed visuo-lingual interaction to condition video features on question intent before localization; (2) factorized MIL cross-attention coupling answer selection with temporal evidence under candidate-level supervision; (3) Gaussian smoothing converts peaked attention into temporally coherent segments; (4) hierarchical multi-modal contrastive loss aligning video, question, and answer embeddings via a two-pass training pipeline.

Result: GroundFormer achieves state-of-the-art performance on NExT-GQA and STAR benchmarks, with substantial improvements in question-discriminative temporal grounding.
