Conclusion: By explicitly conditioning video representations on question semantics and strengthening question injection in the grounding module, GroundFormer effectively resolves the question-invariant grounding failure mode and sets a new SOTA for Grounded VideoQA.

Abstract: We study a critical yet overlooked failure mode in Grounded Video Question Answering: question-invariant grounding, where models predict nearly identical temporal segments for different questions about the same video. We trace this behavior to two structural limitations in prior common designs: (i) modality isolation that fixes video representations before they receive question semantics, and (ii) weak question injection inside the grounding module. To address this, we propose GroundFormer, which conditions video features on question intent before localization via learnable communication tokens that mediate directed visuo-lingual interaction. On top of the question-conditioned features, a factorized MIL cross-attention couples answer selection with temporal evidence under candidate-level supervision, while Gaussian smoothing converts peaked attention into temporally coherent segments. We further introduce a hierarchical multi-modal contrastive loss that aligns video, question, and answer embeddings across a two-pass training pipeline. GroundFormer achieves state-of-the-art grounded VideoQA performance on NExT-GQA and STAR, substantially improving question-discriminative temporal grounding.

</details>


### [115] [Beyond Single Object: Learning 3D Relations with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15710)
*Kohsuke Ide,Ryousuke Yamada,Yue Qiu,Xianzheng Ma,Yoshihiro Fukuhara,Hirokatsu Kataoka,Yutaka Satoh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a framework for fine-grained multi-object 3D reasoning, comprising an instruction dataset (MO3D), a model (Multi-3DLLM) with a Patch-Interaction Transformer for inter-/intra-object modeling, and two application benchmarks (Shape Mating, Change Captioning). Multi-3DLLM surpasses baselines on MO3D and shows positive transfer to single-object tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D-LLMs are limited to single-object/scene description and lack the ability to perform detailed, inter-object comparison and reasoning across multiple 3D objects.

Method: The framework has three components: (1) MO3D, an instruction dataset for fine-grained multi-object comparison; (2) Multi-3DLLM, a model using a minimal Patch-Interaction Transformer (PIT) to capture inter-/intra-object relationships while preserving local geometry; (3) Mini-apps, two application-driven benchmarks (Shape Mating and Change Captioning) probing geometric understanding for practical use.

Result: Recent 3D-LLMs and 2D-VLMs perform poorly on these tasks due to lack of comparison-centric design and geometric awareness. Multi-3DLLM, trained on the proposed mixture data, surpasses all baselines on MO3D and provides positive transfer to single-object classification.

Conclusion: Modeling inter-object relationships explicitly with a minimal patch-interaction design enables strong geometric reasoning in multi-object 3D understanding and also benefits single-object tasks, addressing a fundamental gap in 3D-LLMs.

Abstract: We address a fundamental gap in 3D-LLMs: existing models focus on single-object/scene description, struggling with detailed, inter-object comparison. We propose a framework for detailed object-level reasoning across multiple objects with three components: (1) MO3D (Multi-Object in 3D), an instruction dataset requiring fine-grained multi-object comparison; (2) Multi-3DLLM, using a minimal Patch-Interaction Transformer (PIT) that models inter-/intra-object relationships while preserving local geometry; (3) Mini-apps, two application-driven benchmarks (Shape Mating, Change Captioning) that probe geometric understanding for practical use. Recent 3D-LLMs and 2D-VLMs perform poorly on these tasks, lacking both comparison-centric design and geometric awareness. In contrast, Multi-3DLLM trained on our mixture data learns geometric reasoning, surpasses all baselines on MO3D, and provides positive transfer to single-object classification.

</details>


### [116] [YOLO26-RD: An End-to-End Road Damage Detection Network With Learnable Contrast Enhancement and Edge-Guided Downsampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15713)
*Sompote Youwai,Pawarotorn Chaipetch,Hathairat Samaikul,Theerayut Yonseng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: YOLO26-RD is an NMS-free pavement-distress detector with two lightweight modules (LearnableContrast and EdgeSPD), but a data-first audit on a 7,618-image road dataset falsifies the small-object premise—92% of instances are COCO-large. Removing the P2 detection head (while keeping P2 features in fusion) improves mAP50 by 2.8 points and speeds training by 8%. Best configuration reaches 0.787 mAP50, outperforming a 0.771 stock YOLO26-s baseline, with residual errors attributed to distribution shift, sub-pixel crack width, and label incompleteness—issues no architecture change can fix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors challenge the common assumption that pavement-distress detection is a small-object problem requiring high-resolution P2/4 detection heads. They argue that measurement-driven auditing of the actual data distribution should guide architecture decisions rather than module accretion, and seek to demonstrate this through a data-first audit on real road-survey imagery.

Method: The paper builds YOLO26-RD, an end-to-end NMS-free detector on YOLO26 with two lightweight modules: LearnableContrast (494-parameter differentiable CLAHE analogue for per-tile contrast adaptation) and EdgeSPD (Sobel-gated space-to-depth downsampler adding 2 parameters over SPD-Conv). They perform a data-first audit on a 7,618-image dataset (alligator crack, linear crack, patching) with controlled training recipes, architecture ablations, and failure-mode decomposition.

Result: The audit falsifies the small-object premise: 92% of instances are COCO-large, and linear cracks are extreme-aspect structures (median 10:1) where difficulty is sensitivity rather than localization. Removing the P2 detection level while retaining P2 features in fusion improves mAP50 by 2.8 points and reduces epoch time by 8%. Best configuration achieves 0.787 mAP50 vs. 0.771 stock YOLO26-s baseline, with the largest gain on the rarest class (patching: +2.6 over baseline, +9.4 over unmodified control). Failure-mode analysis attributes residual bottleneck-class error (~0.74 crack mAP) to distribution shift, sub-pixel crack width, and label incompleteness—unfixable by architecture.

Conclusion: For pavement imagery, measurement-driven subtraction (removing unnecessary components based on data analysis) outperforms module accretion (adding components based on assumption). The authors argue that data-first auditing should precede architectural decisions, and release both the audit protocol and the model for reproducibility.

Abstract: Automated pavement-distress detection is commonly framed as a small-object problem, motivating high-resolution P2/4 detection heads and lossless downsampling. We present YOLO26-RD, an end-to-end (NMS-free) detector built on YOLO26 with two lightweight novel modules (LearnableContrast, a 494-parameter differentiable analogue of CLAHE that adapts contrast per tile inside the network, and EdgeSPD, a Sobel-gated space-to-depth downsampler adding only 2 parameters over SPD-Conv), and we subject the design to a data-first audit on a 7,618-image road-survey dataset (alligator crack, linear crack, patching). The audit falsifies the small-object premise: 92% of instances are COCO-large, and linear cracks are extreme-aspect structures (median 10:1) whose difficulty is sensitivity, not localization. Guided by this analysis, we remove the P2 detection level while retaining P2 features in the fusion path, which improves mAP50 by 2.8 points over the full YOLO26-RD model and reduces epoch time by 8%. Trained from scratch at 640x640, our best screening configuration reaches 0.787 mAP50 on the validation split versus a 0.771 project baseline (a stock YOLO26-s of uncontrolled recipe), with the largest per-class gain on the rarest class (patching, 2.6 points over baseline; 9.4 over the unmodified YOLO26-RD control under an identical recipe). A failure-mode decomposition further attributes the residual error of the bottleneck class (crack, approximately 0.74 across all architectures tested) to train/validation distribution shift on crack orientation and length, sub-pixel crack width at 640x640, and label incompleteness, factors no architecture change can address. We argue that for pavement imagery, measurement-driven subtraction outperforms module accretion, and we release our audit protocol alongside the model.

</details>


### [117] [Anatomical and Physical Supervision for CT-less PET Attenuation Correction: BIC-MAC 2026 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15721)
*Petros Chatzitoulousis,George K. Matsopoulos*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper describes a submission to the BIC-MAC 2026 Challenge for CT-less PET attenuation correction using a standard nnU-Net architecture enhanced with anatomical supervision (via frozen TotalSegmentator features), physical supervision (via differentiable attenuation correction factor projection loss), and pretrained weights from SynthRAD MR-to-CT data, achieving effective pseudo-CT synthesis with minimal architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CT-less PET attenuation correction is a challenging task that requires accurate pseudo-CT synthesis from multimodal inputs. The authors aim to improve pseudo-CT quality and downstream PET reconstruction by combining anatomical and physical knowledge with an efficient nnU-Net-based pipeline, rather than relying on major architectural innovations.

Method: The method builds on a standard nnU-Net with: (1) anatomical supervision via a frozen TotalSegmentator feature extractor with anatomy-guided structural constraints and patch sampling; (2) physical supervision via a differentiable attenuation correction factor projection loss using multi-angle attenuation projections; (3) pretrained weights from the SynthRAD Challenge MR-to-CT dataset; and (4) optimizations across the nnU-Net pipeline including preprocessing, plans, and supervision design.

Result: The final submission demonstrates that combining anatomical supervision, attenuation physics, and efficient nnU-Net scaling effectively improves CT-less PET attenuation correction performance.

Conclusion: Anatomical supervision, physical supervision, and pretrained initialization can be effectively integrated into a standard nnU-Net pipeline to achieve strong performance in CT-less PET attenuation correction with minimal architectural modifications.

Abstract: This report describes our submission to the Big Cross-Modal Attenuation Correction (BIC-MAC) 2026 Challenge for CT-less PET attenuation correction through multimodal pseudo-CT synthesis. We build upon a standard nnU-Net architecture and combine anatomical and physical supervision to improve both pseudo-CT quality and downstream PET reconstruction. Anatomical supervision is introduced through a frozen TotalSegmentator feature extractor, anatomy-guided structural constraints and patch sampling, while physical supervision is achieved using a differentiable attenuation correction factor projection loss based on multi-angle attenuation projections. Furthermore, the network is initialized with pretrained weights obtained from training on the SynthRAD Challenge MR-to-CT dataset. Minimal architectural modifications are applied, while performance improvements are pursued across the nnU-Net pipeline, including preprocessing, plans, and supervision design, among other components. Our final submission demonstrates the effectiveness of combining anatomical supervision, attenuation physics, and efficient nnU-Net scaling for CT-less PET attenuation correction.

</details>


### [118] [Identifying Confusion Trends in Concept-based XAI for Multi-Label Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15731)
*Haadia Amjad,Ronald Tetzlaff*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates Concept-based Explainable AI (CXAI) methods (CRP and CRAFT) on multi-label classification using VGG16 and ResNet50 trained on the 20 most annotated MS-COCO labels, revealing three findings: CXAI highlights DNN learning weaknesses, concept distinctiveness reduces confusion, and environmental concepts expose dataset biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DNNs in high-risk domains need to be both accurate and understandable to ensure user trust. Real-world images contain complex backgrounds and heavy annotations, so Concept-based Explainable AI (CXAI) methods need to be assessed for their applicability and problem-solving capacity in multi-label classification tasks.

Method: Two DNNs (VGG16 and ResNet50) are trained on the 20 most annotated labels from MS-COCO dataset. Two CXAI methods, CRP (Concept Relevance Propagation) and CRAFT (Concept Recursive Activation FacTorization), are applied to generate concept-level explanations for multi-label classification, followed by evaluation of their performance.

Result: Three key findings: (1) CXAI highlights learning weaknesses in DNNs, (2) higher concept distinctiveness reduces label and concept confusion, and (3) environmental concepts expose dataset-induced biases.

Conclusion: CXAI has potential to enhance understanding of model generalizability and to diagnose bias instigated by the dataset, making DNNs more interpretable and trustworthy for high-risk applications.

Abstract: Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) deployed in high-risk domains, such as healthcare and autonomous driving, must be not only accurate but also understandable to ensure user trust. In real-world computer vision tasks, these models often operate on complex images containing background noise and are heavily annotated. To make such models explainable, Concept-based Explainable AI (CXAI) methods need to be assessed for their applicability and problem-solving capacity. In this work, we explore CXAI use cases in multi-label classification by training two DNNs, VGG16 and ResNet50, on the 20 most annotated labels in the MS-COCO dataset (Microsoft Common Objects in Context). We apply two CXAI methods, CRP (Concept Relevance Propagation) and CRAFT (Concept Recursive Activation FacTorization), to generate concept-level explanations and investigate the overall evaluations. Our analysis reveals three key findings: (1) CXAI highlights learning weaknesses in DNNs, (2) higher concept distinctiveness reduces label and concept confusion, and (3) environmental concepts expose dataset-induced biases. Our results demonstrate the potential of CXAI to enhance the understanding of model generalizability and to diagnose bias instigated by the dataset.

</details>


### [119] [ES3D: Embedding Semantics into 3D Space for Component-Aware Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15749)
*Xuancheng Jin,Rengan Xie,Jiayuan Lu,Wenting Zheng,Rui Wang,Yuchi Huo,Lincheng Li,Yingfeng Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ES3D is a 3D editing framework that embeds semantic features directly into 3D space, enabling component-aware retrieval and editing of 3D assets using multiple local reference images and optional text queries, producing geometrically consistent and semantically coherent edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D editing methods rely heavily on text-driven editing, which struggles with fine-grained visual changes, often require manual 3D masks, and introduce unintended modifications to untouched regions due to a lack of fine-grained semantic understanding in 3D space.

Method: ES3D constructs a 3D semantic embedding by projecting multi-view semantic features into the voxelized space of the asset, then performs 3D component retrieval via feature similarity between the 3D semantic embedding and image/text query embeddings. Editing is done using a pretrained 3D generative model with an inpainting mechanism to modify retrieved components based on user images while preserving the rest.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that ES3D produces geometrically consistent and semantically coherent edits, enabling robust image-based and text-assisted control for 3D editing.

Conclusion: ES3D provides a novel semantic embedding-based approach for component-aware 3D retrieval and editing that overcomes limitations of text-only editing and manual masking, offering more precise and controllable 3D asset modification.

Abstract: Existing 3D editing methods have made notable progress in controllability, yet they remain limited in several important ways. Most approaches rely on text-driven editing, which struggles to express fine-grained visual changes intended by the user. Moreover, many methods require manually supplied 3D masks or introduce unintended changes to regions that should remain untouched. These limitations largely arise from the absence of fine-grained semantic understanding, making it difficult for existing models to retrieve or modify specific 3D components.
  We introduce ES3D, a framework that embeds semantics directly into 3D space, enabling component-aware retrieval and editing of a 3D asset conditioned on multiple local reference images and optional text queries. We first construct a 3D semantic embedding by projecting multi-view semantic features into the voxelized space of the asset. We then perform 3D component retrieval by computing feature similarity between the 3D semantic embedding and the semantic embeddings of image or text queries. For editing, we employ a pretrained 3D generative model with an inpainting mechanism to modify the retrieved components guided by user-provided images while preserving the rest of the asset. Overall, ES3D is a 3D editing framework that retrieves editable regions based on semantic cues and uses multiple images as conditions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ES3D produces geometrically consistent and semantically coherent edits, enabling robust image-based and text-assisted control for 3D editing.

</details>


### [120] [Beyond Independence: Learning Correlated Views for Variational Incomplete Multi-View Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15757)
*Zheming Xu,Aiyue Tang,Shidi Chen,Xuechao Zou,Congyan Lang,Rogelio A. Mancisidor,Michael Kampffmeyer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a variational framework for incomplete multi-view clustering that goes beyond the standard conditional independence assumption by explicitly modeling learnable cross-view correlations via the covariance structure of posterior estimation errors, parameterized through normalized Cholesky decomposition for positive definiteness and joint training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing imputation-free IMVC methods based on variational inference rely on a conditional independence assumption across views during posterior aggregation, which fails to capture the inherently structured and correlated nature of multi-view data.

Method: A variational framework that models and learns correlations between views by utilizing the covariance structure of posterior estimation errors during aggregation. The correlation matrix is parameterized through normalized Cholesky decomposition to ensure positive definiteness, and the entire model is trained jointly through a unified variational objective.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple IMVC benchmarks show consistent outperformance over state-of-the-art approaches across diverse missing-view settings while introducing only a negligible number of learnable parameters.

Conclusion: Adaptive correlation modeling is effective in variational IMVC, demonstrating the need to go beyond the independence assumption in this setting.

Abstract: Incomplete multi-view clustering (IMVC) aims to uncover shared cluster structures from data with partially observed views. Although recent imputation-free methods based on variational inference demonstrate robustness to missing views, they commonly rely on a conditional independence assumption across views in the posterior aggregation stage, which fails to capture the inherently structured and potentially correlated nature of multi-view data. In this paper, we propose a variational framework that explicitly goes beyond this assumption by introducing a learnable cross-view correlation structure. Specifically, we explicitly model and learn correlations between views by utilizing the covariance structure of posterior estimation errors during aggregation. To facilitate robust and efficient learning, the correlation matrix is parameterized through a normalized Cholesky decomposition, ensuring positive definiteness and enabling the entire model to be trained jointly through a unified variational objective. Extensive experiments on multiple IMVC benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art approaches across diverse missing-view settings while introducing only a negligible number of learnable parameters. These results highlight the effectiveness of adaptive correlation modeling in variational IMVC, demonstrating the need to go beyond the independence assumption in IMVC. The code is available at https://github.com/zmxu196/ACOVA.

</details>


### [121] [RoofGS: Roofline-Guided End-to-End Acceleration of 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15785)
*Yang Luo,Yan Gong,Yongsheng Gao,Jie Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RoofGS is a rendering framework for 3D Gaussian Splatting that uses Roofline analysis to identify distinct hardware bottlenecks (memory-bound front end and compute-bound rasterizer) and applies bottleneck-specific optimizations, achieving a 10.1x end-to-end speedup at 4K resolution with negligible quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting achieves real-time novel-view synthesis but suffers from performance limitations on GPUs, especially at high resolutions. The authors aim to address this by understanding the specific hardware bottlenecks rather than applying generic kernel acceleration.

Method: The paper performs a stage-wise Roofline characterization to identify that the front end is memory-bound (global memory traffic) while the rasterizer is instruction-throughput-limited. Targeted optimizations include: (1) a resolution-adaptive quantized depth sorting key compressed to 32 bits for the memory-bound front end, and (2) a range-aware bit-level fast exponential approximation with per-pixel error bounds for the compute-bound rasterizer. Additional optimizations include kernel fusion, compact attribute storage, culling, and dual-pixel evaluation.

Result: RoofGS achieves a 10.1x end-to-end speedup over 3DGS at 4K resolution on an RTX 4090, boosting throughput from 61 to 616 FPS, with only a 0.028 dB PSNR penalty.

Conclusion: Bottleneck-specific optimizations guided by Roofline analysis are far more effective than generic kernel acceleration for 3DGS rendering, enabling massive speedups at high resolutions with minimal quality degradation.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables real-time novel-view synthesis but remains limited on GPUs at high resolutions. Through a stage-wise Roofline characterization, we identify two distinct hardware bottlenecks: global memory traffic dominates the front end, whereas instruction throughput limits rasterization. Guided by this analysis, we develop RoofGS, a rendering framework that applies bottleneck-specific optimizations rather than generic kernel acceleration. For the memory-bound front end, we design a resolution-adaptive quantized depth sorting key that compresses each key to 32 bits. For the compute-bound rasterizer, we introduce a range-aware bit-level fast exponential approximation tailored to the bounded exponent range after opacity culling, with a derived per-pixel error bound. These two core techniques are complemented by additional optimizations (kernel fusion, compact attribute storage, culling, dual-pixel evaluation) that additionally reduce memory traffic and improve instruction-level parallelism. Experiments show that RoofGS achieves a 10.1$\times$ end-to-end speedup over 3DGS at 4K on an RTX 4090, increasing throughput from 61 to 616 FPS, with only a 0.028 dB PSNR loss.

</details>


### [122] [ChainSpace: A Chained-Reasoning Paradigm for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15788)
*Xiaohan Zhang,Feng Gu,Xudong Rao,Xuhao Pan,Tao Wei,Zhou Pan,Kun Zhan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ChainSpace introduces a chained-reasoning paradigm for spatial intelligence, where spatial questions are organized into state-preserving multi-round sequences with logical consistency. It includes ChainSpace-Bench (a manual multi-round benchmark with a Chain-Aware Metric) and ChainSpace-Pipeline (a simulator-based framework for generating chain-structured supervision data). Experiments show the bench exposes chain-level failures missed by isolated accuracy, and models trained with small amounts of chained data outperform open-source models on the benchmark while transferring well to external benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data-centric spatial reasoning approaches treat questions as independent instances, allowing shortcut-based answering and providing limited supervision for persistent spatial understanding. There is a need for a paradigm that maintains coherent spatial state across interactions with the physical world.

Method: The paper proposes ChainSpace, a chained-reasoning paradigm that structures spatial reasoning as state-preserving multi-round processes where later questions depend on spatial constraints from earlier rounds. It instantiates two components: (1) ChainSpace-Bench, a manually annotated real-world multi-round benchmark with a Chain-Aware Metric, and (2) ChainSpace-Pipeline, a simulator-based chain-structured supervision generation framework for training spatial intelligence models.

Result: ChainSpace-Bench exposes chain-level failures not captured by isolated question accuracy. Models trained by ChainSpace-Pipeline with a relatively small amount of simulator-generated chained data achieve the best performance among open-source models on ChainSpace-Bench and transfer competitively to multiple external spatial intelligence benchmarks.

Conclusion: ChainSpace establishes itself as an effective paradigm for more faithful evaluation and more data-efficient learning of spatial intelligence, demonstrating that chained-reasoning supervision provides benefits beyond those achievable through isolated question-answer training.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence requires foundation models to maintain coherent spatial state across interactions with the physical world. However, existing data-centric approaches typically treat spatial reasoning as independent question-answer instances, enabling shortcut-based answering and providing limited supervision for persistent spatial understanding. To address this, we introduce ChainSpace, a chained-reasoning paradigm that structures spatial reasoning as a state-preserving multi-round process. In this paradigm, spatial questions are organized into logically constrained and jointly consistent chains, where later questions depend on spatial constraints established in earlier rounds. Following this principle, we instantiate ChainSpace-Bench, a manually annotated real-world multi-round benchmark with a Chain-Aware Metric, and ChainSpace-Pipeline, a simulator-based chain-structured supervision generation framework for spatial intelligence training. Experiments show that ChainSpace-Bench exposes chain-level failures that are not captured by isolated question accuracy. Additionally, with a relatively small amount of simulator-generated chained data, models trained by ChainSpace-Pipeline achieve the best performance among open-source models on ChainSpace-Bench and transfer competitively to multiple external spatial intelligence benchmarks. These results establish ChainSpace as an effective paradigm for more faithful evaluation and more data-efficient learning of spatial intelligence.

</details>


### [123] [Emergent 3D Instance Segmentation from Self-Supervised Point Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15796)
*Ted Lentsch,Santiago Montiel-Marín,Holger Caesar,Julian F. P. Kooij*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether a frozen, self-supervised point transformer contains structural information for unsupervised 3D instance segmentation in outdoor LiDAR scans without handcrafted geometric priors. The authors analyze internal representations across three datasets, finding that instance signal concentrates in attention queries/keys (not values/outputs), is bimodal in depth, and is driven by rotary position encoding. They propose TokenGraph3D, a training-free segmenter using connected components on key-similarity graphs that outperforms output-feature baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional unsupervised 3D instance segmentation relies on handcrafted geometric priors like density-based clustering, motion cues, or projected 2D detections. The authors seek to determine whether modern self-supervised point transformers already encode instance-level structural information intrinsically, eliminating the need for such priors.

Method: The paper probes frozen self-supervised point transformer representations across SemanticKITTI, nuScenes, and Waymo Perception datasets. They systematically analyze attention queries, keys, values, and output features to locate instance signals. Based on findings, they propose TokenGraph3D, a training-free segmenter that groups points via connected components on a key-similarity graph without density-based clustering or proximity priors.

Result: Four core insights emerged: (1) instance signal concentrates in attention queries and keys rather than values or output features; (2) output features semantically collapse, merging adjacent same-class objects; (3) the instance signal is bimodal in depth, strongest at shallowest and deepest encoder stages; (4) rotary position encoding (RoPE) drives the signal, with its removal collapsing the advantage. TokenGraph3D substantially outperforms output-feature baselines under identical prior-free conditions.

Conclusion: Frozen self-supervised point transformers do contain emergent 3D instance structure, primarily within attention queries/keys and driven by rotary position encoding. TokenGraph3D demonstrates this structure can be leveraged for effective unsupervised instance segmentation without any handcrafted geometric priors.

Abstract: Unsupervised 3D instance segmentation of outdoor LiDAR scans has traditionally relied on handcrafted geometric priors such as density-based clustering, motion cues, or projected 2D detections. In this work, we investigate whether a frozen, self-supervised point transformer already contains the structural information required to isolate object instances without any handcrafted geometric prior. Using this transformer purely as a feature extractor, we probe its internal representations across the SemanticKITTI, nuScenes, and Waymo Perception datasets. Our analysis yields four core insights: (1) the instance signal concentrates in the attention queries and keys rather than in the values or final output features; (2) output features semantically collapse, merging adjacent same-class objects that the queries and keys keep distinct; (3) this instance signal is bimodal in depth, strongest at the shallowest and deepest encoder stages; and (4) this signal is driven predominantly by the rotary position encoding (RoPE), whose removal collapses its advantage. We put these findings into our method TokenGraph3D, a training-free segmenter that groups points via connected components on a key-similarity graph, using neither density-based clustering nor proximity priors. Under identical prior-free conditions, we substantially outperform output-feature baselines, making the emergent 3D instance structure visible.

</details>


### [124] [PWLR: Pairwise Witness Local Rejection for Boundary-Aware Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15802)
*Chengyao Jia,Ruixuan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PWLR is an OOD detection method that uses an MLLM to generate pairwise local visual cues distinguishing confusable ID classes, filters these cues on ID-only data, and combines pairwise local evidence with global class scores at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-OOD detection is challenging because OOD samples can lie close to ID class boundaries. Existing vision-language detectors use class semantics or LLM-generated concepts but fail to leverage language as explicit boundary evidence to distinguish confusing ID classes.

Method: PWLR operates in three stages: (1) offline generation of pairwise local cue phrases via an MLLM that describe visual evidence favoring one ID class over a rival; (2) screening/filtering these cues using ID-only data under a frozen vision-language backbone to keep only reliable local verifiers; (3) at inference, retaining globally plausible classes, checking local support against relevant rivals, and calibrating the combination of pairwise local evidence with global class scores.

Result: Experiments on ImageNet-100 far-OOD, cleaner/challenging OOD, and near-OOD benchmarks show PWLR consistently improves strong vision-language baselines across multiple backbones.

Conclusion: Using language as explicit pairwise boundary evidence is an effective and consistent way to improve OOD detection over strong vision-language baselines, especially for near-OOD scenarios.

Abstract: Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection remains challenging for image classifiers, especially when near-OOD samples lie close to in-distribution (ID) class boundaries. Recent vision-language detectors improve OOD detection through class semantics, local prompting, or LLM-generated outlier concepts, but seldom use language as explicit boundary evidence between confusing ID classes. We propose Pairwise Witness Local Rejection (PWLR), which uses an MLLM offline to describe visible local cues that favor one ID class over a specific rival class. These cue phrases are then screened with ID-only data under a frozen vision-language backbone, so that only reliable local verifiers are kept. At inference, PWLR first retains a small set of globally plausible classes, then checks whether any of them is locally supported against its most relevant rivals, and finally combines this pairwise local evidence with the global class score through calibration. Experiments on ImageNet-100 far-OOD, cleaner/challenging OOD and near-OOD benchmarks show that PWLR consistently improves strong vision-language baselines across multiple backbones. Source code will be released.

</details>


### [125] [From Generation to Matching: A Development Report on Personalized Chinese Handwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15812)
*Yiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper documents a frozen engineering project on personalized Chinese handwriting that began as few-shot generation of unseen characters but was reset after repeated conflicts between structural pressure and personalization. The project pivoted from synthesis to character-wise matching using a multi-writer CASIA candidate pool, finding real-human candidates for all 197 USER characters and achieving 100/100 coverage on the evaluation subset, with outputs judged as stable practical B-level quality approaching A-level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The initial goal was few-shot generation of unseen Chinese characters from ~200 real handwriting images of one user covering 197 unique characters. However, the canonical-centered personalization routes consistently failed due to a fundamental tension: increasing structural pressure made outputs more canonical while increasing personalization risked damaging identity-defining strokes. This motivated a fundamental rethink of the problem formulation.

Method: The project was reset around real-human character equivalence classes. It used a multi-writer CASIA candidate pool to find USER-compatible realizations, changing the task from synthesis to character-wise matching followed by cross-writer composition into a virtual writer. The system employs real-ink features, character-specific human population percentiles, top-20 candidate pruning, and greedy hardest-first whole-row selection.

Result: All 197 USER characters had real-human candidates, the 100-character evaluation subset was covered 100/100, and held-out comparisons included a row judged visually almost indistinguishable from genuine USER handwriting. A 60-episode stability audit placed all episodes in a predefined A-like machine-proxy region (though not independent human A-level judgments). The final evidence supports stable practical B-level quality with many outputs approaching A-level.

Conclusion: The project demonstrates that generation became unnecessary for this specific case, as matching existing real-human handwriting proved sufficient and superior to synthesis. The report limits its claims to this particular case and explicitly does not claim unrestricted or universal handwriting synthesis capabilities.

Abstract: This paper documents a frozen engineering project on personalized Chinese handwriting. The project started from approximately 200 real handwriting images from one user, covering 197 unique Chinese characters, and was initially formulated as few-shot generation of unseen characters. A sequence of canonical-centered personalization routes repeatedly exposed the same conflict: increasing structural pressure made outputs more canonical, while increasing personalization could damage identity-defining strokes. The project was therefore reset around real-human character equivalence classes. A multi-writer CASIA candidate pool showed that a USER-compatible realization often already existed among valid human samples. The task consequently changed from synthesis to character-wise matching, followed by cross-writer composition into a virtual writer. The frozen system uses real-ink features, character-specific human population percentiles, top-20 candidate pruning, and greedy hardest-first whole-row selection. On the covered target set, all 197 USER characters had real-human candidates, and the 100-character evaluation subset was covered 100/100. Knowncharacter held-out comparisons included a row judged visually almost indistinguishable from genuine USER handwriting. A 60- episode stability audit placed every episode in a predefined A-like machine-proxy region, but these were not independent human A-level judgments. The final evidence supports stable practical B-level quality, with many outputs approaching A-level under the USER-defined criterion. The report records why generation became unnecessary for this case without claiming unrestricted or universal handwriting synthesis.

</details>


### [126] [FlowDance: Music-Driven Dance Video Generation with Parallel Pose and RGB Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15818)
*Genying Li,Boda Lin,Jiachen Li,Zijian Jia,Haojie Zheng,Yiming Wang,Shuchen Weng,Si Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FlowDance is a music-driven dance video generation framework that integrates explicit motion modeling with reference-preserving visual synthesis via parallel pose and RGB streams, introducing timestep-aware pose injection and persistent identity injection. It also contributes a popularity-curated, high-resolution in-the-wild dance video dataset with synchronized multi-modal annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Music-driven dance video synthesis is challenging because it requires joint learning of music-to-motion correspondence, identity-preserving human animation, temporal coherence, and visually realistic video generation—all in one unified framework.

Method: FlowDance employs a parallel pose and RGB stream architecture that separates explicit motion modeling from reference-preserving visual synthesis. It introduces timestep-aware pose injection to adapt structural guidance across denoising steps and persistent identity injection to preserve reference appearance over long videos. A new popularity-curated, high-resolution in-the-wild dance video dataset with synchronized music, RGB, 3D body motion, camera parameters, and 2D pose annotations is also built.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that FlowDance achieves strong performance in both dance motion generation and music-driven dance video synthesis tasks.

Conclusion: FlowDance provides an effective framework for music-driven dance video generation by decoupling motion and appearance modeling, supported by a rich multi-modal dance dataset, yielding state-of-the-art results across motion and video synthesis benchmarks.

Abstract: Music-driven dance video synthesis aims to animate a reference person according to a given music clip. The task is challenging because it requires a model to jointly learn music-to-motion correspondence, identity-preserving human animation, temporal coherence, and visually realistic video generation. We present FlowDance, a music-driven dance video generation framework that integrates explicit motion modeling with reference-preserving visual synthesis through parallel pose and RGB streams. We further introduce timestep-aware pose injection to adapt structural guidance across denoising steps and persistent identity injection to preserve the reference appearance over long video. To support this task, we further build a popularity-curated, high-resolution in-the-wild dance video dataset with synchronized music, RGB videos, 3D body motion, camera parameters, and projected 2D pose annotations. Extensive experiments show that FlowDance achieves strong performance in both dance motion generation and music-driven dance video synthesis.

</details>


### [127] [MITE-Net: SWaP-Optimized 4K Video Tiny Target Perception for Embodied Edge SAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15830)
*Mingshuo Xu,Mu Hua,Jigen Peng,Qi Wang,Shigang Yue*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MITE-Net is a SWaP-optimized cascaded architecture for real-time tiny target perception in high-resolution SAR imagery, achieving 100% search success at 30.33 FPS on 4K maritime data using only 3.19W power on edge devices, while also introducing new standardized datasets (SAR-Tiny) and benchmarking against YOLO models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time tiny target perception in high-resolution imagery is critical for embodied Search-and-Rescue missions, but edge device constraints create a bottleneck: traditional downsampling causes severe feature loss while slice-based processing incurs prohibitive latency. There is a gap between the need for efficient processing and existing approaches.

Method: The paper introduces a three-part framework: (1) MITE-Net, a cascaded architecture combining a bio-inspired, learning-free TTM-RPN with a sub-0.14M-parameter R-CNN-like head; (2) SAR-Tiny Datasets constructed by relabeling SeaDroneSee-Tiny (maritime, 64-256px targets) and UAVID-Tiny (urban, <64px targets); (3) hardware-level benchmarks on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier edge device.

Result: MITE-Net processes 4K maritime imagery directly, achieving 100% search success rate at 30.33 FPS consuming only 3.19W (9.51 FPS/W), vastly outperforming YOLO baselines in target recall and energy efficiency. However, UAVID-Tiny evaluations reveal limitations: the learning-free bionic front-end struggles with urban backgrounds, and the ultra-lightweight head lacks capacity for complex features.

Conclusion: The work delivers an efficient onboard perception paradigm and a rigorous baseline for end-to-end SAR architectures, while also identifying the structural limitations of the current approach in urban/cluttered scenarios to guide future improvements.

Abstract: Real-time tiny target perception in high-resolution imagery is critical for embodied Search-and-Rescue (SAR) missions. However, strict Size, Weight, and Power (SWaP) constraints on edge devices like UAVs create a bottleneck: traditional image downsampling causes severe feature loss, while slice-based processing incurs prohibitive latency. To address this gap, this paper introduces a comprehensive framework encompassing a novel architecture, specialized datasets, and hardware-level benchmarks. First, we propose MITE-Net, a SWaP-optimized cascaded architecture, which couples a bio-inspired, learning-free Tiny Target Motion-Based Region Proposal Network (TTM-RPN) with a sub-0.14M-parameter R-CNN-like head. Second, to standardize 4K tiny target evaluation, we construct the SAR-Tiny Datasets by relabeling two challenging UAV datasets: SeaDroneSee-Tiny (dynamic maritime scenes, tiny targets predominantly of 64-256 pixels ) and UAVID-Tiny (cluttered urban scenes, extremely tiny targets, less than 64 pixels). Third, we benchmark against state-of-the-art YOLO models on an edge device, NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier, where MITE-Net directly processes 4K maritime imagery, achieving a 100\% search success rate at 30.33 FPS. Consuming merely 3.19 W (9.51 FPS/W), MITE-Net vastly outperforms YOLO baselines in target recall and energy efficiency. Conversely, UAVID-Tiny evaluations expose a compound structural limitation: the learning-free bionic front-end struggles against urban backgrounds, while the ultra-lightweight head lacks representational capacity for complex features. Ultimately, this work delivers an efficient onboard perception paradigm and a rigorous baseline guiding future end-to-end SAR architectures.

</details>


### [128] [CardiacMamba: Fair and Robust RGB-RF Fusion for Remote Heart Rate Estimation via State Space Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15831)
*Bo Zhao,Zheng Wu,Yiping Xie,Zitong YU*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CardiacMamba is a fair and robust RGB-RF fusion framework for remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) heart rate monitoring, using state space modeling to integrate optical facial cues with radio-frequency cardiac motion signals. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on EquiPleth with 0.96 bpm MAE and significantly reduces skin-tone bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RGB-only rPPG methods are vulnerable to illumination changes, motion artifacts, and skin-tone-dependent optical reflectance, which limits accuracy and fairness in heart rate monitoring from facial videos.

Method: CardiacMamba introduces three key components: (1) a Temporal Difference Mamba Module (TDMM) to enhance subtle RF temporal variations, (2) a bidirectional SSM-based interaction mechanism to align heterogeneous RGB-RF dynamics, and (3) a Channel-wise Fast Fourier Transform (CFFT) module for channel-domain spectral refinement.

Result: On the EquiPleth dataset, CardiacMamba achieves 0.96 bpm MAE, 3.06 bpm RMSE, and 0.97 Pearson correlation. It reduces the light-dark skin-tone MAE gap to 0.26 bpm and maintains robustness under RGB degradation and RF-missing conditions.

Conclusion: CardiacMamba demonstrates that fusing RGB and RF modalities via state space modeling can simultaneously improve accuracy, fairness across skin tones, and robustness to sensor degradation in rPPG-based heart rate monitoring.

Abstract: Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables non-contact heart rate (HR) monitoring from facial videos, but RGB-only methods are vulnerable to illumination changes, motion artifacts, and skin-tone-dependent optical reflectance. We propose CardiacMamba, a fair and robust RGB-RF fusion framework that integrates optical facial cues and radio-frequency cardiac motion cues through state space modeling. CardiacMamba introduces a Temporal Difference Mamba Module (TDMM) to enhance subtle RF temporal variations, a bidirectional SSM-based interaction mechanism to align heterogeneous RGB-RF dynamics, and a Channel-wise Fast Fourier Transform (CFFT) module for channel-domain spectral refinement. On the EquiPleth dataset, CardiacMamba achieves state-of-the-art performance with 0.96 bpm MAE, 3.06 bpm RMSE, and 0.97 Pearson correlation, while reducing the observed light-dark skin-tone MAE gap to 0.26 bpm and maintaining robustness under RGB degradation and RF-missing conditions

</details>


### [129] [Beyond Visual CoT: Internalized Visual Thinking for Proactive Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15869)
*Xiaoyu Zhu,Xinke Deng,Suresh Taddewadikar,Arnab Kumar Mondal,Zhongyu Jiang,Ian Fasel,Joerg Liebelt*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Internalized Visual Thinking (IVT), a post-training framework that enables multimodal LLMs to predict latent future-frame representations during training while answering directly at inference, eliminating the need for explicit visual chain-of-thought generation. IVT improves accuracy over direct-answer fine-tuning across all six evaluation settings and matches or beats explicit Visual CoT while reducing end-to-end latency by over 5x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual chain-of-thought (Visual CoT) improves reasoning about spatial, temporal, and embodied environments but incurs substantial inference overhead from generating intermediate reasoning images. This is particularly problematic for proactive video reasoning, motivating the question of whether models can internalize visual thinking during training while reasoning directly at inference.

Method: IVT is a post-training framework that jointly optimizes textual prediction and next-embedding prediction over unlabeled videos. Given a partially observed video, IVT learns to predict latent representations of future frames alongside the target textual answer, capturing motion, object transitions, interactions, and latent intent. At inference, it generates answers directly without synthesizing or re-encoding future frames. The paper conducts controlled studies across target representations, decoder designs, prediction horizons, data mixtures, training curricula, and predictive objectives.

Result: IVT improves over direct-answer fine-tuning on all six evaluation settings while retaining the same inference pathway. Compared with explicit Visual CoT, IVT achieves comparable or better performance and reduces average end-to-end latency by more than 5x.

Conclusion: Explicit pixel-space generation at inference time (visual chain-of-thought) may not be necessary for effective proactive video reasoning. Predictive world modeling can be internalized during training to produce multimodal reasoners that are both more accurate and substantially more efficient.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models increasingly use visual chain-of-thought (Visual CoT) to reason about spatial, temporal, and embodied environments. By generating intermediate reasoning images, Visual CoT provides an intuitive mechanism for visual foresight but introduces substantial inference overhead, which is particularly problematic for proactive video reasoning. We ask whether models can learn to think visually during training while reasoning directly at inference. We introduce Internalized Visual Thinking (IVT), a post-training framework that jointly optimizes textual prediction and next-embedding prediction over unlabeled videos. Given a partially observed video, IVT predicts latent representations of future frames together with the target textual answer, encouraging the model to capture motion, object transitions, interactions, and latent intent. At inference, IVT generates the answer directly without synthesizing or re-encoding future frames. We conduct controlled studies across target representations, decoder designs, prediction horizons, data mixtures, training curricula, and predictive objectives. IVT improves over direct-answer fine-tuning on all six evaluation settings while retaining the same inference pathway. Compared with explicit Visual CoT, IVT achieves comparable or better performance and reduces average end-to-end latency by more than 5x. Together, our findings suggest that explicit pixel-space generation at inference time, as used in visual chain-of-thought, may not be necessary for effective proactive video reasoning. Predictive world modeling can be internalized during training to produce multimodal reasoners that are both more accurate and substantially more efficient.

</details>


### [130] [CLARA: Clip-Level Multimodal Alignment with VLM-Derived Rationales for Hateful Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15905)
*Yuchen Zhang,Shuang Dai,Zeyu Fu,Yunfei Long,Ravi Shekhar,Haralambos Mouratidis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CLARA is a clip-level multimodal framework for hateful video detection that models videos as sequences of fine-grained clips rather than single instances, using a Mixture-of-Experts clip encoder, local-global segment contrastive learning, and VLM-derived rationales to capture temporally localized hateful signals. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods on three hateful video datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hateful video detection is underexplored and challenging because hateful meaning often emerges from complex interactions among speech, audio, and visual cues that are brief, implicit, and temporally dependent, making conventional video-level representations insufficient.

Method: CLARA models videos as sequences of fine-grained clips instead of single instances. It employs (1) a Mixture-of-Experts clip encoder for adaptive multimodal alignment, (2) a local-global segment contrastive objective to jointly model short-term cues and long-range temporal dependencies, and (3) VLM-derived rationales integrated via a gated Transformer for high-level semantic guidance.

Result: CLARA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods on three hateful video datasets. Ablation studies and parameter analyses validate the effectiveness of each proposed component.

Conclusion: Clip-level multimodal modeling with temporally localized representations and VLM-guided semantic reasoning is an effective approach for hateful video detection, addressing the limitations of conventional video-level representations.

Abstract: Hateful video detection has become increasingly important with the rapid growth of video-centric social media platforms, given the serious risks that hate speech poses to both individual well-being and social cohesion. Compared with text or static multimodal content, hateful video detection remains underexplored and significantly more challenging, as hateful meaning often arises from complex interactions among multimodal cues, including speech, audio, and visual content. Moreover, such signals are often brief, implicit, and temporally dependent, making them difficult to capture using conventional video-level representations. In this work, we propose CLARA, a clip-level multimodal framework for hateful video detection. Instead of treating a video as a single instance, CLARA models it as a sequence of fine-grained clips, enabling more precise capture of temporally localized hateful signals. We introduce a Mixture-of-Experts clip encoder for adaptive multimodal alignment, a local-global segment contrastive objective to jointly model short-term cues and long-range temporal dependencies, and VLM-derived rationales integrated via a gated Transformer to provide high-level semantic guidance. Extensive experiments on three hateful video datasets demonstrate that CLARA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Further ablation studies and parameter analyses validate the effectiveness of each component.

</details>


### [131] [Comprehensive Benchmarking of Deep Learning Architectures for Lung Cancer Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15915)
*Hadi Hasan,Safaa Salman,Lama Sleem,Ralph Mouawad,Ali Chehab*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study presents a two-stage deep learning framework for histopathological image analysis, combining multi-class tissue classification and pixel-level region segmentation. YOLO11 achieves the best classification accuracy (98.38%) on lung tissue, while DeepLabV3+ excels in segmentation (IoU 0.80), and YOLO11-seg offers comparable performance with 14x fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related mortality, yet histopathological diagnosis suffers from inter-observer variability and heavy manual workload. Although deep learning shows promise in computational pathology, there is a lack of comprehensive benchmarks integrating both tissue classification and region segmentation within a unified analytical framework.

Method: A two-stage deep learning framework is proposed. Stage 1 evaluates six models (custom CNN, VGG16, DenseNet, MobileNetV3, custom Vision Transformer, YOLO11) for lung tissue classification using 39,000 images from LC25000 and LungHist700 datasets. Stage 2 evaluates U-Net, ResNet-encoder U-Net, DeepLabV3+, and YOLO11-seg for gland region segmentation using the GlaS benchmark. Best models from each stage are integrated into an end-to-end framework.

Result: YOLO11 achieves the best classification performance with 98.38% accuracy, 98.21 +/- 0.35% five-fold cross-validation accuracy, and a macro F1-score of 0.98. DeepLabV3+ achieves the highest segmentation IoU of 0.80 and Dice score of 0.89, while YOLO11-seg achieves comparable IoU of 0.79 with approximately 14x fewer parameters.

Conclusion: The integrated end-to-end framework combining the best-performing classification and segmentation models provides an accurate, computationally efficient, and reproducible baseline for automated histopathological image analysis.

Abstract: Lung cancer remains the leading cause of cancer-related mortality worldwide, while histopathological diagnosis is often affected by inter-observer variability and the substantial workload associated with manual slide examination. Although deep learning has shown considerable potential in computational pathology, comprehensive benchmarks that integrate tissue classification and region segmentation within a unified analytical framework remain limited. This study presents a two-stage deep learning framework for multi-class tissue classification and pixel-level histopathological region segmentation, accompanied by a systematic comparison of state-of-the-art architectures at each stage. For tissue classification, six models, a custom convolutional neural network, VGG16, DenseNet, MobileNetV3, a custom Vision Transformer, and YOLO11, are evaluated on a combined dataset of 39,000 images derived from LC25000 and LungHist700. The models distinguish between adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and normal lung tissue. YOLO11 achieves the best classification performance, with an accuracy of 98.38%, a five-fold cross-validation accuracy of 98.21 +/- 0.35%, and a macro F1-score of 0.98. For region segmentation, U-Net, ResNet-encoder U-Net, DeepLabV3+, and YOLO11-seg are evaluated using the GlaS gland segmentation benchmark. DeepLabV3+ obtains the highest Intersection over Union of 0.80 and a Dice score of 0.89, while YOLO11-seg achieves a comparable Intersection over Union of 0.79 using approximately 14x fewer parameters. The best-performing classification and segmentation models are subsequently integrated into an end-to-end framework, providing an accurate, computationally efficient, and reproducible baseline for automated histopathological image analysis.

</details>


### [132] [BagShift: Measuring How Patch Selection Changes the Evidence Seen by Whole-Slide MIL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15970)
*Ruicheng Yuan,Zhenxuan Zhang,Liwei Hu,Anbang Wang,Haijie Xu,Jiawei Luo,Guang Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BagShift is a paired protocol that systematically changes the patch selector for the same whole-slide image while holding features and predictor fixed, revealing that patch count alone does not guarantee equivalent observed evidence. Deployment-style selector changes (e.g., tissue-wide vs. localized sampling) significantly degrade MIL performance on PANDA and CAMELYON16, and unioning repeated local observations before a single nonlinear MIL pass outperforms averaging regional predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whole-slide MIL systems observe only the patches admitted by their selector, yet deployment can alter this selector (via compute limits, tissue masking, or regional workflows) even when the patch count remains unchanged. The field lacks a controlled protocol to isolate how selector changes—rather than case mix—affect performance, and it remains unclear whether patch count is a sufficient descriptor of what the model actually sees.

Method: The authors introduce BagShift, a paired experimental protocol that changes the patch selector for the same case while keeping features and predictor fixed, thereby isolating selector response from case-mix effects. They compare equal 128-patch budgets under different sampling strategies (tissue-wide vs. localized/coordinate-concentrated), evaluate on PANDA (prostate grading), CAMELYON16 (breast metastasis detection), and an external lung subtyping task, and additionally test aggregation strategies by unioning repeated localized observations before a single nonlinear MIL pass versus averaging regional predictions.

Result: On PANDA, tissue-wide vs. localized sampling reduce quadratic weighted kappa by 1.57 and 17.96 points (×100 scale), respectively. On CAMELYON16, localized views retain tumor in only 10.0% of micrometastatic observations, and matched exposure does not consistently recover the loss. The same stressor causes a much smaller response on external lung subtyping, though relative coverage differences make cross-task severity descriptive. Unioning patches from repeated localized observations before one nonlinear MIL pass improves PANDA QWK by 7.87 points over averaging regional predictions.

Conclusion: Patch count specifies computation, not observed evidence. Deployment evaluations should report both what a selector preserves (coverage/content) and how repeated observations are aggregated, as these factors—not merely patch budget—determine model performance and robustness to selector shifts.

Abstract: Whole-slide multiple-instance learning (MIL) observes only the patches admitted by its selector. Deployment can alter this selector through compute limits, tissue masking, or regional workflows, even when the patch count is unchanged. We introduce BagShift, a paired protocol that changes the selector for the same case while holding its features and predictor fixed, thereby isolating selector response from case mix. With equal 128-patch budgets, sampling across the tissue or concentrating around one coordinate exposes markedly different evidence: on PANDA, the two views reduce quadratic weighted kappa by 1.57 and 17.96 points, respectively (QWK reported on the $\times100$ scale). On CAMELYON16, lesion annotations withheld from model development show that localized views retain tumor in only 10.0\% of micrometastatic observations, and matched exposure does not consistently recover the loss. The same fixed-count stressor produces a much smaller response on external lung subtyping, although differences in relative coverage make cross-task severity descriptive. When repeated localized observations are available, unioning their patches before one nonlinear MIL pass improves PANDA QWK by 7.87 points over averaging regional predictions. Patch count specifies computation, not observed evidence; deployment evaluations should report both what a selector preserves and how repeated observations are aggregated.

</details>


### [133] [CM-MAE: A Physics-Guided Cross-Modal Self-Supervised Learning Framework for Vision-Wireless Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15972)
*Yubo Zhang,Yiyao Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CM-MAE is a self-supervised vision-wireless pretraining framework that uses a soft contrastive alignment loss and masked joint decoding to learn transferable cross-scenario representations from synchronized camera frames and 64-beam received-power vectors (DeepSense 6G), improving linear-probe transfer from 24.88% to 29.49% and achieving 77.38–78.69% Top-1 accuracy on unseen scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synchronized camera and wireless measurements observe the same scene through different physical channels, but representations learned in one deployment can fail when viewpoint, traffic, illumination, and propagation geometry change. The core challenge is achieving cross-scenario representation transfer without relying on expensive ground-truth signals.

Method: CM-MAE uses a soft contrastive alignment loss that builds a target distribution from beam-power profile similarities rather than treating only the synchronized pair as positive (avoiding false negatives), plus a masked joint decoder that reconstructs hidden visual patches and wireless angular clusters under modality dropout. A differential-rate fine-tuning rule adapts the fusion head quickly while keeping encoders slow.

Result: Under a sequence-disjoint DeepSense 6G protocol, soft alignment loss improves matched linear-probe transfer average from 24.88% to 29.49%. Mild fusion fine-tuning reaches 77.38% Top-1 accuracy on unseen Scenarios 6–8, and optional transductive normalization adaptation reaches 78.69%.

Conclusion: The results serve as representation-transfer diagnostics rather than proactive beam-prediction or reduced-sweeping claims, since the fusion setting uses contemporaneous 64-beam power vectors at inference.

Abstract: Synchronized camera and wireless measurements observe the same scene through different physical channels. The central difficulty is that a representation learned in one deployment can fail when viewpoint, traffic, illumination, and propagation geometry change. This paper presents CM-MAE, a self-supervised vision--wireless pretraining framework for cross-scenario representation transfer. The evaluated real-data model uses only RGB frames and the measured 64-beam received-power vector available in DeepSense 6G; it does not use ray-traced paths, calibrated depth, or beam-index labels during pretraining. Its central pretraining term is a \emph{soft contrastive alignment loss}. Instead of making the synchronized image--wireless pair the only positive pair, this loss builds a target distribution from similarities between measured beam-power profiles, so nonidentical samples with similar directional responses are not forced apart as false negatives. A masked joint decoder provides the complementary local objective by reconstructing hidden visual patches and wireless angular clusters under modality dropout. After pretraining, a differential-rate fine-tuning rule lets a new fusion head adapt quickly while the encoders move slowly. Under a sequence-disjoint DeepSense 6G protocol, adding the soft alignment loss improves a matched linear-probe transfer average from 24.88\% to 29.49\%. Mild fusion fine-tuning reaches 77.38\% Top-1 accuracy on unseen Scenarios 6--8, and optional transductive normalization adaptation reaches 78.69\%. Since the fusion setting uses the contemporaneous 64-beam power vector at inference, these results should be read as representation-transfer diagnostics, not as proactive beam-prediction or reduced-sweeping claims.

</details>


### [134] [A Plug-and-Play 2D Motion Interface for Real-World Motion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15984)
*Kaname Yokoyama,Norimichi Ukita*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a plug-and-play 2D Motion Interface that allows 3D-pretrained Motion Language Models (MoLMs) to accept 2D motion inputs without retraining, achieving comparable performance to 3D inputs and proving more practical for real-world monocular video applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoLMs typically rely on 3D motion data, but obtaining accurate 3D motions from monocular videos is challenging and limits their real-world applicability. The authors aim to make MoLMs usable with more easily obtainable 2D motion inputs.

Method: The authors propose a plug-and-play 2D Motion Interface that enables 3D-pretrained MoLMs to accept 2D motion inputs without modifying or fine-tuning the original models. They also introduce a real-video adapter and construct a monocular real-world video motion evaluation dataset.

Result: The method achieves performance comparable to 3D motion inputs across multiple MoLMs on public datasets and outperforms training MoLMs from scratch on 2D motions. It also demonstrates the usefulness of 2D motions over 3D motions under real-world monocular pose-estimation settings.

Conclusion: 2D motion provides a practical interface for deploying MoLMs in real-world motion understanding settings, offering a viable alternative to 3D motion when 3D estimation is unreliable.

Abstract: Motion Language Models (MoLMs) typically understand human motions by tokenizing 3D motion and processing the resulting tokens using a language model. However, obtaining accurate 3D motions from monocular videos is challenging, limiting their real-world applicability. To address this issue, we introduce a plug-and-play 2D Motion Interface that enables 3D-pretrained MoLMs to accept 2D motion inputs without modifying or fine-tuning the original models.
  Experiments on public datasets show that our method achieves performance comparable to 3D motion inputs across multiple MoLMs and outperforms training MoLMs from scratch on 2D motions. We further construct a monocular real-world video motion evaluation dataset and introduce a real-video adapter, demonstrating the usefulness of 2D motions over 3D motions under the evaluated monocular pose-estimation setting. These results suggest that 2D motion provides a practical interface for deploying MoLMs in real-world motion understanding settings. Code is available at https://github.com/irajisamurai/2D-Motion-Interface.

</details>


### [135] [Spatial Temporal Synergy: Balancing Change and Invariance in Text Driven 3D Human Motion Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16008)
*Shaohui Lin,Zhenwu Shi,Jingyu Gong,Jiao Xie,Yu Zhou,Baochang Zhang,Lizhuang Ma,Chia-Wen Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CIME is a unified framework for text-driven human motion editing that decouples change and invariance into spatial pose and temporal rhythm dimensions, incorporating omni-supervised positive-negative learning for spatial poses and a Riemannian Non-uniform Integral Manifold Mapping module for temporal rhythms, achieving state-of-the-art results on MotionFix and STANCE Adjustment datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based human motion editing methods struggle to balance text-responsive "change" and inertial "invariance" due to reliance on coarse spatial constraints and rigid uniform time assumptions, leading to spatial distortions and destruction of intrinsic physical rhythms in variable-length editing.

Method: CIME comprehensively decouples change and invariance into spatial pose and temporal rhythm dimensions. For spatial poses, it integrates an omni-supervised positive-negative learning mechanism with hierarchical retrospective feature supervision, subtle motion preservation, and triplet-based semantic alignment. For temporal rhythms, it introduces the Riemannian Non-uniform Integral Manifold Mapping (RNIMM) module for kinematics-aware non-uniform timestamp-based high-fidelity beat reproduction.

Result: Extensive experiments on MotionFix and STANCE Adjustment datasets demonstrate that CIME achieves state-of-the-art performance in editing alignment and structural fidelity.

Conclusion: The unified CIME architecture effectively handles both spatial and temporal editing challenges, validating its effectiveness for text-driven human motion editing, with source code and models publicly released.

Abstract: Text-driven human motion editing aims to modify existing motion sequences according to natural language instructions while maintaining the structural consistency of the original motion. Existing diffusion-based approaches struggle to balance text-responsive "change" and inertial "invariance". They often rely on coarse spatial constraints and rigid uniform time assumptions, leading to spatial motion distortions and the destruction of intrinsic physical rhythms during variable-length editing. To handle these challenges, we propose Change and Invariance Motion Editing (CIME), a unified framework that comprehensively decouples change and invariance into spatial pose and temporal rhythm dimensions. For spatial poses, our method integrates an omni-supervised positive-negative learning mechanism comprising hierarchical retrospective feature supervision, subtle motion preservation, and triplet-based semantic alignment. For temporal rhythms, we introduce the Riemannian Non-uniform Integral Manifold Mapping (RNIMM) module, which achieves high-fidelity reproduction of physical beats in the edited text via kinematics-aware non-uniform timestamps. Extensive experiments on the MotionFix and STANCE Adjustment datasets demonstrate that CIME achieves state-of-the-art performance in editing alignment and structural fidelity, validating the effectiveness of our unified architecture. Our source codes and models have been released at: github.com/ZhenwuShi/CIME.git

</details>


### [136] [Depth-guided Multi-view Exposure Bracketing for HDR Robot Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16014)
*Jinnyeong Kim,Juhyung Choi,Woohyeok Kim,Sunghyun Cho,Seung-Hwan Baek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a large-scale HDR dataset for multi-sensor robotic systems and proposes DMEB, a single-shot HDR method using depth-guided multi-view exposure bracketing across cameras with drastically different exposures, fused via depth-guided confidence-aware fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable single-shot HDR imaging under extreme illumination remains challenging, and no comprehensive benchmark exists for evaluating HDR perception in multi-sensor robotic systems.

Method: The authors collect a dataset using a custom robotic vision platform and iPhone 13 Pro (121 real-world scenes with modest and ultra-high dynamic range, plus 20 synthetic video sequences from CARLA). They propose DMEB, which distributes drastically different exposures across multi-view low-bit-depth cameras and fuses them using depth-guided confidence-aware fusion.

Result: Evaluations show DMEB establishes a strong reference point on the new dataset, demonstrating the promise of this multi-camera and depth sensor configuration for robust HDR perception.

Conclusion: The proposed dataset and DMEB pipeline provide a solid foundation and reference for future HDR perception research in multi-sensor robotic systems.

Abstract: Achieving reliable single-shot high dynamic range (HDR) imaging under extreme illumination conditions remains a long-standing challenge, yet no comprehensive benchmark exist for evaluating HDR perception in multi-sensor robotic systems. To fill this gap, we introduce a large-scale dataset collected via a custom robotic vision platform and an iPhone 13 Pro: 121 real-world scenes spanning modest and ultra-high dynamic range conditions, alongside 20 synthetic video sequences from the CARLA simulator. As a reference pipeline for this dataset, we propose Depth-guided Multi-view Exposure Bracketing (DMEB), a single-shot HDR method that distributes drastically different exposures across multi-view low-bit-depth cameras and fuses them via depth-guided confidence-aware fusion. Evaluations on our dataset show that DMEB establishes a strong reference point and highlight the promise of this sensor configuration for robust HDR perception in diverse multi-camera and depth sensor system.

</details>


### [137] [Multi-scale Decomposed Convolution Refinement Network for Visible-Infrared Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16015)
*Mingsheng Zheng,Zirui Jiang,Bo Liu,Yupeng Chen,Jun Zhang,Kai Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes MDCRNet, a Multi-scale Decomposed Convolution Refinement Network for visible-infrared person re-identification, incorporating a Hierarchical Learning Module (HLM) with four Hierarchical Decomposed Convolution Attention (HDCA) modules for multi-scale spatial dependency capture, and a Joint Discriminative Metric Loss (JDML) with a Granularity Discriminative Loss (GDL) to optimize intra-identity compactness and inter-identity separability. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on SYSU-MM01 and RegDB benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VI-ReID approaches suffer from cross-modal discrepancies and limited discriminative capabilities, exhibiting limitations in semantic mining, cross-modal fusion, and feature constraints, which leads to suboptimal recognition performance.

Method: MDCRNet consists of two main components: (1) a Hierarchical Learning Module (HLM) with four Hierarchical Decomposed Convolution Attention (HDCA) modules featuring lightweight channel attention and multi-scale spatial perception blocks to capture multi-scale spatial dependencies; and (2) a Joint Discriminative Metric Loss (JDML) incorporating a novel Granularity Discriminative Loss (GDL) that jointly optimizes intra-identity compactness and inter-identity separability across modalities.

Result: Extensive experiments on SYSU-MM01 and RegDB datasets demonstrate that MDCRNet achieves state-of-the-art performance on both benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed MDCRNet effectively addresses cross-modal discrepancies and discriminative limitations in VI-ReID, achieving superior recognition performance through enhanced cross-modal feature learning and discriminative metric learning.

Abstract: Visible-infrared person re-identification (VI-ReID) suffers from cross-modal discrepancies and limited discriminative capabilities, leading to suboptimal recognition performance. Current approaches exhibit limitations in semantic mining, cross-modal fusion and feature constraints. To tackle these challenges, we propose MDCRNet, a Multi-scale Decomposed Convolution Refinement Network that enhances cross-modal feature learning and discriminative metric learning. Specifically, we introduce a Hierarchical Learning Module (HLM) containing four Hierarchical Decomposed Convolution Attention (HDCA) modules, each equipped with lightweight channel attention and multi-scale spatial perception blocks to capture multi-scale spatial dependencies. Moreover, we develop a Joint Discriminative Metric Loss (JDML) incorporating a novel Granularity Discriminative Loss (GDL) that simultaneously optimizes intra-identity compactness and inter-identity separability across modalities. Extensive experiments on SYSU-MM01 and RegDB datasets demonstrate that MDCRNet achieves state-of-the-art performance on both benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/Kevin-zms/MDCRNet.

</details>


### [138] [TR-GS: High-Fidelity Sparse-View CT Volumetric Rendering via t-Distribution Gaussian Splatting and Ray-Confidence Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16042)
*Zedong Xiao,Yiren Wang,Zhou Liu,Xiaolin Liu,Zhangji Lu*

Main category: cs.CV
