To enable unified generation and editing of e-commerce posters, we introduce Text Patch Generation and Editing, a unified task formulation that treats text patches as atomic units and covers four operations: poster generation, patch addition, patch deletion, and patch modification, with optional reference-guided style control. Based on this, we propose PosterText, a unified model trained with a four-stage curriculum, including text rendering pretraining, instruction-following training, reinforcement learning for preference alignment, and spatial guidance self-distillation for execution refinement. We further construct a large-scale dataset with patch-level annotations and a comprehensive benchmark for evaluation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PosterText achieves competitive performance against existing generation and editing approaches, validating the effectiveness of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [161] [Cross-View Urban Sensing: Mapping Subjective Streetscape Perception via AlphaEarth Embeddings and Urban Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16310)
*Peilin Li,Pengfei Chen,Jingyu Wang,Zhifeng Yang,Tiansheng Chen,Mengjie Gong,Xiao Cheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CVLNet is a cross-view learning network that predicts street-level perception from remote sensing (AlphaEarth) embeddings and urban contextual data, eliminating the need for street view imagery at inference. It achieves a median road-segment-level Adjusted R² of 0.76 across four Southeast Asian cities and outperforms baselines by 5.9–11.3% across five perceptual dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Street view imagery (SVI) is widely used to assess streetscape perception but suffers from uneven coverage and irregular updating, limiting its scalability for large-scale citywide measurement.

Method: CVLNet uses per-task adaptive gating to jointly model five perceptual dimensions, leveraging AlphaEarth embeddings and multi-source urban contextual data with labels from a pretrained SVI-Percept model as ground truth.

Result: CVLNet achieves a median road-segment-level Adjusted R² of 0.76, outperforms baselines by 5.9–11.3% across five perceptual dimensions, and extends perception estimation from 13–31% SVI-covered roads to complete road networks across four Southeast Asian cities.

Conclusion: Remote sensing serves as a scalable alternative to SVI for citywide streetscape perception mapping, enabling comprehensive assessment of urban environmental inequality when integrated with population data.

Abstract: Residents' perception of the urban streetscape is an important factor in public health, active mobility, and social wellbeing. Street view imagery (SVI) has emerged as a widely used data source for assessing these perceptual qualities, yet its uneven coverage and irregular updating limit large-scale measurement. Here, we present CVLNet, a Cross-View Learning Network that predicts street-level perception from AlphaEarth embeddings and multi-source urban contextual data without requiring SVI at inference. CVLNet applies per-task adaptive gating to jointly model five perceptual dimensions, using labels from the pretrained SVI-Percept model as ground truth. The proposed method is evaluated across four Southeast Asian cities: Singapore, Kuala Lumpur, Jakarta, and Manila. CVLNet achieves a median road-segment-level Adjusted $R^{2}$ of 0.76 and consistently outperforms the baseline models, with gains ranging from 5.9--11.3% across the five perceptual dimensions. Ablation experiments show that AlphaEarth features and urban contextual features contribute complementary information. We further produce citywide road-level streetscape perception maps for five subjective perceptual dimensions across all four cities, extending perception estimation from the 13--31% of the road network directly covered by available SVI to the complete road network of each city. Integrating these maps with WorldPop gridded population data, we quantify exposure inequality across population-density, demographic, and land-use groups using the Deficit Palma Ratio. These results demonstrate that remote sensing can serve as a scalable alternative to SVI for citywide streetscape perception mapping, enabling a more comprehensive assessment of urban environmental inequality.

</details>


### [162] [Deep Thought Alignment: Trajectory-Level Latent Distillation for Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16316)
*Ao Shen,Yongheng Zhang,Yinghui Li,Manning Wang,Di Yin,Xing Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Latent-OPD augments On-Policy Distillation (OPD) with trajectory-level latent distillation for video reasoning, aligning hidden states at trajectory endpoints and using a progressive teacher-lookahead strategy to transfer reasoning capabilities from large to small LMMs efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video reasoning in LMMs is computationally expensive, and although OPD (output-token distribution matching) enables knowledge transfer to smaller models, it only supervises outputs. Video reasoning relies on evidence accumulated across multiple frames, which requires latent representations to be properly constrained, not just output tokens.

Method: Latent-OPD extends OPD by adding trajectory-level latent distillation that focuses on end-of-trajectory hidden states (which summarize accumulated visual evidence and reasoning context). It also introduces a progressive teacher-lookahead strategy that aligns middle-to-late student layers with increasingly deeper teacher layers.

Result: Latent-OPD consistently outperforms output-only OPD across six video reasoning benchmarks. Improvements are especially significant in scenarios with limited frames, long videos, or tasks requiring complex evidence aggregation.

Conclusion: Latent-OPD is a highly effective approach for frame-efficient video reasoning, demonstrating that integrating latent-space distillation with output-level OPD substantially improves knowledge transfer for video reasoning in small LMMs.

Abstract: Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) for video reasoning have long been hindered by the high computational cost of processing vast amounts of visual information. This dilemma motivates the transfer of the reasoning capabilities of large models to smaller, more efficient ones. On-Policy Distillation (OPD) offers a promising solution by matching output-token distributions along student-generated trajectories. However, video reasoning often depends on evidence accumulated across multiple frames. In this context, output-level supervision only captures information expressed through token predictions and does not directly constrain the latent representations formed during reasoning. To address this limitation, we propose Latent-OPD, which augments OPD with trajectory-level latent distillation. Specifically, our method focuses on the position at the end of each trajectory, where hidden states effectively summarize the accumulated visual evidence and reasoning context. Furthermore, we introduce a progressive teacher-lookahead strategy, which aligns middle-to-late student layers with increasingly deeper teacher layers. Experiments on six video reasoning benchmarks show that Latent-OPD consistently outperforms output-only OPD. Notably, the improvements are particularly pronounced in scenarios with limited frames, long videos, or tasks requiring complex evidence aggregation. These results establish Latent-OPD as a highly effective approach to frame-efficient video reasoning.

</details>


### [163] [StreamOPD: A Post-Training Recipe with Spatio-Temporal Cue Gating for Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16320)
*Keming Wu,Baoyi Wang,Kaichen Zhang,Xiang An,Zuhao Yang,Sudong Wang,Haowei Zhu,Tingxuan Huang,Hongcheng Gao,Bin Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces StreamOPD, a post-training recipe combining verifiable streaming-video data, thinking-mode on-policy distillation (OPD), and instruct-mode deployment, which improves streaming video understanding without inference-time memory or retrieval. It also proposes ST-CueGate, a teacher-privilege extension using cue-versus-no-cue likelihood ratios to reweight distillation, and demonstrates that on-policy self-distillation retains most gains while improving hallucination detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming video understanding systems rely on inference-time memory, retrieval, and compression, but a training-free sliding-window baseline already matches them. The authors ask how far post-training alone can go, fixing a memory-free recent-window protocol to isolate the impact of training.

Method: The paper proposes StreamOPD, which combines verifiable streaming-video data with thinking-mode on-policy distillation (OPD) and instruct-mode deployment. It also introduces Spatio-Temporal CueGate (ST-CueGate), which aggregates teacher likelihood ratios between cue and no-cue conditions into a group-relative response score to reweight OPD. Additionally, it explores on-policy self-distillation using a frozen copy of the student's initial policy as a teacher.

Result: StreamOPD raises StreamingBench from 77.9% to 83.9% (within 0.3 points of the 9B teacher) and improves OVO-Bench (excluding HLD) by 9.1 points with unchanged inference. ST-CueGate reaches 71.9% on OVO-Bench (excluding HLD) and 64.9% on Video-MME, and is the only variant staying above the base model on all four benchmarks. On-policy self-distillation retains most gains and lifts HLD to 57.0%, surpassing both the untrained student and the 9B teacher.

Conclusion: Post-training alone can significantly improve streaming video understanding without inference-time mechanisms. On-policy distillation with thinking-mode training is a viable and stable approach, and abstention loss observed in distillation is not intrinsic to the recipe. The work provides a transparent, reproducible reference for open-source streaming-video research.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding demands direct responses from the causally observed prefix of an unfolding video. Existing systems add inference-time memory, retrieval, and compression, yet a training-free sliding-window baseline already matches them. We therefore fix a memory-free recent-window protocol and ask how far post-training alone can go. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards fits this regime poorly, encouraging long ``think-then-answer'' generations, while on-policy distillation (OPD) supplies dense token-level teacher supervision on student trajectories but is stable only when both models train in thinking mode. These observations lead to \textsc{StreamOPD}, a recipe combining verifiable streaming-video data, thinking-mode OPD, and instruct-mode deployment. It raises StreamingBench from $77.9\%$ to $83.9\%$---within $0.3$ points of the 9B teacher---and improves OVO-Bench excluding its hallucination-detection subtask (HLD) by $9.1$ points under unchanged inference. As a teacher-privilege extension, \emph{Spatio-Temporal CueGate (ST-CueGate)} aggregates cue-versus-no-cue teacher likelihood ratios into a group-relative response score that reweights OPD. It reaches $71.9\%$ on OVO-Bench (excluding HLD) and $64.9\%$ on Video-MME, and is the only variant that stays above the base model on all four benchmarks. Replacing the teacher with a frozen copy of the student's initial policy---on-policy self-distillation---retains most of these gains and lifts HLD to $57.0\%$, above both the untrained student and the 9B teacher, so abstention loss is not intrinsic to the recipe. We provide a transparent and reproducible reference for open-source streaming-video research.

</details>


### [164] [LaGSplat: Inferring Physics-Governed Interactive Simulation from Monocular Video Using Latent Lagrangian Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16324)
*Louen Pottier*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LaGSplat infers physics-governed dynamics from one or a few monocular videos, enabling interactive user forces on reconstructed rigid or deformable objects in real time. It uses a low-dimensional latent state that serves both as generalized coordinates of a learned dissipative Lagrangian and as the conditioning variable for a Gaussian Splatting decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable interactive, physics-based manipulation of reconstructed objects from monocular video, where users can apply external forces that were never seen during training—something pixel-space or neural-field decoders cannot support.

Method: A low-dimensional latent state q serves dual roles: generalized coordinates of a learned dissipative Lagrangian and conditioning variable of a Gaussian Splatting decoder with explicit points μ_i(q) that move with the object. Forces applied in image space pull back to latent generalized forces via J(q)^T f and enter the equations of motion.

Result: Validated on test cases increasing in difficulty from rigid to deformable, and autonomous to forced real systems, combining monocular video and sensor data. Demonstrates interactive use with arbitrary force magnitude/direction applied at any time, rendered in real time in 2D or 3D.

Conclusion: Constraining to a dissipative Euler-Lagrange equation over few generalized coordinates trades generality for bounded, plausible responses to unseen forces, where unconstrained predictors diverge.

Abstract: We present LaGSplat (Latent Lagrangian Gaussian Splatting), a framework that infers interactive, physics-governed dynamics from one or a few monocular videos. At inference it lets a user push on the filmed object, rigid or deformable, with an external force that was never measured, annotated, or seen during training. This is possible because a low-dimensional latent state $\mathbf{q} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ plays two roles at once: it is the generalised coordinate of a learned dissipative Lagrangian and the conditioning variable of a Gaussian Splatting decoder. The inductive bias of this decoder, whose primitives are explicit points $μ_i(\mathbf{q})$ that move with the object, is what lets a force $f$ applied in the image pull back into a latent generalised force $J(\mathbf{q})^\top f$ and enter the equations of motion, which pixel-space (CNN) or neural-field (NeRF) decoders cannot do. We validate LaGSplat on test cases of increasing difficulty, from rigid to deformable and from autonomous to forced real systems, combining monocular video and sensor measurements. We further demonstrate interactive use: forces of arbitrary magnitude and direction can be applied to the reconstructed object at any time, its response rendered in real time, in 2D or 3D. Assuming a dissipative Euler-Lagrange equation over a few generalised coordinates trades generality for a bounded, plausible response to unseen forces, where an unconstrained predictor diverges.

</details>


### [165] [GRNEdit: Efficient General Video Editing from a New Binary-Evidence Perspective in Generative Refinement Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16328)
*Feng Xie,Jiagao Hu,Fuhao Li,Zepeng Wang,Yuxuan Chen,Dahua Gao,Fei Wang,Daiguo Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GRNEdit is a lightweight two-stage framework for instruction-based general video editing that encodes editing semantics as local retain-or-flip decisions over individual bits, using a GRN (generalized random network) backbone with a compact evidence encoder. With only 0.6M training pairs and under 3% conditioning parameters, GRNEdit-2B and 8B achieve competitive scores on OpenVE-Bench, outperforming larger open-source models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction-based video editing methods rely on resource-intensive conditioning (heavyweight branches or costly source concatenation). The authors seek an efficient way to model editing intent that avoids these computational overheads while maintaining edit quality.

Method: GRNEdit is a two-stage framework: (1) Stage I uses a compact encoder that translates discrete source codes into continuous evidence signals, which the GRN backbone assimilates during binary refinement. A null instruction is treated as "no edit" and supervised through source reconstruction. (2) Stage II compares each edited state with its source-preserving counterpart, using their discrepancy to revise unresolved target-bit decisions. Editing semantics are modeled as per-bit retain-or-flip decisions.

Result: Trained on only 0.6M pairs with less than 3% conditioning parameters, GRNEdit-2B achieves 4.03 and GRNEdit-8B achieves 4.18 on OpenVE-Bench. The 2B model outperforms multiple 14B open-source editors, while the 8B model performs on par with leading open-source editors.

Conclusion: GRNEdit demonstrates that efficient bit-level representation of editing intent can rival or surpass much larger resource-intensive models, showing that lightweight conditioning is viable for instruction-based video editing without sacrificing quality.

Abstract: Instruction-based general video editing seeks to unify diverse editing operations within a single, intuitive interface. Existing approaches often rely on resource-intensive conditioning, using either heavyweight branches or costly source concatenation. Is there any efficient way to model editing intent? Thus, we introduce GRNEdit, a lightweight two-stage framework. GRN inspires our approach by encoding visual semantics through combinations of bits. Through task-specific fine-tuning, we take this representation further and recast editing semantics as local retain-or-flip decisions over individual bits. Source information is consequently modeled as coordinate-wise evidence supporting the observed binary states, while the GRN backbone remains responsible for resolving their global composition into coherent generative semantics. In Stage I, a compact encoder translates discrete source codes into continuous evidence signals, which GRN assimilates throughout binary refinement. Inspired by null-prompt training for classifier-free guidance, we further assign the null condition an editing-specific meaning: an empty instruction denotes no edit and is supervised through source reconstruction. This identity pathway not only implicitly strengthens evidence utilization and content preservation in Stage I, but also produces a source-preserving state in the same representation space as the edited state. Stage II can therefore directly compare each edited state with its source-preserving counterpart and use their discrepancy to revise unresolved target-bit decisions. Trained on only 0.6M pairs with less than 3\% conditioning parameters, GRNEdit-2B and GRNEdit-8B achieve scores of 4.03 and 4.18 on OpenVE-Bench. The 2B model outperforms multiple 14B open-source editors, while the 8B model performs on par with leading open-source editors.

</details>


### [166] [Unlocking Motion in Expressions: Temporal Calibration for Referring Video Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16332)
*Yiwen Jiang,Zhengtong Zhu,Ruixin Zhang,Jiaqing Fan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes an Expression-driven Motion Calibration (EMC) framework for Referring Video Object Segmentation (RVOS) that explicitly extracts motion semantics from natural language expressions via a Motion Signal Processing module, calibrates motion cue contributions during temporal decision-making with a Motion Influence Calibration module, and constructs expression-relevant temporal stages with a Semantic Temporal Stage Construction module. The method is validated on six standard RVOS benchmarks, outperforming existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RVOS methods incorporate motion information within a unified cross-modal temporal modeling framework but fail to explicitly model how expressions depend on motion semantics, making it difficult to adaptively adjust motion information usage according to different semantic requirements in the language.

Method: The EMC framework consists of three components: (1) a Motion Signal Processing (MSP) module that extracts interpretable motion control signals from expressions, (2) a Motion Influence Calibration (MIC) module that adjusts the contribution of motion cues during temporal decision making, and (3) a Semantic Temporal Stage Construction (STSC) module that builds expression-relevant temporal stages, providing a compact temporal candidate space for motion calibration.

Result: Extensive evaluation on six standard benchmarks (Ref-YouTubeVOS, Ref-DAVIS17, MeViS valid/valid^u, A2D-Sentences, and JHMDB-Sentences) validates the superiority of the proposed method over existing approaches.

Conclusion: The EMC framework effectively unlocks and leverages motion semantics within expressions for RVOS, enabling adaptive use of motion information based on semantic requirements, and demonstrates superior performance across multiple standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Referring Video Object Segmentation (RVOS) aims to segment referred objects at the pixel level in video sequences based on natural language descriptions. Existing methods typically introduce motion information within a unified cross-modal temporal modeling framework, where language cues are used for target localization and segmentation. However, the dependency of expressions on motion semantics is not explicitly modeled, making it difficult to adaptively adjust the use of motion information according to different semantic requirements. To address these issues, we propose an Expression-driven Motion Calibration (EMC) framework for RVOS that explicitly unlocks and leverages the motion semantics within expressions. The proposed method extracts interpretable motion control signals from expressions via a Motion Signal Processing (MSP) module, and employs a Motion Influence Calibration (MIC) module to adjust the contribution of motion cues during temporal decision making. In addition, a Semantic Temporal Stage Construction (STSC) module is introduced to build expression-relevant temporal stages, providing a compact temporal candidate space for motion calibration. Through extensive evaluation on six standard benchmarks, including Ref-YouTubeVOS, Ref-DAVIS17, MeViS (valid/valid$^u$), A2D-Sentences, and JHMDB-Sentences, the superiority of our method is validated. We will release the code on https://github.com/Jeven7/EMC.

</details>


### [167] [SIGMA-Lane: Scale-pyramId Gated MAmba for Temporally Consistent Video Lane Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16338)
*Tiancheng Zhang,Mengmeng Wang,Yan Gao,Xiangjie Kong,Guojiang Shen,Jiaxin Du*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SIGMA-Lane is a video lane detection method that addresses temporal instability caused by vehicle occlusions by treating them as state contamination in State Space Model (SSM)-based temporal modeling, using occlusion-aware gates on SSM write and residual-fusion paths, plus Structural Spatial Retrieval for recovering missing lane structure from aligned historical priors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video lane detection needs frame-to-frame stability, but severe vehicle occlusions break temporal cues. In streaming recurrent models, corrupted observations can pollute the hidden state, causing persistent errors. Existing occlusion-aware methods provide obstacle masks only as auxiliary inputs, leaving the state-update path indirectly protected.

Method: SIGMA-Lane places occlusion-aware gates on the SSM write and residual-fusion paths to control how current observations enter temporal memory and are fused back after temporal propagation. After coordinate-consistent affine alignment, the model combines two complementary paths: SSM-consistent dual-gating for temporal filtering and Structural Spatial Retrieval (SSR) for recovering missing lane structure from aligned historical priors.

Result: Experiments on VIL-100 and OpenLane-V show improved temporal stability under heavy occlusion, with competitive F1 and mIoU scores.

Conclusion: Treating occlusion-induced errors as state contamination in SSM-based temporal modeling, with explicit occlusion-aware gating, effectively improves temporal stability in video lane detection under heavy occlusion while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Abstract: Video lane detection requires predictions that remain stable across frames, yet severe vehicle occlusions can break temporal cues. In streaming recurrent models, corrupted observations may enter the hidden state and produce errors that persist into later frames. Existing occlusion-aware refinements usually provide obstacle masks as auxiliary inputs, so the state-update path is only indirectly protected. We propose SIGMA-Lane, which treats this failure mode as state contamination in State Space Model (SSM)-based temporal modeling. SIGMA-Lane places occlusion-aware gates on the SSM write and residual-fusion paths, controlling how current observations enter temporal memory and are fused back after temporal propagation. After coordinate-consistent affine alignment, the model combines two complementary paths: SSM-consistent dual-gating for temporal filtering and Structural Spatial Retrieval (SSR) for recovering missing lane structure from aligned historical priors. Experiments on VIL-100 and OpenLane-V show improved temporal stability under heavy occlusion, with competitive F1 and mIoU scores.

</details>


### [168] [Depth-Dominant Skeleton Detection for Natural Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16367)
*Chengkun Rao,Yixuan Deng,Min Li,Yangjun Ou,Ye Li,Ziwei Luo,Zhaojing Wang,Junwei Tang,Bangchao Wang,Xiaoyun Yan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new skeleton detection paradigm where depth images serve as the dominant modality and RGB images act as auxiliary, introducing a model called DDSkel that outperforms all state-of-the-art methods on the challenging SymPASCAL dataset with only 36% of the trainable parameters of the current best method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skeleton detection methods all follow the RGB-only paradigm and suffer significant performance degradation on complex-content images. The authors observe that depth images are inherently insensitive to color and texture while providing clear regional contours and spatial relationships, which naturally alleviates the difficulty of skeleton detection in complex scenarios.

Method: DDSkel employs an asymmetric encoder design that fuses RGB information into depth features, where the RGB modality branch has only 12% of the parameters of the depth modality branch. The model follows a depth-dominant paradigm with a simple structure without intricate designs.

Result: With only 36% of the trainable parameters of the current best method, DDSkel outperforms all state-of-the-art approaches on SymPASCAL, the most challenging dataset with a large volume of complex images.

Conclusion: The depth-dominant paradigm proves to be an effective and efficient alternative to the traditional RGB-only approach for skeleton detection, particularly in complex scenarios. This is the first work to establish depth images as the dominant modality in this task.

Abstract: To date, all natural scene skeleton detection follows the paradigm of taking RGB images as the sole input; despite notable progress, methods under this paradigm suffer significant performance degradation on complex-content images. We observe that depth images are inherently insensitive to color and texture, and can provide clear regional contours and inter-region spatial relationships, which naturally alleviates the difficulty of skeleton detection in complex scenarios. Motivated by this observation, this paper proposes for the first time a novel skeleton detection paradigm where depth images serve as the dominant modality and RGB images act as the auxiliary, and accordingly presents a model DDSkel (short for Depth-Dominant Skeleton Detection) under this paradigm. DDSkel employs an asymmetric encoder design to fuse RGB information into depth features, with the RGB modality branch having only 12% the parameters of the depth modality branch. DDSkel has a simple structure without intricate designs. Nevertheless, with only 36% of the trainable parameters of the current best method, DDSkel outperforms all state-of-the-art approaches on SymPASCAL, the most challenging dataset with a large volume of complex images.

</details>


### [169] [Adaptive Post-Processing Drives Instance-Level Detection in Stroke Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16377)
*Qinghui Liu,Jon André Ottesen,Atle Bjørnerud,Kyrre Eeg Emblem*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents Volume-Conditioned Adaptive Post-Processing (VCAP), which adjusts component-size thresholds based on predicted lesion burden, improving Lesion-F1 by 0.032 (6x larger than any architectural change) in ISLES'26. A resolution-aware architecture (Viola2Plus) improved small-lesion detection by 3.7% despite no change in Dice. The post-processed ensemble achieves Dice 0.651 and Lesion-F1 0.614.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most lesion detection pipelines are trained and post-processed for voxel-level overlap alone, while instance-level lesion detection matters in practice. This mismatch is most severe for small lesions, where near-miss predictions (substantial overlap just below instance-matching threshold) score the same as complete misses.

Method: The authors propose Volume-Conditioned Adaptive Post-Processing (VCAP), which adjusts component-size thresholds based on each case's predicted lesion burden. They also test a resolution-aware attention architecture (Viola2Plus) designed for small-lesion segmentation, and combine both in a two-architecture ensemble under 5-fold cross-validation.

Result: VCAP improves Lesion-F1 by 0.032 (unbiased cross-fold estimate), approximately 6x larger than any architectural change tested. Viola2Plus left small-lesion Dice unchanged but raised small-lesion detection rate by 3.7%. The post-processed ensemble achieves Dice 0.651 and Lesion-F1 0.614 vs 0.644 and 0.573 for the unprocessed single-model baseline.

Conclusion: Post-processing adjustments for instance-level metrics matter significantly more than architectural changes for lesion detection. Voxel-overlap metrics alone can miss meaningful improvements in lesion-level detection performance.

Abstract: Instance-level lesion detection has been an increasingly larger focal point in medical image segmentation besides the more standard voxel-level overlap. Still, most pipelines are trained and post-processed for voxel overlap alone. In particular, the mismatch is most pronounced for small lesions, where a near-miss prediction---substantial overlap that falls just short of the instance-matching threshold---scores the same as a complete miss. In our ISLES'26 submission, we found that closing this gap mattered far more in post-processing than in architecture design. Our Volume-Conditioned Adaptive Post-Processing (VCAP) scheme adjusts component-size thresholds to each case's predicted lesion burden, improving Lesion-F1 by 0.032 (unbiased cross-fold estimate)---approximately 6 times larger than any architectural change we tested. A resolution-aware attention architecture (Viola2Plus), designed for small-lesion segmentation, shows why the distinction matters: it left small-lesion Dice unchanged but raised small-lesion detection rate by 3.7\%, a real effect voxel-overlap metrics alone would have missed. Under 5-fold cross-validation on the 1,453-case training set, our post-processed two-architecture ensemble achieves Dice 0.651 and Lesion-F1 0.614, versus 0.644 and 0.573 for the unprocessed single-model baseline.

</details>


### [170] [Synthetic Data Augmentation for Satellite-Based Analysis of Battle-Damaged Agricultural Fields in Ukraine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16380)
*Marta Sumyk,Oleksandr Kosovan,Iryna Voitsitska*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates synthetic data augmentation using GANs and DDPMs to improve satellite-based classification of war-damaged agricultural land in Ukraine, achieving significant accuracy gains (84% to 88% accuracy, 67% to 81% balanced accuracy, and 41% to 69% recall on the underrepresented class) under limited and imbalanced training data conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monitoring war-induced damage to agricultural land in Ukraine is critical for food security and post-war recovery, but computer-vision systems for satellite-based damage analysis are severely limited by the scarcity of labeled imagery, especially for damaged agricultural fields.

Method: The authors train class-conditional Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) and Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) architectures on real satellite images to generate additional bombed and not-bombed agricultural-field samples. Generated images are used only for training augmentation, with evaluation performed on an exclusively real test set. A Vision Transformer classifier is trained under multiple real and synthetic data configurations to assess each generative approach's utility.

Result: The best configuration, based on balanced DDPM augmentation, improves accuracy from 84% to 88%, balanced accuracy from 67% to 81%, macro F1 from 65% to 78%, and recall for the underrepresented not-bombed class from 41% to 69%.

Conclusion: Synthetic satellite imagery generated by diffusion models demonstrates strong potential for improving classification performance in data-scarce geospatial applications, particularly in war-affected regions where labeled damage data is difficult to obtain.

Abstract: Monitoring war-induced damage to agricultural land in Ukraine is important for understanding threats to food security, environmental stability, and post-war recovery. However, the development of computer-vision systems for satellite-based damage analysis is limited by the scarcity of labeled imagery, especially for damaged agricultural fields. This work investigates synthetic data augmentation as a method for improving classification under limited and imbalanced training data. We train class-conditional Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) and Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) architectures on real satellite images and use them to generate additional bombed and not-bombed agricultural-field samples. The generated images are used only for training augmentation, while all downstream evaluation is performed on an exclusively real test set. A Vision Transformer classifier is trained under multiple real and synthetic data configurations to measure the practical utility of each generative approach. The best configuration, based on balanced DDPM augmentation, improves accuracy from 84\% to 88\%, balanced accuracy from 67\% to 81\%, macro F1 from 65\% to 78\%, and recall for the underrepresented not-bombed class from 41\% to 69\%. These results demonstrate the potential of synthetic satellite imagery for data-scarce geospatial applications in war-affected regions.

</details>


### [171] [Self-Routed Tensor Adapters for Parameter-Efficient Universal Visual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16384)
*Suraj Yadav*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SRTA is a compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework for multi-domain visual adaptation that uses self-routed tensor adapters—projecting inputs into low-rank space, computing routing weights via a learnable domain matrix, and blending slices of a shared Tucker core to produce sample-specific adaptation matrices without external gating networks. It achieves competitive accuracy with significantly fewer parameters than MoE-style PEFT baselines across five heterogeneous benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard low-rank adapters use a fixed subspace for all inputs, which is restrictive when domains differ in style, background, and semantic context. MoE-based adapters improve specialization but rely on external routers and large expert banks, adding parameters and separating routing from adaptation.

Method: SRTA projects each input into a low-rank space, computes routing weights from this representation using a learnable domain matrix, and blends slices of a shared Tucker core to produce a sample-specific adaptation matrix. A progressive depth-weighted routing objective supervises routing decisions across adapter layers.

Result: Across five heterogeneous multi-domain visual classification benchmarks, SRTA achieves competitive or slightly stronger average accuracy than MoE-style PEFT baselines while using substantially fewer trainable parameters: 2.77M vs 9.52M (MoLoRA) at rank 64 in the 4-domain setting, and 3.00M vs 14.31M in the 6-domain setting.

Conclusion: SRTA offers an effective accuracy-parameter trade-off for adapting visual foundation models toward universal multi-domain representations, demonstrating that self-contained routing integrated with tensor-based adaptation can replace external gating networks while maintaining or improving performance.

Abstract: Universal visual representations require adaptation mechanisms that adapt across heterogeneous domains without fragmenting knowledge into domain-specific modules. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts frozen visual foundation models efficiently, but standard low-rank adapters use a fixed subspace for all inputs, which can be restrictive when domains differ in style, background, and semantic context. MoE-based adapters improve specialization through multiple expert pathways, but often rely on external routers and large expert banks, adding parameters and separating routing from adaptation. We propose \textbf{Self-Routed Tensor Adapters}, a compact framework for multi-domain visual adaptation. SRTA projects each input into a low-rank space, computes routing weights from this representation using a learnable domain matrix, and uses these weights to blend slices of a shared Tucker core. This produces a sample-specific adaptation matrix without an external gating network, allowing shared visual factors to be reused while supporting domain-aware specialization. To strengthen pathway learning, we introduce a progressive depth-weighted routing objective that supervises routing decisions across adapter layers. Across five heterogeneous multi-domain visual classification benchmarks, SRTA achieves competitive or slightly stronger average accuracy than MoE-style PEFT baselines while using substantially fewer trainable parameters. At rank 64, SRTA uses 2.77M parameters in the 4-domain setting compared with 9.52M for MoLoRA, and 3.00M in the 6-domain setting compared with 14.31M. Overall, SRTA offers an effective accuracy-parameter trade-off for adapting visual foundation models toward universal multi-domain representations. \href{https://github.com/surajyadav-research/SRTA}{GitHub}

</details>


### [172] [Joint Flow Matching Enables Continuous Dose-Conditioned Cell Morphing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16424)
*Lea Bogensperger,Manuela Merlo,Martin Baumgartner,Michael Krauthammer,Bernard Ciraulo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a joint flow matching approach that simultaneously models cell latents and drug concentration via a dual-timestep formulation, enabling dose-conditioned single-cell morphing and concentration estimation from cell morphology, with generalization to unseen doses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing perturbation models either treat perturbation as distribution-to-distribution mapping without explicit concentration handling, or treat concentration as a discrete class label, which precludes continuous dose control.

Method: A joint flow matching approach that simultaneously models cell latents and drug concentration using a dual-timestep formulation, leveraging the invertibility of flow matching to enable dose-conditioned single-cell morphing. This induces a monotonic dose-response geometry in latent space and supports concentration estimation from cell morphology.

Result: Achieves competitive or improved per-concentration metrics on two compounds compared with representative baselines, while enabling capabilities (continuous dose control, concentration estimation, unseen dose generalization) structurally unavailable to discrete-class methods.

Conclusion: The joint flow matching formulation provides a principled way to handle continuous drug concentration in cellular perturbation modeling, expanding the capability space beyond discrete-class approaches.

Abstract: Generative modeling has shown increasing promise for predicting cellular perturbation effects under chemical compound treatments. Existing approaches either model perturbation as a distribution-to-distribution mapping without explicit concentration handling, or treat concentration as a discrete class label, precluding continuous dose control. We introduce a joint flow matching approach that simultaneously models cell latents and drug concentration via a dual-timestep formulation, enabling dose-conditioned single-cell morphing through the invertibility of flow matching. The joint formulation induces a monotonic dose-response geometry in latent space and additionally supports concentration estimation from cell morphology. As proof of concept, we further demonstrate generalization to an unseen dose held out during training. Empirically, our method achieves competitive or improved per-concentration metrics on two compounds compared with representative baselines, while enabling capabilities structurally unavailable to discrete-class methods.

</details>


### [173] [Contrastive Energy Fields for Inference-Time Procedure Planning in Instructional Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16457)
*Mohamed Afham,Christoph Reich,Oliver Hahn,Daniel Cremers,Stefan Roth*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CEFITO is a procedure planning approach that learns an action-conditioned representation space and formulates planning as a task-constrained optimization problem, explicitly omitting irrelevant actions during inference to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy on two benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current procedure planning methods directly predict action sequences from latent representations using feed-forward networks or diffusion models, treating every action as plausible and failing to enforce task-specific logical constraints that render certain actions irrelevant or implausible.

Method: CEFITO learns a predictor to express an action-conditioned representation space, then formulates procedure planning as a task-constrained optimization problem that explicitly reasons over the action space by omitting irrelevant actions during inference-time planning.

Result: CEFITO achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on two established procedure planning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Reformulating procedure planning as a task-constrained optimization over an action-conditioned representation space, with explicit exclusion of irrelevant actions, enables more effective procedure planning than prior feed-forward or diffusion-based approaches.

Abstract: Procedure planning seeks to estimate a sequence of actions to transition from an observed initial state to a given goal state. Current procedure planning approaches directly predict action sequences from latent representations using feed-forward neural networks or diffusion-based inference. These paradigms treat every action as plausible, lacking the ability to enforce task-specific logical constraints that render certain actions irrelevant or not plausible. We propose CEFITO, a procedure planning approach that learns a predictor to express an action-conditioned representation space. Based on this representation space, we formulate procedure planning as a task-constrained optimization problem. Unlike prior methods, CEFITO explicitly reasons over the action space by omitting irrelevant actions during inference-time planning. This reformulation enables effective procedure planning and achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on two established procedure planning benchmarks.

</details>


### [174] [Shared-Structure 4D Spectral Gaussian Representation for Sparse-View Spectral CT Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16463)
*Jiancheng Fang,Shaoyu Wang,Wenjun Xia,Yang Chen,Qiegen Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 4D-SG is a shared-structure 4D spectral Gaussian representation for sparse-view spectral CT reconstruction that learns shared Gaussian geometry from structural projections and predicts spectral density transformations via a GSC-Net, achieving state-of-the-art results across six datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse-view spectral CT requires simultaneous handling of angular undersampling and spectral coupling, which existing methods struggle with due to independent channel geometry optimization and discrete spectral measurements.

Method: The method factorizes spatial structure from spectral variation by learning shared Gaussian geometry from full spectrum structural projections, then uses a Gaussian-wise Spectral Density Curve Network (GSC-Net) to predict raw density transformations, enabling continuous 4D representation for unobserved spectral channels.

Result: On six datasets (synthesized, simulated projection, and real projection) with 50 views, 4D-SG achieves best average performance, improving PSNR from 35.56 to 36.61 dB, SSIM from 0.909 to 0.914, and reducing LPIPS from 0.208 to 0.194 compared to the strongest Gaussian baseline.

Conclusion: The proposed factorization approach effectively separates shared spatial structure from spectral attenuation variation, establishing a continuous 4D-SG representation that proves highly effective for sparse-view spectral CT reconstruction.

Abstract: Sparse-view spectral computed tomography (CT) reconstructs energy-resolved attenuation volumes from limited projection views, requiring simultaneous handling of angular undersampling and spectral coupling. We propose a SharedStructure 4D Spectral Gaussian Representation (4D-SG) that learns shared Gaussian geometry from full spectrum structural projections and uses a Gaussian-wise Spectral Density Curve Network (GSC-Net) to predict Gaussian raw density transformations. This factorization separates shared spatial structure from spectral attenuation variation, avoids independent channel geometry optimization, and establishes a continuous 4D-SG representation from discrete spectral measurements for unobserved spectral channel queries. Experiments on six synthesized, simulated projection, and real projection datasets with 50 views demonstrate the best average performance. Compared with the strongest Gaussian baseline, 4D-SG improves PSNR from 35.56 dB to 36.61 dB, increases SSIM from 0.909 to 0.914, and reduces LPIPS from 0.208 to 0.194, demonstrating its effectiveness for sparse-view spectral CT reconstruction.

</details>


### [175] [Sterilizable Scene Graph Generation for Operating Rooms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16469)
*Nick Lemke,Ssharvien Kumar Sivakumar,Antoine P. Sanner,John Kalkhof,Henry John Krumb,Ghazal Ghazaei,Anirban Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SG-NCA is the first lightweight scene graph generation framework for surgical video based on Neural Cellular Automata (NCA), enabling OR-ready deployment on fanless edge devices with 55x fewer parameters than baselines while achieving comparable performance on cataract surgery and cholecystectomy videos, with downstream applications like surgical video captioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surgical scene graph generation methods rely on large, parameter-heavy deep learning models that are impractical for operating room deployment due to hardware footprint, hygiene constraints, latency, and data privacy concerns.

Method: SG-NCA combines NCA-based multiclass segmentation for efficient object detection and feature extraction with a lightweight relation predictor, all built on a Neural Cellular Automata framework for structured representation learning.

Result: SG-NCA achieves performance comparable to established baselines on cataract surgery and cholecystectomy videos while requiring 55x fewer parameters, and demonstrates successful deployment on fanless edge devices plus downstream surgical video captioning.

Conclusion: SG-NCA demonstrates the potential of NCA-based approaches for affordable, privacy-preserving, and OR-ready intraoperative scene understanding, opening a new direction for lightweight structured representation learning in surgical video analysis.

Abstract: Scene graph generation from surgical video enables a holistic and structured understanding of surgical scenes by modeling objects and their semantic relationships. Despite recent advances, state-of-the-art approaches rely on large, parameter-heavy deep learning models that are impractical for deployment in the operating room (OR) due to hardware footprint, hygiene constraints, latency, and data privacy concerns. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first scene graph generation method built on NCAs and the first NCA framework capable of learning structured representations. We introduce SG-NCA, a lightweight scene graph generation framework based on Neural Cellular Automata (NCA), designed for inference in fanless devices critical for OR hygiene protocols. SG-NCA is the first scene graph generation combining NCA-based multiclass segmentation for efficient object detection and feature extraction with a lightweight relation predictor. We evaluate SG-NCA on videos of cataract surgery and cholecystectomy, demonstrating performance comparable to established baselines while requiring 55x fewer parameters. We showcase deployment on fanless edge devices better suited for the OR and demonstrate downstream applications such as surgical video captioning, highlighting SG-NCA's potential for affordable, privacy-preserving, and OR-ready intraoperative scene understanding.

</details>


### [176] [RISE: Roadside Infrastructure Sequence Understanding across 3D Tracking and Structured Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16480)
*Yanbo Jiang,Haotian Zheng,Jiahao Wang,Hanxiao Ren,Yitao Xu,Yining Xing,Zehong Ke,Hao Cheng,Yiqian Tu,Jinhao Li,Zhiyuan Xuan,Fang Zhang,Jianqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RISE is a framework combining metric 3D tracking and vision-language reasoning for roadside multi-camera sequences, featuring calibration-guided multi-view identity association without LiDAR or task-specific 3D training, plus the RISE-VQA benchmark with 33,910 QA pairs for predictive reasoning evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Roadside multi-camera systems lack a unified framework that integrates metric 3D tracking with structured vision-language reasoning, and existing methods often require LiDAR or layout-specific retraining. There is also a need for large-scale QA benchmarks grounded in real roadside tracking data to evaluate predictive reasoning capabilities.

Method: For tracking, combines SAM3 video identities with calibration-guided mask agreement for multi-view identity association, producing persistent 3D tracks from images only. For reasoning, uses a human-reviewed MLLM pipeline to mine clips and a constrained Oracle to build bbox-grounded predictive QA, yielding the RISE-VQA dataset and the intersection-held-out RISE-Bench for evaluation.

Result: Tracks achieve 66.9 MOTA on 20 human-reviewed clips from six intersections. RISE-VQA contains 33,910 QA pairs from 557 clips across 16 intersections and 61 roadside views. Experiments show benefits from domain adaptation and temporal context but reveal persistent challenges in spatial grounding, future localization, and interaction reasoning.

Conclusion: RISE demonstrates a viable image-only, calibration-conditioned approach to multi-view 3D tracking that generalizes across intersections, and establishes a large-scale VQA benchmark for roadside predictive reasoning. The identified remaining challenges point to future work in spatial grounding, future localization, and interaction reasoning.

Abstract: We present RISE (Roadside Infrastructure Sequence Understanding and Evaluation), a framework spanning metric 3D tracking and structured vision-language reasoning in roadside sequences. For metric tracking, our image-only method combines SAM3 video identities with calibration-guided mask agreement for multi-view identity association, recovering persistent 3D tracks without LiDAR or task-specific 3D training. Its calibration-conditioned geometry allows the procedure to be instantiated at different calibrated multi-camera intersections without layout-specific retraining. On 20 human-reviewed clips from six intersections, the generated tracks achieve 66.9 MOTA within the defined multi-view evaluation scope. For structured vision-language reasoning, a human-reviewed MLLM pipeline mines high-value clips and uses a constrained full-context Oracle to construct bbox-grounded predictive QA without exposing future evidence to evaluated models. The resulting RISE-VQA dataset contains 33,910 QA pairs from 557 clips across 16 intersections and 61 roadside views. Its intersection-held-out RISE-Bench evaluates semantic choices, coordinates, future boxes, and interaction sets with deterministic task-specific metrics. Experiments show consistent benefits from domain adaptation and generally from temporal context, while revealing persistent challenges in spatial grounding, future localization, and interaction reasoning.

</details>


### [177] [Remote-Sensing City Layout Extraction with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16484)
*Zigan Zhou,Kai Li,Yupeng Deng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Code-as-City casts urban-layout extraction from a single top-down image as constrained code generation with a multimodal large language model (MLLM). It produces a renderable 3D city layout and an orthographic semantic projection over shared geometry, achieving 41.1% mIoU and 48.3% gIoU on CityLayout-100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing remote-sensing systems output detection boxes, semantic masks, or vector boundaries, which locate classes and support image-plane scoring but do not form an executable layout preserving object identities, typed relations, topology, and regeneration rules.

Method: An image model generates an aligned five-class semantic layout prior, then three ordered MLLM passes use the image and prior to recover roads, land-cover regions and relations, and buildings. Deterministic normalization converts the accumulated records into a city graph and a restricted layout program, whose execution creates a renderable 3D city layout and an orthographic semantic projection.

Result: On CityLayout-100 (100 scenes), the framework achieves 41.1% mean intersection-over-union and 48.3% global intersection-over-union.

Conclusion: Visual observations can be translated into inspectable, editable city code with coupled planar and 3D outputs, providing quantitative evidence for code-based urban layout representation.

Abstract: Remote-sensing systems usually describe urban content with detection boxes, semantic masks, or vector boundaries. Such outputs locate classes and support image-plane scoring, yet they do not by themselves constitute an executable layout that retains object identities, typed relations, topology, and regeneration rules. Code-as-City instead casts urban-layout extraction from a single top-down image as constrained code generation with a multimodal large language model (MLLM). An image model first produces an aligned five-class semantic layout prior. Three ordered MLLM passes use the image and this prior to recover roads, land-cover regions and relations, and buildings. Deterministic normalization converts the accumulated records into a city graph and a restricted layout program. Executing the program creates a renderable 3D city layout and an orthographic semantic projection over shared geometry. The projection admits pixel-level comparison with remote-sensing masks, while named objects, relations, and editing operations remain available for synchronized regeneration of both views. Evaluated on the 100 scenes of CityLayout-100, the complete framework obtains 41.1% mean intersection-over-union and 48.3% global intersection-over-union. This result provides quantitative evidence that visual observations can be translated into inspectable, editable city code with coupled planar and 3D outputs.

</details>


### [178] [HiFi-BRep: High-Fidelity Latent Representation for Robust B-Rep Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16485)
*Junhao Hou,Chenqi Luo,Pufan Wang,Jiaying Lu,Yusheng Liu,Feiwei Qin,Meie Fang,Kun Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HiFi-BRep is a novel framework for boundary representation (B-Rep) generation that addresses representation and generation brittleness through a topology-aware encoder (using learnable queries and topology-guided attention) and a single-stage parallel decoder with differentiable manifold constraints, achieving state-of-the-art structural validity and geometric fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct synthesis of high-fidelity and structurally valid B-Reps remains a major challenge. Existing deep generative methods suffer from representation brittleness (padding noise, feature contamination) and generation brittleness (sequential error propagation, train-inference mismatch due to non-differentiable validity enforcement).

Method: Two synergistic contributions: (1) a topology-aware encoder that eliminates padding via learnable queries and prevents feature contamination with topology-guided attention; (2) a single-stage decoder that jointly predicts geometry and topology in parallel, embedding core manifold constraints as a differentiable learning objective for mutual guidance without cascaded errors.

Result: Extensive experiments show HiFi-BRep significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both structural validity and geometric fidelity for high-quality B-Rep synthesis. Code and models are publicly available.

Conclusion: HiFi-BRep provides a robust solution for high-quality B-Rep synthesis by overcoming both representation and generation brittleness through topology-aware encoding and joint parallel decoding with differentiable manifold constraints.

Abstract: Boundary representation (B-Rep) generation is a fundamental task in computer-aided design, yet the direct synthesis of high-fidelity and structurally valid B-Reps remains a major challenge. Existing deep generative methods suffer from two forms of brittleness: representation brittleness, caused by padding noise and feature contamination in the latent space, and generation brittleness, stemming from sequential error propagation and a train-inference mismatch due to non-differentiable validity enforcement. We propose HiFi-BRep, a novel framework that addresses these limitations through two synergistic contributions. First, a topology-aware encoder constructs a high-fidelity latent representation by eliminating padding via learnable queries and preventing feature contamination with topology-guided attention. Second, a single-stage decoder jointly predicts geometry and topology in parallel, embedding core manifold constraints as a differentiable learning objective. This design ensures mutual guidance between geometry and topology while avoiding cascaded errors. Extensive experiments show that HiFi-BRep significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both structural validity and geometric fidelity, providing a robust solution for high-quality B-Rep synthesis. Code and models are publicly available at https://github.com/1nnoh/HiFi-BRep.

</details>


### [179] [Towards Real-Time and Adaptable LiDAR Scene Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16490)
*Azhar Hussian,Martin Vossiek,Vasileios Belagiannis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RapidLiDAR is a LiDAR scene completion method that replaces the traditional initialize-and-refine paradigm with a learned adaptive initialization module that predicts spatially varying displacements for partial input points, followed by a multi-scale reconstruction module using voxel and BEV feature maps. It achieves state-of-the-art completion performance on SemanticKITTI and KITTI-360 while completing a full scene in 0.1 seconds—2.3x faster than prior methods and matching the 10 Hz acquisition rate of automotive LiDAR sensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LiDAR scene completion methods are either too slow (generative models that iteratively refine noise) or suffer from limitations in coverage of large gaps and occluded regions due to fixed noise scales that require manual recalibration per sensor configuration. There is a need for a fast, adaptive approach that does not rely on manual noise tuning.

Method: RapidLiDAR consists of two key components: (1) an adaptive initialization module that predicts spatially varying displacement for each partial input point to expand partial observations into a coarse scene initialization adapted to local geometry without manual noise tuning, and (2) a multi-scale reconstruction module that refines point positions by querying multi-scale 3D voxel and 2D BEV feature maps. The architecture replaces point-neighborhood operators (farthest point sampling, k-NN) with voxel- and BEV-based feature extraction for speed and resolution-agnostic handling.

Result: On SemanticKITTI and KITTI-360, RapidLiDAR achieves completion performance on par with the state of the art while completing a full scene in 0.1 seconds, which is 2.3 times faster than the fastest prior method and matches the 10 Hz acquisition rate of typical automotive LiDAR sensors.

Conclusion: RapidLiDAR demonstrates that treating initialization as a learned, data-driven component enables real-time LiDAR scene completion without sacrificing quality, taking a step toward practical deployment in autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: LiDAR scene completion is a key component of 3D perception in autonomous driving, where the scene must be completed in real time to be usable in downstream tasks. Existing approaches typically follow an initialize-and-refine paradigm, in which a coarse initialization of the scene is first constructed, then refined into complete 3D geometry. Generative models are slower because they iteratively refine random Gaussian noise into the scene, while non-generative methods perturb the partial scene with a fixed noise scale, which limits coverage of large gaps and occluded regions and requires manual recalibration for each new sensor configuration. We present RapidLiDAR, a LiDAR scene completion method that treats the initialization itself as a learned, data-driven component. We propose an adaptive initialization module that predicts a spatially varying displacement for each partial input point, expanding the partial observations into a coarse scene initialization adapted to the local geometry, without requiring manual noise tuning. To refine this coarse initialization into a complete and coherent scene, we additionally propose a multi-scale reconstruction module that further refines point positions by querying multi-scale 3D voxel and 2D BEV feature maps constructed from the input scan. By replacing point-neighborhood operators such as farthest point sampling and $k$-nearest neighbor search with voxel- and BEV-based feature extraction, our architecture is faster and can handle different input resolutions by design. Experiments on SemanticKITTI and KITTI-360 show that our method achieves completion performance on par with the state of the art while completing a full scene in 0.1 seconds, which is 2.3 times faster than the fastest prior method. This matches the 10 Hz acquisition rate of typical automotive LiDAR sensors, taking a step toward real-time LiDAR scene completion.

</details>


### [180] [MLLM-Guided Semantic Correction for Text-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16513)
*Junhao Chen,Zheqi Lv,Keting Yin,Shengyu Zhang,Zhou Zhao,Feiyang Chen,Xinyu Duan,Baoxing Huai,Fei Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free, interpretable mid-generation correction framework that integrates multimodal large language model (MLLM) feedback into the diffusion sampling loop to detect and correct semantic deviations during text-to-video generation, using a Semantic Assessment Supervisor and a Semantic Modification Assistant for continuous self-reflection and latent trajectory intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-video generation models suffer from semantic errors such as missing objects, incorrect attributes, or mismatched actions. Existing semantic correction methods either optimize before sampling or refine after sampling, but detecting and correcting semantic deviations during the generation process remains underexplored.

Method: The framework integrates MLLM feedback directly into the diffusion sampling loop through two key modules: (1) a Semantic Assessment Supervisor that generates intermediate preview frames for semantic evaluations and deviation diagnostics, and (2) a Semantic Modification Assistant that corrects semantic drift during inference via controllable latent trajectory intervention.

Result: The method improves semantic alignment, visual fidelity, and temporal consistency without modifying model parameters, validated through extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed training-free mid-generation correction framework effectively addresses semantic errors in text-to-video generation by enabling continuous self-reflection during the diffusion sampling process, offering a parameter-free approach that improves semantic alignment, visual fidelity, and temporal consistency.

Abstract: Recent advances in diffusion models and Transformer architectures have led to significant progress in text-to-video generation. However, these models often suffer from semantic errors such as missing objects, incorrect attributes, or mismatched actions. Although some semantic correction methods perform optimization before sampling or refinement after sampling, how to detect and correct semantic deviations during the video generation process remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce a training-free, interpretable mid-generation correction framework that integrates multimodal large language model (MLLM) feedback directly into the diffusion sampling loop. Our framework achieves diffusion trajectory correction by injecting semantic evaluation signals during video synthesis, enabling the model to optimize the generated content through continuous self-reflection. We propose two key modules: a Semantic Assessment Supervisor that generates intermediate preview frames for semantic evaluations and deviation diagnostics, and a Semantic Modification Assistant that corrects semantic drift during inference via a controllable latent trajectory intervention. Our method improves semantic alignment, visual fidelity, and temporal consistency without modifying model parameters. We validate the effectiveness of our approach through extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks.

</details>


### [181] [Matched Outcomes, Divergent Gaze: How Foveated MLLMs Search Compared to Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16514)
*Mohamed Amine Kerkouri,Marouane Tliba,Aladine Chetouani,Ulas Bagci,Alessandro Bruno*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper compares three multimodal large language models (MLLMs) against human eye-movement scanpaths on goal-directed visual search (COCO-Search18). Driving each model fixation-by-fixation through matched human foveated views, the authors find that models match or exceed humans on target presence decisions and acquisition efficiency, but their gaze processes are not human—producing low-entropy, large-amplitude, highly self-consistent scanpaths that diverge from human temporal dynamics. The gap lies on a temporal process axis that standard answer-alignment and saliency metrics fail to capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether multimodal LLMs, given the same foveated input as humans, search the same way humans do—bearing on their validity as models of human vision and on attention-alignment scoring methods.

Method: Three general-purpose MLLMs are driven fixation-by-fixation through identical, human-matched foveated views on goal-directed search (COCO-Search18). Models are assessed along three dissociable axes: (1) decision of target presence, (2) efficiency of reaching the target, and (3) the gaze process itself (scanpath statistics).

Result: The axes dissociate. Models match or exceed humans on target presence decisions (near-ceiling detection of present targets) and acquire targets on the first saccade more often than humans. However, the gaze process is non-human: all three models share a signature of low-entropy, large-amplitude, self-consistent scanpaths that agree with themselves far more than two humans agree with each other. Matched retinal input reproduces where humans look but not how looking unfolds over time, and no degradation regime recovers human-like search at human-like success.

Conclusion: The human-model gap sits on a temporal/process axis that answer-alignment and saliency metrics do not measure. Such metrics therefore cannot certify human-like vision, and zero-shot models are suitable for outcome and spatial questions but not for temporal, process-level ones.

Abstract: Human visual search is serial: the fovea must land on a candidate to confirm it, and those landings form a scanpath. Whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs), given the same foveated input, search as humans do bears on their use as models of human vision and on attention-alignment scores. We compare three general-purpose MLLMs with human eye-movement scanpaths on goal-directed search (COCO-Search18), driving each model fixation by fixation through an identical, human-matched foveated view and assessing it along three axes: the decision of target presence, the efficiency of reaching the target, and the gaze process itself. The axes dissociate. On the decision and on target acquisition the models match or exceed humans, detecting present targets near ceiling and reaching them on the first saccade more often than people do. The gaze process is not human. Under the human-matched condition, all three share one signature: low-entropy, large-amplitude, self-consistent scanpaths that agree with themselves far more closely than two humans agree with each other. That is consistent with a single-pass, non-serial architecture rather than a limit of acuity. Matched retinal input reproduces where humans look but not how the looking unfolds in time, and no degradation regime recovers human-like search at human-like success. The gap sits on a process axis that answer-alignment and saliency metrics do not measure. Because they miss it, such metrics cannot certify human-like vision, and zero-shot models suit outcome and spatial questions but not temporal, process-level ones.

</details>


### [182] [FLEET: Token-Based Feature Extraction for Event Camera-based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16523)
*Tristan Gottwald,Maximilian Schier,Melanie Schaller,Bodo Rosenhahn*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FLEET is a feature extractor for event cameras that processes event sequences directly using random Fourier features and cross-attention, compressing variable-length streams into fixed-size latent representations. This decouples computational cost from sensor resolution, enables end-to-end RL without auxiliary losses or pretraining, and achieves SOTA performance with improved robustness to observation frequency variations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event cameras offer asynchronous, sparse, low-latency data streams that should be ideal for control policies, but existing RL approaches fail to exploit these properties: CNN-based methods aggregate events into grids (coupling compute to resolution and blurring temporal info), while generative baselines require pretraining on trajectory data. There is a need for a method that fully leverages event camera properties.

Method: FLEET processes event sequences directly using random Fourier features and cross-attention. The architecture compresses variable-length event streams into fixed-size latent representations, decoupling the backbone's inference cost from sensor resolution and enabling end-to-end learning without auxiliary losses or trajectory pretraining.

Result: Validated on a new high-throughput benchmark, FLEET surpasses state-of-the-art performance and demonstrates superior robustness to variations in observation frequencies compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: Sequence-based event processing with FLEET effectively exploits event camera properties, achieving better performance and robustness than existing approaches while avoiding the limitations of grid-based aggregation and pretraining dependence.

Abstract: Event cameras generate asynchronous, high-frequency data streams offering spatially sparse information at lower latency than traditional cameras.In principle, these properties should be ideal for the design of control policies.However, reinforcement learning research in this field remains limited as existing approaches fail to fully exploit the sensor's properties.CNN-based methods negate the sensors benefits by aggregating events into sparse grids. This couples compute cost to sensor resolution and blurs the temporal information. Meanwhile, existing generative baselines rely on the availability of trajectory data to pretrain the model. We propose FLEET (Feature Learning from Events via Efficient Tokenization), a feature extractor that processes event sequences directly. Leveraging random Fourier features and cross-attention, our architecture compresses variable streams into fixed-size latent representations. This decouples inference cost of the feature extractor's backbone from the sensor's resolution, enabling end-to-end learning without auxiliary losses. We validate FLEET on a new, high-throughput benchmark. The results demonstrate that our sequence-based approach surpasses SOTA performance and exhibits superior robustness to variations in observation frequencies.

</details>


### [183] [Automatic Cephalometric Landmark Localization on CBCT-Derived Digitally Reconstructed Radiographs for Skeletal Malocclusion Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16535)
*Benjamin Hou,Konstantinia Almpani,Janice S. Lee,Zhiyong Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CephViT, a Vision Transformer model, automates 2D lateral cephalometric landmark localization achieving 1.28±1.42 mm mean radial error and 92.0% detection rate at 3.0 mm. It was applied to CBCT-derived digitally reconstructed radiographs (DRRs), yielding skeletal malocclusion classification accuracy (70.0%) comparable to manual annotation (68.3%), demonstrating feasibility of automated cephalometric analysis on DRRs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual cephalometric landmark annotation is labor-intensive and difficult to scale, yet essential for craniofacial assessment. An automated solution is needed to streamline this process and enable downstream cephalometric analyses.

Method: CephViT uses a Vision Transformer architecture for automated landmark localization on 2D lateral cephalograms. It was trained on a public dataset and tested on a private cohort where CBCT volumes were converted to lateral cephalogram-like digitally reconstructed radiographs (DRRs). Landmark coordinates were normalized to a common coordinate frame, and skeletal malocclusion classification was performed using shared landmarks between reference and DRR pipelines.

Result: CephViT achieved a mean radial error of 1.28±1.42 mm and 92.0% successful detection rate at 3.0 mm threshold. Skeletal malocclusion classification using DRR-localized landmarks achieved 70.0% accuracy, comparable to 68.3% with manually annotated reference landmarks.

Conclusion: Automated cephalometric analysis on CBCT-derived DRRs is feasible for skeletal malocclusion assessment, with performance comparable to manual annotation pipelines.

Abstract: Manual cephalometric landmark annotation is important for craniofacial assessment but is labor-intensive and difficult to scale. We introduce CephViT, a Vision Transformer-based model for automated 2D lateral cephalometric landmark localization, and evaluate its use in downstream skeletal malocclusion classification. CephViT was trained and benchmarked on a public lateral cephalogram dataset, achieving a mean radial error of 1.28 +/- 1.42 mm and a successful detection rate of 92.0% at 3.0 mm. Because the private evaluation cohort consisted of 3D CBCT scans, lateral cephalogram-like digitally reconstructed radiographs (DRRs) were generated from each volume and used as 2D inputs to the landmark localization model. Landmark coordinates were normalized into a common coordinate frame, and skeletal malocclusion classification was performed using landmarks shared between the reference and DRR-based pipelines. Classification performance using DRR-localized landmarks was comparable to that obtained using manually annotated reference landmarks, with accuracies of 70.0% and 68.3%, respectively. These results support the feasibility of automated cephalometric analysis on CBCT-derived DRRs for skeletal malocclusion assessment.

</details>


### [184] [Supervising the Path to Fine Scales: GalerkinFlow for Scientific-Field and Image Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16546)
*Zikang Zhan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GalerkinFlow is an equation-agnostic super-resolution framework that supervises entire coarse-to-fine reconstruction paths rather than just final outputs, using intermediate velocity loss, pseudo-endpoints, coarse-anchor supervision, and finite-difference constraints to achieve state-of-the-art results on Navier-Stokes and Darcy Flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard super-resolution models only supervise the final high-resolution output, providing no control over how predictions evolve between the downsampled observation and the fine target. This limits learning efficiency and interpretability of the reconstruction process.

Method: GalerkinFlow turns each coarse-fine pair into supervision along an entire reconstruction path. At random intermediate states, the model predicts coarse-to-fine residual velocity and uses coarse-anchor points to define pseudo-endpoints. The reconstruction loss of the pseudo-endpoint is exactly related to the intermediate velocity loss via a known time-dependent weight. Additional supervision is applied to the coarse endpoint used in one-step inference, and a finite-difference objective constrains local spatial variation. The architecture combines convolutional features with scale-conditioned Galerkin operator mixing, requiring no governing equation or physical metadata.

Result: GalerkinFlow achieves the lowest raw-space errors among evaluated equation-agnostic baselines on Navier-Stokes and Darcy Flow, and remains competitive on DIV2K natural image super-resolution.

Conclusion: By providing dense supervision throughout the reconstruction path, GalerkinFlow demonstrates that supervising intermediate states improves super-resolution performance without requiring domain-specific equations, making it a versatile approach across physics and natural image domains.

Abstract: Most super-resolution models learn from paired data by supervising only the final high-resolution output.
