This provides little control over how the prediction should evolve between the downsampled observation and its fine target. We introduce GalerkinFlow, an equation-agnostic framework that turns each coarse--fine pair into supervision along an entire reconstruction path. At a random sample of intermediate states on the reconstruction path, the model predicts the coarse-to-fine residual velocity and uses coarse-anchor point to define a pseudo-endpoint. We show that the reconstruction loss of this pseudo-endpoint is exactly related to the intermediate velocity loss through a known time-dependent weight. Consequently, every intermediate state contributes supervision toward the same fine target, rather than serving only as an internal step toward an endpoint loss. Because intermediate states already reveal part of the missing fine-scale structure, we additionally supervise the coarse endpoint used during one-step inference. A finite-difference objective further constrains local spatial variation. GalerkinFlow combines convolutional features with scale-conditioned Galerkin operator mixing and requires no governing equation or physical metadata. It achieves the lowest raw-space errors among the evaluated equation-agnostic baselines on Navier--Stokes and Darcy Flow, while remaining competitive on DIV2K.

</details>


### [185] [SQuad: Sub-Quadratic Attention Distillation for Efficient Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16585)
*Animesh Karnewar,Denis Korzhenkov,Amirhossein Habibian,Mohsen Ghafoorian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SQuad is a Sub-Quadratic Attention Distillation framework for Video Diffusion Transformers that replaces expensive quadratic self-attention with O(n√n) attention by distilling a pretrained full softmax attention DiT into a SQuad-Attention model via two-stage distillation (Flow-Matching SFT + DMD2). It matches teacher quality on VBench while cutting attention FLOPs by ~67×, latency by ~11×, and enabling video generation in just 6 NFEs instead of 100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video DiTs are bottlenecked by self-attention's quadratic O(n²) cost in the number of tokens, which limits video resolution and duration. Existing linear O(n) and low-rank O(nk) attention surrogates fail to match the expressivity of full softmax attention, leaving a quality gap.

Method: Proposes SQuad-Attention with sub-quadratic O(n√n) complexity, balancing efficiency and expressivity. Instead of training from scratch, distills a pretrained full softmax attention Video DiT into the SQuad model via two stages: (1) Flow-Matching Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and (2) improved Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD2) for faster sampling.

Result: On Wan 2.2 5B text-to-video: SQuad matches the quadratic teacher on VBench (83.20 vs 83.08), reduces per-step per-block attention FLOPs by ~67×, attention latency by ~11×, end-to-end DiT latency by 2×, and generates videos in only 6 NFEs compared to the default 100.

Conclusion: SQuad demonstrates that sub-quadratic attention can match the quality of full softmax attention through distillation, achieving substantial computational savings without sacrificing expressivity, and enables much faster sampling through DMD2 distillation.

Abstract: Video Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) spend most of their compute inside the Self-Attention operation, whose cost grows quadratically, $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$, with the number of latent tokens $n$. For the task of video generation, the token count is large, so this term dominates runtime and memory, and thereby caps the resolution and duration we can generate. Linear $\mathcal{O}(n)$ and low-rank $\mathcal{O}(nk)$ surrogates of Self-Attention trade the full softmax $QK^T$ for cheaper kernels, but rarely recover the original's expressivity, leaving a stubborn quality gap. Motivated by this, we propose SQuad, a Sub-Quadratic Attention Distillation framework that achieves a complexity of $\mathcal{O}(n\sqrt{n})$ in the resulting distilled Attention, naturally balancing the efficiency v/s expressivity trade-off. Instead of training our own Video DiT from scratch, which is prohibitively expensive, we fit a pretrained full softmax Self-Attention DiT into our proposed SQuad-Attention one by distilling the former in two stages: Flow-Matching Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), followed by improved Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD2) which additionally makes the sampling more efficient. On the Wan~2.2 5B text-to-video model, SQuAD matches the quadratic teacher on VBench ($83.20$ v/s $83.08$) while cutting the per-step per-block attention FLOPs by $\sim$$67\times$ and attention latency by $\sim$$11\times$, and end-to-end DiT latency by 2$\times$, all while also generating a video in only $6$ Neural Functional Evaluations (NFEs) instead of the default $100$.

</details>


### [186] [Ultra: Unsupervised Cross-Task Optimization for Reliable Restoration Segmentation Collaboration under Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16589)
*Shiqin Wang,Zhiqian Li,Haoyuan Du,Junming Chen,Jiayuan Li,Tianrun Xu,Haoyang Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes Ultra, a novel unsupervised restoration-segmentation collaborative learning framework for adverse weather semantic segmentation that reframes cross-task interaction as direction selection under uncertainty and causal effect estimation, achieving state-of-the-art results on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UDA-ASS methods assume mutual benefit between restoration and segmentation, but under severe degradation and without target-domain supervision, cross-task optimization directions become unidentifiable, leading to hallucination-driven error propagation.

Method: Ultra introduces two key components: (1) CTDN (Complementary Task Direction Network) which generates candidate optimization directions using complementary visual structures and semantic information and performs cooperative direction selection between restoration and segmentation, and (2) CMIL (Causal Mechanism Intervention Learning) which reformulates cross-task information transfer from correlation-based propagation into causal effect assessment to suppress hallucination propagation.

Result: Extensive experiments on three widely used UDA-ASS benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art segmentation performance. The framework also achieves better unsupervised restoration results than existing UDA-ASS methods and generalizes to unsupervised restoration and object detection collaboration tasks.

Conclusion: Cross-task collaboration in the unsupervised setting should be treated as causal direction selection under uncertainty rather than implicitly assumed mutual guidance, and the proposed framework validates this by outperforming prior methods while also generalizing to other collaborative tasks.

Abstract: Unsupervised Domain Adaptation for Adverse Weather Semantic Segmentation (UDA-ASS) aims to transfer semantic knowledge from labeled normal-weather images to unlabeled adverse environments. Existing approaches implicitly assume that restoration and segmentation provide mutually beneficial guidance. However, under severe degradation and without target-domain supervision, the validity of cross-task optimization directions becomes fundamentally unidentifiable, leading to hallucination-driven error propagation. In this work, we propose a novel Unsupervised Restoration-Segmentation Collaborative Learning Framework (Ultra), which reframes cross-task interaction as direction selection under uncertainty and causal effect estimation, enabling reliable collaboration through candidate direction generation and intervention-based filtering. In detail, we propose CTDN and CMIL. The former exploits complementary visual structures and semantic information to generate candidate optimization directions and performs cooperative direction selection between restoration and segmentation. The latter reformulates cross-task information transfer from correlation-based propagation into causal effect assessment, suppressing hallucination propagation. Extensive experiments on three widely used UDA-ASS benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art segmentation performance. Beyond segmentation, our framework achieves better unsupervised restoration results than existing UDA-ASS restoration methods and generalizes to unsupervised restoration and object detection collaboration tasks. Code and models will be available at https://github.com/Wang-Shiqin/Ultra.

</details>


### [187] [Towards Zero-Shot Domain Generalization for ID Cards Presentation Attack Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16591)
*Mario Nieto-Hidalgo,Juan M. Espin,Juan E. Tapia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Presentation-Attack Detection (PAD) method for national ID cards that requires only four genuine samples per class, using a Prototypical Network head with an EfficientNet-V2-b0 backbone and an episodic training regime that varies card domains while keeping PAD classes fixed. The method achieves ~9% average Equal Error Rate on multi-country datasets and DLC-2021, outperforming softmax and CLIP zero-shot baselines even with single-country training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PAD for national ID cards suffers from a lack of publicly available genuine samples, which limits generalization across countries and makes scalable cross-jurisdictional deployment difficult.

Method: Two innovations: (1) a Prototypical Network head with EfficientNet-V2-b0 backbone requiring only four genuine samples per class to form reliable prototypes; (2) an episodic training regime that keeps PAD classes fixed while varying the card domain, enabling the network to learn universal attack cues independent of specific card types.

Result: Evaluated on a large multi-country dataset and the public DLC-2021 benchmark, the method achieves an average Equal Error Rate of ~9%, outperforming conventional softmax and CLIP zero-shot baselines even when trained with data from a single source country.

Conclusion: The approach provides accurate, privacy-preserving PAD while minimizing data collection requirements, facilitating scalable cross-jurisdictional remote onboarding.

Abstract: Presentation-Attack Detection (PAD) for national ID cards is limited by the lack of publicly available genuine samples, making it difficult for systems to generalize across countries. This paper introduces two main innovations: (1) a Prototypical Network head using an EfficientNet-V2-b0 backbone that requires only four genuine samples per class to create reliable prototypes; and (2) an episodic training regime that keeps PAD classes fixed while varying the card domain, allowing the network to learn universal attack cues.
  Evaluated on a large multi-country dataset and the public DLC-2021 benchmark, this method achieves an average Equal Error Rate of around 9\%, outperforming conventional softmax and CLIP zero-shot baselines even with data from a single source country. This approach provides accurate, privacy-preserving PAD while minimizing data collection, facilitating scalable cross-jurisdictional remote onboarding.

</details>


### [188] [GeoPose: Patient-agnostic CTA-to-DSA registration through projection-space calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16600)
*Rudolf L. M. van Herten,Robert Graf,Paula Feldman,Johannes C. Paetzold*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoPose is a population-trained framework for fast 3D-to-2D registration between intraoperative biplanar DSA and pre-procedural CTA, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy (mPCD 5.8mm) in 0.15s without patient-specific training, with optional low-budget optimization improving to 4.6mm in ~2s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for aligning intraoperative biplanar DSA to pre-procedural CTA are either optimization-based (sensitive to initialization, computationally expensive) or learning-based (require patient-specific training), neither of which is well-suited for rapid clinical deployment.

Method: GeoPose estimates C-arm pose in a learned canonical frame using a population-trained framework, then transfers it to the native frame of unseen CTA via projection-space calibration and transform composition. A population-trained residual network refines the pose, optionally followed by low-budget image-driven optimization. No patient-specific adaptation or explicit inter-volume preregistration is needed.

Result: On 80 DSA observations from 20 held-out patients, optimization-free GeoPose achieved mPCD of 5.8mm and clDice of 0.45 in 0.15s (vs 14.5mm/0.28 for best baseline). With 25 optimization iterations, it reached mPCD of 4.6mm and clDice of 0.58 in ~2s (vs 14.6mm/0.15 for native-initialized optimization under same budget).

Conclusion: GeoPose provides rapid native-frame registration with fixed population-level weights, eliminating the need for patient-specific adaptation while maintaining geometric correspondence required for downstream biplanar 3D vascular reconstruction.

Abstract: Aligning intraoperative biplanar digital subtraction angiography (DSA) to pre-procedural computed tomography angiography (CTA) requires rapid and accurate 3D-to-2D registration. Optimization-based methods are sensitive to initialization and may require hundreds of iterations, whereas learning-based approaches commonly rely on patient-specific training. We propose GeoPose, a population-trained framework that estimates the C-arm pose in a learned canonical frame and transfers it to the native frame of an unseen CTA through projection-space calibration and transform composition. A population-trained residual network refines the pose, followed optionally by low-budget image-driven optimization. GeoPose requires neither patient-specific adaptation nor explicit inter-volume preregistration. On 80 DSA observations from 20 held-out patients, optimization-free GeoPose achieved a carotid mean projected centerline distance (mPCD) of 5.8 mm and a clDice of 0.45, compared with 14.5 mm and 0.28 for the best-performing baseline, while requiring only 0.15 s. After 25 optimization iterations, GeoPose reached an mPCD of 4.6 mm and a clDice of 0.58 in approximately two seconds. Under the same budget, native-initialized optimization achieved 14.6 mm and 0.15, respectively. GeoPose thus provides rapid native-frame registration with fixed population-level weights and the geometric correspondence required for downstream biplanar 3D vascular reconstruction.

</details>


### [189] [Interactive Whole Slide Images for RL-based Tumour Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16607)
*Mohamad Mohamad,Francesco Ponzio,Maxime Gassier,Nicolas Pote,Xavier Descombes*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an end-to-end reinforcement learning framework for sequential tumour segmentation directly on whole-slide images (WSIs), treating the slide as a hierarchical multi-resolution interactive environment navigated by an agent using movement, zooming, and selection actions. The agent uses an actor-critic architecture trained with PPO, achieving comparable segmentation quality to patch-based methods while reducing inference to seconds per slide.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WSI analysis is computationally challenging due to the extremely large spatial resolution of slides and the sparse distribution of tumour regions. Existing methods typically rely on pre-defined candidate patches, which may be inefficient or suboptimal.

Method: The WSI is formulated as a hierarchical multi-resolution environment. An agent navigates this environment using movement, zooming, and tumour selection actions. The agent jointly processes local observations and a global thumbnail representation within an actor-critic architecture trained using proximal policy optimization (PPO).

Result: Experiments on pulmonary adenocarcinoma WSIs demonstrate the feasibility of direct sequential tumour segmentation on full slides, achieving comparable coarse segmentation quality to patch-based approaches at similar magnification levels, while reducing inference time to a few seconds per slide.

Conclusion: Modelling WSIs as interactive environments provides a promising direction for RL-based computational pathology. The paper also analyzes the impact of environment design and action-space granularity on performance.

Abstract: Whole-slide image (WSI) analysis remains computationally challenging due to the extremely large spatial resolution of slides and the sparse distribution of tumour regions. We propose an end-to-end reinforcement learning framework for sequential tumour segmentation directly on WSIs. Instead of treating the slide as a predefined collection of candidate patches, we formulate the WSI itself as a hierarchical multi-resolution environment through which an agent navigates using movement, zooming, and tumour selection actions. The agent jointly processes local observations and a global thumbnail representation within an actor-critic architecture trained using proximal policy optimization (PPO). Experiments on pulmonary adenocarcinoma WSIs demonstrate the feasibility of direct sequential tumour segmentation on full slides, achieving comparable coarse segmentation quality relative to patch-based approaches operating at similar magnification levels, while reducing inference time to a few seconds per slide. We further analyse the impact of environment design and action-space granularity. Our results suggest that modelling WSIs as interactive environments provides a promising direction for RL-based computational pathology

</details>


### [190] [Beyond Accuracy: Assessing Calibration of Geospatial Foundation Models and Their Sensitivity to Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16614)
*Nils Lehmann,Jakob Gawlikowski,Burak Ekim,Isaac Corley,Xiao Xiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper criticizes the standard practice of ranking Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) solely by accuracy on clean benchmarks, arguing that deployment in critical Earth observation tasks requires evaluating calibration and robustness under distribution shift. The authors show that across 16 frozen encoders, 4 classification and 5 segmentation datasets, and 2 stress axes, all encoders degrade under corruption and ranking changes. Notably, EO-pretrained encoders are indistinguishable from ImageNet-pretrained ones on clean accuracy/calibration, but drift further into overconfidence under shift. CKA analysis attributes this to representational rigidity. UQ methods (temperature scaling, deep ensembles) fail to counteract degradation, while a Gaussian-process probe trades clean calibration for improved robustness under severe cloud. Confidence-based abstention cannot defer around confidently wrong predictions, so the authors advocate multi-condition, multi-metric benchmark evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geospatial Foundation Models are typically ranked by average accuracy on standard benchmarks, which is too narrow for critical Earth observation deployment. The paper argues that calibration—the agreement between a model's confidence and its correctness—and robustness under distribution shift are essential additional angles that are currently overlooked.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic empirical study across 16 frozen encoders, 4 classification and 5 segmentation datasets, applying two orthogonal stress axes (corruption types/severity). They measure clean accuracy, clean calibration, and calibration under shift. They use centered kernel alignment (CKA) to analyze representation shifts, apply three uncertainty quantification methods (temperature scaling, deep ensembles, Gaussian-process probes), and run selective prediction/abstention experiments.

Result: (1) Every encoder degrades as corruption intensifies and rankings change. (2) EO-pretrained and ImageNet-pretrained encoders are indistinguishable on clean accuracy/calibration, and EO pretraining provides no more stability under shift. (3) GeoFMs drift further into overconfidence than ImageNet encoders at every severity and corruption family. (4) CKA ties this to representational rigidity—EO embeddings move less under corruption while losing as much task information. (5) Temperature scaling and deep ensembles cannot counteract degradation; a Gaussian-process probe halves ECE under severe cloud but triples it on clean data. (6) Confidence-based abstention fails to defer confidently wrong predictions.

Conclusion: Benchmark rankings should operate across multiple conditions and metrics—including calibration and robustness under shift—rather than clean accuracy alone, to more holistically evaluate model development progress and close the gap toward real-world deployment in critical Earth observation tasks.

Abstract: Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) are most commonly ranked and selected by accuracy on standard benchmark conditions via averaged ranks. We show that this protocol is too narrow: the promised deployment in critical EO tasks requires further angles of analysis, mainly calibration, the agreement between a model's confidence and its correctness. Across 16 frozen encoders, four classification and five segmentation datasets, and two orthogonal stress axes, every encoder degrades as corruption intensifies, and the ranking changes as well. Across the four classification benchmarks, EO-pretrained and ImageNet-pretrained encoders are indistinguishable on clean accuracy and clean calibration, and EO pretraining provides no more stability under shift than ImageNet pretraining. Under shift the GeoFMs drift further into overconfidence than the ImageNet-pretrained encoders, at every grade and in every corruption family. A centered kernel alignment (CKA) analysis ties this to representational rigidity: EO-pretrained embeddings move less under corruption while losing just as much task information and remaining overconfident. We apply three commonly explored uncertainty quantification methods and find that temperature scaling and deep ensembles cannot counteract the degradation, while a Gaussian-process probe roughly halves ECE under severe cloud only by tripling it on clean data. In selective prediction experiments, we find that confidence-based abstention cannot defer around confidently wrong predictions, and advocate that benchmark rankings and evaluations should therefore operate across a multitude of conditions and metrics to more holistically evaluate model development progress and close the gap to real world deployment scenarios.

</details>


### [191] [HarmTrace: Anchor-Calibrated Decoupled Optimization for Fine-Grained Target Identification in Harmful Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16622)
*Yujia Li,Yiqun Zhang,Zihan Cheng,Yijie Huang,Tenglong Ye,Zihan Wang,Xiaocui Yang,Shi Feng,Yifei Zhang,Daling Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper extends harmful meme detection from binary image-text harmfulness classification to fine-grained target identification (who/what is attacked and where), introducing the Meme3W dataset with human-verified annotations, a strict Joint Record Accuracy (JRA) metric, and HarmTrace—an anchor-calibrated decoupled optimization framework incorporating entity-aware SFT and Conditional Target-identification Policy Optimization (CTPO) to jointly improve harmfulness accuracy and JRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing harmful meme detection only predicts whether a meme is harmful, but a model can be correct on harmfulness while misidentifying the attacked target or its evidence. This creates a need for fine-grained target identification (target type, entity, textual mention, visual region) to make detection more reliable and interpretable.

Method: The paper introduces Meme3W, a unified human-verified dataset for fine-grained harmful meme target identification, and proposes HarmTrace, a framework with two stages: (1) entity-aware supervised fine-tuning to strengthen target-entity supervision, and (2) Conditional Target-identification Policy Optimization (CTPO) that decouples harmfulness and target-identification advantages, restricting optimization to label-correct harmful responses, using a Virtual Positive Anchor (VPA) for advantage normalization.

Result: HarmTrace improves both JRA and harmfulness accuracy across evaluated backbones, with JRA on Qwen3-VL-8B jumping from 17.58% to 52.51%. There is a substantial gap between harmfulness accuracy and JRA in representative multimodal LLMs, which HarmTrace narrows significantly.

Conclusion: Fine-grained target identification is a valuable and challenging extension of harmful meme detection, and the HarmTrace framework effectively narrows the gap between harmfulness accuracy and joint record-level target identification performance. The dataset, metrics, and code are released publicly.

Abstract: Multimodal harmful meme detection is typically formulated as image--text harmfulness classification. A model may correctly predict harmfulness while misidentifying the attacked target or its supporting evidence. We therefore extend harmful meme detection with fine-grained target identification, asking what type of target is attacked, who is targeted, and where the target appears in the meme. The model predicts harmfulness for every meme and, for harmful memes, outputs the target category, target entity, textual mention, and visual region. To support this task, we introduce Meme3W, which unifies multiple public harmful meme datasets and provides human-verified annotations for harmful instances. We further introduce Joint Record Accuracy (JRA), a strict record-level metric requiring the harmfulness label and all target-identification fields to be jointly correct. Experiments with representative multimodal large language models reveal a substantial gap between harmfulness accuracy and JRA. To narrow this gap, we propose HarmTrace, an anchor-calibrated decoupled optimization framework. HarmTrace strengthens target-entity supervision through entity-aware supervised fine-tuning. It then applies Conditional Target-identification Policy Optimization (CTPO) to decouple harmfulness and target-identification advantages, restricting target-identification optimization to label-correct responses for harmful examples. CTPO uses a Virtual Positive Anchor (VPA) as a fully correct reference for target-identification advantage normalization. HarmTrace improves both JRA and harmfulness accuracy across the evaluated backbones, with JRA on the Qwen3-VL-8B backbone increasing from 17.58\% to 52.51\%. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/llly1234/HarmTrace-for-Harmful-Memes.

</details>


### [192] [DRAFE: Domain-Robust Asymmetric Fusion of Heterogeneous Detection Transformers for Cross-City Fine-Grained Traffic Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16632)
*Divine Yao Agbobli,Geoffery Eyram Agorku,Israel Afriyie,Kwadwo Amankwah-Nkyi,Marvin Osei-Kuffour,Richmond Owusu Duah,Bright Seglah,Kelvin Asamoah Terkper,Kwabena Amoako Adjei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces DRAFE, a domain-robust ensemble framework combining LW-DETR and RF-DETR detectors for cross-city fine-grained traffic object detection, achieving 0.4022 mAP and 6th place among 25 teams on AI City Challenge 2026 Track 6.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning object detectors face challenges in achieving both fine-grained vehicle recognition and robust cross-city domain generalization, which are critical for intelligent transportation systems.

Method: DRAFE combines independently trained LW-DETR and RF-DETR detectors using a two-stage training strategy: (1) pretraining on diverse public traffic datasets with pseudo-label expansion and human-in-the-loop annotation refinement to create a curated corpus of 6,049 images and 203,619 annotations, followed by (2) challenge-compliant fine-tuning on Project Hafnia Track 6 data. At inference, the framework applies anchor-conditioned class-consistent matching, reliability-weighted coordinate fusion, agreement-aware confidence recalibration, and complementary hypothesis recovery.

Result: DRAFE achieves 0.4022 mAP on AI City Challenge 2026 Track 6, ranking sixth among 25 participating teams, and improves by 0.0553 mAP over a preliminary ensemble under identical benchmark conditions.

Conclusion: The DRAFE framework demonstrates that complementary detector ensembling with a robust two-stage training strategy and sophisticated fusion techniques can effectively address cross-city domain generalization for fine-grained traffic object detection.

Abstract: Deep learning-based object detectors are fundamental to intelligent transportation systems, enabling traffic monitoring, vehicle analytics, and infrastructure management. However, achieving both fine-grained vehicle recognition and robust cross-city domain generalization remains challenging. We present the Domain-Robust Asymmetric Fusion Ensemble (DRAFE), which combines independently trained LW-DETR and RF-DETR detectors for cross-city fine-grained traffic object detection. DRAFE employs a two-stage training strategy that first pretrains complementary detectors on diverse public traffic datasets using pseudo-label expansion and human-in-the-loop annotation refinement, producing a curated corpus of 6,049 images and 203,619 annotations, before challenge-compliant fine-tuning on the Project Hafnia Track 6 dataset. At inference, DRAFE applies anchor-conditioned class-consistent matching, reliability-weighted coordinate fusion, agreement-aware confidence recalibration, and complementary hypothesis recovery. On AI City Challenge 2026 Track 6, DRAFE achieves 0.4022 mAP, ranks sixth among 25 participating teams, and improves by 0.0553 mAP over a preliminary ensemble evaluated under identical benchmark conditions.

</details>


### [193] [Training-Free Reconstruction-Based AI-Generated Image Detectors Are Inherently Vulnerable to Adversarial Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16646)
*Roman Demchenko,Jonas Ricker,Asja Fischer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces two novel adversarial attack methods targeting reconstruction-based AI image detectors. By adding imperceptible perturbations, the attacks inflate the reconstruction error distance, causing fake images to be misclassified as real. The attacks are shown to be effective across three generators and three detectors, robust to real-world degradations, and naturally transfer across detectors due to their shared underlying principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstruction-based detectors are promising for transparent, training-free synthetic image identification, but their adversarial robustness is poorly understood compared to classifier-based methods. This gap motivates investigating whether these detectors can be easily fooled by adversarial examples.

Method: The authors propose two novel attack methods that construct imperceptible adversarial examples to artificially increase the distance between original and reconstructed images, causing fake images to be wrongly classified as real by autoencoder-based detectors.

Result: Evaluation across three state-of-the-art generators and three detectors shows significantly decreased detection performance, even when attacked images undergo real-world degradations. The adversarial examples also naturally transfer across detectors since they share the same reconstruction principle.

Conclusion: Reconstruction-based detectors have an inherent vulnerability to adversarial attacks, as demonstrated by the attack transferability and robustness to degradations, pointing to a fundamental weakness in this detection paradigm.

Abstract: The impressive visual quality and ubiquity of AI-generated images call for reliable and robust detection methods. Reconstruction-based detectors have emerged as a promising direction for transparent and training-free identification of synthetic images. However, due to their fundamentally different mode of operation (compared to standard, classifier-based methods), little is known about their adversarial robustness. In this work, we propose two novel attack methods targeted at detectors that leverage autoencoder reconstruction error. We find that by constructing imperceptible adversarial examples, the distance between original and reconstruction can be artificially increased, causing fake images to be wrongly classified as real. Our evaluation including images from three state-of-the-art generators and three detectors demonstrates that detection performance is significantly decreased, even if attacked images additionally undergo real-world degradations. Critically, our adversarial examples naturally transfer across detectors, as they all share the same principle, pointing towards an inherent vulnerability of reconstruction-based detectors.

</details>


### [194] [X$^2$Localizer: Cross-grained Alignment for Progressive Cross-view Video Geo-localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16658)
*Zichao Zeng,Weijia Fan,Yufan Chen,June Moh Goo,Junwei Zheng,Ruiping Liu,Kunyu Peng,Jiaming Zhang,Rainer Stiefelhagen,Jan Boehm*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Progressive Cross-view Video Geo-localization (PCVG), a new deployment-oriented evaluation protocol and extension of CVG that supports varying temporal budgets, prefix-based inference, random-start evaluation, and long-range localization. The authors propose X²Localizer, a cross-grained alignment framework with global prefix-to-aerial retrieval and token-aggregated frame-aerial-tile matching using a budget-dependent asymmetric objective, plus a Sliding-Window Re-Localization (SWRL) strategy for failure recovery. Results show maintained full-video performance with improvements in early localization and significant gains in single-frame settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Cross-view Video Geo-localization (CVG) methods rely on fixed-length inputs and post-hoc refinement, which prevents online-oriented localization under partial or dynamic observations in real-world deployment scenarios.

Method: The paper introduces PCVG as a new evaluation protocol enabling progressive localization. X²Localizer jointly supervises global prefix-to-aerial retrieval and token-aggregated frame-aerial-tile matching with a budget-dependent asymmetric objective, augmented by a Sliding-Window Re-Localization (SWRL) strategy for dynamic candidate region refreshing.

Result: X²Localizer preserves conventional full-video performance (+0.1 Recall@1, +0.3 Recall@10) while substantially improving early localization. In single-frame settings, it achieves +4.7 Recall@1 and +11.5 Recall@10 over the previous state-of-the-art. With SWRL, robust progressive localization is enabled under random-start and long-distance scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed approach narrows the gap between benchmark evaluation and real-world deployment by enabling progressive, online-oriented cross-view video geo-localization while maintaining competitive full-video performance.

Abstract: Cross-view Video Geo-localization (CVG) aims to localize ground-view videos by retrieving their corresponding geo-tagged aerial images. However, CVG approaches rely on fixed-length inputs and post-hoc refinement, hindering online-oriented localization under partial or dynamic observations. In this work, we formulate Progressive Cross-view Video Geo-localization (PCVG) as a deployment-oriented extension and evaluation protocol of CVG, enabling localization under varying temporal budgets, prefix-based inference, random-start evaluation, and long-range localization with interruptions. To explore PCVG, we introduce X$^2$Localizer, a cross-grained alignment framework that jointly supervises global prefix-to-aerial retrieval and token-aggregated frame--aerial-tile matching with a budget-dependent asymmetric objective. Furthermore, we introduce a Sliding-Window Re-Localization (SWRL) strategy that dynamically refreshes candidate regions for failure recovery and long-range deployment without full-sequence reprocessing. Extensive experiments show that X$^2$Localizer preserves conventional full-video performance, with marginal gains of +0.1 Recall@1 and +0.3 Recall@10, while substantially improving early localization. In the challenging single-frame setting, X$^2$Localizer improves coarse retrieval by +4.7 Recall@1 and +11.5 Recall@10 over the previous state-of-the-art method. With SWRL, our approach further enables robust progressive localization under random-start and long-distance scenarios, narrowing the gap between benchmark evaluation and real-world deployment.

</details>


### [195] [Turning spectra into images improves plant trait retrieval with 2D-CNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16661)
*Javier Lopatin,Teja Kattenborn,Eya Cherif,Sebastián Moreno*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether transforming 1D hyperspectral reflectance spectra into 2D image representations improves multi-trait prediction in plants using CNNs. Comparing nine transformations with EfficientNet-B0 on the GreenHyperSpectra dataset, a simple direct reshape performed best (R²=0.684), improving on the 1D SOTA baseline (+0.097). A 2D masked autoencoder pretrained on 139,000 unlabeled spectra also exceeded all 1D self-supervised counterparts. However, cross-dataset generalization was poor. Attribution analysis via Integrated Gradients and Grad-CAM validated model interpretation for traits with sharp absorption features (protein, leaf water) but not others (carotenoids, LAI).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning approaches process hyperspectral spectra as 1D sequences, which limits their ability to capture long-range inter-band dependencies. The authors ask whether converting spectra into 2D image representations could improve multi-trait prediction with CNNs.

Method: Nine different 1D-to-2D transformation methods were compared using EfficientNet-B0 on the GreenHyperSpectra dataset (7,897 labeled spectra, eight traits, 400-2450 nm), benchmarked against published 1D CNN results. A 2D masked autoencoder (MAE-2D) was pretrained on 139,000 unlabeled spectral images with linear probing evaluation. Integrated Gradients and Grad-CAM were applied and unfolded back to the spectral axis for interpretation, validated against PROSAIL radiative-transfer model simulations.

Result: The simple direct Reshape transformation performed best (R²=0.684±0.001), improving on the 1D baseline (R²=0.587, +0.097). Linear probing with MAE-2D reached R²=0.646, exceeding all 1D self-supervised counterparts including fine-tuned MAE-1D (R²=0.641). Cross-dataset evaluation showed significant accuracy loss across all models. Attribution analysis showed good agreement with PROSAIL for protein (r=0.45) and leaf water (r=0.33), but poor agreement for carotenoids (r=0.06) and LAI (r=-0.11).

Conclusion: The representational advantage of 2D spectral images—rather than architectural complexity or ImageNet pretraining—is what drives the performance gain over 1D approaches. However, cross-dataset robustness remains a challenge, and the model's interpretability aligns with established leaf chemistry primarily for traits with sharp absorption features.

Abstract: Hyperspectral reflectance spectroscopy enables non-destructive estimation of plant functional traits, yet current deep learning approaches process spectra as one-dimensional sequences, which limits how they capture long-range inter-band dependencies. We asked whether transforming 1D spectra into 2D image representations improves multi-trait prediction with convolutional neural networks (CNN). We compared nine transformations using EfficientNet-B0 on the GreenHyperSpectra dataset (7,897 labeled spectra, eight traits, 400-2450 nm), benchmarked against published 1D CNN results on the same split. Trained from scratch, the simplest transformation, a direct Reshape of the spectrum into a 2D grid, performed best ($R^2 = 0.684 \pm 0.001$) and improved on the state-of-the-art 1D baseline ($R^2 = 0.587$, $+0.097$). We then pretrained a 2D masked autoencoder (MAE-2D) on 139,000 unlabeled spectral images. Linear probing, which freezes the encoder and trains only a multilayer perceptron head, reached $R^2 = 0.646$ and exceeded every 1D self-supervised counterpart, including the fine-tuned MAE-1D ($R^2 = 0.641$). Under cross-dataset evaluation all models lost most of their accuracy and none beat the 1D baseline significantly. To identify which wavelengths drive each prediction, we applied Integrated Gradients and Grad-CAM and unfolded band importance back to the spectral axis. Protein ($r = 0.45$) and leaf water ($r = 0.33$) agreed with sensitivities simulated by the PROSAIL radiative-transfer model, while carotenoids ($r = 0.06$) and leaf area index ($r = -0.11$) did not, showing that the model reads established leaf chemistry for traits with sharp absorption features. The representational advantage of 2D spectral images, rather than architectural complexity or ImageNet pretraining, drives the gain over 1D approaches.

</details>


### [196] [Concept-based explanation of gene expression prediction from H&E images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16669)
*Amos Muench,Jonathan Thielmann,Reduan Achtibat,Maximilian Dreyer,Philip Bischoff,Caroline Forsythe,Hamidreza Parand,Thomas Walter,David Horst,Sebastian Lapuschkin,Wojciech Samek,Teresa Gabriela Krieger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces an explainable framework for virtual spatial transcriptomics from H&E images that combines ViT-aware layer-wise relevance propagation with relaxed archetypal TopK sparse autoencoder-based concept discovery, providing both local heatmaps and global morphological concept explanations tied to transcriptional programs. Applied to colorectal cancer ST data, it accurately predicts iCMS subtypes (F1=0.872 spatially, 0.819 aggregated, 0.770 in external TCGA COAD), stratifies patient outcomes, and builds a relevance-based concept atlas linking morphology to molecular phenotypes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explainability methods for ViT-based pathology foundation models are limited to local heatmaps and fail to reveal how morphological concepts contribute to spatial transcriptomics predictions, leaving a gap in interpretable links between tissue morphology and transcriptional programs.

Method: A ViT-based framework for virtual ST prediction that integrates ViT-aware layer-wise relevance propagation with relaxed archetypal TopK sparse autoencoder-based concept discovery, providing both local explanations and global morphological concept insights aligned with transcriptional programs.

Result: The framework accurately predicts clinically relevant ST signatures and molecular phenotypes. iCMS classification achieves weighted F1 scores of 0.872 (spatial), 0.819 (aggregated), and 0.770 (TCGA COAD external validation), with both predictions stratifying patient outcomes. Relevance-derived concepts provide a more direct link between morphology and predictions than activation-based concepts.

Conclusion: The framework establishes a general strategy for concept-based explanation of spatial prediction in pathology, providing interpretable links between tissue morphology and molecular phenotypes, and is readily applicable to a broad range of ViT-based pathology models.

Abstract: Recent advances in pathology foundation models have enabled accurate prediction of spatial transcriptomics (ST) from routine H&E images. However, existing explainability methods for vision transformer (ViT)-based models are largely limited to local heatmaps and do not reveal how morphological concepts contribute to ST predictions. Here, we introduce an explainable framework that combines relevance propagation and concept discovery to link transcriptional programs to tissue morphology. We developed a ViT-based framework for virtual ST from H&E images that combines ViT-aware layer-wise relevance propagation with relaxed archetypal TopK sparse autoencoder-based concept discovery. This approach provides both local explanations and global insights into the morphological patterns associated with transcriptional programs. We applied the framework to colorectal cancer ST data from the HEST-1k cohort and evaluated its generalizability in TCGA COAD. Our architecture accurately predicts clinically relevant ST signatures and accompanying molecular phenotypes. Measured and predicted gene expression profiles reveal substantial spatial heterogeneity of the colorectal cancer subtypes iCMS2 and iCMS3 across a large number of samples. Spatially resolved and aggregated iCMS classification achieve weighted F1 scores of 0.872 and 0.819 (0.770 in TCGA COAD), respectively, and both stratify patient outcome. Beyond prediction, our framework establishes a relevance-based concept atlas linking molecular phenotypes to histopathological representations. Comparison of activation- with relevance-derived concepts demonstrates that relevances provide a more direct link between tissue morphology and downstream predictions. We establish a general strategy for concept-based explanation of spatial prediction, and our framework is readily applicable to a broad range of ViT-based pathology models.

</details>


### [197] [How Sampling Strategy Affects Imbalance Mitigation in LiDAR Segmentation: A Study of Structured vs. Random Point-Based Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16673)
*Antonis Savva,Christos Kyrkou,Theocharis Theocharides*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks six reweighting schemes and five imbalance-aware losses for LiDAR point cloud semantic segmentation across three datasets and two architectures, finding that inverse-frequency weighting degrades performance while uniform weighting often matches complex losses, with effectiveness depending on the interplay between sampling strategy, imbalance severity, and data acquisition characteristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class imbalance is a significant challenge in LiDAR point cloud semantic segmentation for autonomous navigation and urban mapping, yet while many mitigation techniques exist for 2D vision, their effectiveness in the 3D domain remains poorly understood.

Method: A systematic benchmark of six reweighting schemes and five imbalance-aware losses across three datasets (DALES, S3DIS, STPLS3D) using two architectures (KPConv, RandLA-Net), complemented by loss landscape analysis to understand the geometric properties underlying performance differences.

Result: Inverse-frequency weighting degrades performance by up to 12% compared to uniform weighting with catastrophic minority class failures. Uniform weighting performs within 2% of complex losses for structured sampling (KPConv), but sees up to a 4.6% gap for random sampling (RandLA-Net). Loss landscape analysis shows imbalance ratio shapes landscape geometry for structured sampling on real LiDAR data but decouples on synthetic data, while random sampling shows high sensitivity to dataset geometry regardless of imbalance ratio.

Conclusion: The effectiveness of class imbalance mitigation approaches in point-based architectures depends on the interaction between sampling strategy (structured vs. random), imbalance severity, and data acquisition characteristics, suggesting that no single mitigation approach is universally effective across settings.

Abstract: Class imbalance in LiDAR point clouds poses challenges for semantic segmentation in autonomous navigation and urban mapping. While 2D vision has numerous mitigation techniques, their effectiveness in 3D remains unclear. We benchmark six reweighting schemes and five imbalance-aware losses across three datasets (DALES, S3DIS, STPLS3D) using two architectures (KPConv, RandLA-Net). Inverse-frequency weighting degrades performance by up to 12% compared to uniform weighting, with catastrophic failures in minority classes. Uniform weighting performs within 2% of complex losses for structured sampling (KPConv) but benefits less for random sampling (RandLA-Net, up to 4.6% gap). Loss landscape analysis reveals a complex interplay: for structured sampling, imbalance ratio determines landscape geometry on real LiDAR data but decouples from it on synthetic data; for random sampling, landscapes show high sensitivity to dataset geometry regardless of imbalance ratio. For the two evaluated point-based architectures, these results suggest that the interaction between sampling strategy (structured vs. random), imbalance severity, and data acquisition characteristics shapes which mitigation approaches are effective.

</details>


### [198] [Bridging the Gap between Labeled and Unlabeled Data via Unified Flow with Feature Memory Bank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16681)
*Shanwen Wang,Xin Sun,Danfeng Hong,Junyu Dong,Patrick Le Callet*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UFFM is a novel semi-supervised semantic segmentation method for remote sensing that introduces a unified flow combining an external visual foundation model with an RS domain teacher to generate less biased pseudo-labels, and a feature memory bank that dynamically aligns class-specific features between labeled and unlabeled data, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In semi-supervised semantic segmentation (S4), independently training labeled and unlabeled data causes labeled data to dominate, severely degrading pseudo-label quality. This domain imbalance creates a gap in both optimization and feature representation between labeled and unlabeled data in remote sensing scenarios.

Method: UFFM consists of two key components: (1) Unified Flow (UF) - a new training flow that generates less biased pseudo-labels by combining an external visual foundation model (VFM) with an RS domain teacher, and jointly optimizes labeled and pseudo-labeled data under a unified training objective; (2) Feature Memory Bank (FMB) - a memory module that dynamically updates class-specific features during training and reduces feature discrepancy between labeled and unlabeled data through class-feature alignment.

Result: Extensive experiments on RS datasets demonstrate that UFFM outperforms state-of-the-art S4 methods, and ablation results confirm the effectiveness of both contributions in bridging the optimization and feature representation gaps between labeled and unlabeled data.

Conclusion: The proposed UFFM effectively addresses the labeled-data dominance problem in remote sensing semi-supervised semantic segmentation by unifying the training objective and aligning feature representations, achieving superior performance over existing methods. Code is released publicly.

Abstract: Although semi-supervised semantic segmentation ($\text{S}^4$) utilizes abundant unlabeled data to reduce manual labeling burdens, independent training of labeled and unlabeled data causes the former to dominate, which severely degrades pseudo-label quality. To address this challenges, we propose a novel remote sensing (RS) $\text{S}^4$ method via unified flow with feature memory bank (UFFM). Specifically, UFFM comprises two key innovations: unified flow (UF) and feature memory bank (FMB). The UF is a new training flow that generates less biased pseudo-labels by combining an external visual foundation model (VFM) with an RS domain teacher, and jointly optimizes labeled and pseudo-labeled data under a unified training objective. The FMB is a novel memory module for $\text{S}^4$ that dynamically updates class-specific features during training and reduces the feature discrepancy between labeled and unlabeled data through class-feature alignment. To verify the effectiveness of our model, we conduct extensive experiments on RS datasets. The experimental results show the superiority of our method over SOTA $\text{S}^4$ methods. Moreover, the results demonstrate the effectiveness of our contributions in bridging the optimization and feature representation gap between labeled and unlabeled data. Our code is released at \href{https://github.com/wangshanwen001/RS-UFFM}{https://github.com/wangshanwen001/RS-UFFM}.

</details>


### [199] [AnchorScore: A CLIP-Based Diagnostic of MLLM Annotation Difficulty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16690)
*Yan Ma,Lizhuo Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes AnchorScore, a low-cost a priori diagnostic (per-class zero-shot CLIP accuracy) for ranking classes by expected MLLM annotation difficulty. It correlates significantly with per-class MLLM accuracy (rho=0.769, p=0.002) and is validated across multiple datasets and models, enabling practical applications like CLIP/MLLM routing that saves ~44% MLLM cost with up to +23pp accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs are widely used for automated annotation but have highly variable per-class accuracy (12%-98% across classes). Measuring this accuracy is expensive (14 hours for one 27B MLLM on 5,416 images vs. 3 minutes for frozen CLIP). A low-cost signal for ranking classes by expected MLLM annotation difficulty a priori is needed to direct expensive MLLM evaluation to the most informative classes.

Method: The paper systematically evaluates AnchorScore (full-frame per-class zero-shot CLIP accuracy, building on the AnchorProxy construct) as an a priori difficulty diagnostic. It compares AnchorScore against alternative difficulty predictors (DINOv2, ResNet-50, SigLIP, MLLM self-verbalized uncertainty) and validates on classroom behavior data (SCB5, 13 classes, 6 MLLMs), with independent replication on Stanford40 Actions and additional domain tests (medical, satellite imagery). It also develops three practical applications: hybrid CLIP/MLLM routing, prompt disambiguation, and review-priority prediction.

Result: AnchorScore correlates with per-class MLLM accuracy (Spearman rho=0.769, p=0.002, n=13). No alternative predictor showed significant class-level correlation. Cross-model consensus control suggests AnchorScore captures a shared class-difficulty factor. Replication on Stanford40 Actions yields rho=0.817 (p<0.001), with strongest association on activity-recognition data. The routing application achieves up to +23pp over CLIP-only at ~44% MLLM cost savings.

Conclusion: AnchorScore does not estimate exact MLLM accuracy but provides a low-cost ranking signal that directs expensive MLLM evaluation to the classes where it is most informative, enabling cost-effective hybrid annotation strategies.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are widely used for automated annotation, yet their per-class accuracy varies widely (e.g., 12%-98% across the 13 classes of three classroom sub-datasets) and is expensive to measure: evaluating one 27B MLLM on 5,416 validation images takes roughly 14 hours, whereas a frozen-CLIP pass over the same images completes in about 3 minutes. A low-cost signal for ranking classes by expected MLLM annotation difficulty a priori remains underexplored. Building on the AnchorProxy construct (per-class zero-shot CLIP accuracy) introduced in the companion study, this paper systematically evaluates its full-frame formulation, termed AnchorScore here, as an a priori diagnostic that flags the classes MLLMs are least likely to annotate reliably.
  On classroom behavior data (SCB5, 13 classes, 6 MLLMs), AnchorScore correlates with per-class MLLM accuracy (Spearman rho = 0.769, p = 0.002, n = 13). None of the alternative difficulty predictors (DINOv2, ResNet-50, SigLIP, or MLLM self-verbalized uncertainty) showed a significant class-level correlation at n = 13. A cross-model consensus control suggests AnchorScore primarily captures a shared class-difficulty factor rather than a CLIP-specific signal. An independent replication on Stanford40 Actions yields a nearly identical effect (rho = 0.817, p < 0.001); the association is strongest on activity-recognition data and attenuates on medical and satellite imagery.
  Three practical applications follow: a deployable hybrid CLIP/MLLM routing strategy (predicted-class routing: up to +23 pp over CLIP-only at roughly 44% MLLM cost savings), prompt disambiguation on hard classes (exploratory), and review-priority prediction for human verification. AnchorScore does not estimate exact MLLM accuracy; it provides a low-cost ranking signal that directs expensive MLLM evaluation to the classes where it is most informative.

</details>


### [200] [MIRROR: Multimodal Intelligent Radiology Reasoning and Observation Reporter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16709)
*Vignesh Nagarajan,Sriram Venkatapathy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MIRROR is a research prototype that separates machine learning classification from report generation in radiology by having a report writer that receives only labels, probabilities, and anatomical regions—never the image—so it cannot fabricate findings the classifier didn't make. It uses a multi-label classifier, Grad-CAM localizer, and decoupled language layer, with a shared registry for multiple modalities. The paper highlights that while the classifier achieves decent discrimination (macro AUROC 0.729 on ChestMNIST), aggregate metrics like Brier score can flatter models that essentially do nothing under class imbalance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiologists reading model outputs face two problems: models return numbers without reasoning, and systems that convert numbers to prose can add claims the model never made. MIRROR is built to separate these failures and ensure generated reports are auditable against the underlying probability vector.

Method: MIRROR chains three components: (1) a multi-label classifier that outputs labels and probabilities, (2) a Grad-CAM localizer that maps positive findings to named anatomical regions, and (3) a report writer that receives only the labels, probabilities, and regions—never the image. A unified registry holds taxonomy, anatomy, and phrasing for chest X-ray, brain MRI, and head CT, making modality addition a data change rather than a code change.

Result: On ChestMNIST, the classifier reaches macro AUROC 0.729 with better-than-chance ranking on all 14 labels, achieving 1.6–6.8x the precision of a random ranker. However, at the default 0.5 threshold it emits no positive prediction for 11 of 14 labels, and its Brier score of 0.045 nearly matches the 0.047 from a predictor that ignores the image entirely. The paper also demonstrates a report sentence stating a cardiothoracic ratio the system never measured.

Conclusion: The discrimination achieved by the classifier is real, but the decisions are not—under class imbalance typical in radiology, aggregate metrics like Brier score and AUROC can flatter models that effectively do nothing. Metrics should be reported against a baseline floor (e.g., an image-ignoring predictor), and the decoupled architecture of MIRROR ensures generated report findings remain auditable against the probability vector.

Abstract: A radiologist reading a model's output faces two problems. The model returns a number and no reason, and any system that turns that number into readable prose can quietly add claims the model never made. MIRROR is a research prototype built to separate those failures. It chains a multi-label classifier, a Grad-CAM localizer that turns each positive finding into a named anatomical region, and a report writer that receives the labels, probabilities, and regions but never the image. Because the language layer cannot see pixels, it cannot assert a finding the classifier did not make. We are precise about what that buys: a MIRROR report's findings are auditable against the probability vector, while the sentences framing them are ordinary generated text, and we show one stating a cardiothoracic ratio the system never measured. One registry holds the taxonomy, anatomy, and phrasing for chest X-ray, brain MRI, and head CT, so adding a modality is a data change; all three are routed and tested, one is trained. On ChestMNIST that classifier reaches macro AUROC 0.729 and ranks better than chance on all 14 labels, at 1.6 to 6.8 times the precision a random ranker would get. Yet at the default 0.5 threshold it emits no positive prediction at all for 11 of them, and its excellent-looking Brier score of 0.045 sits beside the 0.047 earned by a predictor that ignores the image. The discrimination is real; the decisions are not. Under the class imbalance normal in radiology, aggregate metrics flatter models that do nothing, and should be reported against that floor.

</details>


### [201] [PersonaShot: Benchmarking Person-Centric Narrative Continuity in Multi-Shot Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16717)
*Yuji Wang,Yuheng Chen,Teng Hu,Ran Yi,Yijia Hong,Han Feng,Weijian Cao,Chengjie Wang,Lizhuang Ma,Jiangning Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PersonaShot is the first person-centric benchmark for evaluating narrative continuity in multi-shot video generation, containing ~1,000 multi-shot segments and 16 metrics spanning physical continuity, affective dynamics, and cinematic grammar. It introduces human-aligned specialist evaluators distilled from a large multimodal teacher, revealing that current models show a clear gap between perceptual quality and cross-shot narrative continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks only assess character appearance or individual-shot quality without measuring whether physical and emotional states remain coherent across cuts. They also lack criterion-specific evaluation methods, even though physical continuity, facial dynamics, and cinematic relations require different types of evidence.

Method: PersonaShot provides a benchmark with ~1,000 multi-shot segments and 16 metrics evaluated across three temporal levels (within-shot states, cross-shot transitions, sequence-level trajectories). It includes lightweight criterion-specific evaluators distilled from a large multimodal teacher, each grounded in the visual, temporal, or relational evidence required by its metric, and aligned with expert human judgments.

Result: Evaluation reveals distinct capability profiles across state-of-the-art models and a clear gap between perceptual quality and cross-shot narrative continuity. Even visually compelling videos frequently exhibit physical-state resets, abrupt affective shifts, and broken cinematic relations across shots. Human studies demonstrate strong agreement between the proposed evaluators and expert judgments.

Conclusion: PersonaShot fills a critical gap in video generation evaluation by focusing on person-centric narrative continuity across shots, and its specialist evaluators provide criterion-specific, human-aligned assessment that reveals limitations invisible to perceptual quality metrics alone.

Abstract: Video generation is rapidly evolving from single-shot clips to multi-shot narratives, where the human character serves as the core narrative anchor. However, existing benchmarks mainly assess character appearance or individual-shot quality, without measuring whether physical and emotional states remain coherent across cuts. They also rarely provide criterion-specific evaluation methods, although physical continuity, facial dynamics, and cinematic relations require different visual, temporal, and relational evidence. To address these limitations, we introduce PersonaShot, the first person-centric benchmark for narrative continuity in multi-shot video generation. PersonaShot contains approximately 1,000 multi-shot segments and 16 metrics spanning physical continuity, affective dynamics, and cinematic grammar. \textbf{\textit{1)} Narrative Continuity Benchmark:} We evaluate character coherence across three temporal levels: within-shot states, cross-shot transitions, and sequence-level trajectories. \textbf{\textit{2)} Human-Aligned Specialist Evaluators:} We distill reasoning from a large multimodal teacher into lightweight criterion-specific evaluators, each grounded in the visual, temporal, or relational evidence required by its metric, and align them with expert human judgments. \textbf{\textit{3)} Systematic Evaluation and Insights:} Our evaluation reveals distinct capability profiles across state-of-the-art models and a clear gap between perceptual quality and cross-shot narrative continuity. Even visually compelling videos frequently exhibit physical-state resets, abrupt affective shifts, and broken cinematic relations across shots. Human studies further demonstrate strong agreement between our evaluators and expert judgments.

</details>


### [202] [CytoFormer: A Molecularly Supervised Cell Foundation Model for Histopathology Cell Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16718)
*Jialu Yao,Songhao Li,Alina Yu,Zhi Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CytoFormer is a cell foundation model trained on 15.4 million H&E image patches paired with spatial transcriptomics-derived cell type labels across 16 organs, achieving 0.85 accuracy on spatially held-out tissue and outperforming six pathology foundation models on expert-annotated benchmarks, including in label-efficient active learning settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying cell types directly from routine H&E histology would enable single-cell analysis at scale, but existing approaches rely on manual pathologist annotations which are slow, expensive, and unreliable for many cell types. The authors propose using imaging-based spatial transcriptomics as molecular supervision to overcome these limitations.

Method: The authors assembled 81 paired Xenium sections spanning 16 organs, derived per-cell labels through clustering, marker-gene annotation, organ-wise human review, and quality control, mapping them onto 23 commonly reported cell types. They trained CytoFormer, a cell foundation model with a multi-task per-organ classification head, on 15.4 million cells each paired with an H&E image patch and cell type label.

Result: CytoFormer achieved 0.85 accuracy and 0.78 macro-F1 across all 16 organs on spatially held-out tissue, reproduced tissue architecture on held-out sections, outperformed six pathology foundation models on four expert-annotated benchmarks (including unseen organs and cell types), and achieved an F1 of 0.82 in active learning from few annotations, leading the strongest baseline by 0.13 in F1.

Conclusion: CytoFormer demonstrates that paired H&E and spatial transcriptomics can be transformed into a reusable, label-efficient representation for cell-level analysis of routine histology, enabling accurate cell typing directly from standard H&E sections and generalizing beyond pretraining domains.

Abstract: Identifying cell types directly from routine haematoxylin and eosin (H&E) histology would enable single-cell analysis at scale, but training such models has relied on manual pathologist annotations, which are slow, expensive and unreliable for many cell types. We instead supervise morphology with molecules. Imaging-based spatial transcriptomics profiles individual cells in situ on a section that can afterwards be stained with H&E, so that molecular identity and morphology are observed for the same physical cell. We assembled 81 such paired Xenium sections spanning 16 organs, derived per-cell labels by clustering, marker-gene annotation, organ-wise human review and quality control, and mapped them onto the cell types commonly reported in each organ. This yielded 15.4 million cells, each with a paired H&E image patch and one of 23 cell types, on which we trained CytoFormer, a cell foundation model with a multi-task, per-organ classification head. On spatially held-out tissue CytoFormer reached an accuracy of 0.85 and a macro-F1 of 0.78 across all 16 organs, and its predictions reproduced the tissue architecture of an entire held-out section. The representation also transfers: with the encoder frozen, a linear head on CytoFormer features performed better than six pathology foundation models on four expert-annotated benchmarks, including on organs and cell types that were not part of pretraining. Finally, in an interactive active-learning setting, CytoFormer's embeddings are markedly more label-efficient than existing pathology foundation models, detecting normal epithelium amid look-alike tumour with an F1 of 0.82 from only a few annotations and leading the strongest baseline by 0.13 in F1. CytoFormer turns paired H&E and spatial transcriptomics into a reusable, label-efficient representation for cell-level analysis of routine histology.

</details>


### [203] [GenRouter: Unified Workflow Routing for Agentic Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16721)
*Harold Haodong Chen,Zhiyu Hou,Wen-Jie Shu,Weilin Ruan,Yingjie Xu,Litao Guo,Ying-Cong Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GenRouter is a unified workflow routing framework for agentic image generation that standardizes diverse pipelines into universal primitives and adaptively routes prompts to optimal workflows, achieving superior visual alignment while reducing execution costs by over 95% and latency by 65% compared to heavyweight static pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic image generation workflows operate in isolated silos with fixed one-size-fits-all topologies, leading to severe compute-mismatch where simple queries are forced through unnecessarily heavy pipelines.

Method: GenRouter first formulates GenCanvas to standardize diverse agentic pipelines into universal foundational primitives and executable templates. It then adaptively routes heterogeneous prompts to optimal workflows via three components: (i) demand profiling, (ii) experience matching, and (iii) Pareto filtering.

Result: Extensive experiments show GenRouter achieves superior visual alignment while reducing execution costs by over 95% and latency by 65% compared to heavyweight static pipelines. The system also self-evolves via accumulated experience, enabling robust zero-shot generalization that boosts performance and halves computational overhead.

Conclusion: GenRouter demonstrates that adaptive workflow routing with continuous self-evolution can effectively bridge the compute-mismatch gap in agentic image generation, delivering both better visual quality and dramatically lower computational costs.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of text-to-image (T2I) generation models has effectively solved the foundational challenge of raw pixel synthesis, shifting the community's focus toward fulfilling increasingly intricate user requests. While recent agentic image generation workflows enhance static inference with advanced capabilities like external knowledge retrieval and iterative reasoning, they mostly operate in isolated silos with fixed ``one-size-fits-all" topologies. This inevitably leads to severe compute-mismatch, where simple queries are forced through computationally heavy pipelines. To bridge this gap, we present GenRouter, the first unified workflow routing framework for agentic image generation. We first formulate GenCanvas, standardizing diverse agentic pipelines into a universal set of foundational primitives and executable templates. Operating over this unified space, GenRouter adaptively routes heterogeneous prompts to their optimal workflows via (i) demand profiling, (ii) experience matching, and (iii) Pareto filtering. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that GenRouter achieves superior visual alignment while reducing execution costs by over 95% and latency by 65% compared to heavyweight static pipelines. Furthermore, the system continuously self-evolves via accumulated experience, enabling robust zero-shot generalization that boosts performance and halves computational overhead.

</details>


### [204] [Unsupervised Anomaly Detection for Image Dataset Quality Assurance in Multi-Center Breast MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16725)
*Chiara Tappermann,Steffen Renisch,Lars Ole Schwen,Hans Meine,Horst K. Hahn,Eike Petersen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper builds a controlled benchmark of 17 QA-relevant anomaly types from six public breast MRI datasets and evaluates four unsupervised anomaly/OOD detection methods for automated dataset quality assurance in medical AI pipelines. The 3D reconstruction-based approach achieves the best balance of detection performance (AUROC 0.936) and generalization, while the projection-based method with positional encoding achieves the highest overall AUROC (0.954), though both hybrid methods and specific anomalies like implants/mastectomies remain challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Corrupted, inconsistent, or anomalous data threatens the safety and reliability of medical AI, yet scalable automated dataset quality assurance remains underdeveloped despite growing regulatory recognition of QA needs for high-risk medical AI applications.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark of 17 realistic anomaly types organized into a taxonomy based on human visual perception, spanning near-, medium-far-, and far-OOD samples from six public multi-center dynamic contrast-enhanced breast MRI datasets. They evaluate four methods: a projection-based method with domain-specific feature extraction and novel positional encoding, a reconstruction-based approach extended to 3D volumes with augmented training, and two unmodified hybrid OOD detection methods.

Result: Medium-far and far-OOD samples are reliably detected across methods. The 3D reconstruction-based approach achieves AUROC 0.936 with the best balance of detection and generalization to unseen institutions. The projection-based method with positional encoding achieves the highest overall AUROC of 0.954. Both hybrid methods show critical failure modes, and implants/mastectomies remain unsolved for all methods.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a foundation and practical guidance for scalable unsupervised QA in medical AI pipelines, demonstrating that domain-specific adaptation is necessary and that methods validated for one modality or anatomy may not generalize without modification.

Abstract: Corrupted, inconsistent, or anomalous data silently threatens the safety and reliability of medical AI. Despite growing regulatory recognition of dataset quality assurance (QA) for high-risk medical AI, scalable automated detection remains underdeveloped. We employ unsupervised anomaly detection (AD) and out-of-distribution (OOD) detection as an automated dataset QA mechanism for multi-center dynamic contrast-enhanced breast MRI.
  We build a controlled AD benchmark of 17 realistic QA-relevant anomaly types from six public datasets (protocol violations, processing errors, incorrect anatomical regions) and propose a taxonomy of radiological image anomalies based on human visual perception, enabling fine-grained analysis of AD failure modes. The benchmark includes near-, medium-far-, far-OOD samples, as well as in-distribution and external normal data. Four methods are evaluated: a projection-based method extended with a domain-specific feature extractor and a novel positional encoding, a reconstruction-based approach extended to full 3D volumes with an augmented training objective, and two unmodified hybrid OOD detection methods.
  Medium-far- and far-OOD samples are detected reliably, whereas near-OOD samples and external normal data from unseen institutions expose method-specific differences. The 3D reconstruction-based approach best balances detection performance (AUROC: 0.936) and generalization to unseen institutions. The projection-based method with positional encoding achieves the highest overall detection performance (AUROC: 0.954). Both hybrid methods exhibit critical failure modes, confirming that methods validated for one modality or anatomy may not generalize without domain-specific adaptation. Implants and mastectomies remain an open challenge for all methods. Our results establish a foundation and practical guidance on scalable unsupervised QA in medical AI pipelines.

</details>


### [205] [VicEdit: Learning to Edit Videos from Visual In-Context Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16745)
*Yuji Wang,Teng Hu,Yuheng Chen,Ran Yi,Han Feng,Weijian Cao,Chengjie Wang,Lizhuang Ma,Jiangning Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces Visual In-context Editing, a new paradigm for video editing that uses multi-modal visual guidance (single images, image pairs, video pairs) alongside textual instructions. It curates VicEdit-400K, the first large-scale dataset (400K samples across ten task types), and proposes the VicEdit framework with Modality-Adaptive Semantic Distillation and Dual-Context Injection to unify visual and textual editing signals, achieving state-of-the-art results on VicEditBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Textual instructions alone are insufficient for instruction-based video editing because they struggle to convey fine-grained textures and complex dynamics. The authors propose bridging this perceptual gap by elevating video editing from purely textual instructions to multi-modal visual guidance.

Method: The authors (1) curate VicEdit-400K, the first large-scale dataset for visual in-context video editing, using an automated pipeline with multi-dimensional filtering for quality; (2) propose VicEdit, a unified framework that bridges visual and textual contexts; (3) design Modality-Adaptive Semantic Distillation to extract modality-specific semantic tokens from heterogeneous visual references; and (4) use Dual-Context Injection to integrate visual tokens with textual instructions.

Result: VicEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance on VicEditBench across both basic instruction editing and visual in-context editing tasks, establishing visual in-context learning as a powerful and controllable paradigm for video editing.

Conclusion: Visual in-context learning is a powerful and controllable paradigm for video editing, enabling richer editing capabilities than text-only instruction-based approaches.

Abstract: Despite progress in instruction-based video editing, unimodal textual instructions inherently struggle to convey fine-grained textures and complex dynamics. To bridge this perceptual gap, we propose Visual In-context Editing, a new paradigm elevating video editing from textual instructions to multi-modal visual guidance encompassing single image, image pair, and video pair. To facilitate this paradigm, we curate VicEdit-400K, the first large-scale dataset for visual in-context video editing. We develop an automated pipeline to generate 400K high-quality samples across ten task types, ensuring superior visual fidelity and semantic consistency through multi-dimensional filtering. Leveraging this foundation, we introduce VicEdit, a unified framework to bridge visual and textual contexts. To adaptively extract editing semantics from heterogeneous references, we design Modality-Adaptive Semantic Distillation, which produces modality-specific semantic tokens from visual references. These tokens are then synergistically integrated with textual instructions through Dual-Context Injection, enabling the generation process to benefit from both visual and textual signals. Extensive evaluations on VicEditBench demonstrate that VicEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance across both basic instruction editing and visual in-context editing tasks, establishing visual in-context learning as a powerful and controllable paradigm for video editing.

</details>


### [206] [Beyond Uncertainty: Generalizable Failure Monitoring for Surgical Segmentation under Acquisition Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16748)
